EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION 

For many years principals and teachers have felt the need of a one-volume history which would review the major ideas and movements in Jewish history on a level suited to the needs of the eighth grade and the high school. Doctors 

Golub and Green have, in their "Short History of the Jews, set out to accomplish this purpose. Their main object was to present a unified picture of Jewish history, emphasizing not the details of the story, but rather the significant ideas and ideals, institutions and movements which one encounters when viewing the Jewish scene throughout the ages. 

The work is divided into four parts. Book I covers the period of the First Commonwealth; Book II, that of the 

Second Commonwealth; Book III, the Middle Ages; and Book IV, the Modern Era. 

For technical reasons we are issuing the book in three parts in an experimental mimeographed edition. Both the authors and the editor are exceedingly interested in securing the suggestions and the criticisms of teachers using this book. They are eager to know in what respects they have succeeded and particularly in what respects they have fallen short, and what changes should be made to improve the book.

In the meantime, we hope that this volume will serve as a review course for boys and girls in the upper grades of our schools.

EMANUEL GAMORAN 

ii

BOOK I 

THE FIRST COMMONWEALTH

ROC

H 

FROM PALESTINE TO THE UNITED STATES 

You are Jewish boys and girls, therefore, you are sent to a 

Jewish school. What makes you a Jewish boy or girl, and why are you sent to this school? The answer is, of course, simple. You are a child of Jewish parents; your parents are members of the 

Jewish people; and the Jewish people wants its children to study about Jewish things. But what is the Jewish people, and where does it live? We live in the United States, but do all other 

Jews live in the United States also? Do they all speak the English language? No. It cannot be, because you have heard of

Polish and German Jews. Clearly, therefore, not all Jews live in the United States, and not all speak English. In fact, the 

Year Book of 1944 showed that there were then about 14,000,000 Jews in the world, of whom only 4,800,000 lived in the United States. The majority of these have lived here only a short time. 

Sixty-five years ago there were only 250,000 Jews in the United States, and one hundred years ago only about 50,000. The 

Jews must have come here from some other country. 

True, all Americans are immigrants, but did the Jews come with the first settlers, or did they arrive later? And above all, where did they come from?

WHICH IS THE JEWISH HOMELAND? 

You say you understand. The Jews are an immigrant people, which means that they come from some land which is their home, where the majority still live. Englishmen live in England, Scotsmen in Scotland, Frenchmen in France, Germans in Germany. 

But where do the Jews live? If you will inquire which country is supposed to be the Jewish homeland, you will be told that it is Palestine. You will therefore expect that the Jews in America should have come from Palestine, but instead you learn that there are Polish Jews and German Jews, Hungarian, Roumanian, English, and French Jews, and also some Palestinian Jews, but very few of the last. Indeed, in Palestine there are altogether only 600,000; and almost all of these have come there during the last fifty years.

WHICH IS THE JEWISH LANGUAGE? 

All immigrant groups speak their own language. The Frenchman speaks French; Germans, German; Italians, Italian. What language did the Jews bring? Their language is supposed to be 

Hebrew, yet instead the great majority spoke a language called Yiddish, which is really a form of German. They do use Hebrew, but mainly for religious services. Some Jews speak and write in 

Hebrew, but this group is small in number. 

What name shall we call the Jews 

a race, a nation, a people? Some call the Jews a nation, some call them a race, some

call them a religion, and some call them a people. hard for everyone to agree on what the Jews are? 

WHY IS OUR RELIGION DIFFERENT? 

Why is it so

We have a religion that is at once very much like, and yet different from, the other religions about us. Christianity comes from Judaism, and so does Mohammedanism. You would, therefore, imagine that these religions would be very friendly to the Jews, and that there would be a feeling of brotherhood between them. 

Yet in almost every country there is a strong feeling of opposition to the Jews on the part of many, due, in some measure, to religious reasons, though there are other causes also, in addition to the religious.

Why is there this opposition to the Jews? And why is our religion different? Why do we have holidays that are different from those of our non-Jewish neighbors? Why do we have Chanuko when they have Christmas, and Passover when they have Easter? These, and many other questions must naturally arise in your mind. 

Or, to take another problem, why, for example, do so few Jews live in the country and till the soil, whereas among other peoples a considerable part of the population lives in small villages and on farms? Why are there so many Jewish tailors and fürriers and storekeepers, and so few miners and iron and steel workers? Why are there so many Jewish doctors and lawyers? 

HOW DID THE JEWS* COME FROM PALESTINE TO THE UNITED STATES? 

Let us start with our first question, then. How did the 

Jews come from Palestine to the United States? Most of them came from Eastern and Central Europe, especially from the countries of Poland, Russia, Roumania, Germany, and France. (Why they came we shall tell later.) Another smaller section of Jewry, called

S'fardic Jews, came originally from Spain and Portugal. Some of these reached America. indirectly after living for generations in other lands.

But how in the first place did the Jews come to the great centers of Eastern and Central Europe, on the one hand, and to Spain and Portugal, on the other? They came from the Eastern shores of the Mediterranean. That means, as you will see when you look at the map, that they came from the cities of Greece and Asia Minor, from Morocco and ancient Carthage and Egypt. 

How did they get there? Mainly from Babylonia. And they came to Babylonia from Palestine. 

Several words are used to describe the Jewish people 

Hebrews, Israelites, and Jews. Before their entry into Palestine they were known as Hebrews. During the period of the first commonwealth they were called Israelites. After 586 B.C.E., when there remained mainly descendants of the tribe of Judah, they were known as Jews.

The 

This, then, is the list of our ancestors' stopping places. 

When they were first forced to leave Palestine, they went mainly to Babylonia, though some also left for Egypt and Asia Minor. numbers going to these places increased till there were millions of Jews in both countries. In the meantime more Jews began spreading westward along the north and south shores of the Mediterranean, settling in Greece, Italy, France, and Spain, and in all the lands of North Africa. They spread further, into Germany and, later, into the distant land of England. 

Great persecutions in these countries drove the Jews eastward to Poland, but persecutions in Poland later forced many back to Germany. In the meantime the Jews of Spain and Portugal were being compelled either to give up their religion or to leave the country. Many left, returning to their original homes in the East, especially to the newly created government of Turkey. Others pretended to accept Christianity, but secretly practiced their Judaism. Spain and Portugal set up religious courts, called the 

Inquisition, which searched out these pretending Christians and punished them with fines, imprisonment, and very often with public burning at the stake.

Many of these Marranos, as the secret Jews were called, then sought to escape the lands of terror. They fled to the New World which was then being discovered, settling in South America and the West Indies. Others fled to Holland, which at the time was the most liberal country and permitted the Jews to worship freely. 

From Holland, too, many Jews left for the American colonies. Thus the first Jews to settle in America were mainly Spanish and Portuguese.

In the beginning of the nineteenth century, Jews began to arrive in the United States from Germany. They came because of lack of opportunity in the old home, or because they were opposed to the absolute rule of the king and were forced to flee the country to escape arrest, especially when a revolution failed in 1848. 

In 1881, after a wave of terrible pogroms in Russia, Russian Jews began to pour into the United States in large numbers, and they continued coming until the United States passed a law permitting only a very small percentage of immigrants to enter the country. In the last few years we have had another stream of German Jews from Germany, Austria, and other lands invaded by the Germans, who came here as refugees from the cruel government of Hitler.

WHY DID THE JEWS LEAVE PALESTINE? 

But why did our ancestors leave Palestine in the first place? 

Did they not care to stay in Palestine? Were they not attached to their homeland?

The Jews cared very much for Palestine. Palestine was their home, and therefore they have never forgotten it. The land was taken from them by force, but the Jews never left it completely, and they have always hoped that many will some day once more re-

turn there. Actually hundreds of thousands of Jews have returned there in our own lifetime. 

QUESTIONS 

1. How many Jews are there in the world and where do they live? 2. 

From what countries did Jews come to the United States? 

What language did they speak?

In which ways are they like and in which unlike their fellow Americans 3. What stopping places did the Jews have on their way from 

Palestine to the United States?

EARLY BEGINNINGS 

Before they conquered Palestine, our ancestors had no fixed home. They were half nomads; that is, clans or tribes which migrated between several districts in search of water and grass for their flocks. At times they remained long enough in one place to plant a crop, but they were not settled permanently. When the crop was harvested, they returned to one of their other grazing lands with their flocks of sheep, goats, cattle, and camels.

They wandered on the outskirts of Palestine, mainly in the Negev, or dry country, which is the name given to the southern portion of the land.

As nomads they were very poor. They had no houses, but flattopped tents which they carried on the backs of their camels and which they pitched in every new encampment. They had scanty clothing, little food, and few possessions. Aside from a hand mill with which they ground their corn, and a round piece of leather which served them for a table, as well as a bag in which to carry provisions, some mats which they used for chairs and beds, and some cooking utensils, they had few other household belongings. 

Naturally they could have very few, since they had to carry all their possessions on their camels.

Nomads know few arts and occupations, and what little was known every man had to know, since there could be no division of tasks such as we practice at present. 

NOMADIC BELIEFS AND QUALITIES 

The

Like other wandering tribes, their beliefs were simple. moon and the stars guiding them in their nightly travels were believed to be gods or the abode of the gods. They also believed in friendly spirits who lived in springs, trees, stones, and bodies of water. On the other hand they thought the world was filled with hostile spirits or demons. Sharing the common superstitions of the time, they believed that dreams are a forewarning of something that is to happen, that signs in the road, such as crossed sticks or the passing of a certain animal, have meaning for human beings. The dead, they believed, could be brought back and talked to, and magic could change the fate of a person or influence the conduct of God.

On the other hand, they shared the virtues of nomadic and semi-nomadic peoples. They were hospitable and were happy when strangers came to share the shelter of their tent. They feasted the strangers, who became their lifelong friends after partaking of their food or drink. They also enjoyed a sort of democracy and equality. All the members of the tribe regarded themselves as one large family, felt toward one another as brothers. If someone of one tribe killed any member of another all the members of the tribe which suffered the loss were in honor bound to take blood revenge, that is, they were to kill any member of the guilty person's tribe. Naturally, when that happened, the other tribe

had to take its blood revenge, and thus long feuds were carried on until the dispute was compromised, or until one of the tribes was exterminated.

The sheikh, called the father or patriarch, treated his fellow tribesmen not as subjects of a king, but as free sons and equals. The father took council with his sons, consulting them on all important matters. The spirit of equality which marked the early years of our desert wandering stood our ancestors in good stead when later kings tried to act as tyrants. Our great prophets reminded the kings that since desert days all Israelites are equals and must not be oppressed.

After possibly hundreds of years, several of the Hebrew tribes united into the tribes of Israel. In later times this union was described as though the fathers or founders of these tribes were all sons of one man, Jacob, later called Israel. The tribes being of different importance and renown, the differences were said to be due to different mothers, the wives of Jacob. 

Story telling, which among nomads is one of the few arts to be highly developed, found rich material in the legendary account of the Hebrew ancestors. The stories, told again and again at camp-fires and polished with each telling, have come down to us in the Bible. When these stories came to be written down there were often different versions of the same fact. Thus, in one account, Abraham is said to have come from Ur in Babylonia, while in another story his home is given as Aram. We thus realize that these stories are not history in the strict sense, but the rich weaving of the imagination of our early story tellers. 

The account which has come down to us in the Bible is briefly as follows: 

The first great patriarch of the Jewish people was Abraham. 

Abraham left his home in Babylonia (or Aram) and migrated to southern Canaan.* When with his wife, Sarah, and his entire tribe, he came to Canaan, he felt that this was the land which he was looking for. Two sons were born to Abraham. The older, Ishmael, by his wife Hagar, is said to be the forefather of the Arab desert people. The Jews reckon their descent from the son whom Sarah bore to him in his old age, Isaac. Abraham at first believed that it was the duty of a father to sacrifice his most precious son to God, but when he actually was ready to sacrifice Isaac, he heard a voice telling him that God does not want human sacrifice. Thus, the cruel custom of child sacrifice was abolished among the Hebrews. Once every several hundred years the nomadic tribes living on the fringes of settled lands or in the desert would set out on a migration to find a better watering place or better grazing lands. The cause of these migrations might be that the old water holes would dry up, or they might be conquered by an enemy. 

Abraham's wandering refers to some such migration.

Of Isaac little is known except that he married Rebecca and had two sons. The older one, Esau, is the father of the people of Edom, a small nation living in the southeast corner of Palestine. The second son, Jacob, is considered the real founder of the people of Israel.* His twelve sons gave the names to the tribes of Israel. 

During a period of famine, part of the Hebrew tribes migrated to Egypt. The Story tellers had it that the first to go was Joseph, Jacob's favorite son, who was sold there as a slave by his jealous brothers. Joseph successfully interpreted 

Pharaoh's dreams and was made viceroy of Egypt. Thereupon he invited his father and brothers to settle in that country also. 

HOW OUR ANCESTORS ACQUIRED A LAND 

Even in Egypt, however, our people were still semi-nomads. 

And like all such people, our ancestors looked forward to acquiring a land in which to settle. Then they could raise food and be assured of a sufficient supply the year round. Their homes would improve and with it their health and their dispositions. 

RESULT OF TWO CAUSES 

Looking back upon the past it all seems a very simple story. 

But its actual accomplishment was very difficult. A land is won only after a hard struggle. Besides, something out of the ordinary had to happen to force our ancestors out of their regular daily course and make them want to conquer a country. Two causes drove our ancestors out of their accustomed groove and set them on the road to nationhood. One of these was a great man; the other, a great event. The man was Moses, and the event, the 

Exodus, which means the going out from Egypt. 

Egypt, at the time, was one of the great empires of the world, while the Israelites were as yet in their early beginnings. Egypt was already an old nation whose population practiced agriculture, built homes of sun-dried bricks, and engaged in many handicrafts and arts. Over six thousand years ago the Egyptians had already invented a form of writing and had worked out a calendar of 365 days. Their great pyramids were built about 4800-4500 years ago. 

On the walls of their pyramids and on their tombs in general the Egyptians painted scenes showing their wealth and elegance, as well as their occupations and forms of worship. They had a rich literature and considerable scientific knowledge, including medicine. 

For thousands of years the Egyptian kings limited themselves to their own country and its surrounding territory. Then they set out on a career of conquest which carried them through Palestine, which was then called Canaan, and Syria, up to the banks of the Euphrates. A weak dynasty for a time lost control over the distant lands. But during the period of our story a war-like ruler reconquered the lost territory, and he in turn was succeeded by Bible scholars believe that the Jacob story represents a second migration of Hebrew tribes from their old Aramean home, which united with some of the earlier tribes to form the Israelite nation.

Ramses II, greatest of world builders.

He built entire cities, among them the store-city, Pithom, and a new royal residence city,

Per-Raamses, with which our ancestors were concerned. 

The Israelites had lived for generations on the fringe of Egypt's civilization, separated from the Egyptians in language, customs, and, above all, occupations. occupations. Suddenly a calamity overtook them. A cruel king, probably King Ramses II, requiring the services of thousands of laborers for his building enterprises, forced our forefathers into slavery and made them, together with hundreds of thousands of others, build treasure cities for him. 

Our ancestors would have continued working endlessly had they not been suddenly blessed with exceptional good fortune. A great leader arose who saw their suffering and decided to free his people from Egyptian bondage. This great leader was Moses. 

MOSES 

There are many lovely legends telling of Moses' birth and early life. It is told, for example, that when Moses was born the 

Pharaoh had passed a cruel law that all baby boys were to be thrown into the Nile. Moses' mother, fearing for her little son, put him in a basket in the bulrushes on the shore of the Nile and left him there in the hope that some kind person would take pity on him. It happened, so the story goes, that the princess, on her way to bathe in the river, heard the cries of the infant and sent one of her maidens to see where they came from. She was delighted when her maid brought the little child to her. She adopted him and raised him herself, securing Moses' own mother to act as his nurse. Thus Moses was brought up in the royal palace, and by his own mother.

Whatever may have happened in Moses' earlier years, it seems certain that he soon learned that he belonged to the oppressed people. With sorrow and rage he watched the Egyptians mistreat his brothers, until one day, no longer able to control himself, he rushed upon a taskmaster who was striking a helpless Hebrew slave and in his fury, killed him. Of course, as soon as the matter became known Moses had to flee his native land. He escaped into the wilderness, where, on an oasis, he came upon the tribe of Midian. 

The Midianite priest, Jethro, received Moses kindly. Moses became a shepherd in Jethro's household and in time married the priest's eldest daughter, Ziporah.

Moses might have lived happily in his quiet retreat had not the troubles of his people always been on his mind. He felt as though he were a coward escaping his people's fate. Finally he felt that God was calling him to return to his people and deliver them. Taking leave of his family, Moses left for Egypt. The years had so changed him that he was no longer recognized there. 

With the aid of his brother, Aaron, Moses set about arousing a spirit of rebellion among the Hebrew slaves. Day after day he spoke to them and strove to awaken in them a feeling for liberty, He told them about a land far away that would some day be theirs and gave them an understanding of God, the most powerful of all

gods, Who was ready to accept them as His people and to lead them into a land of freedom if they would only follow Him into the desert and agree to worship Him.

Few believed at first, but their number grew, and events in Egypt played into their hands; for Egypt seemed to be suffering from a series of plagues, attacks of wild animals, locusts, spoiled water, and many diseases. Moses described all of these as the punishment which God brought upon the Egyptians for the hard-heartedness of their king. And thus, one night, when an epidemic was raging in Egypt and there seemed to be no house without its dead, the Israelites arose in the middle of the night, and, taking with them dough which they did not even have the time to bake into bread, they fled from the land of Egypt into the wilderness. A company of Egyptian soldiers set out in pursuit but the Israelites succeeded in escaping their pursuers perhaps by crossing a body of water.

THE PASSOVER 

Thereafter the Israelites always remembered the narrow escape that they had at the hands of the Egyptians. They wanted to be sure that they would recall this happening forever, and that their children after them would always think back upon the day when they were saved from Egyptian bondage. They therefore set aside a festival in memory of this occasion. They called the holiday 

Passover in remembrance of the night when it was believed the angel of death swept through the land of Egypt and slew every

Egyptian firstborn but passed over the houses of the Hebrews. 

TEACHING THE LAW 

After some time spent in the wilderness, Moses led the Hebrews to the oasis of Kadesh where they were to make a covenant, or treaty, with their new God. There Moses, organized the people, it is said, upon the suggestion of his Midianite father-in-law, appointing officers over thousands, hundreds, and tens. These were to judge the people as well as to lead them in war. But Moses himself was the chief judge, and in his judgments he laid the foundations of our religion. Certainly some, if not all, of the Ten 

Commandments originated in Moses' teaching. It may well be that he commanded them to honor their parents; forbade stealing, murder, and the bearing of false witness. So strongly was Moses' work felt, that all later legislation was tied to the name of Moses in order to give it authority in the eyes of the people. 

LESSONS LEARNED IN THE WILDERNESS 

The children of Israel remained many years in the wilderness. 

During that time, two very important teachings were mastered by them, both of which were to change the course of their lives forever. 

First they learned to accept Yahweh as their God Who, Moses told them, was the God, of their fathers. This God, being greater than any that man could think of or imagine, demanded not only

sacrifices, but right conduct, a life of justice and kindness to one another.

The second lesson they learned was to draw closer together for common action. It took a long time for the tribal differences to be smoothed out. But under the direction of Moses, the heads of the tribes learned to cooperate in a common enterprise so that they were strong enough to march against Canaan and conquer it. 1.

III 

ISRAEL'S UNITED KINGDOM 

Moses died and left the command of his forces to Joshua, who had the task of entering Canaan and subduing it. It seems that already in Moses' lifetime Hebrews had occupied the eastern bank of the Jordan, but the western side presented a more difficult problem. Palestine is not an easy country to conquer; its geography makes it difficult.

Let us review briefly the geography of Palestine. Standing with the Israelites on the eastern banks of the Jordan, we are on a mountain range which rolls gradually eastward into the desert. 

Below us is the Jordan River, which divides the country into half. 

The Jordan flows between two large lakes - Kineret in the north and the Dead Sea in the south. The Jordan Valley slopes very steeply downward, far below sea level, the Sea of Kineret already being about 600 feet, and the Dead Sea almost 1,300 feet, below sea level. The Jordan Valley is thus the lowest point on the surface of the earth.

On the western side of the Valley, mountains rise steeply in the south and somewhat more gently in the north. About twothirds of the way north the mountains are divided by a wide valley, the Vale of Jezreel. West of the mountains are a series of foothills, and west of these is the plain of the seacoast, mainly sand, running into the sea.

Palestine is thus a country of many different surface features and even different climates and rainfalls. Such a country is difficult to conquer. The only fact that made the conquest easier was that each geographical division formed series of independent kingdoms or city states. With the country broken into sections like a checker board, each city was able to remain independent of any other and to form a separate state. Joshua was thus able to attack one king after the other, and while he did not conquer the entire country, he established himself firmly in the northern mountains, which now came to be known as the hill country of Ephraim. A number of tribes later conquered the southern mountains and renamed them the mountains of Judah. 

GEOGRAPHY INFLUENCES UNION 

Unhappily for our ancestors, the union formed in the wilderness proved imperfect, especially when they did not possess a strong central government. Settled in a land so divided by nature and difficult of communication, it was natural that the bonds of unity which held the Israelites together should be somewhat loosened. Judah was altogether separate from Ephraim, being divided from it by the city of Jerusalem, which was at the time held by a people called the Jebusites. But even in the north for

a long time there was no regularly organized government. A strong leader, called a judge, would arise in one tribe, and for a time the near-by tribes would recognize his authority. But dangers continued to threaten the new nation. Now the Moabites attacked and were on the point of conquering the country but were driven back by the judge Ehud. The Canaanites living in the midst of the 

Israelites gained the upper hand, but were annihilated by the victorious armies of Barak and Deborah. But it became more and more difficult to secure common action without better organization. 

The situation became especially critical when a new people, the Philistines, which had settled on the southern seacoast of Palestine, became so strong that it was almost able to overrun the entire Israelite territory.

A KING IS CHOSEN 

Samuel, a prophet, was at the time the ruler of the Hebrews, but he was already old, and there was as yet no arrangement whereby a father who was head of the people would be followed by his son; nor was Samuel a military leader who could help his people fight their battles. The Israelites, therefore, asked of Samuel that he choose a king for them. Samuel, accordingly, selected 

Saul, the son of Kish, a man taller, stronger, and braver than any of his fellows, a gallant fighter and valiant general. Under

Saul's leadership, the Hebrew nation began its first organized life as a kingdom.

SAUL'S FAILURE 

At the outset Saul was very successful. The first act which proved him worthy of the crown was a quick march across the Jordan to rescue the city of Jabesh Gilead from the Ammonites. These had offered as a condition of surrender the removal of the right eye from every male Israelite. Saul saved the city and punished the 

Ammonites. Then he defeated the Philistines and undertook an expedition against the Amalakites, the age-old enemies of the Israelites. Brave in battle, and assisted by equally daring sons, he made Israel feared and respected among the surrounding peoples. 

But unfortunately Saul was a moody man who would fall into fits of brooding and jealousy. Early he quarreled with Samuel and thus felt forsaken by God. Then he became jealous of a young Judean leader, David, who was serving as his own musician and armorbearer. The cause of the jealousy was a song which the maidens had composed upon the army's return from the war declaring that Saul had slain thousands and David ten thousands. David was obliged to flee and after a long pursuit by Saul was forced into the camp of Saul's enemies, the Philistines. Finally, broken and in depressed spirit, Saul, together with his brave sons, especially 

Jonathan, fell in battle fighting the Philistines.

DAVID'S SUCCESS 

But what Saul could not do was left to be accomplished by Israel's greatest king, David. Many charming legends have

gathered about the personality of David. It is told, for example that when the prophet Samuel became dissatisfied with Saul, he went to the house of Jesse, father of David, to seek out one of his sons as the future ruler. Jesse had six tall, muscular, brawny sons who were led before Samuel one after the other; but 

Samuel looked at each one and said, "No, this is not the one. 

Finally Jesse said, "I have one more son, but he is a young lad, and he is tending the sheep. "Send for him," Samuel commanded.

Jesse sent to fetch David, and when Samuel saw him he declared joyously, "He is the one I want. Whereupon he poured oil over his head and anointed him king of Israel. 

Another story tells of an encounter between David and the Philistine giant Goliath. The Philistines and the Hebrews were in battle, and, in accordance with the custom of the time, the Philistine chief challenged the Israelites to meet their champion in a single combat which was to decide the victory. No Israelite dared accept the challenge, fearing the mighty Goliath, the rod of whose spear was as a weaver's staff. It happened, however, that at the moment David, the young lad, came to the camp bringing food for his brothers. Hearing of the challenge, he begged to be permitted to accept it in behalf of the Hebrews; and the king, having no other choice, consented. David attempted to put on armor but found himself uncomfortable in it. In his ordinary clothes and with no other weapon than a shepherd's sling, he went into battle. With deadly aim he drove a smooth small stone into the 

Philistine's temple and slew him, thus gaining a victory for Israel.

CONQUERED JERUSALEM 

We do not know whether all these events really happened as described, but we do know that David was a bold man and that he did more than any other king for the Hebrew people. At first crowned by Judah, he was soon sought as king by the North as well. 

When the Philistines learned that David had become king of a united kingdom, they became suspicious that he might seek to throw off their rule. But before the Philistines were quite prepared, David defeated them and not only freed Israel, but established his rule over the Philistines as well.

By David then accomplished another feat which was to make his name immortal. He wrested from the Jebusites the city of

Jerusalem, which for a long time had separated the north from the south of Israel. Jerusalem being neutral ground and located between the two parts of his kingdom, David made it his capital. royal decree the Ark was installed there, and thus it became as well the center of the religious life of Israel. David, we are told, wanted to build a temple for the Ark, which at the time was housed in a tent. However, the prophet advised him that as a man of war it was not becoming for him to build a temple. His son, a more peaceful king, would build that temple.

With the conquest of Jerusalem, David's career had but begun. 

The nations surrounding the Hebrew kingdom were to be brought under the sway of the new capital. David, in a series of successful conquests, defeated all the nearby countries, Edom, Moab, Ammon, and even distant Damascus. All these states now became a part of the Hebrew empire, paying rich yearly tribute to the conqueror. The Hebrew kingdom was probably the largest that had ever existed in that part of the world. In addition to his success in war, David was also a gifted poet. Some of the beautiful Psalms, the world's outstanding religious poetry, were composed by Israel's great king. Indeed, so highly did later generations consider his poetic gifts, that they ascribed all the Psalms to him. 

Even though David accomplished so much for his people, his reign was not altogether free from rebellions. The uprisings, however, were unsuccessful, and David died at the age of seventy, at peace and highly honored. Before his death he was able to hand over his kingdom to his favorite son, Solomon. 

SOLOMON'S BRILLIANT REIGN 

King Solomon's reign is regarded as the most glorious in Hebrew history. During this period of close to forty years the 

Hebrew kingdom witnessed its greatest growth in commerce, industry, and building, and it made considerable progress in literature and other arts. The king himself had a reputation for wisdom, and many wise sayings and riddles are said to have come from his pen. 

Especially did Solomon distinguish himself as a man of peace and a great builder, just as his father had been primarily a warrior. 

The king built new cities and fortified them. He constructed roads and built extensive chariot stations. He reorganized the army and for the first time introduced horses and chariots, which Israel had never possessed before. Jerusalem was adorned with many beautiful buildings, and elsewhere the king built summer and winter palaces. It was a period 

It was a period of activity such as Israel had not yet witnessed.

For purposes of taxation, King Solomon succeeded in wiping out all old tribal divisions and uniting the country as one people. 

The king divided the land into twelve districts, regardless of tribal boundaries. He appointed governors over each district, obliging it to provide the needs of the King's household for one month.

GREAT BUILDING ACTIVITY 

Most important of all, Solomon built a temple which was to become the nation's chief shrine, the center of the national life during the entire period of its existence. In later years it was declared the only rightful place where an Israelite might worship and offer sacrifices; nowhere else might he bring a gift to God.

The Temple, like the more ancient high places, was planned so that the service would be conducted in the open air; sacrifices were offered and hymns were sung in the open court. The Temple proper had two rooms; one, a large hall in which stood the ever-lighted 

Menorah, the table of shew bread, and the altar of incense; while

37

in an inner room, in the Holy of Holies rested the Ark of the Covenant, containing the two tablets of stone. 

In addition to the Temple, Solomon built palaces, reception halls, judgment halls, throne rooms, palaces for his many wives, and a beautiful throne which became the subject of many a legend. 

The Israelites would have enjoyed all this splendor but for one fact - it cost too much. In order to pay for all of his buildings Solomon needed large sums of money. He earned much money from copper mines which he worked in the Sinai Peninsula. 

Ships bearing the products of Canaan went to Ophir, whence they returned laden with gold and silver, sandal-wood, peacocks, and apes. But the income from the mines proved insufficient. Nor did Solomon receive all the taxes which his father David used to get, because the Moabites, Ammonites, and Arameans rebelled against his rule and declared their independence. 

TAXES AND FORCED LABOR 

Solomon was, therefore, obliged to tax his own subjects heavily, and probably for the first time the tax collector came to exact payments from the poverty-stricken farmers. But even that was not enough. Solomon had made an agreement with Hiram, king of Tyre, to build his temple and palaces for him. Hiram was to deliver logs by sea from the Lebanon which Solomon's workers were to cut together with Hiram's. In order to obtain enough workers, Solomon enslaved all the Canaanites who still lived in the country. But even that step proved insufficient, and Solomon was forced to demand labor from the Israelites as well. Every 

Israelite became obligated to devote one month out of every three to the service of the king.

RESULT IS REBELLION 

No wonder, therefore, that there was grumbling and rebellion even during the lifetime of Solomon. A revolt broke out with particular violence after Solomon's death. The union of North and South, of Judah and Israel, had never been very firm. 

It may be that the tribe of Judah was not taxed, or, even if it was, it enjoyed the benefits of Jerusalem. The North, however, felt it was paying out its money and its lifeblood to help adorn a city which until recently had belonged to the heathens. "What portion have we in David, neither have we inheritance in the son of Jesse; to your tents, O Israel, became their rallying cry. Accordingly, when Rehoboam, King Solomon's son, came to be crowned king by the northern tribes (after being already crowned king of Judah), the people demanded that he lighten their heavy duties and taxes. 

But the king, disregarding his older counselors, who advised him to yield, followed instead the advice of the young men who urged him to be more severe even than his father. The angry people, thereupon, declared the independence of the North, cast off the rule of the House of David and set up their own king instead-- Jeroboam, the son of Nabat. The Hebrews became permanently divided into two kingdoms.

2.

This was the first ancestors in Palestine.

A SERIOUS SETBACK 

serious misfortune which overtook our A united kingdom might have been a strong power, able to ward off all attacks. As two smaller kingdoms, our ancestors would constantly have to fight for their existence, and in the end suffer defeat at the hands of the giant nations, of which we shall tell presently. 

Let us remember that even as two nations our ancestors were larger and more united than the peoples who had lived in Canaan before them. Israel and Judah were large kingdoms in comparison with the city states into which Canaan was divided before the conquest. 

Had they been so broken up they would not have been able to exist at all. But even though they were fairly sized states for their time they paid a price for their disunity.

IV THE DIVIDED KINGDOM 

The divided Hebrew kingdom was, as already mentioned, no longer as strong as it was when united. It was natural, therefore, that it should lose its position of importance in its little world and take a lower rank. 

Nevertheless, even as two states, the Hebrew governments were not of the very weakest. There were three types of states at the time. One type might be called the "giants"; another type might be described as dwarfs"; and the third, as 'middling." The giant states were Egypt, Assyria, and later, Babylonia. At one time 

Egypt had occupied the entire land of Palestine, but with each conquest Egypt used up her young men. When these were exhausted, she had to hire soldiers to fight her battles, and these soon took over the Egyptian government for themselves and set up their own king. 

Thus beset by inner strife, Egypt naturally lost the role which she once played and barely managed to maintain peace within her own borders.

Assyria, the second "giant" of the time, was a new government formed as a colony of Babylonia. Gradually it swallowed up one kingdom after another, until from out of the East it came to the West to attack and invade. Assyria wanted to reach the West for many reasons. It was eager for a seaport in the Mediterranean for trade as well as for military purposes. The rich timberlands of the Lebanon forests tempted them to conquer and possess that country. Most important of all, along the shores of the Mediterranean ran the road to Egypt. That country was the greatest prize desired by the Assyrians, and, of course, they would have to possess the road before they could conquer the country. Fortunately it took many years for the Assyrians to force their way into the West. When they did finally break through, their coming proved almost fatal to our ancestors, but until they came the Hebrew states were able to flourish. Between Assyrian invasions our ancestors were able to set up their independent states and to play their part in the world.

But while our ancestors were no match for the giants, neither were they among the dwarfs. Ammon, Moab, Edom, even the once mighty Philistines were most often forced to pay tribute to the Hebrew states. Aside from Aram, or Syria, a state the equal of, and often a trifle stronger than Israel, the Hebrew states were the most important in their part of the world. 

THE STORY OF ISRAEL 

The story of the two kingdoms of Israel and Judah may be briefly told. They were small kingdoms which fought small wars, and even of these and of their other deeds in times of peace the Bible tells us very little. We must, therefore, try to piece together some stories of the happenings in Israel and Judah to place them before us in the form of pictures.

Y:

But Rehoboam is king. He has just been refused the crown by 

Israel, and he decides to take it by force of arms. Judahı has a large army, with many chariots, organized by King Solomon. 

Judah, therefore, invades Israel and seems to be victorious. suddenly an army appears from Egypt. Caught on two fronts, Rehoboam is overpowered. The Egyptians take away the gold and silver and all the treasures which Solomon had accumulated. Israel, too, is invaded, but it does not suffer as seriously. Rehoboam is now weakened. He cannot carry on the war against Israel successfully again. The war drags on until Israel, with its superior numbers, has the telling advantage. 

OMRI AND AHAB 

There have been two revolutions in Israel, from which General Omri emerges the winner. Omri has made a great stir in the land. 

He has built himself a mighty city on the hill called Samaria, and so important is this new city that the whole country will be called after it. He built himself a beautiful palace with halls and temples and courts. The entire country is talking about the great building enterprises of the king. The king has organized a mighty army. He has fought Moab and received large tribute every year. 

He has fought Aram and lost, but his son Ahab will make good what his father lost. The nations about him are linked in close friendship. At last the quarrel with Judah has been ended, and the king of Sidon has been brought into an alliance by the marriage of his daughter to Ahab of Israel. But there is trouble on the distant horizon. A mighty conqueror, one of the giants, is on his way. 

Assyria has undertaken to invade the west land, and Israel is in consternation. All of Omri's work will be undone, the proud city walls may be destroyed, all the treasures received from Moab may be taken away by the king of Assyria--and so Ahab decides to fight.

He helps to organize a league in which other nations are included. He himself contributes the largest number of chariots, which at the time were like our tanks. The allies finally meet 

Assyria in a large battle. The Assyrians claim the victory, but they are forced to retreat that is the telling proof. Israel is saved, and Ahab is hailed as a great king. 

Ahab would have remained a great king but for two mistakes for which he paid a dear price. First of all, he permitted the introduction of the worship of Baal, the idol-god of the Phoenicians, by his queen Jezebel, daughter of the king of Sidon. 

This worship spread to the king and all the noblemen. The faithful followers of Israel's God were outraged and sought to avenge themselves on the worshipers of Baal. 

The same woman who was responsible for Baal worship led the king into another misfortune. She 

She persuaded him to rob an honest farmer of his vineyard. Naboth, a resident of Jezreel, had a vineyard which he prized because it was the possession of his fathers, which should not, according to the law of Israel, leave the tribe. The king wanted the vineyard and offered Naboth a better one, but Naboth insisted that he did not want to sell his property. Being unable to obtain the vineyard by legal means,

Queen Jezebel laid a plot to accuse Naboth of blasphemy and had him executed. This aroused a storm of opposition in Israel which even the queen's cruelty could not suppress. The prophet Elijah denounced the king, and the king himself recognized the serious blunder which he had permitted himself to fall into. 

THE REBELLION OF JEHU 

Ahab died (853 B.C.E.) before the storm broke, but his son was to face the most bloody revolution that Israel had yet seen. Jehu, leader of the army before Aram, zealous for the God of Israel, marched against Samaria, slew the king and especially the queenmother and all the Baal worshipers. He began a new dynasty which once more restored the worship of Israel's God. 

Jehu was very destructive. He killed the entire royal family, many of the generals, and a large portion of the nobility. The people were thus left divided and without experienced leaders. No wonder, then, that Aram gained victory after victory and that Jehu was forced to plead before the king of Assyria: "I am your servant; 

I am your slave," and to bring him tribute. As soon as Assyria became involved in troubles in the East and could no longer interest herself in the West, Aram fell savagely on Jehu and reduced his kingdom to a mere shadow. Ten thousand footmen, fifty horsemen, and ten chariots were all that Aram permitted the son of Jehu. Israel was trodden down as the dust.

But the fortunes of war changed. Assyria invaded Damascus once more and greatly weakened her. But then for a time Assyria herself declined. A series of weak kings held the throne and had all they could do to fight off rebellion in the nearby countries. 

The more distant provinces easily threw off their allegiance. 

With Damascus thus reduced in power and Assyrian overlordship removed, Israel came into its own. For a brief golden period, under 

King Jeroboam II, it set out upon conquests far and wide, regaining all the lands that had once belonged to David's Kingdom, from Hamath to the Dead Sea. For about forty years Israel was once more the leading nation in the west land.

ASSYRIAN INVASION 

Then Assyria went on the march again, and once more the former enemies, Israel and Aram, joined hands to stop her. The two kings wanted Judah to join them, and when that country refused, they declared war upon her. King Ahaz of Judah then sent pleading messages to the king of Assyria. The Assyrians, whether in answer to the pleas of Judah or because they were intending to attack of their own accord, invaded Damascus, destroyed the city, and brought to an end the long, gallant fight which Aram had been carrying on for existence.

Soon, however, it was the turn of Israel to feel the might of Assyria. The country was overrun in 727 B.C.E. It sued for peace and accepted a king whom the Assyrians appointed. But within two years Israel was again in rebellion, and once more an Assyrian army descended on the land. The Assyrians laid siege to Samaria,

and for three years they were kept from the city by its brave defenders. At last Israel fell to the overwhelming might of the giant. Thereafter, to crush all possible future rebellions, the conquerors took the leaders of the Israelites into captivity somewhere in Assyria and in their place brought settlers from Cutha, a Babylonian city. These, merging with the Israelites who had remained, gradually adopted a form of the religion of Israel, and today their descendants are known as the Samaritans, who still practice a form of Judaism. 

THE BEGINNINGS OF THE ANSWER 

Thus we have the beginning of an answer to our question why the Jewish people is no longer an independent nation. After a period of self-government lasting almost four hundred years, a mighty empire cruelly destroyed the northern kingdom and transplanted its leaders far away in a strange land. And what happened to these prisoners of Assyria? Legend calls them "the lost ten tribes. Most of them were lost among the people of Assyria why, we shall tell presently. Some of them may have clung to their religion and become merged with the new exiles who came to Babylonia from Judah. But that is in advance of our story. Let us return to see the fortunes of the other Hebrew state, namely, Judah. 

THE STORY OF JUDAH 

We know as little about the history of Judah as we do of Israel. However, there are some interesting facts to remember about Judah. For one thing, it was distinguished from Israel in that one royal family, the House of David, reigned over Judah throughout its history with one brief interruption. The temple at Jerusalem, too, was a unifying bond among the Judeans. The one sanctuary where God was worshipped without images proved a powerful force to keep the people together. 

Though weaker than Israel, Judah was also in the class of middling states, holding its own throughout, except, of course, against giant armies. It could not successfully oppose Assyria, and it suffered at times from the Egyptians. The Judean state met its end at the hands of the third of the ancient giants, the new Chaldean State in Babylonia, which destroyed Assyria and fell heir to all its possessions. As in the case of Israel, Judah made a brave fight for its freedom, but it was no match for the mighty Chaldeans, and so it lost its independence in the year 586 

B.C.E.,outlasting Israel by 138 years.

Now, to tell the story of Judah in greater detail, Rehoboam's reign, as we already noted, was marked by war against Israel which might have been successful but for the Egyptian invasion which we described on page 19. Fortunately, the Egyptians were not able to hold Judah permanently. However, Judean power was broken. 

Thereafter the war with Israel dragged on until, under the third king, Asa, Israel was already stronger. Asa was obliged to turn for help to Aram against Israel. He sent the Aramean king large gifts of gold and silver to invite his aid against King Baasha. 

The Arameans attacked Israel and Judah was thus spared.

King Jehoshaphat (872-849 B.C.E.), an energetic ruler, conquered Arabia and Philistia and was partially successful against Edom. Like King Solomon, he undertook trading ventures over the high seas, but his fleet was lost in a storm at sea, and this put a stop to his ambitions. During his reign peace was established between Israel and Judah, and a royal alliance was made, Jehoshaphat's son marrying Athalia, daughter of Jezebel.

Soon Judah was to experience her first and only seizure of the throne by an outsider at the hands of the half-foreign queen, Athalia. When her husband died and her son, who followed him on the throne, was murdered by Jehu of Israel, the queen mother Athalia seized the throne against the wishes of the people and had all the members of the royal family killed. For six years she thus held power. Fortunately, she overlooked the king's youngest son, Joash, who was brought up secretly by the high priest. When seven years old, he was proclaimed king by the priests, who did away with Athalia and thus restored the throne to the House of David.

King Uzziah (769-739), who reigned during the period of Jeroboam II of Israel, is another king to remember. Like his fellow king in the north, he, too, widely extended his kingdom. 

He promoted trade and advanced agriculture and animal husbandry. 

He conquered the Philistines, as well as Edom and Ammon and all the country to the border of Egypt, fortifying the harbor of Elath, improving the army and the defenses of Jerusalem, and introducing engines of war into Judah for the first time. 

THE ASSYRIAN PROBLEM 

But what was Judah to do about Assyria? It seemed clear that as soon as Assyria would be through with Aram it would attack Israel. Then, after Israel, it would be Judah's turn. Israel and 

Aram invaded Judah, trying to force her to join them against Assyria. Thereupon Ahaz hastily sent a tribute with letters to the King of Assyria, begging for his help. Ahaz thus made of Judah a tributary of Assyria.

But King Hezekiah, who followed Ahaz, was made of sterner stuff. He chafed beneath the Assyrian yoke and sought to make his people free. Under his leadership, a rebellion was considered in 

711 B.C.E., in which the Philistines, and especially the Egyptians, promised to join; for the Egyptians feared that they would be conquered if Judah and the Philistines fell. But so quickly did 

King Sargon dispose of the Philistines that Judah became frightened and kept her peace. However, when in 705 B.C.E. a new king, Sennacherib, ascended the throne of Assyria and revolts broke out in all parts of the kingdom, the Palestinian states also began to plot secession, with Hezekiah as the leader. Again Egypt promised to send assistance.

But, alas, Egyptian help never arrived, and, after disposing of the other states, Sennacherib was soon free to invade Judah. 

The country was overrun; most of its principal cities were levelled to the ground; and the king was obliged to pay enormous sums

gold and silver, precious objects of all sorts, and even the persons of his wives and daughters. But after he had obtained all that he had asked for, Sennacherib felt he would not be satisfied till he had reduced the defenses of Jerusalem to the ground. 

Accordingly, he demanded the surrender of the city, and when King Hezekiah refused, he laid siege to it. Never did the outlook for a war seem more gloomy Yet, notwithstanding the general despair, one man did not lose hope. 

He was the prophet Isaiah. How or when, he could not tell, but he was confident that no harm could come to Zion, God's chosen dwelling place. Thus, when Sennacherib's messengers demanded the surrender of the city, speaking loudly to frighten the men on the walls, King Hezekiah in despair prayed to God and awaited word from the prophet. And the prophet Isaiah promptly advised him: "Thus saith the Lord concerning the king of Assyria: He shall not come unto this city, nor shoot an arrow there, neither shall he come before it with shield, nor cast a mound against it. By the way that he came, by the same shall he return, and he shall not come unto this city, saith the Lord. For I will defend this city to save it, for Mine own sake, and for My servant David's sake.' (Isaiah, 37:33-35) And as the prophet foretold, so, indeed, it turned out. A plague struck the army of Sennacherib, and the 

Assyrian king was forced to hurry away with all his men and machines. Judah celebrated a triumph, and there was great rejoicing throughout the land. 

Norwithstanding this withdrawal, Judah remained a vassal of

Assyria and paid taxes loyally through the remainder of the reign of Hezekiah and during the long reign of Manasseh (693-639 B.C.E.) and his successors. In the meantime, Egypt itself had suffered the fate of Judah. In 673 B.C.E. Egypt was conquered by the 

Assyrians, who thus achieved the goal of their ambitions, the occupation of the great empire of the Nile. 

THE BABYLONIAN CONQUEST 

But the giant empires proved to have feet of clay. Within a short time after their greatest triumph, they weakened and fell apart. Egypt regained her freedom from Assyria in 645 B.C.E. In 

626 B.C.E. a great Assyrian king died, and after his death his kingdom collapsed. The very capital of Assyria, Ninevah, fell before the might of the new Babylonian kingdom of the Chaldenas in 612 B.C.E. Assyria was no more, and all her subject peoples were free again for the moment.

And now new nations came forward to claim the heritage of Assyria. Egypt from the south and Chaldea from the northeast each set out to seize the various peoples which had been part of 

Assyria's empire.

On his way to fight the Chaldeans, the Pharaoh of Egypt stopped in Palestine to secure the allegiance of Judah, and when King Josiah set out to oppose him, he defeated the Judeans, slew the king, and set himself up as the new master of Palestine. However, in 605 B.C.E. the Egyptian army was thoroughly routed at the 

Battle of Carchemish by the Chaldeans, who took over all of the

vassal states, including Judah. 

For three years the new king, Jehoiakim, remained true to King Nebuchadnezzar of Babylonia. Then, believing that he had an opportunity to gain his freedom, he rebelled. But Nebuchadnezzar was too great a general to be trifled with. Rapidly he swooped down with an army and captured Jerusalem (597 B.C.E.). 

Ten thousand prisoners, the picked men of Judah, all the nobles, all the leaders, and all the smiths and weapon-makers were led away as captives to Babylonia.

Nebuchadnezzar left in charge of the country the third son of Josiah, the twenty-one year old Mattaniah, whose name he changed to Zedekiah. Surely, he thought, his own chosen king would serve him faithfully. But our ancestors were desperate to regain their freedom. After remaining loyal for a number of years, Zedekiah finally rebelled again in 588 B.C.E.

Nebuchadnezzar then invaded Judah and laid siege to Jerusalem. 

Taking Jerusalem was no easy task; the conqueror surrounded the walls, but he could not storm them. The city had to be starved into submission, and not before a year and a half was Nebuchadnezzar finally able to breach the walls. But starved and cut off from its water supply, the city fell on the ninth day of the month of Ov in the year 586 B.C.E. Since then that date has been a day of mourning for our people. 

This time the Chaldeans were bent on ending Judean independence. The beautiful Temple of Solomon was sent up in flames, and thousands of Judeans were massacred by the war-crazed enemy. 

All the men of any consequence at all were led away captive to Babylonia to make sure that they would never rebel again. Only the poorest families, the shepherds and vine-tenders, were permitted to remain in Judah. 

A governor, Gedaliah, the son of Ahikam, was left to administer this poor remnant. But some misguided patriots would not tolerate a ruler who was not of the House of David. Accordingly, they plotted and murdered Gedaliah. This act once again brought down the vengeance of Nebuchadnezzari More Judeans were led away into exile, and this closed the first chapter of the life of our people. Thus, after three fruitless revolts, one under King 

Jehoiakim, one under King Zedekiah, and the last sponsored by the misguided murderers of Gedaliah, Judah was destroyed. Like Israel, its cities were now in ruins and its territory lay open to the inroads of its neighbors.

But of course we know that, although one chapter was closed, the book of Jewish life is still open. The Jewish people are still continuing, and will continue, we believe, forever. 

What happened to our ancestors when their state was destroyed?

Why did not the same thing happen to them as to other peoples whose existence ended with the destruction of their government? 

This was due to something which our ancestors had learned during the time that they were an independent people, and what it was they learned that helped to keep them alive we shall now proceed to tell.

V 

WHAT KEPT THE JEWISH PEOPLE ALIVE? 

Our ancestors, we saw, were conquered by an enemy stronger than they. They tried to fight the enemy off, but they were defeated, and the important men were led away into exile. Yet the 

Jewish people did not disappear. Even the exiled Judeans did not all become mixed with the Babylonians, though some undoubtedly did. Most of them continued as a separate people and many even returned to their homeland to help in the reestablishment of their nation.

PROGRESS IN EARNING LIVELIHOOD 

What is responsible for the enduring life of the Jewish people? Was it the fact that the Jews were so comfortable in their own land that they could not imagine themselves anywhere else? It is true, the Jews had grown considerably in the arts of civilization in Canaan. Let us examine for a moment how settled life in Canaan had changed our ancestors. Whereas they began life as wanderers tending sheep and cattle, they were now mainly farmers. So much, indeed, were they engrossed in farming that their very language became full of expressions drawn from farm life. Earlier the Israelites had been largely peasant-proprietors; they owned their own farms; but as among other peoples large landowners were buying up many of the small estates and forcing their owners to work for them as laborers or slaves. 

The Israelites still lived within large families, or clans. 

If anyone was unfortunate enough to lose his lands because of poverty, his clansmen had to purchase it or redeem it for him, so that the land would remain within the clan. 

As farmers, our ancestors were quite skilled, raising the most delicate plants, various varieties of grapes, olives, and balsam, together with grains and vegetables. 

Nor were our ancestors merely farm dwellers. Many farmers and many handworkers lived within the walled cities, which had spread out to considerable areas and which now had massive walls with many gates. Our ancestors had learned many occupations during their stay in Canaan. They had mastered work in clay, treading it with their feet and shaping it on the wheel. From clay they also made bricks. Early they learned carpentry, and in the construction of King Solomon's temple much of the work was done by Jewish carpenters. Jewish masons quarried their own stone and set it in place. Many Jews worked in King Solomon's copper mines, which were located in the Sinai peninsula. Smithing, which began as a Philistine occupation, soon spread to the Israelites. 

Many also learned the skilled art of gold-smithing and especially the carver's art, engraving signet rings, which were in great demand.

They had cooks and bakers and cheese-makers, tanners and

26

spinners and weavers, and tailors and dyers. A whole town in southern Judea was found to have lived largely on spinning and dyeing. The bakers, dyers, goldsmiths, and cheese-makers even had streets of their own in which they settled and where their markets were concentrated.

There were also traders who bought and sold in their own and in foreign cities. The small traders were largely the handworkers themselves, who sold the goods they produced. As for the large traders, the chief merchant was the king, and many of the leading men also engaged in commerce. 

What the Israelites could not buy they imported. Merchants visited Palestine from Tyre, Egypt, Damascus, Aram, and Mesopotamia, offering such luxuries as purple cloth, glassware, Grecian pottery, ivory, tapestries, birds, gold and silver ornaments, spices, cosmetics, fine coverlets, feasting tables, Grecian lamps, braziers, and amulets. Foreign workmen and architects, designers of jewelry and furniture, as well as builders, came to offer their services to the wealthy Israelites. The rich already had glass windowpanes, a luxury which was just then being introduced. 

In other practical arts, our ancestors were making progress. 

Thus they were already using money instead of barter, which means the exchange of one article for another. Though the money was not yet standard, consisting mainly of large pieces of precious metal which had to be weighed, the use of money is in itself an evidence of progress. They also were developing measures and weights, something which primitive peoples do not have. They even already had a time-piece introduced in the king's palace; all of which they had heretofore not known about. 

BABYLONIAN LIFE MORE ADVANCED 

Important though all these things must have been to our forefathers when they were in their own land, and proud though they undoubtedly were of their progress, nevertheless the countries to which they were exiled were far more advanced than they in all these practical arts. Babylonia had many more beautiful buildings than Jerusalem, and trading and industry were far more highly developed. That which was a novelty in Palestine, such as the sundial, was quite a common thing Babylonia, and all the beautiful things for which Palestine was dependent on foreign workers were produced in Babylonia itself. Were it, therefore, merely the love of comfort and fine things which made our ancestors desire to hold on to their own nationhood, they would have had ample reason to abandon it in favor of the greater wealth of Babylonia. 

OUR ANCESTORS POSSESSED GREAT BOOKS 

Our ancestors, however, wanted to retain their nationhood because they had great books which taught them lessons which wealthy Babylonia or Assyria did not know. These were the books of the Bible, the books which teach us our religion. They were unwilling to abandon their own nationhood, because they knew they possessed a religion far superior to that of the Babylonian or Assyrian.

What is a great book? It is one which is read for many years. A poor book is forgotten almost as soon as it is published. A somewhat better book is read for a few years and is gradually forgotten. A great book is remembered for many years, and the greater it is the longer it is read. 

Our Bible, the books produced by our ancestors during their stay in Palestine and Babylon, is among the greatest of books, for, notwithstanding the passage of three thousand years, it is still read and enjoyed almost as on the day it was written. Not only in the original Hebrew, but even in translation, the Bible has influenced the every-day speech of the nations of the world. The 

English language was especially affected, many expressions now accepted as common usage in English having really originated in the Bible. When, for example, we say, "by the sweat of his brow, are really quoting the words of God to Adam.

The Bible also helped to shape the opinions fo the leaders of the modern age who sought freedom from tyranny and equality of opportunity for the common man. The fathers of the American republic were deeply moved by the Bible in writing the constitution of this country, so that America, as a land of liberty, owes no little debt to the inspiration and the example of the Bible. 

The very inscription on the Liberty Bell reads: "Proclaim liberty throughout the land unto all the inhabitants thereof.' 

(Leviticus 25:10) WHAT THE BIBLE TEACHES 

What, then, is the Bible, and what does it teach? We cannot go into details in short text such as this but must satisfy ourselves with brief illustrations. The Bible, to begin with, teaches us that there is but one God. There are no two or more gods fighting in the world. There is no god of evil, as the 

Greeks or Persians believed; there is no god for thieves and drunkards; nor are there many gods whose conduct, as described in mythology, would shame human beings. The one God is a moral God who demands of human beings that they live uprightly and act justly. 

God is greater and stronger than anything in the world. We take that as a matter of course, but in olden days people did not quite understand it. They thought there were ways of forcing God to do what He did not want to do. Those ways were called magic. 

By means of magic, a person was supposed to be able to compel God to do something for him. Israel's God could not be influenced by magic.

God is the father of all men. The Bible teaches that God created men in His own image; therefore every man, even the lowliest, is an image of God and is therefore the equal of the greatest. Kings were no more entitled than commoners to privileges beyond those allowed them in the Book of the Law. Even the kings were not above the moral law. When King David took away the wife of one of his lieutenants, the prophet Nathan denounced him. 

Such criticism, which among other peoples might have brought the

prophet the death penalty, resulted in repentance on the part of the Judean king.

In order to maintain this equality everyone must know the law. The Jews were, therefore, commanded to educate their children, it being the duty of every father to teach his child the law. Schools were, of course, as yet unknown, but the commandment to teach was already a great step in advance over many other peoples which even to this day are in a state of ignorance. 

In order that all persons might truly be equal, they must be treated justly. There must be just weights and just measures. The poor, the weak, and the defenseless must be protected, especially the orphan and the widow who had no natural protectors to defend them or to take blood revenge for them if anyone hurt them. Workmen's wages must be paid promptly as per agreement. Nor may the judge show partiality to the poor person when he is in the wrong. 

On the other hand, justice alone is often too severe a rule to go by and must be tempered with mercy. The Bible accordingly has many commands urging merciful behavior. Thus, one may not take interest from a poor Israelite borrower. A poor man's coat taken in pledge must be returned in the evening, since it serves its owner as his only blanket. Millstones which the poor family needed for making its daily bread might not be pledges at all. Found articles must be restored to their owner. Even the donkey of an enemy struggling beneath its load must be assisted 

ISRAEL'S RELIGION 

These few illustrations may give you an idea of the type of religion our ancestors were taught which made them so eager to cling to their people. The religions of other nations were mainly for the rich, for the king and for the nobles. They alone were considered favorites of the gods and were the concern of the gods. 

It was Judaism which taught the democracy of the common man. 

Actually, not all the Israelites or Judeans were as attached to their religion as might have been expected during their life in Canaan. Many of them violated the law against making graven images and kept little figurines of Astarte in their homes. 

Astarte was believed to be the goddess of Canaan, with Baal as the male god. The simpler Israelites came to think of their own 

God as Baal, which means owner of the land and giver of fertility, and with him they joined the worship of Astarte. 

Both at home and in public they worshiped like the Canaanites whose holy places they found and which they continued to use. 

These sacred places, or high places, as they were called, were in the open air, usually on top of a mountain or within a forest clearance. A large flat rock, usually with cup holes and channels for running off the blood, was the altar on which they offered sacrifices. A sacrifice of a sheep or an ox was considered a gift to God, or a meal eaten together with God. 

The high places usually contained several tall stones, about six or seven feet in height and one or several poles or trees.

These represented their gods.

Around the whole space there was an enclosure in which the worshipers gathered. For many years the prophets, who were the teachers of the true religion of Moses, fought against the practice of worshiping at these high places, because they represented a lower form of religion. 

Another difficulty which the Israelites found in observing their religion was in reaching the high standards which the prophets demanded of them. The prophets asked for perfect moral conduct, which the average man found hard to live up to. 

In an age, too, when magic and superstition surrounded them at every turn, it was difficult for Hebrews to be entirely free of such practices. Thus, even in the Temple at Jerusalem there hung a brazen serpent which people believed had the power to cure those bitten by a snake if they looked at it.

SHRINES 

Very early in their history the Hebrews had a special shrine which contained the two tablets of stone on which were engraved the Ten Commandments. These tablets were kept in an ark, and wherever the ark was, there was the central temple. 

An ark was said to have been built by Moses for the wilderness tabernacle. The first place where we meet this ark in Canaan is in a town in northern Ephraim called Shiloh. When we first read of it in the Bible, the aged priest Eli is in charge of it, and the boy Samuel is being raised as priest-prophet. Then the ark was captured and for many years was held by the Philistines. The Philistines returned it because they feared it brought them bad luck, and for a long time it was kept in a private home. Finally it was brought to 

Jerusalem by King David and established in the Temple by Solomon. 

Jerusalem thereupon became the center of the religion of Moses, where worship was carried on without images of any sort. 

When Jeroboam declared his rebellion against Rehoboam, Solomon's son, he wanted to keep his people from going to Jerusalem. He therefore established two royal sanctuaries, one at 

Beth El and one at Dan. Both these places had been holy cities since ancient times. But, unlike the temple at Jerusalem, God was worshiped there through images, in accordance with a more ancient practice of pre-wilderness days. There were idols in these sanctuaries in the shape of golden bulls. Some believe that these bulls represented God, and others think they were merely pedestals upon which the invisible God was supposed to have rested. 

But whatever may have been the purpose of these bulls, it is clear that none but the God of Israel was worshiped through them. 

Only for a brief time was an attempt made to substitute the worship of the Phoenician Baal for that of the God of Israel. King

Ahab, you will remember, tried to replace the traditional worship with the Baal religion because of his friendship for the king of Sidon. However, the people were so aroused by the neglect of their national God that, under Jehu, they massacred not only the house of Ahab but all the Baal worshipers. But Israelite worship continued with images and at the high places, with an admixture

of Canaanitish practices.

In Judah the center of religious life was, of course, the Temple of Jerusalem, but worship was also carried on in the many places throughout the country. For a long time there was no objection raised to the high places. But gradually, as the prophets observed the superstitions which were tied up with the Canaanite shrines, they began a struggle against them and demanded that there be only one rightful place of worship, namely, in the Temple of Jerusalem. For many years the struggle went on till, as we shall see, it was crowned with success under Josiah. 

FOREIGN INFLUENCE 

A serious blow was received by the national religion, however, when Judah became a province of Assyria. Ahaz, the first king to submit to the Assyrians, saw a beautiful altar at Damascus and ordered a copy of it made at Jerusalem. This was the beginning of the introduction of Assyrian rites into Judah. Hezekiah, however, who followed his father Ahaz, was a religious reformer and was the first to attempt the abolition of the high places. It was he, incidentally, who ordered the destruction of the brazen serpent which had long been almost as an idol in the Temple. 

Hezekiah's reforms, however, did not outlive him. No sooner had he died than his son Manasseh completely accepted the Assyrian religion, including the worship of the sun-god, in whose honor a golden chariot was placed in front of the Temple. The golden chariot was supposed to represent the passage of the sun-god during the day from the east to the west. The cruel practice was also introduced of offering child sacrifices. Children were burned alive or buried alive under the foundation stones of buildings, and some families would put to death their first-born sons. 

The prophetic party, protesting against these innovations, was persecuted, many prophets suffering death and many, undoubtedly, going into hiding.

JOSIAH'S REFORMATION 

These conditions continued until the ascension of Josiah (638-609 B.C.E.). Josiah was a great religious reformer bent on carrying out the prophetic program. The king's reforms were considerably re-enforced by a new book which was discovered, or perhaps written, at the time, and which we today identify as the Book of Deuteronomy. That book contained the laws which we have already described, and altogether it is a warm plea to the Judeans to remain faithful to their God. Moved by this book, the king was inspired to start the religious reformation. He permanently destroyed the high places and brought the country priests to Jerusalem, so that they would be taken care of and not be tempted to reopen their old shrines. The Temple was repaired and cleansed of Assyrian idolatry. The religion of Moses, without images and without rivals, was supreme in Jerusalem. 

Unfortunately, Josiah died too soon. His reforms had barely been in effect for a dozen years when the king, as we saw, fell in battle fighting the Egyptians. The people, disappointed that

a religious reformer had not fared better at the hands of God, turned from the pure religion of Moses to their former ways. Thus, to the very last days of Judah popular religion contained a large measure of idol worship. It was only when exiled from their country and forced either to accept the ways of the peoples about them or to seek comfort in their own past that the Judeans began to realize what their religious teachers really wanted of them. 

Then they began to turn wholeheartedly to the religion that had come to them as a heritage from Moses. A Judaism more pure and noble than any heretofore practiced was molded in the crucible of the exile. Instead of feeling crushed, the Jews emerged a new and spiritually stronger people. 1.
