VI 

THE PROPHETS 

We have seen that the Jews had a religion which they were most unwilling to give up and to which they clung even in exile. 

However, we saw also that the Jews were not always so devoted to their religion. A long period had passed during which a struggle had gone on between the followers of the true faith and those who were ready to backslide. Who were the leaders in this struggle? 

What sort of men were they? What were their names, that we might remember them with gratitude to this day?

We call them the prophets, but except for a few, we do not know who they were, how they lived, or anything about them. We only know what they did: They taught a religion of justice and of mercy to their own generation, and through them to the entire world down to our own day.

WHO WERE THEY? WHAT DID THEY TEACH? 

They

Children think of prophets as of fortune tellers. imagine them as men who can foretell what is to happen in the future. That is because in ancient times the early prophets, or seers as they were then called, were often consulted about the oracle. The oracle among the Jews was a sort of sacred casting of lots. This might be done by means of straws, sticks, little stones, or sacred dice. Whichever way the straws, sticks, stones or dice fell, that side determined how the person should behave. 

The sacred lots were cast by prophets in charge of the various high places and of the more important temples. 

But the real prophets soon gave up that practice. Indeed, they did not want to be called prophets at all; they were ashamed of the term and would have nothing to do with fortune tellers and their oracles. They were rather statesmen and leaders, social reformers who were concerned with the lives led by their fellow men and with the course which their nation took. The official representatives of religion, the priests, may not have regarded them as religious leaders at all, for the prophets often attacked public sacrifice and public assemblies. They taught, rather, that religion is more concerned with what happens between one man and another. Religion, generally, is said to consist of two parts; the relation of man to God and of man to man. The prophets taught that the relation of man to God is important only if it results in finer dealings between man and man, for what does God want but that man shall be more just and more kind to his fellows? 

The prophets believed with all their being that God ruled the world justly, and in order that the world might run properly it was essential that people treat each other justly also son his neighbor, and one nation the other. If injustice is practiced anywhere, there is bound to be a collision; the world cannot go on. When, therefore, they saw injustice practiced, they attacked it. They spoke their minds freely, declaring that

evil-doers are bringing harm to themselves and to the entire community. 

What is justice? Justice demands that every man be free; that no man be a slave to any other; that every man have enough to live on enough food, clothing, and shelter. It demands, further, that every man be respected for his own worth. No man must be used for selfish interests by anyone stronger or wiser than he. This, the prophets preached, and this they tried to enforce in the lives of the Hebrew people. 

WHAT EVILS DID THEY ATTACK? 

They attacked two kinds of wrong, which were really oneidolatry and oppression. The worship of strange gods meant that 

Israel was untrue to the covenant which its ancestors had accepted in the days of Moses. Some prophets fought idolatry more than oppression, and some fought the other more, but both really had the same thing in mind. The fight was to be carried on against inborn cruelty and love of mastery of one man over another. Each man desired to rule his neighbor. He sought revenge and wanted to see his enemy defeated. The prophets fought against these human weaknesses as well as against all inequalities created by so-called civilization. But

Originally, as we remember, all Israelites had lived in clans, in which everything was owned and shared in common. as city life developed, the possession of private property increased, and as some grew richer, it was but natural that others should grow poorer. Until the present time, we have as yet not found any system whereby people can own property without depriving their neighbors. There have not been enough goods in the world for everyone. Only now do we seem to have found methods of making enough of all the good things of life sufficient for all, yet even now too many rich people are living at the expense of others. In olden times it was much more true. 

Even we today are not altogether mindful of the needs of others when we eat, drink, and make merry while millions of Europeans starve. Perhaps it is natural that most of us should not feel suffering in other parts of the world too closely. But it is equally natural that there should be some persons who do feel strongly for others and who remind us of our guilt. Actually the world cannot continue half hungry and half satisfied. Only through the good of all is the good of any of us assured. The prophets in the days of old were among the first to remind their people of these simple truths. The Jews were not more selfish than any other people. Possibly they were less selfish, because the prophets could appeal to them, and because they gave ear to the prophets and only rarely did them harm.

The prophets taught that the state can succeed only if it has the loyal devotion of its citizenry. The state enjoys such devotion if it looks after its citizens and provides them with what they need - if it keeps them well-fed and well-sheltered; if it does not, the citizens will not look after the state, and when

1.

an enemy comes the state is destined to fall.

HOW WERE THEIR TEACHINGS RECEIVED? 

For teaching these lessons the prophets were often considered unpatriotic. For declaring that their nation would suffer defeat and exile because it violated the laws of right, the prophets were attacked as enemies of their own people. Nevertheless, they continued to teach what they considered the truth, and we today are witnesses to the fact that their words were true. 

There are prophets in every age. In every generation some men take upon themselves the task of pointing out the path of right to their fellows but they are not often listened to with pleasure. Among our ancestors there was, perhaps, a greater striving for religion than among other peoples, and therefore the prophets received more of a hearing. Certainly in later years their words were considered very important, and their writings were accepted as part of the national literature. 

MOSES 

But, again, who were these men? As we have already said, most of them must remain unknown. There are, for example, the unknown authors of the Pentateuch (the five books of Moses), great prophets who taught that there is but one God, Ruler of the entire world. Some believe that all of the Pentateuch was written by Moses, while others felt that there were other authors in addition to Moses. But whoever wrote these books were men of exceptional genius. Think, for example, of the great moral code included in the Ten Commandemnts. Certainly a law-giver of extraordinary greatness crowded so much morality into ten short sayings. Or think of the man who undertook to remind the Jews of their own lowly origin in order that they not turn oppressors against others. How often it has happened, unfortunately, that nations which were themselves oppressed a short time before turn into oppressors when they gain their own freedom. Against that 

Israel was warned time and again by the writers of the Pentateuch. 

Or the Pentateuch warns not merely to protect but to love the stranger, because we ourselves were once strangers in the land of Egypt. It is also distinguished for its laws that make provision for persons in need - leaving corners of the field uncut, that the poor might take the grain; permitting the gleanings of the harvests and everything forgotten in the fields to be picked up by the needy. Laws of a different character forbid slander or hatred, even the bearing of tales. Indeed, the writers of the Pentateuch fulfilled their mission in planning a law that might make of us a nation of priests and holy people. 

SAMUEL AND OTHER EARLY PROPHETS 

The first great prophet whom we encounter after Moses is Samuel. His mother, we are told, was childless, and Samuel's birth came as an answer to a prayer which she addressed at the Temple of Shiloh. In gratitude she left him at Shiloh, where he was educated by the priest Eli. When Eli died, Samuel took over the leadership of the Hebrew people. Samuel was in part an

---

oracle prophet, or a seer. Thus, when Saul lost his donkeys, he wanted to go to Samuel to find out where they were. But Samuel was already the statesman prophet as well, who crowned kings, judged the people, and was bold enough to disagree with and cast off a ruler when he felt he was not acting in accordance with the will of God.

The Bible tells of the prophet Nathan who denounced King David when the latter had Uriah the Hittite slain in order to marry his wife. Few prophets in other countries dared reprove their kings. The Bible, however, records the story which Nathan told David: "A rich man, 11 the prophet related, "had robbed his poor neighbor of the only little ewe lamb that he had. When

David cried out that the man was deserving of death, the prophet turned on him and declared, "Thou art the man. David understood 

Instead of abusing the prophet, he fasted and prayed for forgiveness.

Similarly, the Bible tells us of Ahijah of Shiloh, who tore his cloak into twelve parts and cast ten parts on Jeroboam, the son of Nebat, thus urging him to rebel against King Solomon. 

ELIJAH 

Of the earlier prophets the one of whom most is written is Elijah. To him the Bible devotes a number of very fascinating chapters. We can mention here only two or three of the highlights of his career, but every Jewish boy and girl should read the story of Elijah in the Bible. Elijah came from Gilead, west of the 

Jordan, where nomadic ways lasted longer than elsewhere. The simple life in which everything was shared by everyone and where there were no really poor because there were no rich impressed itself upon Elijah as something which all Israel should strive for. Elijah was exceedingly devoted to God and regarded with horror King Ahab's worship of the Phoenician Baal. When, therefore, a period of drought occurred and for a number of years there was no rain, Elijah appeared before King Ahab and denounced him as the oppressor of Israel. Because of the king's Baal worship, Elijah declared, God was withholding rain from his people. 

Queen Jezebel pursued Elijah and sought to have him slain for his boldness. Elijah had to flee into hiding outside of the borders of Israel. But after some years, when the anger of the queen quieted, Elijah once more appeared and suggested to the king that a dramatic test be made of the powers of Israel's God and that of the Phoenician Baal. Sacrifices were to be offered, and the God who would send down fire would be declared the true God. 

What actually happened at Mount Carmel we do not know, but apparently it was considered a victory for Elijah, who thereupon called upon the people to fall on the priests of Baal and to slay them all. Naturally this brought upon Elijah and his party the vengeance of Queen Jezebel. Elijah was forced to flee again, this time southward beyond the kingdom of Judah, outside of the reach of the Hebrew monarchs. He fled to Sinai or Horeb, the holy mountain.

After a number of years, we meet Elijah again in the capital of Israel. King Ahab had just committed a crime of the utmost gravity. He had permitted a poor, innocent farmer by the name of 

Naboth to be sentenced to death on falsified charges in order that he might acquire Naboth's vineyard. The whole population of 

Israel was aroused by the king's disregard of Naboth's right to preserve the property of his fathers in his possession, but what others were whispering, Elijah boldly proclaimed. "Hast thou murdered and taken possession?" Elijah cried at the king. "At the spot where Naboth's blood was spilled dogs shall lick your own blood. Whereupon, we are told, the king recognized the seriousness of his crime and prayed for forgiveness.

Thereafter Elijah disappears from the scene. A beautiful tale tells that he was taken up to heaven in a chariot of fire. And legend has never ceased weaving stories about him how he comes down to earth to help the righteous poor and how he visits the home of every Jew on Seder night. Elijah was indeed a hero of the people.

AMOS We are obliged to skip over the work of Elisha, who started a rebellion in Israel, as well as in Syria, and the work of Michaia ben Imla and other prophets who flourished at about this time. 

We come now to a man who was one of the greatest of the prophets, a great poet who is the first prophet to have left us his words in writing. Altogether there remains of him no more than nine chapters, but what little there is marks him as one of the great geniuses of our people. Every Jew should know about the great prophet Amos.

It is strange that Amos was a prophet at all. By trade he was a shepherd and a tender of sycamore fig trees. But while he tended his sheep and trimmed his fig trees, he was constantly studying conditions, both in his own country and in other countries about him. With deep pain he saw the laws of God violated both by other peoples and by his own. He saw injustice committed among other peoples, cruelty, deceit, treachery. He denounced the foreign nations and declared that their day of doom would surely come, when they would pay for all their misdeeds. But above all he was interested in the fate of his neighboring Hebrew kingdom, Israel.

ATTACKS INJUSTICE IN ISRAEL 

In Israel, too, injustices were spreading. The new wave of prosperity which the reign of Jeroboam II had brought had introduced extensive commerce into Israel. With commerce came the quest for luxury, large possessions and inequality. The poor were getting the worst of it. The rich were enforcing the laws strictly, and poor men were being sold for debts no greater than a pair of shoes. Pledged garments were no longer returned. The stranger was no longer treated hospitably. Worst of all, even impartial justice was no longer being administered. For these very crimes, Amos felt, God had destroyed the Amorites, the former inhabitants

of the country, and had given their land to Israel. 

In order to make certain that Israel would behave better than the Amorites, God had sent them prophets and teachers to teach them. But Israel was no better than its neighbors. True, according to popular ideas, the people were very religious. Daily they brought their sacrifices, and on new moons and Sabbaths they thronged the Temple courts. But these displays, the prophet declared, were not what God wanted. "I hate, I despise your feasts," he says in the name of God. "I take no joy in your solemn assemblies....Turn thou away from me the noise of thy songs, but let justice well up as water, and righteousness as a mighty stream. 

But Israel was not responding with justice, and therefore, much as it grieved him, he had to pronounce its doom. Israel, the prophet declared, would suffer the same fate as the Amorites. 

The prophet warned that the day of God would come, a grim and terrible day, when Israel, together with the other nations, would stand in judgment before the ruler of heaven and earth, Being found guilty, it would be driven out of its country and led away into exile to a distant land beyond Damascus. Repeating these threats often, chanting a funeral song to attract attention the prophet became well-known in the capital of Israel, and many especially the poor whose cause he was championing eagerly thronged to listen to him.

AT BETH-EL 

One day when a great national feast was being celebrated, he was determined to force his way into the very royal sanctuary at Beth-El and to speak his mind there before the nation's nobility. 

To the great surprise and bewilderment of the royal high priest, a rough, poor-looking Judean shepherd arose in the midst of the elaborate service and, uninvited, began preaching to the assemblage. Priestly policemen quickly seized him and brought him before the high priest. The latter reported to the king that 

Amos had said that Jeroboam would fall by the sword and Israel would be led away captive.

The king, whether God-fearing or not, taking the prophet seriously, released him but ordered him banished to his own country. Amos returned broken-hearted to Judah. He would no longer preach in Israel, but he determined upon a more enduring form for his message - he set his prophecies down in writing. As a part of the Eible these writings have survived for thousands of years, and in every generation they have served the cause of the poor and the down-trodden against tyranny and oppression. ISAIAH

The next prophet about whom we shall tell briefly is Isaiah. 

Isaiah was a court prophet, a member of the nobility; neverthe less, his heart bled for his poor brothers, whose lot he sought to improve by demanding justice for them.

The tide of prosperity which had swept over Israel in the days of Jeroboam II reached Judah also under King Uzziah. Here,

T.

too, commerce was developing. Many Judeans were getting rich and were amassing private property. Men, the prophet declared, were joining house to house and field to field, until there was no more room for the poor man, as though the rich were to remain alone in the land. He saw grasping and robbery and drunkenness. 

Against all of these he cried out and warned the people of a day of doom, even as Amos had done before in Israel. The hand of God would fall heavily upon them he warned, and would exterminate all those who were guilty of oppressing God's people. 

STATESMAN 

But in addition to his concern over social conditions, the prophet was even more occupied with problems of state. King Ahaz had ascended the throne and, as you recall, was threatened by a combination of the kings of Israel and Aram. Ahaz was eager to appeal for aid to the king of Assyria. To the prophet this spelled not only surrender of freedom but also yielding to foreign religious influence which might destroy Judean worship. Bitterly, therefore, the prophet fought against the king's surrender. He pleaded with him to wait and let matters take their own course. 

The king of Assyria was bound to strike at Syria and Israel, and Ahaz merely needed to hold out a while. But Ahaz refused to listen and sent his messengers with gifts to the king of Assyria offering the surrender of Judah. Assyria struck at Israel and Aram, and Judah was free. But Judah became tributary both for payment of taxes and for religious influence, as the prophet and foretold. 

SH'OR YOSHUV 

Seeing that his entire policy was opposed by the king, Isaiah, went into retirement for a time and kept away from royal affairs, He gathered about him a band of disciples whom he instructed in the word of God and spoke of them as sh'or yoshuv, "a remnant shall return. But soon a new king ruled who was more inclined to listen to the prophet. King Hezekiah was a God-fearing king who was anxious to enforce the prophetic teachings. With the support of the king, the high places where God was worshipped in the form of Baal were suppressed. A brazen 

A brazen serpent, said to have been in the Temple since the days of Moses, was smashed to pieces. Better justice was enforced in the law courts, and altogether it seemed as if a new era was dawning for Judah. 

OPPOSES REBELLION 

con- Unfortunately, however, political affairs continued to fuse the situation. King Sargon II of Assyria, the conqueror of 

Israel, had recently suffered defeat, and it seemed as if the vast Assyrian empire were prepared to rebel against him. Egypt, was especially fearful that its turn of conquest would come soon, tried to stir up the Palestinian states against the king of Assyria. King Hezekiah was to lead in the rebellion. 

With all his energy Isaiah set himself to oppose any rebellion on the part of King Hezekiah. To dramatize for the people what would happen to them if the Assyrians were victorious, Isaiah walked about barefoot and clad only in a loin-cloth to show

his people how the Assyrians would lead the Judeans into captivity if they should rebel. For a time Isaiah was successful and 

Hezekiah abandoned thoughts of rebellion. But when a new king, Sennacherib, came upon the throne of Assyria and it seemed that he was having difficulty in establishing his rule at home, the Egyptian party forced the king's hand, and Hezekiah was in open revolt. The

We already told in an earlier section what had happened. 

Assyrians overran Judah, conquered the cities, and demanded and received large ransom, including the king's treasures and even his harem. But the Assyrians were not satisfied. They demanded that the king surrender his capital, Jerusalem, and when he refused they laid siege to it.

CHANGES ATTITUDE TOWARD ASSYRIA 

Isaiah, who had opposed the war from the start, now suddenly changed his views. The king of Assyria had boasted that he would reduce Jerusalem as he had reduced ather cities, and that he would deal with the God of Israel as he had dealt with other gods. 

The slight to God was more than the prophet could endure, Since Israel's God was the God of the entire world, he had merely made use of the Assyrians as a weapon of punishment, as an ax with which to cut down the tree; but when the ax prides itself over the one who wields it, then it is time for it, itself, to be struck down. 

In the time of the greatest national despair, Isaiah declared that the Assyrians would never take Jerusalem, that God would somehow save the city.

LAST DAYS 

And, strangely, things happened as the prophet had predicted.

A plague smote the Assyrians in their camp, and they hastily withdrew from Jerusalem and fled to their own country. The prophet was proven right; and thereafter for a time the prophet's demands were listened to. 

Unfortunately, the happier era did not last after the death of the king. A new ruler came to the throne who persecuted the prophets and may have sentenced many of them to death. In a world torn by war and oppression, the prophet found little joy or peace. But while perhaps despairing of the present, the prophet never lost his hope for the future. He dreamed his dream of a golden age when the lion would lie down with the lamb and a little child would lead them; when swords would be beaten into ploughshares and spears into pruning hooks; when nation would no longer lift up sword against nation, nor would they learn war; any more. 

JEREMIAH 

The last prophet of the Kingdom of Judah was Jeremiah. He lived during the closing days of the state, when it was beset by enemies who brought about its fall and the destruction of the Temple. As a prophet, Jeremiah foresaw what was happening more

clearly than others, and therefore his life was most unhappy and tragic.

Born in 650 B.C.E., his youth was brightened for the moment by the reforms of Josiah, about which we told earlier. But after the king was slain, the people were disappointed that God had not taken better care of his champion, and so they returned to Assyrian idol worship and to all forms of corruption and oppression that went with it. The prophet accused the people of stealing, murder, swearing falsely, and burning incense to Baal. The prophet thought he might begin by reforming his own townspeople, but when he began to denounce them for their wrongdoing, his own family cast him out and drove him from the city, forcing him to go to the capital at Jerusalem.

FIGHTS EVILS 

In Jerusalem the prophet met not only the evils of his own small community but greater vices which outraged him. The practice of idolatry had spread so far that once again children were being offered up by their parents as sacrifices. Jeremiah was infuriated. He took an earthen pot to the Valley of Hinnom on the outskirts of Jerusalem, where this practice was carried on. There he smashed the pot to bits in the presence of an assembly of worshipers. In the same way, he told them, God would smash the backbone of the Kingdom of Judah because they had turned the pleasant valley into a valley of abomination. For his boldness the prophet suffered the consequences. Priestly guards seized him, slapped him in the face, and arrested him. Jeremiah was put in the stocks overnight like a common prisioner.

In Jerusalem Jeremiah noted a false feeling of security on the part of the people. The belief had spread since the days of 

Isaiah that, happen what may, God would not permit His Temple to be destroyed. Jeremiah determined to shatter that false confidence. On one occasion, when a large assemblage was gathered, Jeremiah arose and proclaimed the doom of the Temple. Only righteousness, he declared, lasts forever; a temple is only a structure of wood, stone and mortar. Unless the Israelites mended their ways, God would no more spare the Temple of Jerusalem than He had saved the Temple of Shiloh, which was destroyed by the Philistines. 

By the

Consternation ran through the crowd. Jeremiah was at once arrested and brought up for trial on charges of treason. merest good fortune, the prophet's life was spared, but he was warned to stay in hiding in order not to enrage the people of 

Jerusalem any further.

But evils were multiplying in Jerusalem. The king and nobles oppressed the poor, and the lot of the common people was growing worse. In the meantime, internal disorders were increasing, and the prophet foresaw the doom of the city. Egypt especially had again become active. Once more she was fearful of invasion, and she was trying to incite the Judeans into rebellion in order to keep the enemy from herself. In the midst of this great national uncertainty a solemn fast day was arranged in the year 600 B.C.E.,

3N p 1, 12072 02.00 J. A

Ca 12. 7000 ! ས།་ זי J. 05

and the entire nation was invited to come to Jerusalem to pray.

PROPHECIES READ BEFORE PRINCES AND KING 

Jeremiah determined to use this occasion to impress a lesson upon the people. He called his scribe Baruch and dictated to him all the prophecies which he had uttered since the beginning of his career, the prophecies of doom and fear and the prophecies that called for repentance. Baruch copied these messages and then came before the assemblage and read them aloud. Everyone was greatly moved. One of the noblemen was so impressed that he invited Baruch to read the scroll before his fellow nobles, and these in turn invited him to read it before the king. 

But the king remained unmoved. He had set his heart on rebelling against the Chaldeans, and as Jeremiah's words were read to him warning him against this course and also denouncing idol worship and greed and selfishness, he took each page as it was handed to him, cut it up, and threw it into the fire. 

Jeremiah was deeply grieved at the loss of his precious scroll, but he ordered Baruch to recopy the prophecies and many others for the future; for he realized that with the king disposed as he was Jerusalem could not be saved.

DISASTROUS REBELLION 

Very soon the king Jehoiakim was in open rebellion, and the Chaldeans were besieging the city. The king died, and his son 

Jehoiachin ascended the throne, but within a few months he was obliged to surrender it to the Chaldeans. He was led away captive together with ten thousand of the leading noblemen and best mechanics. The new king swore an oath to be faithful to the king of the Chaldeans and to pay tribute. 

But once more the Egyptians were active and after considerable agitation managed to persuade the new king, Zedekiah, to break his allegiance to King Nebuchadnezzar. A Chaldean army was at the time engaged in the siege of Tyre, a siege which took the Babylonians over thirteen years without conclusive results.

But Nebuchadnezzar dispatched part of his army against Judea, and in 587 B.C.E. the army laid siege to the city of Jerusalem. 

The people fasted and prayed. They released their Hebrew slaves in accordance with the law and promised to fulfill all of God's commands if they were saved from the terrible fate which seemed to await them. Soon there came good news. An Egyptian army was on the march, and the Babylonians raised the siege of Jerusalem to encounter the Egyptians. At once there was a let-down. Idolatry once more became widespread, and all the Hebrew slaves who were freed were enslaved once again. 

PROPHET THROWN INTO PRISON 

The prophet, bitter at this treachery, delivered a stinging denunciation of the nobles and their behavior. He warned them that the Chaldeans had not left for good but would soon return

.i

and take their vengeance.

The nobles became enraged against Jeremiah and waited for an opportunity to arrest him. When Jeremiah tried to leave the city for his native village on some private business, he was arrested on the charge of deserting to the enemy and was thrown into a dungeon of mire. He would have died there had he not been saved secretly by one of the king's servants. 

The king, while continuing the war, really did not have his heart in it.

He wished that he might have followed the advice of the prophet, but he was afraid. However, he kept Jeremiah secretly in the guard-house and provided him with food. 

As the prophet foretold, so it happened; the Chaldeans returned. The city, not too well provided with provisions, now received large numbers of refugees who needed to be fed. Soon the supplies were consumed, and the city was faced with starvation. 

The defenders began to lose heart. On the 17th of the month of 

Tamuz the walls of the city were breached. Three weeks later, on the 9th of Ov, the Temple was in flames, and the Chaldeans were masters.

SPARED BY CHALDEANS 

END VIOLENT 

Jeremiah was spared by the Chaldeans, who had heard of his peaceful disposition during the entire time. Nebuchadnezzar wanted him to accompany the army to Babylonia, but Jeremiah felt, now that the worst had happened, that he might as well spend his last days consoling the remaining Judeans in their homeland. But even that comfort was denied him. The governor appointed by Nebuchadnezzar was, as we told earlier, murdered by a band of zealots. 

The remaining panic-stricken Judeans fled to Egypt and forced the aged Jeremiah to accompany them.

In Egypt, we are told, the Jews lived much after the manner of the Egyptians. They worshiped the goddess of Heaven in addition to their own God and when Jeremiah, outraged by such open idolatry, denounced them, they turned on him fiercely. Legend has it that Jeremiah met his death at the hands of the enraged 

Jews of Egypt.

We now have a part answer to our question: How did the Jews come from Palestine to the United States? An enemy overran Judea and led our ancestors away as exiles to Babylonia, thus taking us on the first stage of our journey to the United States. Our ancestors were deeply attached to their land and fought valiantly to preserve their independence. But a power far mightier than they forced them to leave their land. Now the best elements of the people, the nobility, the soldiers, the handworkers, the most industrious farmers, were in Babylonia. What was to be the next stop in the life of our people? Was their life over in Judea, and did they now become a Diaspora people? Let us go on to the next portion of our story and see how it unfolds.

BOOK II 

THE SECOND COMMONWEALTH

BOOK II 

HOW WAS THE JEWISH STATE RESTORED? 

You might well imagine that the destruction of Jerusalem by Nebuchadnezzar spells the end of the story of the Jewish people. 

Yet you know that it is not the end, because you and I are here, continuing Jewish life. How, then, did it happen that in spite of the loss of their state the Jewish people was able to rally once again and recover from the terrific blows it received? And where did it reestablish itself, in Babylonia or Judea? Were the exiles able to organize themselves into a community in Babylonia, or were there enough Judeans left in Judea to start the Jewish people anew? There were, undoubtedly, many poor farmers left in Judea, yet these were both too few and, especially, too powerless to start organized life again. The best elements of 

Judea had been taken captive to Babylon, while large numbers had perished in the war or had fled the country. Already at that time 

Jews were scattered throughout the then known world. 

There were some Jews in Egypt in a military colony, but they were far too steeped in idol worship to care for the future of their people as a whole. They even had their own temple, a sign that they were quite comfortable where they were. 

THE BABYLONIAN EXILES 

As you have probably guessed, it was the Babylonian Jews, those who had been taken away as captives, the nobles and the leaders, the smiths and the handworkers, the soldiers and farmers, who reestablished their people. And they established themselves not in Babylonia but in Judea. They did something which had never happened before they returned to the land from which they had been exiled and reestablished their state. We can well understand how our ancestors felt, because in our own day Jewish pioneers from all parts of the world are trying to reestablish a home for themselves in Palestine. 

But why did they do that? Were they dissatisfied with Babylonia? No, they had no reason to be dissatisfied. Strangely enough, it turned out that economically life in Babylonia was far more confortable than life in Palestine. The farms there yielded larger harvests, and the more adventurous entered commerce or industry and soon became exceedingly wealthy. In that respect 

Babylonian Jews were very much like Jews in the United States. 

Earlier the ten tribes of Israel had been settled near the River 

Chebar, where the city of Cutha was located. The Cuthites, you will recall (Page 20), had been exchanged for them and settled in Israel, where they became known as the Samaritans. When Assyria fell and Babylonia succeeded to her greatness, the capital of Babylonia was located not far from the Jewish settlement. The new 

Jewish exiles were settled next to their former brothers, and thus

i rus D vdw di

they found themselves in the very center of wealth and culture. 

Babylonia was a highly developed country. country. It had a manybranched industry, and its commerce extended all over the ancient world. It was among the first to devise a system of writing, methods of bookkeeping, and accurate commercial law. The weights and measures of Babylonia are the basis of our own, and its scientific system of astronomy is the foundation for the science of today. Doctors, writers, and poets were prominent there, and a very active religious life was carried on. The Jews were in the midst of the highest civilization which the period afforded. 

GREAT RELIGIOUS CHANGE 

Why, then, should they have wanted to leave the new country and return to poor Judea? The answer to this question is a sort of miracle which happened in Babylonia. Judaism proved itself able to live on under conditions which for other religions would have spelled death. Other religions of the time were limited to their own country. Their god was believed to rule only the territory which his people inhabited; therefore, if the people left its land, it also left its god behind. The Jews were the first nation to proclaim that their God was the Ruler of the entire world. Now their test would come. Would they still feel that their God was as much with them in Babylonia as He had been with them in Palestine? Our ancestors stood the test, and the Jewish people was saved.

A change had come over the Judean exiles. Many who in their homeland may have doubted the teaching of the prophets now saw only too clearly how the prophetic warning had come true. Jerusalem was in ruins, Judea lay waste, and their nation was in a strange land. They felt now that had they listened to their prophets, had they kept their religion faithfully, this calamity might not have come upon them. Then they remembered that the prophets had also spoken words of comfort and cheer. If the people repented and returned to God, God would have mercy on them and restore them to their land again. Since the first part of the prophets' warning had come true, so would the last. The majority of the Jews now turned to the prophetic party. What life in Judea could not accomplish, life in Babylonia did. The experience of the exile forced them to think more deeply about the meaning of their lives and their faith. one.

Of course, the return to the prophets did not include every- Undoubtedly many Judeans began to doubt the power of their God, who had thus permitted them to be led away into exile. A 

God who cannot protect His own people, they thought, was not a very powerful God.

But most of them felt that they wanted to continue to be Jews regardless of the attraction of the gilded gods and temples of their new home. And wanting to be Jews, they would take the necessary steps to make their Judaism live. They feared that the weaker ones among them, especially their children, who were growing up without having seen Jerusalem and its Temple, would gradually be weaned away from the religion of their fathers, They

C tí.

were very much troubled, also, by their inability to worship their God in what they considered the right way. They had been accustomed to worshipping through sacrifices, and now, in a strange land, the produce of which they considered impure, they could no longer offer their customary gifts to God.

SYNAGOGUES ESTABLISHED 

Their need for frequent meetings both for talking over their conditions and for expressing their religious feeling led to the organization of what we today would call synagogues. Some scholars believe that synagogues had existed among our ancestors even during the period of the First Temple. Certainly in Babylonia, gatherings for prayer and study became frequent. At first meeting in the house of an elder, the synagogue may later have been established in special quarters.

On Sabbaths, and especially on the festivals, the Judeans gathered to celebrate the occasion, to recite prayers, to read from the Torah, and to recall Israel's ancient greatness. And in these synagogues they kept the hope alive that some day it would be possible for some of them to return to their homeland to re-establish the Temple and their sacrifices. 

PROPHETS PREACHED THE RETURN 

In the meantime they set themselves carefully to study all the writings which they had brought with them. These books were now arranged and carefully edited. A new great book of history was composed to teach the exiles the lesson that their God would be with them if they obeyed His laws. Thus through prayer and through study the hope for return was kept alive. Especially was it raised to a high pitch by several prophets who were living at the time and who were preaching a message of hope and courage. 

The leader of these prophets was Ezekiel. He had come from Judea with the first group of exiles in 597 B.C.E. At first, like other prophets, he thundered at the Judeans for seeking to rebel against the king of Babylonia and warned of the coming destruction of Jerusalem. But when the Temple fell and the great exile came 

Babylonia, he changed his manner and turned instead to comforting his brothers, promising them the deliverance of God. God, to

A fear had been eating at the hearts of the exiles. they had been taught, is a jealous God, avenging the sins of fathers on sons and grandsons. Would they not now be made to suffer for the sing of their fathers? Ezekiel came forward with a new religious teaching: Fathers would not be punished for the sins of the sons, nor sons for the sins of their fathers, but each one would be judged in accordance with his own merits. This teaching inspired confidence in the downcast exiles, who had feared that they would forever be consigned to punishment. In a vision telling how God restored life to a heap of dried bones, the prophet encouraged the people to believe that even the driest bones meaning by that even the most hopeless people could still be restored by God to independence and nationhood. 

---

TRI

A short time after the death of Ezekiel, the mighty Babylonian empire fell before the armies of Cyrus, king of the Medes and Persians. At that time a new prophet appeared on the scene whose name is unknown to us but whose writings in the Bible appear after those of Isaiah, and whom we, therefore, call the Second Isaiah. The famous proclamation of Cyrus permitting the Judeans to return may already have been issued, or at least it was expected. This prophet now proclaimed that Israel's period of exile was at an end and that it would be rewarded by God doubly for all its suffering. In a fiery call he urged upon his people to get ready to return. "Comfort ye, comfort ye, my people, the prophet cried out. "Clear a path in the wilderness, a highway for our God. A new life, he promised, awaited the returning exiles, a life of prosperity and happinesss, a life of glory and grandeur. Kings and princes would rebuild Jerusalem's walls and gates. They would vie with one another for the privilege of serving the Jewish people. 11 The unknown prophet especially directed his darts against those who had taken to idol worship. Making sport of the idols, he describes a man cutting a piece of wood, using most of it as fuel to cook his dinner with and fashioning the rest of it unto a god. Similarly, against those who may have begun to follow the new Persian belief in two gods, one of light and one of darkness, Isaiah thundered that the God of heaven was the author of light and of darkness, that there is but one God and not two. 

PERMISSION FOR RETURN FINALLY SECURED 

The labor of the prophets and their followers finally bore fruit. Cyrus, friendly to all religions, saw, in addition, the value of a Jewish state loyal to Persia on the road to Egypt. 

Accordingly, he gave permission for as many Jews as wanted it to return to their ancient home and re-establish their worship. The enthusiasm was great. "When the Lord restored the captivity of 

Zion we were like unto them that dream," the psalm writer sang about this event. Tens of thousands of Judeans undertook to return to the ancient homeland. Those who could not go in person showered upon those returning gifts of gold and silver for the new Temple.

The new community was under the leadership of two men. 

Zerubbabel was the political head, who, it was hoped, would be the king or ruler, he being a descendant of the house of David. 

Joshua, son of Jozadak, of the priestly family, was to be the high priest.

DISAPPOINTMENT GREETS RETURNED EXILE 

The arrival in the land of their fathers was far different from the glowing picture the prophets had painted. Jerusalem lay in ruins, and whatever parts could be inhabited were settled by strangers. The newcomers' first thought was to re-establish their traditional worship through sacrifices. For the time being, they set up an altar, and on the high holydays they offered the first sacrifices. They then began to lay plans for rebuilding the 

Temple. They gathered the material, and possibly they mav have

JUC ས་ ཀ 1.0 

Javid A

TIK EL 

1 ་༈* i.

already laid the foundations, when the new community was overwhelmed with troubles.

First of all, there were religious difficulties. The Judeans who had remained in the country had been used to a form of worship that had in it elements of idolatry and impurity. Many may have persisted in their worship at the old high places. This was, of course, an abomination to the newly returned exiles from Babylon.

In addition, there were troubles from without. Ammonites, Moabites, Philistines, and Samaritans had found their way into the deserted country and had occupied some of the best lands. These now had to be dispossessed or compromised within some form. 

Special difficulty was presented by the Samaritans. The problem with them was not that they were unfriendly but that they wanted to be too friendly. They came to the Jews offering help in the building of the Temple, which really meant that the Samaritans wanted to join with the returning Judeans into one community. fortunately, the Samaritans had preserved some of the old superstitions and idolatrous beliefs which had been practiced in early Israel, to which they added many from their own native country. 

For the Judeans to accept them would have meant for them to give up their pure faith and their beliefs, for which they had made such great sacrifices. Naturally they were unwilling to do so. Un But the Samaritans, feeling rebuffed, turned from friends to enemies. They slandered the Jews before the Persian court and said that the Jews planned to set up an independent state and revolt against Persia. An order came for the Persian court stopping the building of the Temple. It seemed now that the entire trek from Babylon to Judea had been in vain.

Added to these hardships were famines caused by bad harvests. 

The land had been neglected so long that now it was difficult to till it, and the newcomers had not the experience needed to till land in rocky Judea. All in all, therefore, the fifteen years which followed the setting up of the altar were discouraging. bright dreams roused by the prophets of Babylonia were far indeed from being realized.

PROPHETS REVIVE HOPE FOR A TIME The 

But there was always a party which kept alive the hopes that had guided the band across the desert. New prophets arose, the last in the line of prophecy, to call for one final supreme effort. 

Now a new government was reigning in Persia. The king was friendly, and permission to rebuild the Temple was sought and received. 

In the year 520 B.C.E. the building of the Temple began, and in 

516 B.C.E. it was completed. Though some of the older men wept because the old Temple which they recalled had been a far grander structure than the new one, the prophets predicted a great future for the new house of worship.

But times continued hard, and political conditions were difficult. Zerubbabel disappeared from the scene. We do not know whether he died or was recalled; at any rate, no one succeeded 1

1.

to his authority. The new community was now ruled by the high priest, over whom a Persian governor exercised authority. Taxes were heavy, and the double taxes which the Jews had to pay, both to the Persian government and to their own self-government, were almost impossible to bear. As the years of famine became more frequent, the rich lent money to the poor and took as security their goods, or often even the very person of the debtor or his children. When a debtor was unable to pay his debt, he was sold into slavery.

The neighboring nations were still occupying large parts of Judea, and in order to make friends of them, many Judeans intermarried with them. Thus it came about that many Jewish children had non-Jewish mothers who spoke the languages of their own country, and the children did not even learn to speak Hebrew in their homes. Such a condition was naturally most distressing to the religious party, which strove to maintain its ideals in this unhappy society.

EZRA SETS OUT TO HELP 

News about the difficulties in Judea reached the religious party of Babylon, which was following with keen interest everything that was happening in the new-old homeland. Clearly something out of the ordinary had to be done to regain control over the situation. The Babylonian party took counsel and decided to send to Judea its chief religious leader, Ezra. Ezra was called the Scribe because he was both a great scholar and editor of the books of the Bible. It was decided that Ezra would go over in person to throw his own influence into the scale and bring about real reforms in Judea.

Ezra asked and received permission from the Persian king to set out with a large company which was eager to join him. He was even offered an escort, which he declined. Five thousand men left with Ezra, and in the year 458 B.C.E. set out upon the journey which their brethren had taken about eighty years before. 

Ezra received a grand reception such as was fitting for the head of Babylonian Jewry. But no sooner did he become familiar with conditions in Judea than he was moved to tears and to deep and earnest prayer. The situation was far worse than he had dared to picture to himself. The faith which had so painfully been cultivated in Babylonia seemed to be disappearing in Palestine. Similarly, the careful family records of pure descent which the Babylonians, both Jews and non-Jews, so highly prized, were no longer kept.

The presence of Ezra in their midst, however, strengthened the religious party in Judea. The leaders issued a call to the heads of the families to gather at a meeting with Ezra and offered to do whatever Ezra declared necessary for the welfare of the 

Jewish religion. 

Ezra had a very difficult decision to make. What was to be done about the foreign wives? Men had not yet learned how to make converts to their religion. Among non-Jews a person who came

to a new country accepted its religion, and when he left he took up the religion of the new land to which he came. The Jews had been the one exception. When they came into Palestine they brought their own religion with them. But the Persian religion was also observed in Palestine. Thus newcomers other than the 

Jews were free to worship either the Jewish religion or the Persian or even to keep their old religion. Even those who did accept 

Judaism must have kept many customs of their former idolatrous worship. Had there been but a few of these they would have been lost in the larger body of Jews, but there were many of them and they were among the most influential families. What was to be done? Either Judaism as taught by the Babylonian prophets would disappear, or else the Jews had to take a very radical step, even if it was harsh. Ezra, determined to save the Jewish religion, decided upon the harsh step. He demanded that those who had married foreign women send them away, together with their children, back to their parents' homes.

Severe though the measure was, many of the families obeyed the decree. But the dismissal of their wives naturally brought upon the Judeans the anger of their foreign fathers-in-law, who threatened them with violence if they did not take their wives back. Many, too, changed their minds about parting with their wives and children. Ezra's decree thus was soon disregarded and after thirteen years Ezra had made but little headway. of his reforms, too, seemed to be losing ground. 

NEHEMIAH TO THE RESCUE (445-433 B.C.E.) 

All Finally, however, a leader appeared who was able to enforce the demands of Ezra and to bring a new spirit into the country. 

This man was Nehemiah, cup-bearer of King Artaxerxes of Persia. 

Nehemiah, as cup-bearer, was one of the highest nobles of Persia, but unlike some important Jews today who forget about their brethern, he did not lose his interest in Judea. But the news of 

Judea that reached him left him more and more distressed and sorrowing. The king one day asked him why he looked so sad. "Why shall I not look sad," Nehemiah is reported to have said, "when the heritage of my fathers lies in the dust and there is none to lift it up? And then Nehemiah confessed to the king his ardent wish to go to Judea and put matters aright. When the king expressed unwillingness to part with him, Nehemiah promised faithfully that he would return after a short time.

Nehemiah was given permission to go to Judea for the stated period of twelve years and, even more, he was appointed governor of the province. Armed with this authority, he hurried westward. 

Arriving secretly in Jerusalem, he quietly inspected the state of safety of the city. He found that because Jerusalem had no defenses, bands of the neighboring peoples were constantly breaking in and pillaging the residents. Like Ezra, too, he was shocked to find that not only the poor but the highest nobility as well had intermarried with the neighboring peoples. The very high priest had built a pavilion for an Ammonite in the Temple precincts, and the high priest's son had married a daughter of the 

Samaritan ruler.

BUILDS WALL AROUND JERUSALEM 

Nehemiah set himself as his first task to fortify the defenses of Jerusalem. Arming his workers so that in one hand they held the trowel and the other the sword, and himself constantly vigilant both night and day, he began rebuilding the walls of the city. The enemy, at first mocking the workers, soon discovered that Nehemiah was in earnest and that it was already too late to interfere. 

When the walls were completed, Nehemiah became concerned with giving Jerusalem a larger population. Accordingly, he selected every tenth man in the provinces to come to live in Jerusalem. 

In order to relieve the conditions of the poor, he freed them of the governor's tax, excused the debts which the poor Judeans owed him, and called upon the heads of the rich families to do likewise. It would be a disgrace, he declared, for Judeans to enslave their brothers, when all of them had come for the common purpose of setting up their divine worship in their Holy Land. 

The rich men were shamed into following Nehemiah's lead. They all excused the poor their debts, and for a while peace and good will reigned in the country. 

HOLDS GREAT ASSEMBLY 

Then Ezra and Nehemiah called a great assembly on Rosh Hashono. A huge crowd gathered at the water gate, and Ezra, on a raised platform, began to read the law to many who had perhaps never heard it before. The law made a deep impression upon all who heard it. They wept and rent their garments and declared, "We have sinned, we have sinned." Ezra reassured them and stated that they need have no fear, that they might go home and enjoy the feast secure in God's forgiveness if they promised to obey the law and send away their foreign wives.

Once more the people pledged to heed the demands of Ezra and Nehemiah. Now foreign wives were sent away, for the people felt secure under the leadership of their Jewish governor, and the neighboring peoples could not harm them any longer.

An assembly of the heads of the Jerusalem families was organized to govern the internal Jewish affairs together with the priest. Alongside of the Temple, synagogues were established where study was to be carried on. The Torah became the constitution of the new state and was to be read publicly on market days, every Monday and Thursday, as well as on the Sabbath and on festivals. The Judeans promised to observe the Sabbath and festivals faithfully and to refrain from business on those days. They also agreed to pay their taxes to the Temple priests and Levites. 

GOES BACK TO PERSIA AND RETURNS 

No sooner had Nehemiah returned to the Persian capital, than once again the old evils reasserted themselves. The priests and 

Levites were not given the tenth part of the crop, foreign wives were taken back, Sabbaths were violated, and the Temple tax was

The 

Hearing of these developments, Nehemiah asked permission to return to Judea once again. Upon his return, he dealt severely with the transgressors. He expelled the priests who had intermarried with the non-Jews, especially the grandson of the high priest, who had married the daughter of the Samaritan chief. 

Samaritan ruler, to console his son-in-law, built him a temple on Mount Gerizim and declared his complete separation from the Jews. Otherwise Jewish life continued to run quietly and uneventfully. At any rate, we know very little of what happened for the next hundred years or thereabouts, until the great Alexander, marching in from Greece, conquered Judea on his way to Egypt and began a new era for our ancestors. 

JUDEA AGAIN THE CENTER 

There were Jews in other countries of the world, large numbers in Babylonia, Egypt, and Syria. But Judea held the central position, even though more Jews lived outside of it than in it. 

The Jews of Palestine were far poorer than those of Babylonia. 

The area which was inhabited by Jews was small only Judea, the small area around Jerusalem. The occupations of the people were mainly agricultural, and so were the principal exports, which consisted of wine, olive oil, and fruits. Handwork was already also practiced, and clothmanufactured in the homes was sold to the 

Phoenician merchants. Some also undertook sea voyages in the interest of trade, and there was a small merchant class. But as yet these were only beginnings.

The Jews were under the rule of the Persians, who appointed governors over them and collected the taxes. Judea was influenced so greatly by this foreign rule that very soon the language of every day use ceased to be Hebrew and became Aramaic, the language that was common at that time.

RICH IN RELIGIOUS LEADERSHIP 

But if Judea was poor in economic goods it was rich spiritually, and it was the center of the Jewish religion. The Jews were a religious nation with religious leadership. The officers were the priests who ministered in the Temple. The priests held their office by descent, son following father in the priesthood. 

There was, however, another class of leaders more democratically chosen. This class consisted of the scribes, or the great rabbis of the period. Just as the prophets before them were in no sense officials of the government, so the scribes now taking their place were volunteer leaders holding no public office and receiving no public reward. But, though unofficial, they were the real leaders of the people, for they were the teachers, the heads of the synagogue, and the interpreters of the Law. They were the men who knew the Torah, and therefore whenever a question arose involving a dispute, the parties naturally turned to them. The scribes were teachers of the grownups, for among our ancestors the first ones to go to school were not the children but the fathers, who then had the duty of teaching their children. The

scribes were also editors of the Bible. 

Ezra, the greatest of the scribes, is said to have collected and arranged the Five Books of Moses and the early prophets. Later, when the Samaritans refused to recognize the books of the prophets as part of the sacred literature, the scribes ordered that portions of the later prophets should be read in the synagogue every Sabbath, to be known as the Haftoro, and included the later prophets as part of our Bible. Thus in our Bible we have the living message of the prophets, while the Samaritans have only the Five Books of Moses and the Book of Joshua.

EDIT AND WRITE BOOKS 

Not only did the scribes arrange and edit the existing books but they added books of their own which they themselves composed. 

They wrote the Book of Chronicles and the stories of Ezra and Nehemiah. Others who were gifted poets wrote beautiful psalms. Men of wisdom composed proverbs and gathered them into collections. 

Books of fiction were written, and the lovely story of Ruth was probably composed at this time. Some scholars believe that the writer of the Book of Ruth wanted to oppose Ezra's reform about sending away foreign wives and so showed how a Moabite woman proved not only to be a good Jewess but was even the great-grandmother of King David himself.

There were even doubters among the Jews. They had been taught that God rewards the good and punishes the wicked. But in life they often saw many good people suffering and many wicked people prospering. And so one questioner who could not understand the ways of God wrote the Book of Job, which tells of a good man suffering seemingly unjustly and having an argument with God. 

His friends try to convince him that he must have done some wrong otherwise God would not have punished him. But God finally answers Job that man with his small mind cannot hope to understand all of God's mysteries.

INTRODUCE IMPORTANT CHANGES 

During this time many important changes were instituted in Jewish life. Prayers were selected from the Bible or were newly written. Sabbath observance was made more severe, and various 

Sabbath laws were enacted. The laws regarding permitted and prohibited foods must have been strengthened at that time. 

A very interesting reform is the change of the alphabet from the old Hebrew-Phoenician to the more easily read and written 

Assyrian-Aramaic script. Someone recently tried to change the

Hebrew alphabet to the Roman so as to make the Hebrew more easily read by others, but the change was not accepted by the Jewish people. In Ezra's time, however, Ezra's great influence helped to carry through his reform. How wise this change was can be judged from the Samaritans, who kept the old script and whose writings therefore very few people today can read. 

The Jews also adopted the Babylonian names of the months in order to bring their business documents in line with other countries.

Aside from the spiritual changes very little else is known

about Judea during this period. 

Someone once wrote that a nation is happy if its history is brief. Our ancestors were not exactly happy because many of them were very poor, and the struggle to earn a living was difficult. Nevertheless there were few disturbing political factors, and in that sense they were happy certainly happier than during the first part of the next period, to which we shall now turn. 

QUESTIONS 

1. Which Jews helped to restore the Jewish state? 

Why were they interested in returning to Palestine? 2. 

Who were the prophets who preached the return? 

What were their teachings? 

3. Who gave the Jews permission to return? 

4. What difficulties beset the newcomers? 5.

Who was Ezra, and what effect did his coming have on the 

Judean community? 

6. Who was Nehemiah, and what did he accomplish?

In what respects was Judea poor and in what rich? 7. 8.

What literary work was carried on during this period? 9. 

What new laws and reforms were introduced?

ADDITIONAL READINGS 

Golub, J. S., In the Days of the Second Temple, pp. 3-55 

Levinger, L. J., Story of the Jew, pp. 34-36 

Goldberg, I. and Benderly, S., Outline of Jewish Knowledge, Vol. III, pp. 5-48; 131-162 

Gamoran, M. G., The Voice of the Prophets, pp. 23, 28, 34, 44, 105, 120, 217, 225, 228, 235, 236 

Harris, M. H., A Thousand Years of Jewish History, pp. 17-25 

Cohon, B. D., The Prophets, Chapters 10, 11, 12, 13, 14

Baily, A. E., Daily Life in Bible Times, Chapter 10

II 

THE GREEKS COME TO JUDEA 

We have seen how, after an absence of about fifty years, our forefathers once more established in Judea a center of Jewish life for the hundreds of thousands, perhaps millions, of Jews who were already scattered in the countries around Palestine. But Judea lost that position. Why? How did the second Jewish government in 

Palestine disappear?

This will be the subject of the story of the next few chapters. We shall see how, one after another, waves of conquest overran Palestine. Until now the conquerors had been Asiatic or 

African. Egyptians had come from Africa, and the Assyrians and 

Babylonians were Asiatic. Now new conquerors were to come from 

Europe. Their methods were to be different from those of the mild, easy-going Persians, who merely collected taxes but otherwise left the country to itself.

The first of these conquerors, the Greeks, in time proved weak. The Jews rebelled and gradually won their independence. But the second conquerors, the Romans, were perhaps the greatest the world has ever known. The Jews, already accustomed to independence, tried to drive out the Romans as they had the Greeks. Unfortunately, that proved too great an undertaking. Against the mighty rock of Rome the Jewish armies were dashed again and again until they were utterly exhausted.

But let us begin to tell the story of the first conqueror, the Greeks. The Jews, we said, gained their independence from the Greeks. Independence is gained only after a stubborn war, but how and when did the Jews fight? When we saw them last, they had not even an army. We shall be surprised to learn that this war began very largely as a civil war and that as the result of this war the Chanuko festival was instituted. Thus we wonder further what could have led to civil war in the peaceful Judean country and what could have happened to call forth the establishment of a holiday.

THE GREEKS UNDER ALEXANDER 

The answer to these questions begins with Alexander the Great. Among the countries which Alexander conquered on his march to India was Palestine. When Alexander marched against Jerusalem, the high priest, dressed in his robes of office, came out to greet him at the head of a body of priests. Legend reports that when 

Alexander saw the high priest he dismounted from his horse and bowed before him, declaring that he had had a dream in which a high priest such as this one had foretold his conquest of the Persians.

Alexander, therefore, gave the Jews many privileges and excused them from taxes on the Sabbatical year. When the Samaritans rebelled against him he gave the Jews some of their land.

In Alexandria, the city which Alexander built in Egypt, Jews were installed as citizens, the equals of the Greeks. And in the East, too, where many Jewish soldiers served Alexander's armies, the Jews were given privileges, among them that of being excused from building the temple of Baal and Nebo to the Babylonian gods. 

After Alexander died (323 B.C.E.), a long period of warfare began between his generals for the division of the large spoils. 

Judea, too, became involved in these wars, changing masters frequently. Finally, after some twenty-five years of war, Judea was apportioned to the king of Egypt, and it remained under the rule of Egypt until the year 198 B.C.E. In that year, after a successful war, Antiochus III, king of Syria and Babylonia, conquered Palestine and annexed it to his territory. 

During this entire period the Jews were content to remain the vassals of their ruling governments. Often they were dissatisfied with one ruler and hoped for another, but it never entered their minds that they might ever gain freedom or independence.

INFLUENCE JEWISH LIFE 

But something was happening to stir up the Judeans. It was happening unconsciously, as the result of the fact that the Greek world had come to Syria and Palestine. Gradually the Jews discovered that they were ringed around with a circle of Greek cities. Some were new cities, but mostly they were older cities, Philistine, Ammonite, and Moabite cities, even ancient Israelitish cities, which had changed their form of life, their language, their self-government, and, in a measure, their religion and had become Greek, or, as we call it, Hellenized. 

Greece was a country that was very rocky and rather poor for agriculture but with excellent harbors which encouraged its men to travel on the seas and engage in commerce. Greek soldiers, too, were given land by their generals in the conquered countries. 

Thus there were Greek settlements all about Palestine. These cities were ruled by their leading citizens, who, whether Greeks or Syrians, were given special privileges of citizenship. They coined their own money, regulated their own trade, and established those various institutions which every Greek city possessed, such as the theater, the gymnasium, the baths and temples. 

For a time Judea remained unaffected. It had not rebelled against Alexander, and therefore none of its lands was distributed to his soldiers. The country itself was too poor and its trade too insignificant to invite any large numbers of Greeks to settle there.

But there were Jews in Jerusalem who also became eager for the ways of the Greeks and especially for the establishment of Greek city-states in Palestine. These men began to dream of a growing trade for Palestine. Some were becoming wealthy and wanted to undertake larger enterprises. In order to do it, they preferred to be ruled by their own rich group rather than by the high priest and the community elders. They were also eager for

the pleasanter sides of Greek life. The Greek gymnasium appealed to them as well as Greek clothes and Greek hats. They liked to play games and to see performances at the theater. Not that they wanted to give up Judaism. They were probably content to remain 

Jews, but their own pleasures seemed for the moment more important to them than the fortunes of the rest of the community. 

HELLENISTS LED BY SON OF TOBIAS 

At the head of these men was one Joseph, the son of Tobias. 

It happened at the time that the high priest had refused to pay the yearly tax to the king of Egypt. The king sent his representative to demand the money. Joseph met the royal messenger and assured him that the tax would be paid. He then proceeded to 

Egypt and there so found favor with the king, that he persuaded him to let him be his tax collector for Judea, Samaria, and Syria. 

The king gave him a company of soldiers to enforce his demands. 

Very soon, by extorting high payments, Joseph became a very rich man. He employed his friends and kinsmen, and they too became wealthy. Having acquired wealth, they were now ready to use it in a way that would give them the most pleasure, and the Greek forms of living seemed to promise them the greatest satisfaction.

THE CHASIDIM 

While a party favoring the Greeks was forming on one side, an opposite party was formed, called the Chasidim, which saw a danger to the Jewish religion in Greek ways and customs and which therefore sought most zealously to guard and protect every least Jewish practice. This party was especially severe on the question of prohibited and permitted foods, the laws of purity, and the observance of Sabbaths and festivals. Indeed, so severe was it in Sabbath observance that its members refused to protect themselves when attacked on the Sabbath, and many suffered death because of this, as we shall soon see. 

HELLENISTS UNDERMINE HIGH PRIEST 

King Antiochus III, conqueror of Judea, was defeated by the Romans and forced to pay a heavy tribute. In order to meet this large tax, he tried to rob some temples. Temples at the time contained rich treasures, since private citizens hid their money there, just as today they hide it in banks. The temple was considered sacred, and only the worst thief would lay hands on sacred property. When, therefore, Antiochus violated the holiness of the Temple, the people rose against him and slew him. 

A son,

Seleucus IV, who came to the throne, was also faced with a shortage of money, and he too tried to rob the temples.

At this time trouble was already brewing between the high priest, Onias III, in Jerusalem and the Hellenist party, which wanted to control the government. One of the Hellenists, named 

Simon, supervisor of the temple, was dismissed from his post by the high priest. In order to avenge himself, he secretly informed King Seleucus that large stores of gold were to be found in the Temple of Jerusalem. 

dorus, to take this gold. getting the Temple gold.

The king sent his treasurer, Helio- Somehow Heliodorus did not succeed in 

Legend reports that two young men robed

in white fell upon him and beat him until he fell unconscious.

However, Simon, being thwarted once, accused the high priest of being in league with the Egyptian party. Since Egypt and Syria were constantly at war over Palestine, this amounted to treason. 

Onias, the high priest, had to go to Antioch to clear himself. the meantime, King Seleucus died, and the throne was taken by Antiochus IV, the man about whom the Chanuko story centers. 

ANTIOCHUS IV COMES TO THRONE 

In

Antiochus IV was a queer monarch. He sought to be popular with the mob, and would leave his palace and wander about disguised to find out what the people thought of him. Often, when he heard that someone was giving a party, he would come there with an orchestra. At times, dressed in golden garments and lilies, he would walk through the streets throwing either golden coins or stones at the passers-by. He liked to take part in theatricals and used to dance on the stage together with the other dancers. 

For thirteen years he had been a hostage at Rome, and, having learned to know and love the ways of Rome, Antiochus now set out to Romanize Syria. He instituted gladiatorial combats and armed his forces in the Roman style. He built temples after the fashion of Rome and even pretended to be elected in Roman fashion, going about and begging the citizens to vote for him. He had a passion for building Greek cities to spread Greek culture as well as to gain allies, for he felt that the Greek cities above all others would remain faithful to him. One

Unfortunatley for him, he was not a very good general. by one the states of his empire revolted, only Syria and Mesopotamia remaining. Thus losing large revenues, he, like other kings in his line, resorted to robbing temples. He even planned to marry a goddess in one of the temples in order to take all the treasures of the temple as his dowry. 

HELLENISTS SEIZE CONTROL THROUGH BRIBERY 

With a king such as this, the Hellenists now felt that they had the chance to gain what they could not have gained until now. 

Jason, the leader of the Hellenists and a brother of the high priest, decided to make a bid for the high priestly crown. Accordingly, he offered the king a large sum of money if he instead of Onias would be permitted to become high priest. He also agreed to build a gymnasium and other Greek buildings in Jerusalem, to establish a self-governing council, and to call the city by the name of Antiochia in honor of Antiochus, the citizens of Jerusalem to be known as Antiochians.

The king, needing money badly, was glad to give his consent. 

But the method was an unfortunate one for Jason, because others could imitate his treachery. When, three years later, he sent a certain Menelaus with his taxes to the king, Menelaus offered the king more money than Jason did, and the king readily displaced Jason and put Menelaus in charge of the Temple.
