. 

Menelaus, in order to pay the large bribes that he had prommised, was obliged to rob the Temple itself. Leaving the Temple in charge of his brother, he proceeded to the king. When his brother also robbed the Temple in order to bribe the local Syrian governor, this treachery became more than the Jews could endure. 

They rose in rebellion, led by Jason, who now returned, and slew the brother of Menelaus. A delegation of Jews then came before 

Antiochus to plead with him that he remove Menelaus. But the petitioners were put to death, while Menelaus was sent back with a 

Syrian guard to preserve order in the city.

JEWS ARE MASSACRED 

A short time thereafter King Antiochus was making war on Egypt. Rumor spread that he had been killed in the war, and that called forth great rejoicing in Judea. But the rumor was false. 

Antiochus had not been killed, but instead he had been blocked by Rome. A Roman messenger had come before Antiochus and, drawing a circle around him with his cane, ordered him to decide before he left the circle whether he was for peace or war with Rome. Antiochus, thus forced to give up the conquest of Egypt, was doubly enraged when he heard that the Jews had rejoiced in the belief of his death. 

Antiochus marched against Jerusalem and ordered a massacre of the population. Then whatever persons were not killed were to be sold into slavery. Lands were to be divided among the king's soldiers, and the very Temple of Jerusalem was to be converted to the worship of the Olympian Zeus. Antiochus robbed the Temple of Jerusalem of its altar of incense, the table of shew bread, and the menorah. Menelaus was left with a Greek garrison, and the 

Hellenists were in charge of the city. 

UNORGANIZED RESISTANCE BEGINS 

Here and there, there were uprisings against the king of Syria. Small bands of Syrian soldiers were murdered on the highways, and their attackers fled to the hilis. This only angered the Syrians the more and led them to acts of greater terror. The story is told of two thousand Chasidim who hid in a cave on the Sabbath. The Syrians lit moist twigs over the entrance of the and all those within choked to death from the smoke, since they would not defend themselves on the Sabbath.

Another story is told or Hannah, the mother of seven sons, who permitted all her sons to die rather than to bow before an idol that King Antiochus set up. The aged Eliezer, a respected member of the community, was asked by the king's officers to eat forbidden food as an example for others to follow. This he indignantly refused to do. The officer then thought of a clever device. He would let Eliezer eat kosher food, but would pretend it was forbidden. But Eliezer, even more disdainfully, refused to be guilty of deceiving his brethren, and preferred to die rather than..to yield.

AT LAST OPEN REBELLION UNDER HASMONEA NS 

The king's soldiers went from village to village forcing the unwilling Jews to offer up swine on the altar of Zeus. There

seems to have been little interference with the soldiers until they came to the village of Modin, where lived an aged priest of the family of the Hasmoneans, Mattathias, and his five brave sons, Johanan, Simon, Judas, called the Maccabee, Jonathan, and Eliezer. 

Others had obeyed from fear, but Mattathias was not frightened. 

When the king's officers asked that he sacrifice a pig on the altar to Zeus, Mattathias declared in a loud voice that he and his family would remain faithful to the God of Israel. Then one cowardly Hellenist came forward to sacrifice as the soldiers demanded. Thereupon the aged Mattathias rushed at him and struck him down. His sons and the assembled Jews fell upon the soldiers and dispatched them likewise.

This was open rebellion. Mattathias and his sons retired to the hills and gathered about them all those Chasidim who were willing to fight for their faith. Shortly thereafter Mattathias died, and the command over the little group was taken by Judas. 

Judas was a fearless fighter, a born general, and brave as a lion. The Syrians soon had occasion to find this out. The governor of Syria, Apollonius, thought a troop of his soldiers would quell what seemed to him a small rebellion. But Judas thoroughly defeated Apollonius and left the general himself dead on the field of battle. The king, alarmed, sent a large army under a general named Seron, but how surprised was he when this general met the same fate as Apollonius, and Judea still remained unsubdued.

SYRIAN ARMIES DEFEATED 

The king then decided that he would end this war once and for all. He sent a very large force under three generals with orders to destroy Judea and to sell the Jews as slaves to Syrian merchants. Indeed, many merchants with money bags accompanied the expedition ready to buy slaves cheaply, as they hoped.

The Syrian army encamped on two hills opposite Judas at Emmaus. Judas was informed by his scouts that in the middle of the night one of the Syrian generals was planning to surprise him. Stealthily Judas left his camp with his followers and, marching in the direction opposite the one taken by the Syrians, he fell upon the general who had remained in camp. Taken completely by surprise, the Syrians fell an easy prey. The soldiers fled in confusion, and Judas' men were now ready for the general who had marched against them. He, returning to his camp after vainly seeking Judas, discovered him in his own camp. It was now his turn to flee. His army was cut down, and only remnants escaped to the land of the Philistines. All the money bags, as well as quantities of weapons, now fell into the hands of Judas. the Jewish army could be equipped from the captured booty. 

At last

Three times foiled, King Antiochus decided to send his viceroy, the second in command next to himself, with a large army. 

But to the king's consternation, his viceroy fared no better than his other generals and also suffered defeat.

1.

JUDAS CLEANSES THE TEMPLE 

Judas was now free to march into the city of Jerusalem. What the Jews saw in Jerusalem made them sad indeed. The Temple was a heathen shrine; its most important furniture had been stolen, and an altar was set up for the worship of the Greek gods. Judas and his men at once set about cleansing the Temple and rebuilding the missing furniture. They carried away the old altar stones on which sacrifices had been made to Greek idols and brought fresh stones for a new altar. New oil was purified for kindling the menorah, and preparations were made to re-establish the worship again. When the Temple was finally ready for rededication, on the 25th day of Kislev, Judas and his men arranged a grand parade. 

They took boughs of palm and myrtle, and in happy procession they marched around the Temple walls singing songs, dancing and rejoicing before the Lord. In memory of these days the festival of 

Chanuko (which means dedication) was established, so that forever in the future we might remember the time when our forefathers fought for and won freedom of worship. 

FORCED TO CONTINUE WARS 

Two years passed before Judas was troubled again by the king of Syria, but these two years were far from idle ones for the Jewish hero. You may remember that Judea was surrounded by Greek cities in which many Jews also lived. The defeat of the Greek armies caused a wave of hatred to break out against all Jews living in the Greek cities. The Greeks, as though taking the part of their defeated governor, wanted to punish the Jews living in their midst. Fortunately, the Jews now had someone to call to their assistance. Judas himself and his brothers were forced to undertake expeditions east of the Jordan, as well as northward and southward, to punish the offenders. From places where the 

Jews formed a small minority of the population they were brought to Jerusalem for protection.

But the war with Syria was far from over, for in the year 163 B.C.E. the Syrians returned again with a large army. Eliezer, the youngest of the Maccabean brothers, performed a feat of heroism in that battle, although it proved useless. The Syrians employed elephants as armies today use tanks. Seeing a gorgeously attired elephant and thinking that the king himself was riding on it, Eliezer rushed beneath it and stabbed it in its belly. 

The elephant fell upon him and crushed him, but the rider on the elephant was not the king.

Judas was forced to retire to Jerusalem. The Syrians laid siege to the city, and hunger began to bother the defenders. But word reached the Syrian general that a revolution had broken out against him at home. Hurriedly,

Hurriedly, therefore, he patched up a peace with Judas. Judas recognized the rule of the Syrian king, but he, in turn, was granted freedom of worship, the main thing for which the Chasidim had been fighting. The Syrians even carried Menelaus away and after a short time had him executed. 

But Judas knew that the war was not yet over. A new king came to the throne, and again one of the Hellenists, Alcimus, offered the king money to be chosen high priest. The Chasidim, who only wanted religious freedom, were ready to accept Alcimus

and end the war, but Alcimus': official appearance in Judea was marked by the massacre of sixty opponents, by the Syrian general sponsoring him. Judas marched against Alcimus and defeated him. Naturally, Alcimus appealed to the king of Syria, who sent 

General Nicanor to save his appointee.

Nicanor behaved in a very haughty manner. When he was greeted by the elders, who recited prayers in his behalf and offered sacrifices for his victory, he made sport of them. He threatened to kill them all unless they delivered. Judas into his hands. 

There was, therefore, great rejoicing when in the battle that followed, not only was Nicanor defeated, but he lost his own life. 

In memory of the event the Jews established a festival on the 

13th day of Adar, the day before Purim, known as Nicanor Day, ana for many years that festival was observed in Palestine. 

JUDAS FALLS JONATHAN TAKES OVER 

Unfortunately, after this victory Judas' men thought there would be a period of rest, and they scattered to their homes. 

So far as most of them were concerned, they had nothing more to fight for. Religious freedom had been won, and they did not share Judas' ambition for complete independence. But with lightning speed the Syrians sent another expedition which Judas could oppose with only a handful of soldiers. His advisers urged him to retreat until a more favorable occasion presented itself, but Judas would not retire from a fight. He joined battle and this time lost. Judas himself was killed on the battlefield, fighting for his people to the end. The remnants of his army, under the leadership of his brother Jonathan, hid in the marshes of the Jordan. But, though defeated for the time being, the 

Hasmonean brothers continued the fight, and, as we shall see, were victorious.

The Greeks had put garrisons in the Judean cities. Jonathan, from his place of hiding, fell now upon one city, now upon another, chastising the Hellenists. When, a few years later, in 159 B.C.E., Alcimus died, the Jews considered his death a punishment of God for breaking down a partition in the Temple which separated the court of the Jews from that of the gentiles. More willingly,. therefore, they turned to Jonathan for leadership.

Once more a Syrian general returned with an army, but this time he decided to end matters once and for all by making peace with the Hasmoneans and throwing over the Hellenists. Except for 

Antiochus IV,, the Greeks were not interested in interfering with the religion of their dependencies. The Syrians, therefore, recognized Jonathan as the leader of the Jews, thus officially ending the struggle. Instead of an enemy, Jonathan now became an ally of the king of Syria. Some time later, when two rivals were quarreling over the throne at Antioch and each wanted Jonathan's friendship, one of them appointed him as the high priest of Judea. 

Thus the once hunted Hasmoneans, rose from rebels to the highest office in the land, with Jonathan as the official head of the 

Jewish nation.

JONATHAN SLAIN 

UN 

SIMON HEAD 

Jonathan, together with his brother Simon, now set about extending the territory of Judea. They conquered Galilee and Phoe nicia, as well as the Mediterranean shore of the Philistines. 

When the king of Syria tried to stop them, Jonathan defied the king and openly became a rebel against him. But Jonathan was not destined to enjoy the fruits of his victories. A jealous Syrian general who was seeking to seize the throne became afraid of the growing power of the Jew. Treacherously he lured Jonathan into his presence and had him slain. But the Syrian gained nothing by his treachery. The leadership of the Jews was taken over by the last living Hasmonean, Simon, who, acting both as high priest. and as military leader, brought fresh glory to his people. For as the Syrian kingdom was growing weaker, Simon, in exchange for a promise of loyalty and a large sum of money, was granted the long-coveted prize 

complete independence. Thus what began as a revolt against religious persecution ripened into a movement for complete independence and ended victoriously for the Jews.

After four hundred years of life in Judea under foreign rule, the Jews at last had their own government. 1.

JUDEA INDEPENDENT 

We started this section by asking why the Jewish state in Palestine did not continue in the leadership of Jewish life up to the present time, and we finished with the story of Jewish independence. Unfortunately, however, Jewish independence did not last very long barely seventy-eight years. 

The period started off well enough with Simon, who reigned for about eight years, which were on the whole prosperous. Simon regained the seacoast cities for Judea and helped to begin Jewish seafaring, which later was to be a very important branch of Jewish occupations. But he was murdered by his own son-in-law, whose name was Ptolemy. Ptolemy is a Greek name, and it shows that even the Hasmoneans intermarried with the remaining Hellenists. 

Simon was followed by his son, John Hyrcanus, who reigned from 135 to 104 B.C.E. In the early days of his reign he almost lost his independence to the Syrian king, but soon the latter became involved in difficulties, and John was able once more to free his kingdom. But, although he was himself almost a victim of wars, he now decided to make wars on other countries. He conquered Edom and the Samaritans and forced them to accept Judaism. 

He was followed by his son Aristobulus, whose name is also Greek, thus again showing how far the Hasmoneans had themselves become Hellenized. Aristobulus reigned for only one year. His brother, Alexander Yannai, too, was a warrior whose reign, from 103 to 76 B.C.E., was almost a continuous battle. The king scored successes in East Jordan, but he finished off with a civil war in which thousands of Jews lost their lives and about which we shall tell more presently.

After him his wife Alexandra Salome reigned for nine years. 

Hers was an era of peace and among the happiest in that war-torn period. But when she died her two sons, who were to divide between them the high priesthood and the kingship, fell to quarreling and invited Pompey, the Roman general who was already invading Syria, to help them settle their difficulties. Pompey decided to take Jerusalem for himself, and thus abruptly ended Jewish independence after a brief seventy-eight years. 

We must remember, of course, that no small kingdoms could exist at that time. Rome, like Germany in 1941, conquered all smaller nations and made them parts of her empire. Our ancestors had the misfortune to be in the path of the Roman advance. Rome swallowed them as she did greater and mightier peoples, though not without a struggle of the fiercest violence.

... 

WHY ARE WE INTERESTED IN THIS PERIOD? 

we might almost pass by a period of seventy-eight years of independence as not worth stopping at too long, but it is important to us for several reasons. First of all, that period represents the most prosperous condition of Palestine. During the time of the last independent Jewish state our ancestors in Palestine lived more comfortably and more normally than they did before or after. There probably were also more Jews in Palestine then than at any other time. If, therefore, today we are interested in getting some picture of what Jewish life in Palestine looked like at its best we must look to the Hasmonean period. Thus if we wonder how many people Palestine can hold today or how large a population will be able to earn a living there, we have to look to Hasmonean times for an answer. And why, you may ask, do we need to refer to ancient times?. Why can we not judge the country as we find it now? Because large stretches of Palestine are still neglected; sand dunes have been permitted to develop because the land was treeless and winds from the sea carried the sand inland. The sands also causėd· marshes to form near streams. More than half of Palestine is today considered unfit for settlement. The only time when Palestine was fully cultivated was during the times of which we are now telling. We

A second reason why we are interested in the Palestine of the Hasmoneans is because it is the period immediately preceding the loss of independence and later the loss of the homeland. want to find out whether the Jewish people did something in Palestine or learned something which enabled them to continue as a people without a temple and without a land. We shall see that the Jews did learn something very important about governing themselves. They learned to choose their popular religious leaders as members of their government, and these kept them together through the power of religion even after their land was lost.

HOW MANY PEOPLE LIVED IN PALESTINE? 

How, then, did the Jews live in Palestine during the Hasmonean period? Let us notice a strange fact in which respect 

Palestine is the opposite of most other countries. Whereas the population of the civilized world has increased greatly over that of ancient times, Palestine in ancient times supported a larger population than it does today. Today there are about a million and a half people in Palestine, but in Hasmonean days Palestine supported well over two million Jews and a large number of non- Jews.

Second, ancient Palestine had many more cities than modern Palestine. Of course, there was no city like Tel Aviv with a population of 200,000, but Jerusalem was almost as large then as it is today, and many cities which are only hamlets today, such as Ascalon, Gaza, or Caesaria, were flourishing centers of population. Other cities, such as Yavneh, Dora, Straton's Tower, or Appolonia, are not even known today. There were ten cities

organized in a league on the east side of the Jordan where today there is only one city, which is really an overgrown village. 

As for villages, there were hundreds of them, probably much closer together than even the Jewish colonies in modern Palestine. The presence of many cities shows that the country contained a population which was accustomed not only to farming but to handwork and to trade, because cities are usually the seats of handworkers and traders.

WHAT WERE THEIR OCCUPATIONS? 

Of course the occupations of Palestine at the time were mainly agricultural. A thousand years had passed since our ancestors first learned farming, and during this thousand years they acquired a knowledge of many of the finer operations in farming and of the care of delicate plants. Thus our ancestors raised a large variety of crops - wheat, barley, garden vegetables, and fruits. Their most famous products were wines made from different kinds of grapes - black grapes, white grapes, reddish grapes, Sharon grapes - as well as spiced wines, raisin wine, date wine, and apple wine. Judea, Sharon, and Carmel were especially famed for their grapes. They also grew many and very fine olives, the oil of which was exported to Rome. Much oil came from Galilee, where a large Jewish population was now settled. In addition to olive oil, Galilee also produced nut oil, poppy seed oil, and some vegetable oils. Oil was in very great demand for cooking and for the general preparation of food in days before there was ice on which to keep other fats. It was also widely used after a bath for anointing oneself. Palestinian dates, obtained mainly from the forests of Jericho, were a delicacy enjoyed by the emperor of 

Rome. From them were made date wines, date honey, and date cakes.

Perhaps the most precious plants of all were the spices which grew richly in Gilead; also the balsam of Ein Gedi, which sold at two pounds of gold for a pound of balsam. The country also grew figs, pomegranates, citrons, almonds, and other nuts.

In the cities, as we have already mentioned, there were handworkers, butchers, bakers, shoemakers, builders, iron, gold, and silver smiths, brass and iron weaponmakers, carvers in ivory, weavers in wool and flax, dyers of beautiful colors, potters, glass makers, perfumers, druggists, and members of other various trades. There was also already a class of merchants, though it was not very numerous. The better textiles, as well as better shoes, furniture, and household utensils were still imported, but the articles of more common use were already made in the land itself.

A special occupation which was beginning to develop at this time, in which the Jews were to engage very widely in years to come, was seafaring. Many more cities were used as harbors in ancient Palestine than are used today. Thus, for example, Jaffa, Yavneh, Dora, Straton's Tower, Haifa, Acre, and other cities to the number of eleven were engaged in the sea trade. Later, when 

Rome conquered Judea, it boasted that it had overcome "Judea Navalis, Judea of the seas. There was also extensive fishing going on in the Mediterranean and especially in the Sea of Kin-

nereth. Hundreds of boats floated on the Kinnereth which caught fish for the domestic market as well as for salting and exporting to distant countries. Palestinian caviar was famous at Rome.

Thus we see that Palestine of old had a larger population than it has at present, and we may therefore conclude that there is probably room in Palestine for many more settlers today. We find, too, that in olden days the country engaged in a variety of occupations and that it had a richly developed agriculture and an industry the exports of which went to distant lands. 

Surely, if Palestine of old was so developed, we today, with modern methods and machinery, should be able to support a much larger population than they had then. 

WHAT DID JEWS CREATE IN PALESTINE? 

Now we come to our second question. What made it possible for the Jews to continue living as a people even after it had lost its land? We shall see that the Jewish people was able to continue for three reasons:

First of all, it became more democratic. When a people lives under its own government, the government may compel each person to obey its laws. But when there is no longer an independent government, then either the people obey their leaders willingly or else they break up and lose their nationhood. In the case of our ancestors, the development of a democratic institution, the synagogue, enabled them to follow their leaders willingly.

Second, they continued to develop their religion so that it became even more important in their lives than before. When they were deprived of their land, religion took the place of the homeland which they lost. 

Third, our ancestors spread education. Without education there could be no true democracy, because the people remain ignorant and do not know how to govern themselves. Together with Torah, or study, went worship and deeds of lovingkindness.

Let us examine each of these factors separately. 

DEMOCRACY 

First, we said that the Jews became more democratic. At the time of which we are telling, there were two sets of leaders - the priests and the rabbis, called the scribes. The priests were the officials of the Temple. They represented the rich and noble classes who consituted the government of the people. The scribes, on the other hand, were the leaders of the synagogue. 

They were the teachers and the leaders of the middle and poorer classes. It was they who studied the Torah and explained its meaning and in many instances judged between persons who had disputes.

32

But In the course of their studies the scribes and rabbis developed much law that was not found in the Torah. If some new question arose which was not in the Torah, they naturally had to decide it in accordance with their own understanding. they always tried to picture what the Torah would have said regarding such a case. Laws that were thus formed were called the oral law. The pious Jews observed the oral law together with the written law, but there were many who paid no attention to the oral law and declared that it had no authority. 

The scribes who made the oral law claimed that only scholars had the right to make new laws. They argued that the laws were not really new. They were all in the Bible, but one had to understand the Bible in order to find them. On the other hand, the priests insisted that the oral law was new law and the Torah gave them alone the right to be law-makers. This dispute went on for many years, but already before Hasmonean days the right of the scribes to take part in law-making was recognized. They were admitted into the Jewish parliament, called Chever Ha-yehudim (The Jewish Brotherhood), later called the Sanhedrin. Now again under Simon and John Hyrcanus the representatives of the common people were for a time admitted into the government. But soon a struggle was to take place which left an unfortunate mark on the history of the Jewish people. 

PHARISEES AND SADDUCEES 

At this time there were two parties in the Jewish state. 

One party, consisting of the priests and nobles, was called the Sadducees, and the other, composed of the common people, the former Chasidim, was known as the Pharisees. King John Hyrcanus at first favored the Pharisees and admitted them on an equal basis with the Sadducees in the government of the Jewish state. 

During his later years, however, he quarreled with the Pharisees and expelled their members from the Sanhedrin. This led to a bitter conflict which was decided only at the very close of the period of Jewish independence in favor of the Pharisees. 

What were the differences between the Sadducees and the Pharisees? The Sadducees were the representatives of the upper classes. They controlled the government and were anxious to make the Jewish state like all the other Hellenized states around them. They wanted to engage in foreign affairs, to make war and peace, and within their country to have those institutions which we already saw a Greek city usually possessed. They observed the Torah, but they kept strictly to the letter of the Law, claiming that none but the priests had the right to make new legislation. Any new laws which they passed and which were not directly commanded in the Bible, were in the Hellenistic spirit of their own day.

The Pharisees, on the other hand, were eager to make the Jews a separate people from the rest of the nations. Pharisee means one who separates himself. They wanted the Jewish people not to be concerned with making wars like the other peoples about them but to be wholly devoted to the service of God, to the

cause of justice and righteousness. They wanted every individual 

Jew, too, to be separate and distinguished in that he too in his life would exemplify the will of God. 

The parties quarreled over the control of the government. 

As soon as the Pharisees began to become a power, they demanded a share in the administration of the country. We already saw what was the basis of the opposition. Both sides agreed that the Torah was the Jewish constitution, but the Sadducees and the Pharisees disagreed about its interpretation. The Sadducees believed in the strict interpretation, that is, in keeping only those laws which the Bible directly commanded and in refusing to make any others. The Pharisees, on the other hand, claimed that many laws were hidden in the Bible and it required wisdom to find these laws. Since they were the teachers who studied the Bible and were able to find many new laws in it, they felt that they had the right to take part in law-making. They even insisted that the chief judicial officer should be a member of the Pharisees. 

Since the Sadducees represented the men of wealth and the Pharisees were mainly the lower middle classes, there would naturally be other differences among them. The Pharisees, for example, feeling that life in this world was hard and unhappy, thought that a just God must repay men for their suffering in this world by giving them their reward in the world to come, after their death. The Sadducees, on the other hand, thought this world sufficient for them. They believed in being happy while they were alive, for after death nothing more would happen to them. Hasmonean independence, they felt, had brought about the age of the Messiah about which the Jewish people were dreaming.

The Pharisees, on the other hand, refused to see a Messiah in their warrior priests. In the first place, In the first place, they had not brought the golden age which the Messiah was expected to usher in. Besides, the Messiah was to be a descendant of the house of 

David, and, since the Hasmoneans were not such descendants, they were not even rightful kings.

The Pharisees believed that one should lead a simple life without using any luxuries, which, of course, the Sadducees denied. On the other hand, the Pharisees believed in a joyous 

Sabbath and in lighting candles as part of the celebration, while the Sadducees observed a mournful Sabbath and sat in the dark. 

The Pharisees practiced a ceremony of water-drawing on Sukos as a symbol for rain, while the Sadducees made fun of it. The 

Pharisees believed in angels, and the Sadducees did not. differed in the date of Shovuos and in many other detailed matters. 

THE KINGS AND THE PARTIES 

They

Let us remember, however, as we said at the outset, that behind all the disputes there was the basic question as to who was to rule the Jewish people. The rich sided with the Sadducees, while the moderate classes and the poor favored the Pharisees. We already mentioned that King John Hyrcanus turned 

Sadducee toward the end of his life. His sons Aristobulus and

i...

Alexander Yannai were also both Sadducees, and during their time the Pharisees were kept out of the Sanhedrin. But soon trouble was to break out. King Alexander Yannai once put on the high priestly robes and performed the ceremony of pouring water over the altar in prayer for rain. The Pharisaic multitude was angered that a man of war like Alexander Yannai, a brutal bloodthirsty tyrant, should also want to be a high priest. Fighting and being high priest, they felt, did not go well together, and therefore, if the king loved to make wars, he should leave the high priesthood to a peace-loying person. Then the king angered: the Pharisees further by pouring the water on the ground instead of on the altar, as if to make sport of them. The angry populace began pelting him with their citrons. Thereupon the king called out his troops, and a general massacre followed in which six thousand Pharisees were killed. Many of the Pharisalc leader fled the country. But the Pharisees awaited their opportunity for revenge. When some time later the king suffered defeat in a war with the Arabs and hastened back to Jerusalem, he found the city barred against him. A civil war had been started by the 

Pharisees against the king. For six years the war was carried on, and fifty thousand Jews are said to have lost their lives. 

Only later was the king finally victorious, and once more he massacred his Pharisaic enemies. But on his death bed he advised his queen, Alexandra Salome, who was to take over the rule, to make peace with the Pharisees. She heeded his advice, and under the leadership of her own brother, who was. one of the principal 

Pharisees, she restored the Pharisees to the Sanhedrin. The nine years during which she ruled proved to, be the golden age of the Hasmonean, dynasty.

Thereafter the Pharisees, as leaders of the poorer classes, always remained in religious authority. Even when Roman generals or kings ruled them, the Jews always enjoyed religious democracy. 

RELIGION 

The second reason we mentioned which helped the Jewish people to survive was their highly developed religion, which they could take with them wherever they went. The Jewish religion was one of strict monotheism, which means absolute belief in the existence of one God Who rules the entire world. There was always a temptation to worship idols, because all the peoples around, even the civilized Greeks, sacrificed to them. But the 

Jewish people as a whole did not give way to such temptation. 

They continued to serve an invisible god, even though foreigners could not understand them and often made sport of them. To the 

Jews, God was too great to be pictured in any way. Whatever picture they could make of Him was bound to make Him smaller or less important than He really was. Many heathen wise men mocked the Jews; they circulated reports that the Jews in their 

Holy of Holies worshipped the head of an ass. But, on the other hand, many others were drawn to the pure religion of Israel. 

Daily there were throngs of visitors to the Temple at Jerusalem, where an imageless God was worshiped. Thousands of gerim, or converts, sought the fold of the Jewish religion. Some accepted only part of Judaism, such as the Sabbath or the laws relating. to kosher and trefa food, while others accepted all the laws

and became complete Jews.

The idea of God further underwent great change. Early men had spoken of God as a God of war and the Lord of Hosts. Gradually there was a change to the idea of God as a loving Father of His people. A father loves his children and may be approached and addressed by his children freely. All his children are equal before him. Thus religion helped to give a democratic flavor to Jewish life. 

God as a Father is merciful and long-suffering. Earlier beliefs stressed God as a God of justice; now He was also described as a God of mercy, and in imitation of God the Jews were ordered to be merciful also. The rabbis of this period declared that the world rests on a triple foundation on Torah, avodo or Temple worship, and g'milus Chasidim or the performance of acts of loving-kindness. Each of these branches of Jewish life was actively cultivated in this period. The Torah was recognized as the basis of life. The Jewish religion must be no mystery - everybody must know it. Other religions strove to keep the masses in ignorance. Even during the Middle Ages for a very long period it was forbidden to translate the Bible from Latin into one of the languages of common use, lest the people find out what it contained. The Jews sought the very opposite. "Raise up many disciples, said an early rabbi. Our rabbis were indeed eager to spread learning.

LEARNING 

Learning, we saw, was the third reason which kept our people together. Learning has had an ancient history among the Jews. 

During the days of the First Temple, the fathers were commanded to teach their children, and most fathers undoubtedly fulfilled the commandment. But in order that fathers might be able to perform their duty as teachers, schools were established for grownups to teach them Torah. It was found, however, that not enough grownups were qualified to enter these higher schools of learning and that it was necessary to establish schools for youths so as to prepare them for the higher academies. High schools were therefore organized in which young men were educated. But the high schools proved insufficient, because many an older boy who had never been to school before was unruly and disobedient. 

Besides, there were orphans who had no parents to teach them, and therefore they were not fitted to enter the high school. 

Schools were then started for poor orphans, but it was soon realized that even children who had fathers would profit more by attending schools than by learning at home. The fathers were often busy and could not give the children the needed attention. 

Thereupon Joshua ben Gamala, the priest living shortly before the destruction of the Temple, organized schools for children, in all Judean cities. We do not know whether as yet these schools were compulsory, but very many Jews availed themselves of the privilege, and education among our people made great strides everywhere. time.

Avodo, or worship too, made considerable progress at this 

Ezra had ordained that the Torah should be read on Mondays

and Thursday as well as on the Sabbath.

The main worship was, of course, still carried on at the Temple, where, in addition to the sacrifices, prayers were recited. These included the 

Sh'ma, the blessings before and after it, the Ten Commandments, and portions of the Amido, or as we call it today, the eighteen benedictions found in the Orthodox prayer book. Little by little the habit was formed of daily worship in the synagogues, and soon the custom grew of three daily services. 

Performing acts of loving kindness, too, increased greatly during this time. Whereas in earlier periods that law of the Sabbatical year may have been only partly observed, now it became rigidly followed. Every third year, too, the Israelite farmer was supposed to give a tenth of his crop for the benefit of the poor. In general, every community had overseers over the poor, and it was considered a great distinction to be among the charity wardens of the city. Every Jewish community boasted of fulfilling the following seven kinds of charity: 

1) Feeding the hungry and giving drink to the thirsty. 

2) Providing clothes for those in need of them. 

3) Visiting the sick. 

Burying the dead and comforting the mourners. 

5) Redeeming captives who were captured in war or by pirates. 

6) Educating the fatherless and sheltering the homeless. 

7) Providing poor brides with dowries.

Thus, through making their inner organization more democratic, through refining their religion, and through practicing Torah, worship, and deeds of loving kindness, the Jews laid the basis for a spiritual life that could be lived anywhere, even outside of Palestine if necessary.

THE ESSEN ES 

Since we have spoken of the two parties, the Sadducees and the Pharisees, we may also mention a third which existed at the time, namely, the Essenes. What the name means we do not know. 

This party, instead of being interested in controlling the government, wanted rather to be left alone, so that its members might live their lives in peace undisturbed. Every man who joined the party swore to give up all his possessions and live communistically, that is, to share everything with his fellows. They employed no slaves but did all their work themselves. All luxuries were forbidden them. Their meals were simple and their garments were coarse, though white as a symbol of purity. 

The Essenes tried to lead holy lives which should be both physically and spiritually pure. They bathed every morning before prayer, and they considered their meals so holy that they had priests bless them. They carried their ideas of holiness into their occupations. Trade was regarded by them as leading to deceit and they therefore frowned upon it. The making of weapons and other harmful substances was also strictly forbidden.

them.

They were mainly farmers and handworkers whose services were prized because of their dependable labor. 

After the fall of the Jewish state the party disappeared from Jewish life.

IV JUDEA UNDER ROME 

She

How did the Jewish state come to an end, and now were we forced to leave Palestine? Rome was responsible for it. - Rome,. the haughty mistress of the world, the destroyer of nations. had overcome Carthage, conquered Greece, and was engaged in a war with Mithradates, king of Asfa Minor. Egypt was already a vassal state. Palestine and Syria were next in the line of conquest.

Rome was a very cruel conqeror. So is every country whose government is corrupt and whose officials seek only their own power. Rome was supposedly a republic, but in reality she was governed by her men of wealth. Her rich men sought public office through bribery and through providing amusement for the common people in the form of gladiatorial games in which men fought each other to the death with swords. To be a Roman official a man thus had to spend much money bribing the voters directly and indirectly. If elected, the cost of running his office was largely borne by him. The government provided him with no clerks or assistants or even with an office. The office-holder therefore looked forward to the time when he would rule some distant province. There he would repay himself for all the expense which he had undergone since he entered politics by squeezing out taxes from an unwilling people.

Even though, therefore, the Romans permitted the various conquered peoples to practice their own religion (and we shall see soon that they did not even quite do that), they drew the very last coin out of the 'oppressed provincials, leawing them impoverished and helpless. It was this giant power: which now descended on small Palestine. This is how it happened. 

BROTHERS QUARREL ROME STEPS IN 

After the death of Queen Alexandra, a struggle broke out between the Pharisees, headed by her older son, Hyrcanus, and the Sadducees, led by her younger son, Aristobulus. The latter was victorious and became king.

But Hyrcanus had a general, Antipater, from Edom, whose people, you remember (page 69), had been converted to Judaism by force. Antipater sought personal advancement through Hyrcanus. 

If Hyrcanus were king he would rule Hyrcanus and thus the Jewish people. Antipater, therefore, incited Hyrcanus against his brother, told him that his life was in danger, and urged him to secure the help of the Arabs of Nabatea to regain his throne. 

The king of the Arabs gladly responded to the invitation of Antipater for the promise that the cities of Trans jordan would be returned to him. The Arabs, together with the bulk of the 

Pharisees, forced the king to retire to the Temple Mount, where, however, he was able to hold out against his attackers.

At this time the Romans, under the famous general Pompey, were fighting in Asia Minor. Pompey had been selected by the Roman 

Senate to clear the pirates out of the Mediterranean, which he promptly did. He then defeated Mithradates, who had held out against Rome for a long time, and was ready to proceed to the conquest of Syria.

Both brothers now decided to submit to Rome and to ask Pompey to decide who of the two should be the king of the Jews. At first 

Pompey sent a general to Judea who decided in favor of Aristo-. bulus. The Roman general ordered the Arabs to return to their country, and these, fearing the might of Rome, promptly hastened to do so. .

But a few years later Pompey himself arrived at Damascus, where Aristobulus and Hyrcanus once more came to plead their cause. Hyrcanus, probably coached by Antipater, accused his brother of making wars on distant lands and of helping the pirates whom Pompey had fought. Aristobulus, on the other hand, argued that his brother was unfit to be king. The common people were tired of both. contestants. They sent their own delegation before 

Pompey to plead that the king be removed altogether and that the rule of the high priests be once more restored. 

Pompey, suspecting that Aristobulus was too hot-tempered, demanded of him that he surrender the leading fortresses. Aristobulus returned to Jerusalem ready to give up the city, but his followers closed the gates to Pompey and fortified themselves on the Temple Mount. Pompey thereupon stormed the city and massacred many of the inhabitants. But he respected the Temple and ordered it opened and its worship restored. 

JUDEA A ROMAN PROVINCE 

Judea now became a province of Rome, and, while Hyrcanus was appointed ethnarch, an office lower than king, Antipater really became the important man in the government. The cities of the coast and Trans jordan were taken away from Judea and added to Syria, and Aristobulus and his son were carried to Rome to march in Pompey's triumphal procession., Many Jews were taken prisoners to Rome, and these were among the first Jewish citizens of that city.

As soon as Rome took over Palestine, things began to go badly.

We have already told of Pompey's massacre. We also saw how 

Pompey detached the seacoast and other parts from Judea and made the country smaller. The loss of the seacoast meant the stopping of important sources of revenue from import and export trade. 

The general whom Pompey left in charge, disregarding the unity of the country, divided it into five districts in order thereby to break the united spirit of the Jewish people. 

Conditions might have improved with Pompey's successor, Julius Caesar. Julius Caesar defeated Pompey for the rule of 

Rome and for about three years was at the head of the great empire. Caesar was friendly to the Jews. He was ready to permit 

Aristobulus and his sons to return to Palestine to set up their

kingdom again, but evil fortune dogged the steps of the Jewish rulers and of their subjects. Aristobulus was poisoned, his son was killed, and in the meantime Antipater, the scheming Idemean, won the friendship of Julius Caesar by providing food for his armies in Egypt. He was able to do it through the influence of the Hasmonean Hyrcanus, the ruling king and high priest of Judea, but it was all for his own benefit. Antipater now appointed two of his sons to high office, Phasael as governor of Jerusalem and 

Herod as governor of Galilee.

HEROD SEIZES POWER 

In Galilee there had been a group of rebels fighting the Roman rule. They fought like guerillas, waylaying Roman troops and destroying small detachments here and there. As soon as 

Herod became governor, he captured the leader of this Jewish guerilla band together with some of his followers and had them all executed. A great outcry arose; it was demanded that Herod come before the Sanhedrin and stand trial for murder. Herod appeared before the Sanhedrin, but before he could be sentenced King Hyrcanus was ordered by the Roman head of the province of Syria to free him. Later Herod returned with an army to take vengeance unon Hyrcanus, who he thought wanted to sentence him to death, and only his father's pleas made him turn back.

Some patriots hoped that by poisoning Antipater they would rid the country of him and of his influence, but his son Herod was already too powerful. He marched down with an army, executed the plotters against his father, and himself became betrothed to the granddaughter of King Hyrcanus as a sign of his entering the. 

Hasmonean royal house.

Now something happened in Rome by which the Jews thought they might benefit. Caesar was killed, and Mark Anthony and Octavian divided the rule of the world between them. Anthony was to rule the East, including Syria and Palestine. The Jews accordingly sent a delegation imploring him that Herod and his brother be removed. But Herod was there before the Jewish messengers. 

With gifts and bribery he won the heart of Anthony, who re-established him in his rule more firmly than ever. 

Nothing short of a major war could now upset Herod's power. 

A major war did come. The Parthians, who were ruling in Mesopotamia and Babylonia, and who were the traditional enemies of Rome, broke their bounds and with the aid of the Jewish population conquered Syria and Palestine. The younger son of Aristobulus, Antigonus by name, was chosen ruler in place of the aged Hyrcanus. 

Antigonus ordered that Hyrcanus' ears be cut off in order that as a person with a blemish he would no longer be able to serve as high priest.

For a time the power of Herod seemed broken. Herod's brother captured by the enemy, committed suicide, but Herod himself fled to Rome, where he was duly appointed king of the Jews and authorized to raise an army to reconquer his kingdom. 

It was no easy struggle for Herod to rewin his throne. But

44

after the Romans defeated the Parthians, the decision seemed clear. The Romans helped Herod return to power. 

FRIEND OF ROME BUT NOT OF JEWS 

Outwardly the Jewish condition was very favorable. Herod was considered a friend of the Roman people. Aside from giving the Romans aid in her wars, he was free to rule his own people as he pleased, to impose what taxes he wanted, and to make all internal improvements which he might desire. Had Herod been a patriotic ruler, this period might have been a prosperous one for the Jews, and their experience with Rome might have continued pleasant for many years. But Herod, the friend of the Romans, was no friend of the Jews. Perhaps one could not be both. At any rate, Herod began his reign with a series of official murders which sent a shudder throughout the kingdom. He murdered his own brother-in-law, whom he had appointed high priest, and another brother-in-law whom he suspected of being in love with his own wife. He then murdered his wife and her mother and the aged king 

Hyrcanus, whom he persuaded to return from exile in Babylonia. 

Later he murdered his own sons. In addition, many men of wealth were executed in Jerusalem, the king confiscating their money. 

The high priesthood, the principal office of the Jewish religion, was brought low by Herod's changing the high priests frequently and appointing men of wealth who were ready to pay him for the honor.

Whatever Herod might be at home, he pretended to be a civilized Greek or Roman to the non-Jews. He spent his people's funds liberally, building Greek temples, theaters, streets of columns, and circuses in his own country as well as in distant foreign cities. Places as far away as Antioch, Rhodes, Athens, and Sparta partook of Herod's liberality. In his own country he built cities, towers, fortresses, theaters, and temples. He instituted games in honor of the emperor, even though these were forbidden by Jewish law. Gladiators fought each other in his arenas, and wild beasts struggled against humans in order to entertain the mobs, mainly non-Jewish, which thronged the cities. 

The money for all these extravagances was pressed from the Jewish people. Taxes mounted higher than ever. Every transaction in the market was subject to a tax, and there were taxes even on homes in Jerusalem, which had never been taxed before. 

Even the little good that Herod did for his people was spoiled by an act of meanness. Herod did for a time win the good wishes of his subjects by distributing grain in time of famine. 

Among his beautiful buildings he rebuilt the Temple at Jerusalem. 

The small temple of Zerubbabel, Zechariah, and Joshua the high priest looked altogether outmoded alongside of the beautiful buildings which Herod was putting up in Jerusalem. Accordingly he had the Temple rebuilt, and in order to please the religious feelings of the Jewish people he followed in the building the strict desires of the Pharisees and the priests. For eight years the building and the beautification of the Temple continued. But

་ ་་་་ ༣༢ T.

after the Temple was completed and the Jews were ready to rejoice in it, Herod put up a golden eagle over the Temple entrance, the symbol of submission to Rome. Since all statuary was forbidden to the Jews, they regarded the golden eagle as an idol placed in their holiest precincts. Toward the close of Herod's reign, when he became ill and seemed to be on the point of death, some rabbis with their pupils tore down the golden eagle. But Herod still had enough life in him to put them to death by burning them alive. At last Herod died, and never did a tryant's death call forth greater joy.

DEMOCRACY UNDER HEROD 

HILLEL 

How, you may wonder, did our ancestors' efforts toward democracy fare during the days of Herod? Surely Herod would not permit representatives of the people to direct him in his government. Did the people, then, lose all voice in the conduct of their own affairs? The answer is that they did and did not. During Herod's reign the Sanhedrin was a powerless body under the thumb of the king, who told it what to do and how to decide. But alongside of the official Sanhedrin there was an unofficial one, a voluntary body, which really made the laws that were respected and observed by the people as a whole. The place occupied by the 

Sanhedrin was now really taken by the schools, the heads of which decided in large measure what the laws should be for the Jewish people.

The heads of the schools were men of great learning and distinction, but by far the greatest of them all, a man who left his imprint on all of Jewish life, was the famous Hillel. He was known as the sage, the patient, the great lover of his fellow men. Hillel was a Babylonian. He came of a distinguished family tracing its ancestry back to King David. Nevertheless, he was very poor, and it is said that in his youth he worked as a woodcutter to earn a livelihood. He divided his meager earnings into two parts; half he spent for his daily needs, and the other half he paid the doorkeeper to permit him to enter the lecture room of the distinguished teachers Shemaya and Avtalyon. Once, it is reported, in the month of Teves, on a bleak day, Hillel failed to earn anything at all. Unwilling, however, to miss his lesson, he climbed upon the roof of the house of study and tried to listen through the skylight. Soon snow began to fall, but, unconscious of snow or cold, Hillel lay there in rapt attention to the words of the rabbis. These, looking up, saw a human figure against the skylight. Hurriedly they brought him down and revived him, for he was almost frozen. Thereafter the guard had instructions to admit Hillel free. 

HEAD OF ACADEMY 

Some years later there was a dispute in the synagogue which the rabbis could not settle themselves, and someone suggested that Hillel the Babylonian be consulted. They sent for Hillel and presented the question to him. Hillel tried to prove in 2 logical way what the answer to the question should be, but the Palestinians would not believe him, having no confidence in the learning of the Babylonian. It was only when he declared that

[... 

UNILOM LI 9822 ,! 9:

-- Shemaya and Avtalyon had ruled in this manner that they accepted his judgment. But soon he became head of the academy, and then his words were listened to with great respect. In most disputes between himself and the other great rabbi, Shammai, who was leader of a rival academy, the decisions were always in accordance with the opinions of Hillel.

FAMED FOR PATIENCE 

Hillel is known for his great learning and for his logical mind. Earlier rabbis tried to find new laws in the Bible, some successfully and some unsuccessfully. Hillel showed how it could be done by means of certain principles through which one could tell whether a new law was in accordance with the spirit of the Bible or not. Thereafter all the schools followed his method. 

But more important even than his scholarship was his exceptional character. Many of his famous sayings have come down to 

It is told, for example, that once a heathen came to Shammai and offered to become a Jew if Shammai could teach him the entire Torah in as long a time as he could stand on one foot. Shammai, a stern, dignified man, thought he was making sport of him and chased him away. The heathen then came to Hillel with the same request, but Hillel was not at all disturbed. On the contrary, "I can teach you the whole Torah, he said, "while you stand on one foot. The entire Torah can be summed up thus: 'What is hateful unto thee do not unto thy neighbor.' The rest is merely and explanation of how to put this principle into practice; go and learn it." It is said that the heathen felt much ashamed of his lightheartedness and became a convert to Judaism. 11 11 A very famous legend tells of how a man once made a bet of four hundred zuz that he could anger Hillel. Hillel was taking his Friday afternoon bath when this man came knocking loudly at the door and disrespectfully cried out, "Where is Hillel? Where is Hillel?" Hillel quickly put on a robe and came out. "What is it, my son?" he asked. I want to know said the stranger, "why the Babylonians have round heads. "It is because their midwives are not skillful, answered Hillel. The stranger left and after a while returned again, crying as before, "Where is 

Hillel? Where is Hillel?" Again Hillel appeared and patiently asked the questioner what he wanted. "Why have the Palmyrans weak eyes? the questioner asked, "You are asking a very important question, said Hillel. 'It is due to their living among sands of the desert. A third time the stranger returned. Не wanted to know why the Africans have flat feet, and again Hillel answered with sweet kindness: "Because they live in swamps. The stranger then called out in anger, "Would that there be not many like you in Israel! On account of you I have this day lost four hundred zuz. "Better that you lose four hundred zuz said

Hillel, "than that Hillel should lose his temper." 11 

LEFT MANY WISE SAYINGS 

11 11 Many are the wise sayings of Hillel. "Do not trust yourself all the day of your death, said Hillel, "and do not judge your neighbor until you place yourself in his position. 

Hillel was

very public-spirited. His motto was: "Do not separate yourself from the community. He also said: "If I do not take care of myself, who will? But if I care for myself only, what am I?" Many of his sayings dealt with the study of Torah. Hillel felt that through the study of Torah there would be peace in the world. He said: "Be of the disciples of Aaron, loving peace, pursuing peace, loving their fellow creatures and drawing them near to the Torah. 

He also said: "He who does not increase his knowledge diminishes it, and he who does not study deserves to die, and he who makes a worldly use of the crown of the Torah (that is, one who tries to show himself superior to others becuase of his learning) shall waste away." A boorish man, he used to say, cannot be sin-fearing, nor can an ignorant person be pious. Finally, Hillel was a great believer in the idea that justice ruled the world. It is reported that he once saw a skull floating on the water, and he said to it: "Because thou drownest others, they have drowned. thee, and at last they that drowned thee shall themselves be drowned.
