THE JEWISH STATE COMES TO AN END 

Herod brought only misfortune to his people. dead, What was to come next? 

Now he was

When Herod's son Archileus ascended the throne, he had power over only a part of Herod's large kingdom. This part, however, included Judea. When the people asked him for reforms, especially that he abolish the head tax and the tax on food, which fell heavily on the poor, he consented. Then a group of men came to him and demanded that he punish those who at his father's command had executed the rabbis who tore down the golden eagle from the temple. Soon the festival of Passover occurred. Large multitudes gathered, and all began to demand the same thing. Archileus sent some soldiers to quiet them, but they stoned the soldiers. Thereupon the king ordered his army to attack the populace, and three thousand were killed. A mission left for Rome asking that Archileus be not made king but that Rome rule Palestine directly. 

When Archileus finally was appointed there were so many complaints against him that soon the emperor removed him and sent him into exile in Vienna.

DIRECT ROMAN RULE WORSE 

Bad as Archileus was, Roman rule was worse. The Jews could not foresee the mistake they were making in inviting Rome to rule them directly. Rome appointed governors called procurators, and these seemed to do everything that was humanly possible to oppress the Jews, to offend them and to outrage their feelings.

The Romans oppressed the Jews with taxes. The procurator collected a head tax and a land tax from every person. In addition, the procurator rented out to persons called publicans the right to tax the people on all articles which were bought and sold. The publicans pressed out sufficient taxes to enrich themselves and the procurators. The latter usually 

The latter usually expected that there would be complaints against them and that they would have to answer to charges when they returned to Rome. They therefore made sure to supply themselves with sufficient cash to bribe all the necessary Roman officials and to leave enough of a fortune for themselves to enable them to spend the rest of their lives in comfort. Thus the people were mercilessly drained of their last penny.

The Jews might have tolerated this economic oppression if they were otherwise left in peace. Officially the Romans were supposed to respect other people's religions. Thus no Roman soldier was permitted to enter the inner court of the Temple under penalty of dealth. No statues were permitted in Jerusalem, not even of the emperor, who was elsewhere worshipped as a god. 

The Romans did not even carry their banners through Judea, for on them were engraved figures of men and animals.

But one insane emperor, Caligula, did insist on having his statue worshipped as a god in Jerusalem. He ordered the governor of Syria to set out with two legions to enforce the order. But as soon as word of the intended plan reached the common people, they left their fields by the thousands, although it was in the middle of the agricultural season, and hurried to the governor to plead with him not to defile their city. The governor delayed. 

He set out on a trip of inspection to Galilee, but the people followed him there. He threatened to kill all the people who protested, but the people were quite ready to face death rather than to have a statue in the Temple. At last the governor, seeing that the matter could be accomplished only through great bloodshed, wrote the Emperor that he could not fulfill his order because it would mean starting a war. The Emperor in anger wrote the governor to kill himself, but fortunately the news was delayed by a storm at sea, and before it reached the governor the Emperor himself had been murdered. But the strain on the nerves of the people remained.

Another incident developed when the Jews felt insulted over the conduct of one of the Roman soldiers. Soldiers were placed to watch the Jews at their services, especially during the pilgrimages on Passover, Shovuos, and Sukos, when, it was feared, a rebellion might break out. One of these soldiers made a coarse joke which angered the Jews. When they demanded that the soldier be punished, the procurator advised them to let the incident rest. 

But when the Jews persisted in demanding punishment, the procurator called out his army, at the sight of which the Jews were frightened and began a stampede in which many were trodden to death.

So we might multiply happenings, one worse than the other, which wore down the patience of the Jews. The Samaritans, for example, used to attack Jewish pilgrims on their way to Jerusalem. 

When, after repeated complaints, nothing was done about it, the Jews took the law into their own hands and punished some of the Samaritans. The procurator thereupon seized a number of Jews and crucified them. The Jews were forced to appeal to Rome, and there their grievance was upheld, and the procurator was punished. 

But the next time the Jews had reason to complain a different emperor ruled in Rome. The matter was much more serious and the outcome far more disastrous. 

The Greeks and Romans living in the city of Caesarea, which was in Jewish territory, claimed that the city had been built for them and that the Jews should have no rights in their own country. The Jews naturally were angered at such arrogance. 

Troubled relations began between the two peoples which finally broke out into open fighting. When the Greeks were getting the worst of the fight, the Roman governor sent his men into the fray, and these massacred many Jews. The Jews then sent an appeal to 

Rome protesting the loss of rights and the massacres. But the 

Greeks and Romans had stronger friends at court, and the case went against the Jews. They were deprived of rights in one of their own cities on their own territory. Surely injustice could go no further.

BRIEF JEWISH REIGN, AND AGAIN THE ROMANS 

Rome appointed 

Agrippa happily

For a brief time the Jews were given a rest. as king one of the grandsons of Herod, Agrippa I. turned out to be altogether unlike his grandfather. If he was not entirely God-fearing as the Pharisees might want him to be, he was nevertheless very careful of the feelings of the people and in his outward dealings strictly observed Pharisaic teachings. 

He was altogether devoted to the interests of the country and tried to build fortifications around Jerusalem which would have made the city almost proof against siege. He also tried to assemble the small kings around Palestine to take a stand against Rome. But unfortunately, in both of these undertakings he was stopped by Rome, and before he had reigned four years he died in the Greek city of Caesarea.

After Agrippa the procurators became more shameless. 

One accepted bribes to free prisoners, even thieves and murderers, and another went so far as to share loot openly with highwaymen. 

Under such conditions the country could only be demoralized. 

The poor people were growing poorer. Poor farmers were losing their land, and tenant farmers found that it did not pay them to work the soil. The young men especially saw nothing hopeful, and many of them joined gangs. There were patriotic gangs formed to fight the Romans and half-patriotic gangs which fought the friends of Rome among the Jews. These often made a little distinction between one rich Jew and another. Many a Jew fell at the hands of these slayers, who were called Sicarii, a word which comes from sica, a short dagger, in Latin. 

JEWS DRIVEN TO DESPAIR 

The Jews thus felt that they had reached the end of their endurance. They were prepared to fight a war against Rome, feeling that they had nothing to lose even if the war went against them. 

And while preparing to fight Rome, the poor Judean planned also to rise against his own Jewish wealthy classes, because he considered his own rich as oppressive as the Romans. Landlords and creditors would be wiped out in order to enable the poor man to start life anew.

The trouble started again in the city of Caesarea. The 

Greeks, having deprived the Jews of rights in the city, now sought to make sport of them. In a narrow roadway leading to the synagogue one Sabbath morning, a Greek set up an altar to sacrifice doves. The dove was sacrificed when a person recovered from a disease called leprosy. There had been a tale invented by 

Egyptian anti-Semites that the Jews in Egypt under Moses were a company of people suffering from leprosy whom the Egyptians drove out from Egypt. This Greek was thus trying to act out the story through the dove sacrifice and to show that the Jews were still lepers. Naturally, the Jews took offense at this insult. fight started between the Jews and the Greeks. Some pious Jews, fearing for the Torah scrolls, carried them off to a nearby village. But after the affray, instead of punishing the Greeks for beginning the disturbances, the governor punished the Jews.

1.1

He even imposed a penalty on the Jews who had carried off the scrolls because they acted without his permission. In additon, he imposed a fine of seventeen talents of gold on the Jewish community.

Everyone knew that the gold was intended for the governor's own pocket. The Jews of Jerusalem were outraged. Some, in mockery of the procurator, took collection boxes and went around pretending to make a collection for "poor" Florus. When Florus heard of it he was in turn enraged and, proceeding to Jerusalem, crucified as many Jews as he could catch in his dragnet. His soldiers murdered and slaughtered Jews in the city till they themselves were tired of slaughtering.

OPEN REBELLION 

Not being contented yet, Florus wanted to insult the Jews further. It was a custom that when Roman legions passed a community the people went out to greet them, and the legions returned the salute. Florus now demanded that Jews go out to greet his two legions which were marching in from Caesarea. At first the 

Jews were unwilling, but the older and calmer men persuaded them not to anger the Romans. But when the Jews saluted the legions, the soldiers, as if to make fun of the Jews, did not return the salute. As the Jews began to grumble and complain, the soldiers charged into them, trampling many of them with their horses. At this the Jews became infuriated and, running into nearby houses and on roofs, began to pelt the soldiers with stones and javelins. 

It was now the Romans' turn to flee, and many a soldier was cut down on his way from the city.

This attack on the Roman legion marked the beginning of the revolution against Rome. It also marked the beginning of the revolution against the wealthy Jews of Judea, for the mob, now gaining control of Jerusalem, burned the house of the records where their debts were written down; they also burned the palaces of the rich. A new government was appointed consisting of the leaders of the war party, and, as a sign of defiance of Rome, the daily sacrifice on behalf of the emperor was suspended. 

The party of the high priest and of the men of wealth did not surrender without a struggle. A Jewish king, Agrippa II, the son of Agrippa I, was ruling a little kingdom north of Jewish Palestine. He was given the power by Rome of appointing the high priests in Jerusalem, and to him the high priest now sent for assistance.

Agrippa sent three thousand men to help maintain the high priest in power. But on the day when offerings of wood were made for the Temple, many pilgrims came into Jerusalem from the provinces. Among them were many Sicarii and other sympathizers with the revolution. With their aid the revolutionary party overcame the party of the priests. Upon surrendering, the soldiers of 

Agrippa were permitted to leave for home, but the Roman legion which was stationed permanently in Jerusalem was slaughtered. 

Shortly thereafter the rebels had reason to rejoice because

54.

of a victory over a regular Roman army. Cestius Gallus, the governor of Syria, marched on Jerusalem with thirty thousand men. 

He encountered no difficulties on the way, conquering many cities and villages. But when he finally encamped against Jerusalem he stayed for a brief while and then retreated. The season may have been too late, or he was not sure of his supplies. The road back lay through high mountain ranges, and as the Romans tried to pass these, suddenly from every rock and from every nook shots and missiles began to fall upon them. Rocks and spears cut down the 

Roman retreat, and only the fall of night saved the remnants of the Roman army. All its siege machinery and all the heavy arms had to be left behind. The Jews captured them and triumphantly returned to Jerusalem.

INTENSE PREPARATIONS 

A period of intense preparation then followed. The walls of 

Jerusalem were strengthened, water and food were supplied, and soldiers were drilled. But unfortunately the Jews themselves were torn by internal strife. There was no central leadership. The leaders of the rich were not trusted by the poor, and so there could be no inner harmony. As an example, the governor of Galilee chosen by the revolutionary party was Josephus ben Mattathias, who later became famous as a historian of the Jewish people. He was of the aristocratic party, son of a priest, who had been to Rome and knew how strong Rome could be. During his entire career as governor he was hesitating between fighting and submitting to Rome. The Zealots, the fighting party that had vowed to die and never surrender, distrusted him. Yet he was the one to carry on the war against Rome.

THE WAR IN GALILEE 

With the defeat of Cestius Gallus, Emperor Nero of Rome was finally stirred to action. Apparently the war in Palestine was no mere local uprising. Accordingly, he dispatched his most famous general, Vespasian, with sixty thousand men, and he enlisted the help of his son Titus, who brought a legion from Egypt. Together they set about reducing Galilee first before venturing against 

Jerusalem. 

Galilee fought back bravely enough. The principal battle was fought at the city of Jotapata, where Josephus had his headquarters. The Romans, who thought they might take the city easi y in a day or two, were destined to disappointment. They began to build towers from which their archers could shoot into the city. 

But the Jews made a sally out of the city and burned the towers. 

The Romans built new towers, but the Jews began raising their walls above the towers and in order to prevent the Romans from interfering with their work, stretched wet hides on poles above the workers, so that the arrows and stones slid down and did not harm them. 

Then the Romans, despairing of taking the city from above, decided to make a breach in the walls. They set the battering ram to hammer against the wall, but the Jews lowered sacks of chaff in the way of the battering ram to soften the blows. When the Romans finally cut down the bags and breached the wall they found to their dismay that the Jews had built another wall immediately behind this one. And finally the Romans, maddened by the long de-

lay, tried to scale the walls on ladders with their shields held tightly above their heads. But the Jews poured boiling oil on the advancing army, and the Romans retreated, mad with pain. 

However, the city finally fell through treachery. A deserter told Vespasian that the Jewish guards permitted themselves a nap in the early morning hours when the night fighting was over. At dawn, therefore, Vespasian's men stealthily climbed the walls and opened the gates. A massacre took place in which all the inhabitants of the city were killed.

Forty men hid in a cistern, among them being also Josephus, the governor.

Josephus counselled surrender, but the men turned at him and were ready to kill him, because they had vowed never to surrender to Rome. They decided to kill each other and to draw lots for the order in which they were to die. Josephus, it happened, drew the last lot, and so when all the men were dead but the last one, Josephus persuaded him to surrender to the Romans. Later 

Josephus went about with the Romans counselling the Jews to surrender, and for that reason many have considered him a traitor to the cause of his people.

After Jotapata few cities could withstand the Roman siege. 

Some tried, nevertheless. The city of Gamala put up a brave fight. 

There was a large precipice outside of the city, and on it the defenders gathered to give last battle to the Romans. But suddenly a storm arose which was so violent that it carried back their spears and javelins. Feeling thus deserted even by God, the brave defenders decided to die rather than surrender. They, together with their wives and children, threw themselves from the precipice and were dashed to pieces below.

In another city, Gischala, the defenders asked for twentyfour hours to consider surrender. The time was used by the leader of the Zealots, John of Gischala, to leave the city with a band of men for Jerusalem, where we shall hear about him again. 

Galilee fell, and the entire district was now subject to Rome. 

Some cities had surrendered voluntarily, and many were taken through violent assault. The conquerors were now prepared to march southward against the strongholds of Judea. 

CIVIL WAR IN JERUSALEM 

The end of the war in Galilee turned the enemy's attention to Jerusalem, but Jerusalem unfortunately was the scene of an internal quarrel. John of Gischala, who had arrived there with a band of followers and set himself up as the ruler of the Zealots, wanted to share the government with the ruling high priest. This the high priest was naturally unwilling to do. Besides, John's soldiers, villagers from the north, were not well disciplined and went about the city robbing the homes of the rich. A civil war thus broke out. In the course of it, the party of the high priest, feeling too weak to manage the situation alone, invited Simon bar Giora with twenty thousand men into the city. These soon turned against the party that invited them, considering them friends of Rome whom they were sworn to destroy. But neither were they pre-

pared to accept the leadership or John and his army. The civil war thus went on; neither side was able to overcome the other, but each continued to do damage to the other, especially to the food supplies. Thus in the course of this civil war the food which the 

Jews had stored up for their siege against Rome was burned by themselves. When finally the Romans appeared and both parties realized that this was no time for civil strife, it was already too late. Hunger was facing them.

TITUS PREPARES TO ATTACK 

The Romans had delayed their attack because their general, Vespasian, had been chosen emperor by his troops, and he was more concerned to make certain that the choice would be approved by the Roman Senate, than with the Jews. In the meantime, however, the 

Romans were not idle. They were capturing city after city around 

Jerusalem. When Vespasian finally was confirmed as emperor, he left for Rome and entrusted the charge of the war to his son Titus. 

As soon as Titus approached Jerusalem, John of Gischala and Simon bar Giora made peace and agreed to lead the war together. 

Braver generals were probably never seen. They gave the Romans no peace. When the Romans were building their camp, the Jews came out of their city and almost destroyed it. It was saved only by 

Titus' coming up with his main forces. Twice the Romans built towers and mounds, only to have them burned by the Jewish defenders of Jerusalem. The Romans were forced to bring new wood from a distance of twenty miles, all the other having been burned. 

FAMINE IN THE CITY 

But hunger was working destruction within the city. Men fell dead from hunger in the streets, and houses were piled with corpses. 

Whoever had food hid it, and the soldiers, having nothing to eat at their posts, went about looting. Some Jews tried to escape to the Romans, but there an equally unhappy fate awaited them. A rumor spread that some people had swallowed gold pieces in order thus to carry them out of the city, and the cruel Roman soldiers ripped their bodies open to look for them. Other fugitives, long hungry, fell upon the coarse food which they found at the Roman camp and became sick from indigestion. Still others were cut down by Jewish sentries who were posted to watch against deserters. The 

Romans, learning how desperate the food situation was in Jerusalem, built a wall all around the city to keep the Jews from going out or coming in. Now all smuggling of food was effectively stopped.

TEMPLE IN FLAMES 

The battering rams were set against the walls, and after weeks of hammering the walls began to give way. The first wall was breached, then the second and the third; but when the Romans thought they might break into the city, they found a new wall which they had to scale. Finally, driven beyond their walls, the 

Jews fortified themselves on the Temple mount. When Titus saw that the battering rams could have no effect on the stones of the walls of the Temple area, since these were too heavy, he finally ordered fire to be set to the gates of the Temple. Through the

burnt down gates Roman troops began to pour in. Still the Jews held them at bay, till the great national disaster occurred. 

Although, it is believed, the Romans had planned to spare the Temple, one of the Roman soldiers tossed a burning firebrand into the Holy of Holies. It was on the 9th of Ov, at the height of 

Palestine's hot summer, when no rain had fallen for months. The dry wood of the Temple caught fire at once, and soon the whole Temple was in flames. Seeing the Temple on fire, the Jews lost courage. The priests threw themselves into the flames, and many a soldier, too, preferred to perish in the burning ruins. The loss of the Temple was considered by the Jews as a sign that God was no longer with them, and without God's aid they felt they had lost. 

There was no use continuing the struggle. The way to the lower city was now open to the Romans. They broke in; they murdered and burned and looted to their fill. 

The Jews still held the upper city and for eighteen days resisted the Romans. But finally there, too, the defenders were overcome, and the Romans were in complete possession of Jerusalem. 

Massacre and pillage followed. The beautiful Temple vessels were taken by Titus to be displayed in his march of triumph at Rome. 

Whatever young men were not killed fighting were rounded up to be taken to the arenas of Rome to fight gladiators and wild beasts, and thousands were sold as slaves throughout the markets of the world.

John of Gischala surrendered, while Simon bar Giora tried to scare the Romans at night by appearing as a ghost, but he too was caught and brought before Titus.

In Rome the grateful Senate built an arch of triumph in honor of the victor of Judea. This arch can be seen to this day. Its sculptures show the Romans carrying away the table of shewbread, the menorah, and the holy trumpets. 1.

VI 

THE SCATTERING OR DIASPORA 

We set out to discover how the Jewish people became spread throughout the world, and we saw that Rome was largely responsible for our scattering, or diaspora, as it is called. But there were many Jews outside of Palestine long before Rome appeared on the Re- Jews had lived in Babylonia since the first exile under a ruler of their own called the exilarch. We shall have occasion to tell more about the Jews of Babylonia presently. For the time being, let us merely note that on the whole they lived comfortably, engaging in farming and handicrafts as well as in trade. cords have been found of a banking house which may have been Jewish and which for many years carried on an extensive business with Jews. King Alexander the Great ordered Jewish soldiers to help rebuild a temple for the Babylonian god Baal, but the Jews asked to be and were excused for religious reasons. There were academies in Babylonia where students studied the Torah, but advanced students were obliged to complete their studies in Palestine. 

There were Jews, too, all around Palestine - in Syria and Asia Minor as well as in the cities of Greece, Rome, and even in 

Spain and Gaul.

JEWS IN EGYPT 

The most important group, however, lived in Egypt, where over a million Jews found a home. The life of the Jews in Egypt is very interesting to us, because it so resembles Jewish life in the United States. In the United States the Jews form a large portion of the population of the city of New York. In Egypt the Jews were about two-fifths of the population of the city of Alexandria. Here we speak and write the English language, while in Alexandria the Jews spoke and wrote the tongue of the city, namely, Greek. All the rights and privileges of citizenship that are enjoyed by us were also shared by the Jews of Alexandria. Our religious life is carried on in Hebrew and English, as theirs was in Hebrew and Greek. 

We are free to engage in all occupations, and so were the Jews of Alexandria. Actually they engaged in handicrafts trade, largescale shipping, and in military service. Indeed, so highly valued were Jewish fighting men that several Jewish military colonies were established in places of danger to guard the frontiers. Early in the first century two Jewish generals led the armies of the queen of Egypt against foreign enemies.

TRANSLATE BIBLE 

At the present time, when the Jews establish themselves anywhere, one of the first concerns is to translate the Bible in the language of the new country. The Egyptian Jews too had a Bible translation, but theirs was the very first one, and therefore the preparation of that translation was an event of exceptional importance in the history of the Bible and of the world. A story is told in connection with this translation. The king of Egypt,

Ptolemy Philadelphus had collected the largest library of his 

The time. Being eager to gather all the known books, he was informed that the Jews possessed great works which were not yet available in Greek. The king, desiring to have these books translated, wrote to the high priest of Jerusalem, asking him to send him scholars versed in Hebrew as well as in Greek with a copy of the Bible. high priest is said to have sent seventy-two scholars. These were well received. They were quartered on the island of Pharos, near 

Alexandria, and each was set to work on the task of translation. 

For seventy-two days they thus labored. When the work was completed, each man read his translation, and all the copies agreed in every detail with each other. The king was very pleased with the work, showered honors on the translators, and sent them home with rich gifts.

This story is partly true and partly legendary. The entire 

Torah could not have been translated in seventy-two days, nor would all seventy-two copies be exactly alike. But certain it is that the Torah was translated, and little by little the other books, the Prophets, were also rendered into Greek, until by about 132 B.C.E. most of the books of the Bible were already translated. 

The Jews of Egypt rejoiced and made a celebration when their Bible was translated, because they no longer knew Hebrew, and now they would be able to read their Bible in a language which they could understand. In Palestine, on the other hand, there was sadness that the Torah should have been translated into a foreign tongue.

BUILD TEMPLE AND PERFECT ORGANIZATION 

The Jews made themselves further at home in Egypt by building a temple there. Of course, the principal temple was in Jerusalem, but in Egypt, too, a smaller temple was built where sacrifices were offered.

In America there is no central body into which all the Jews of the country are organized. We have an American Jewish Committee, an American Jewish Congress, a B'nai B'rith, a Jewish Labor Committee, all of them speaking for the Jews of the United States. 

Recently an American Jewish Conference was formed uniting the several organizations. But the American Jewish Committee withdrew from the conference while the other agencies, too, are each carrying on their work as before. In Egypt the Jews were organized under one head called the ethnarch, who, with a body of advisors, was in charge of the Jewish affairs of Egypt. Indeed, so well organized were the 

Jews that the Greeks were jealous of them. They felt that the Jews were enjoying a double advantage in having their own government as well as the government of the city. 

RELATIONS WITH NON-JEWS NOT ALTOGETHER FRIENDLY 

Of course, the most important question in any diaspora settlement is: How well did the Jews get along with their neighbors?

And here, unfortunately, the record is not altogether a happy one. 

The Jews had two groups of neighbors, the Greeks and the native Egyptians. The native Egyptians, strangely, enjoyed no rights

at all in their own city. The Romans had deprived them of all rights. They therefore envied the Jews their privileged position. 

On the other hand, the Greeks were divided in their feelings towards the Jews. Some of the Greeks thought the Jews a nation of philosophers and honored them very highly. Others, on the other hand, considering themselves Egyptians, were irritated by the fact that the Jewish writings contained harsh statements about the ancient Egyptians. Therefore, in opposition to the Jewish account of the Exodus, they invented a story that the Jews were a tribe of people sick with leprosy, a very foul disease, whom the Egyptians drove out of their country because they did not want to keep them. there any longer.

Unlike our situation in the United States, the feeling between the Jews and the non-Jews in Egypt was not very friendly, and in the middle of the first century it broke out into open hostilities. 

The ruling Roman governor at the time had fallen out of favor with the authorities at Rome. Eager to be re-established in the graces of the insane Emperor Caligula, he demanded of the Jews that they worship the Emperor's statue as a god, and he placed the statue in the main synagogue of Alexandria. The Jews naturally objected violently. The governor then permitted the Greek mob, together with the Roman soldiers, to attack the Jews on the pretext that they were living in districts not officially permitted them. Four hundred Jewish homes were thus burned, as well as Jewish ships in the harbor. Shops were pillaged, and many persons were killed. The community of Alexandria required many years to recover from this blow. The Jews sent a commission to complain before the emperor but the insane ruler would not even give them a hearing. Only when 

Emperor Caligula died and a new emperor came to the throne did the. massacre at Alexandria receive attention. The governor was punished, the ring leaders were put to death, and the Jews were at least partially restored the property they had lost. But the feeling between the Jews and the Greeks did not improve, and there were to be more outbreaks in the future. Thus we have a picture of one of the early diaspora centers with its light and shade, good and bad very much as is the position of the Jews today in many parts of the world.

2. In what ways was Jewish lire in Egypt like present Jewish life in the United States? 

3. What is of interest to us in the first Bible translation?

4. Were the Jews of Egypt better or worse organized than the Jews of the United States? 

5. Was there any anti-Semitism in ancient Alexandria?

11 

VII THE RISE OF CHRISTIANITY 

Before we go on with our story, you may have noticed that at this time our count of years begins to change. Before this time we wrote B.C.E. after each date, which means "before the common era, and now we have reached C.E., which means "the common era, or the way in which people generally now count time. Something must have happened in those days to make the Christian world change its way of counting time.

The year 1, which begins the "common era," represents the birth year of Jesus, who, as you know, was the founder of Christianity. You probably also know that Jesus was a Jew. But who was Jesus, and what did he teach? How did it happen that he was the founder of Christianity?

JEWISH BELIEF IN MESSIAH 

Christianity had its origin in the Jewish belief in the Messiah. The Messiah, in Jewish tradition, was believed to stem from the House of David. His coming, the Jews hoped, would restore to them their national glory, with the Messiah as their king. Under the reign of the Messiah, there would be no more wars nor hatreds nor greed for wealth. Men would live in perfect happiness under God.

When would the Messiah usher in this golden era? The Jews expected the Messiah after a long period of wars and civil disturbances, during which time the suffering of the common man became unendurable.

Life in Judea was sufficiently unbearable at this time for many Jews to imagine that the Messiah was long overdue. A man who expressed this feeling was John the Baptist, who announced that the Messiah would soon come. John, or Johanan, as his name is in 

Hebrew, lived in the wilderness. Like Elijah, the Prophet, he wore only a loin-cloth of camel's hair and fed on honey and locusts.

John went about preaching that the Kingdom of God was at hand. He advised everyone to prepare for the coming of the Messiah by repenting of all sins. John baptized his followers in the Jordan River as a sign that they were beginning a cleansed, pure life. So great was his following that Herod Antipas, the ruling prince of Galilee, feared a popular uprising. The prince ordered John arrested and beheaded.

JESUS OF NAZARETH 

After John died, his followers awaited the Messiah eagerly. 

Soon a man appeared who led many to believe that he was the expected Messiah. This man was Jesus of Nazareth. 

Jesus is the Greek form for the Hebrew of Joshua, or Jeshu. 

We know little about the birth and early home life of Jesus.

102It is thought that his father was a poor carpenter named Joseph' and his mother, Mary. The family lived in Nazareth, where Jesus received his early education. It was customary for Jews of some learning to read and explain the Bible portion of the week in the synagogue. Jesus, according to Christian records, was often given that privilege. Besides that, he helped his father in the carpentry shop. his

When he was about thirty years old, Jesus was baptized by John the Baptist. This experience was the turning point in life. More than ever he began to devote himself to teaching and preaching. His speech was simple and spiced with many lively little stories and parables to hold the attention of the common folk. Besides, he sought out the lowly, poor people whom the more fashionable preachers passed by. He even associated with publicans - tax collectors, whom everyone hated - and with public sinners.

Jesus also reached the hearts of the sick and suffering, for he was thought to possess powers of healing. The crippled and mentally ill flocked to him in great numbers. Undoubtedly his sympathy helped the troubled and sick. His followers believed that the sick were possessed of devils whom Jesus expelled. The rabbis, on the other hand, claimed that his cures were effected through the aid of Beelzebub, king of the devils.

MESSAGE OF JESUS 

"The Kingdom of God is at hand. That was the principal message of Jesus. His words brought consolation and hope to the. poor and lowly, for he promised them a special place in the Kingdom of Heaven. In the Kingdom of God, he preached, wealth would play no part at all. Those eager to enter the kingdom were counselled to divide their wealth among the poor. Of a person who was given such advice but refused to follow it, Jesus declared: "It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the Kingdom of Heaven. 11 Besides expecting his followers to give up wealth and position, Jesus urged them to be kind and loving even to their enemies. "Resist not evil," he said, "but whosoever shall smite thee on the right cheek, turn to him the other also." "And if any man. take away thy coat let him have thy cloak also. But is it always to the best interest of humanity to love our enemies, even if it were possible? The great world wars have proven that we must resist evil and uproot it, especially when it aims to destroy democracy and justice.

Likewise, Jesus taught his followers to ignore the practical needs of life, such as food and clothing. "Therefore take no thought saying: 'What shall we eat or what shall we drink or wherewithal shall we be clothed?' Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow; they toil not, neither do they spin. Jesus was certain that God would provide for his creature man, as he does for the fowl and the flowers. "Take therefore no thought for the morrow: for the morrow shall take thought for the things of itself."

Our society believes in planning to meet emergencies and in the dignity of labor. Would the theories of Jesus seem practical today? Would they have seemed any more practical to the Jews of his time?

There was little in the teachings of Jesus radically different from that of the rabbis. If Jesus taught brotherly love, so did 

Hillel and other rabbis. Jesus, like the rabbis, based his ideas on the Torah, which says: "Love thy neighbor as thyself." To this Jesus added the command to love one's enemies. But Jesus himself found it difficult to live up to this command in his relation with the Pharisees. Jesus was in the habit of opening his remarks with words to the effect that while the Torah urges one course of action he commands another. Naturally the Pharisees, scribes and teachers of the Torah and leaders of the people, resented his claim to be above the Torah. Jesus, angered by their disapproval, called the Pharisees hypocrites, blind leaders of the blind, and vipers. Thus, to this day the term Pharisee has come to mean hypocrite, although in the tradition of Judaism the Pharisees were respected for their spiritual qualities and their devotion to the Torah.

JESUS DECLARES HIMSELF THE MESSIAH 

When Jesus felt that his close companions and disciples, of whom there were twelve, believed in him as the Messiah, he decided to set out for Jerusalem. He chose this time particularly because he expected large crowds of pilgrims coming into the capital for the Passover holidays. Peter and some of the others were fearful for him, for they knew that the people did not accept him as a messiah. At most, some thought of him as a prophet. But Jesus would not be restrained by thoughts of personal safety. He ordered his disciples to bring him a donkey in order that he might enter Jerusalem in the manner of the Messiah. For, in accordance with 

Jewish tradition, the Messiah was expected to ride into Jerusalem on a donkey as a sign of his humility. As rumors developed that the 

Messiah was on his way to Jerusalem, crowds gathered, waving branches and spreading their garments before him, crying: 

Blessed is he that cometh in the name of the Lord. crying: "Hosanna:

When Jesus reached the Temple, he was shocked to see that the outermost court was used as a market. There were several courts in the Temple area before one reached the Temple proper. The first or outermost was the Court of the Gentiles, where the high priest permitted the sale of animals and doves for sacrifices. 

There were many Jews come from distant lands for the Passover celebrations in Jerusalem. These brought foreign moneys with them with which to buy gifts for the Temple. There were money-changers in the outer court of the Temple who, like our bankers of today, exchanged the coins for native currency. Jesus, pained at seeing all these merchants in the Temple, ordered his followers to drive them out. Money tables were upset, sheep stampeded, and doves flew about frightened as Jesus' followers drove the tradesmen. 

from the Temple area.

HIS ARREST AND EXECUTION 

The high priest, the elders of the people, and the scribes were greatly disturbed at the high-handed manner of Jesus in the Temple court. They feared that he might incite the large Passover crowds and disturb the holiday celebration. The high priest, therefore, gathered the council and elders of the people to sit in judgment upon Jesus. The judgment called for his arrest. After the Seder supper with his disciples, Jesus was arrested by the officers of the high priest. One of his own followers, Judas Iscariot, pointed him out to the police by kissing him. When brought before the high priest, Jesus was questioned as to whether he called himself the Messiah, king of the Jews. Jesus answered: "Thou hast said."

The high priest was shocked at such blasphemy and rent his garments in mourning. Not having the power to sentence Jesus, he brought Jesus before the procurator, Pontius Pilate. Pontius asked 

Jesus if the accusation was true that he claimed to be the Messiah and king of the Jews, to which Jesus replied, "Thou sayest. This

Pontius Pilate regarded as political rebellion against Rome. Thereupon Pontius ordered Jesus to be crucified. He had the words, "King of the Jews," written over the cross in mockery. Crucifixion was a particularly cruel Roman custom of punishment at the time. 11 The crowds that passed by wagged their heads, saying: "You who said you can destroy the Temple and rebuild it in three days, if you are indeed the Messiah, come down from the cross, and we'll believe you. 

But no miracle occurred. Jesus died on the cross crying: "Eli, Eli, lomo sh'vaktani," which means, "My God, My God, why hast Thou forsaken me?" 11 

HIS FOLLOWERS ORGANIZE A SOCIETY 

Some of the followers of Jesus had buried the body. But his women disciples soon spread the rumor that Jesus had arisen from the dead and would shortly reappear to them. Accordingly, the disciples and followers of Jesus organized a society to await the second coming of Jesus. In every way they were like other Jews, observing the laws and following Jewish customs. The only difference between them and the Jewish community was that they believed the Messiah had already come, whereas other Jews were still awaiting him. 

The new sect, being poor, sent out messengers, or apostles, as they were called in Greek, to all the Jewish communities in Greece, Rome, and Spain to preach about Jesus and to receive financial help. 

Among the messengers so sent was one, Paul of Tarsus. Paul was a 

Jew whose Hebrew name had been Saul. He had been one of the per secutors of the new sect, which the rabbis were regarding with suspicion. One night Saul, who was given to trances, had a dream in which he saw Jesus. Jesus is reported to have said to him, "Saul, why do you persecute me?" Startled by his dream, he now changed. from a persecutor of the new sect to one of its followers. Soon he was admitted to the leading group and sent out to be the special apostle to the -Gentiles.

While Jews were slow in joining the new society, since more than ever after the death of Jesus conditions were the reverse of the promised "golden age," the non-Jews were much more ready to

to believe and join. Everywhere; men were giving up the belief in -idols, yet found no substitute religion. The teachings of the new religion, Christianity, a form of Judaism, were thus readily acceptable to large numbers of the non-Jewish world. It appealed to the latter especially because it was a religion of ethics without involved practices and observances. Paul stressed that salvation came through faith in Jesus and not through observance. 

Hereafter, those who believed in Jesus were considered the real Jews by the new sect, while most of Jews, who had not accepted 

Jesus, were looked upon as cast off by God.

When the Jews were engaged in the great war with Rome, the new Christians. felt no interest in the struggle. They left Jerusalem and separated themselves from most of the Jews, thus making the breach between themselves. and the rest of the Jews even wider. 

Thereafter they were to go on their own way as a separate sect. 

The Gentiles soon far outnumbered the Jews. Gradually, and as a result of the large Gentile following, the fundamental aspect of the new religion changed. Jesus, from being merely a Messiah, was now actually raised to the position of a God. The God of Jewish tradition was God the Father, Jesus was considered God's Son, and a third power was called the Holy Ghost. Thus the Christians worshipped a Trinity, three gods in one. They changed all their days of celebrations and festivals to commemorate incidents con-. nected with the life of Jesus. Judaism and Christianity became two separate religions.

I. 

CENTER OF JEWISH LIFE LEAVES PALESTINE 

We have now completed the second leg of our journey from 

Palestine to the United States. The destruction of the Second 

Temple practically ended Jewish life in Palestine and led the Jews in search of new homes. The change did not happen immediately. For a number of centuries Palestine still continued as the home of the Jewish people. But it was already spent; it could not hold out much longer.

Outwardly, the destruction of the First Temple seemed more serious than the second. In 586 B.C.E. the leading people were exiled to distant lands and only the poorest remained in Judea. 

Even these were soon involved in difficulties over the murder of their governor. After the Roman war, it is true, many were killed and large numbers fled the country. Nevertheless the basic population remained. It turned out however that Babylonia was soon defeated and the new ruler, well disposed to the Jews, gave them permission to return to their land. Rome, however, was not defeated. It continued in power for hundreds of years. 

The first exile lasted about fifty years, The second exile is not over yet for our brother Jews of Europe.

PROBLEMS CREATED BY ROME 

Rome thoroughly destroyed the Jewish State. Jerusalem was in ruins and large parts of Judea were desolate. The best lands had been given to the soldiers or added to the estates of the emperor. Among the Jews only the well-to-do loyalists to Rome saved their possessions. The poor sharecroppers or day laborers were now much worse off than before the war. As refugees they fled to the coastal cities which the war had hurt least, waiting for conditions to quiet down before taking up again their wretched existence.

Organized life as a nation, especially, the Jews felt, had come to the end of the road, They no longer had the Temple and the sacrifices, and they could not imagine continued existence as a people without them. Only in Jerusalem could sacrifices be offered, but Jerusalem was in Roman hands. The loss of Jerusalem was a blow not only to Palestinian Jews, but to the Jews in the Diaspora as well. These had been accustomed to make pilgrimages to Jerusalem at the time of the festivals. They brought gifts, exchanged experiences, and thus felt themselves a united people. 

Now they could no more gather for the festivals. A very strong bond of Jewish unity was thus lost. 

What was to become of the Jewish religion? The Sanhedrin, and especially the Academies of Palestine, had exercised the highest authority over Judaism. They had decided what was right and what was wrong, what was permitted and what was prohibited. 

Who would do it now? The Jews were spread all over the world.

Without some central authority to declare what was the law, Judaism might break up into quarreling sects or disappear altogether.

Our ancestors stood before a great test. If they would continue to live as a united people they needed some authority over them and they required especially the guidance of the Oral Law. 

Only the Oral Law could tell them now what was right to do and what was wrong. The Bible had been written much earlier. Many new situations had arisen which in Bible times had not been foreseen. But could they have such an authority without a government in Palestine?

Very likely, if organized Jewish life had completely ceased in Palestine at that time the Jewish people might have disappeared altogether. Fortunately, Palestine was able to maintain itself long enough to prepare the Jews for life without a country and without a Temple. It did it by organizing the Oral 

Law into a code which could be taken out of Palestine and taught to Jews everywhere. This code, as we shall see, was taken to 

Babylonia, where the Jews were almost at home, and was further developed. In time, Jewish law became so fully developed that Jews could live according to it wherever they might find themselves. 

Their law bound them together in place of a country and enabled them to live as a religious people to this day. Wherever a group of great rabbis was found who knew the Talmud very well, there a Jewish center would be formed. The center continued for several hundred years until conditions forced its abandonment and its starting somewhere else anew. Thus has the center moved from place to place until today it has come to the United States. 

HOW SOME PROBLEMS WERE SOLVED 

Let us return to Palestine after the destruction of the Temple. Painfully, economic life began to return more or less to normal. Palestine being an agricultural country, even the 

Romans had to have their farms worked or else there would be no profit for them. A large Jewish population soon returned about 

Lydda, Yabneh, Hebron, and Bethlehem to till the soil as day laborers or tenant farmers. Town life, too, recovered, and shops and markets became once more active. Poverty, however, increased greatly. Many poor tenant farmers had no homes whatsoever and were obliged to sleep in the open fields. So short of food were they that many ate the unripe grain. Even the Romans were forced to distribute charity to impoverished Judeans. 

The most difficult task was to form a new government. The priestly classes, those who had governed the Jews until now, had lost their position of authority when sacrifices were no longer being offered. Fortunately there were other leaders, the Pharisaic rabbis. These were grouped in two schools - Beis Shamai, which had favored the war, and Beis Hillel, which stood for peace. 

Because of the turn of events, Beis Hillel was now in favor. At the head of Beis Hillel was a great man Rabban Johanan ben Zakkai 

to whom it now fell to organize a government.

ori

GOVERNMENT FORMED 

Rabbi Johanan was already an outstanding man in Jerusalem before the war. Priestly descent and wide learning had singled him out for the new Sanhedrin. He was a student of Hillel and, like his master, was known for kindliness and love of his fellow But he was very old and would have preferred to retire from active life had not a national calamity called him to take steps to save the Jewish people. When he saw that the war was lost, he succeeded in inducing the Romans to spare the city of Yabneh and its sages. Now that Jerusalem was destroyed, he undertook to establish some form of self-government; at Yabneh through the organization of an academy for the study of the Torah. The academy was both a center of study as well as a law-making body. 

In this effort he had many difficulties to overcome. Yabneh was not Jerusalem and some argued that new laws could be made only in Jerusalem and nowhere else. But so great was the veneration in which Johanan was held that his word was accepted and it became law that Judaism was not dependent on any particular city. 

Torah, he said, could be a substitute for the Temple and sacrifices, and Torah could be studied anywhere. It was Johanan ben 

Zakkai who made the Torah the main foundation of Jewish life for the future.

DETERMINE PRAYERS AND BIBLE 

The major tasks of government Johanan left to the head of the College who followed him, Gamaliel II. Among the important reforms which Rabban Gamaliel introduced was the holding of three daily services. Rabban Johanan ben Zakkai had declared that the synagogue was to be a substitute for the Temple and prayer was to take the place of sacrifice. Rabban Gamaliel now determined to give the statement practical effect. The Jews had been in the habit of hearing their Torah read on market days, Mondays and Thursdays, and on the Sabbath. In connection with the Torah readings they had recited the Sh'ma with prayers before and after it. Now regular prayer meetings were to be held thrice daily for which a new prayer was introduced called the 

Amida, a prayer which is recited standing up. 

In this prayer the Jews expressed all their hopes for personal well-being as well as for their national welfare. The prayer asks for health and fertility as well as for the restoration of Jerusalem and the Temple. 

Among other decisions taken by the court of Rabban Gamaliel was the determination of the books which should constitute the Bible. The Christians had begun writing books of the New Testament which they claimed were part of the Bible. It was, therefore, important for the rabbis to decide which books, in accordance with Jewish law, were to be considered holy. 

UNSOLVED PROBLEMS 

Thus Jewish self-government became once more established. 

The Torah was substituted for the Temple, and a new bond was

forged to replace those which the Romans had broken. Yabneh did fair to replace Jerusalem. But there were several sources of weakness in the new Jewish state which could not be remedied. 

First of all a large foreign population now lived in Palestine discharged Roman soldiers and others who followed their camp. 

These were often a cause of violence to the Jewish population, robbing them of fields and possessions. Secondly, the Jews had to encounter religious competition in their own country. Many women especially were attracted to Roman men as the victors. 

The new sect, the Christians, too, claimed that God had destroyed the Temple because of the Jews' refusal to accept "His Son. In

But the main difficulty which the Jews experienced was that of adjusting themselves to Rome. Other peoples had accepted Roman overlordship. Though it was difficult to live under Rome, the peoples had accustomed themselves to do so for the sake of peace. The Jews, however, were not yet prepared to make their peace with the Romans. The Romans were cruel masters, though there was nothing they did especially to arouse the Jews. time they even recognized the Jewish government and dealt with it on behalf of the Jewish people. However, the Jews regarded the Romans as far beneath them. They felt that their own civilization was finer and purer than the Roman. The Romans, they believed, worshipped gods who were no gods, whereas they were worshippers of the only living, all-powerful Lord of the world.

They hoped that their God would in their own lifetime repay them for the suffering they had endured and would give them dominion over their enemies. A new generation that had not fought in the former war sought revenge. Judea was preparing for another war.

OUTBREAK IN DIASPORA 

The outbreak of the war was delayed somewhat by the great Roman peace which embraced the world. Rome was at peace at that time, her legions were idle and it seemed dangerous to begin any disturbance. Besides, Gamaliel and his successor, Joshua ben Hananiah, had both been to Rome and knew how mighty she was. But when these men died and Rome went to war in the 

East, then Jewish patience broke loose. Not in Palestine but in 

North Africa and Cyprus did the Jews decide to challenge Rome, and for two years kept the Roman world at bay. Embittered by 

Roman treatment of themselves the Jews did to the Romans as the Romans had done to them. They sold them into slavery or made them fight in the arenas. But the Romans could afford to wait. 

After two years they starved the Jews out and then they wreaked their vengeance. Alexandrian Jewry was exterminated and only a small fraction of the original community returned later to the city. On Cyprus the Jews were forbidden to set foot even if shipwrecked.

Palestine was still outwardly at peace. The new Roman 

Emperor Hadrian, who came to the throne fearing an uprising in Palestine, promised the Jews that he would rebuild their Temple. 

There was naturally great rejoicing and celebrating, but as time passed and nothing was done to realize their hope, the Jews began

AZ 

TA

to murmur their discontent.

Finally the plan for the Temple was announced. Not only was the Temple to be smaller than the original one but what made it utterly unacceptable for the Jews, it was to be built in a place different from the original one. 

Jews now felt they had been fooled, and their anger knew no bounds. They began preparing for the war in earnest. 

PALESTINE AT WAR 

The Two leaders appeared at this time - one a great Rabbi, Akiba, and the other a general, Simeon bar Kochba. You undoubtedly know the romantic story of Rabbi Akiba--how he worked as a poor shepherd for the wealthiest man in Jerusalem and how his master's daughter fell in love with him. Disowned by her father for marrying a poor shepherd, she went to live in a little hut where she supported herself at odd tasks while Akiba, at a mature age, began to study his alphabet. After a number of years he became a great scholar and a brilliant speaker. He was a passionate patriot and his school at Bene Brak attracted thousands of students. 

As for Simeon bar Kochba, little is known of his early life.

He probably came from a little town called Koziba, and was called Simeon bar Koziba, He was a man of magnificent appearance and superhuman strength. Akiba was so strongly attracted to him that he thought he must surely be the Messiah. Benaming him Bar 

Kochba, Son of a Star, he went out to announce him to the Jews in and about Palestine. Simeon was proclaimed king, and new coins were struck with his name and the new year of Jewish freedom. The city of Bethar was his temporary capital. Simeon gathered a group of especially heroic men about him. It is told, probably in legend, that in order to test his men's valor he would have them ride by a sapling and tear it up with its roots. 

The local Roman armies which tried to advance against Simeon were thoroughly defeated. The emperor then realized that he had a major rebellion on his hands and that he had better bring the most experienced general that he had. Accordingly he recalled 

Julius Severus from distant Britain and entrusted him with the conduct of the war against the Jews.

Even Julius Severus was afraid to proceed directly against the Jews. So long as the Jewish land was intact and the Jewish army was whole he feared to endanger his own soldiers. First he would starve the Jews then attack them city by city. Thus, after keeping the people from working their fields and making certain that they were facing starvation, Severus began his attack.

We have no record of the heroism of the Jews and how bravely they fought in their last great war against Rome. We merely know that city after city was attacked and resisted bitterly. The fortified cities at the entrance to Samaria fell after strong resistance. Then came the turn of Judea where over fifty battles had to be fought in accordance with the Roman account. Finally

.0.זי J ཎྞཾ ** 募 ​> pe. 130 / 2 VL 14 رام IM AJ. 7 1.0

all of Judea was occupied except Bethar and there the last great battle of the war was fought. Again we do not know how long the city held out though it held out considerably. Treachery on the part of a Samaritan was said to have helped in subduing it. When the Romans finally conquered it the general omitted the usual message - I and my army are well - because the army was not well. 

It had suffered too many casualties. So, too, however did the Jews.

There were more killed in this war than in the year 70. 

A half-million persons are believed to have perished. Thousands were exiled and sold into slavery or sent to arenas to fight the wild beasts. Many fled to distant Babylonia or even to Rome or 

Spain or to the wilds of Arabia.

ROMAN PUNISHMENT 

After the war of the year 70 Jerusalem was lost but Judea was still Jewish. Even without Jerusalem, Judea was still home. 

Now the Judean cities had suffered the worst destruction. Jerusalem itself was rebuilt as a Roman city and its very name was changed to Aelia Capitolina. The Jews were forbidden_under_penalty of death to be seen within three miles of its walls. Thus

Judea was lost to the Jews and whatever Jewish life still continued had to be transferred to Galilee of the Gentiles. 

Bent on crushing the Jews completely Rome decided to prohibit the practices of the Jewish religion. The observance of the festivals, and dietary laws, as well as prayer gatherings were all strictly prohibited. The Romans were especially severe against anyone teaching the Torah, and of course it became illegal for the Sanhedrin to meet. Organized Jewish government was thus at a standstill. But how were the Jews to get on without a government? The rabbis, braving the anger of Rome decided not to yield. They held secret meetings in order to guide the people and above all they decided to continue teaching the Torah even if that were to call down upon them a martyr's death. 

The first to suffer execution at the hands of the Romans was the great Rabbi Akiba. When he was led to his death he declared he was happy to have had the privilege of serving God with all his might. Another rabbi, Judah ben Baba, arranged a secret meeting of his students in order to confer upon them the degree of Rabbi so that they might carry on after he died. The meeting was discovered by the Romans, The students managed to escape but Rabbi 

Judah died a martyr's death. Eight other famous martyrs are listed as having died at that time. 

DIFFICULTY OF RECONSTRUCTION 

After the war of Bar Kochba most of the work of reconstruction accomplished by the rabbis of Yabneh was undone. Again there was a slow recovery and the people went back to work. A new and more kindly Roman government repealed the edicts against the Jewish religion and the Jews could now once more practice their laws openly. A brilliant group of scholars reorganized the schools and the Sanhedrin. Once more there was open centralized authority in Jewish life. But after the war there were

JJ14 3.17 1.

long periods of famine and failure of harvests. Demobilized soldiers organized bands and carried on a reign of lawlessness in the mountains of Galilee. Conditions continued to grow worse and the leaders of the Jews feared for the future. The Nasi, the head of the Sanhedrin, Judah I, determined upon a bold step. 

He would make an official record of the Oral Law. 

GATHERING OF MISHNAH 

Judah was one of the most important men to hold the office of Nasi. He was a person of great learning and immense wealth. 

So extensive was his authority that he practically ruled alone without the Sanhedrin and without advisers. The period of his rule was one of great poverty during which few scholars could devote themselves to a life of study. Rabbi Judah distributed large sums of money to relieve distress among his students. 

Nevertheless he feared that after his death the study of the Oral Law would be neglected altogether. Since Jewish learning depended mainly upon passing of the law by word of mouth there was great danger that it might be altogether forgotten. forgotten. Rabbi

Judah therefore decided to set about collecting the Oral Law into volumes which he called the Mishnah. He and those who worked with him collected all the laws they could find. They gathered laws dealing with property and with family problems as well as those concerning the Temple and sacrifices; for they hoped that the Temple would speedily be restored and they would need these laws once more. Now a new and powerful bond united the Jews of the entire world the law code. True, it weakened the already weak position of Palestine. Hereafter, Jews in the Diaspora need no longer be dependent on Palestine for the source of their 

Oral Law. But the Oral Law was a substitute for Palestine. 

With the aid of the Mishnah the Jews could now live anywhere and know their law. They had a code which regulated their lives. 

Rabbi Judah died at about the year 200 C.E. His fears for the immediate collapse of Jewish life were happily not realized. 

Indeed Rome was more friendly to the Jews than before and gave them the right of citizenship in the year 212. New rabbis arose, who studied the Mishnah as the Bible had been studied before in order to find new laws. Many new oral laws gathered about the 

Mishnah which in time became organized into the Palestinian Talmud. But Rome, though friendly, was itself plunged into anarchy. Legions raised and dethroned emperors one after another, keeping the lands in constant uncertainty. Large armies were stationed in Palestine and the Jews had to bear the burden of maintaining them. Palestinian Jewry was so poor that it could no longer meet the expense of local government. The Palestinian Nasi or patriarch, as the Romans called him, was forced to ask for contributions throughout the Jewish world. Schools became impoverished and scholars were few. 

ROME ACCEPTS CHRISTIANITY 

Yet the Palestinian Jews were becoming accustomed to their new hardships, and might have endured them for many years had

not a new calamity overtaken them. 

Rome officially accepted Christianity in 319. Thereupon the pagan religions were decreed illegal and to be eradicated. Judaism was declared a tolerated religion. It was permitted to continue but only in order to bear witness to the truth of Christianity. The Jews were to be made to suffer in order to serve as an example of what happens to a people which "kills its God. The Jewish religion was abused and called many vile names. But worse than that, a series of laws was enacted to deprive the Jews of most of the rights which, as citizens, they had enjoyed. These laws aimed to restrict 

Judaism to the Jews and to degrade the Jews. Intermarriage with 

Jews was forbidden on the penalty of death and so was conversion to Judaism. The Jews might build no new synagogues. There was even an attempt to legislate how the Jews should conduct their worship.

The personal rights of the Jews, too, were severely restricted No Jew might hold the office of judge or town magistrate or even minor posts such as clerk of court, prison jailer or public health officer. Teaching in the universities was closed to the Jews as was also army service. 

Bishops now preached openly and boldly against the Jews, declaring their synagogues to be dens of iniquity and abodes of Satan. This preaching led the mobs to attack and destroy the synagogues. Strong kings put down the outbreaks but weak ones overlooked them and thus encouraged them. 

Not merely were laws passed against the Jews but they were worded in abusive language. This wording of the royal laws later found its way into the codes of all the new nations which were formed upon the ruins of Rome. This was an added cause for discrimination against Jews throughout the Middle Ages, RABBIS CUT REMAINING BONDS 

The growing uncertainty made the rabbis fear once more that the end of organized Jewish life might be at hand. There was still one mighty bond which bound the Jews of the world to Palestine, and that was the determination of the calendar. Hillel II, Nasi at about the year 350, decided to cut away this last bond between Palestine and the rest of the Jewish world. He would proclaim publicly a method of calculating the calendar. 

The Jewish calendar is a lunar one, which means, that it is determined by the appearance of the new moon. There are several problems involved in a lunar calendar. First, one must know when the new moon appears. Second, the solar year is 365 days and a fraction, whereas the year of 12 lunar months consists only of 354 days. Since Jews must observe Passover in the early spring and 

Shovuos in the late spring, they had to equalize the lunar and the solar years, otherwise the time would come when the month of Nison, the month of the Passover would fall in the winter and so would the other festivals fall at times different from their expected seasons.

PL

The determination of the date of the new moon was at first done through the observation of witnesses. Two men would testify that they had seen the new moon, whereupon the Sanhedrin offically declared the first day of the New Moon. Bonfires were then lit on the mountain tops to spread the news to all the communities, and messengers were sent to the more distant countries outside of Palestine. Since there was danger of these messengers arriving late, after the start of a holiday, it was ordained that Jews outside of Palestine should observe two days of each holiday, a practice which Orthodox and Conservative Jews still follow but which Reform Jews have eliminated.

Fearing that the Sanhedrin would never meet again, Hillel II proclaimed a simple formula for deciding the date of the new moon. The months must alternate between 29 and 30 days except for the first two months, Tishri and Cheshuon, which might both be either 30 or 29 days or one 29 and one 30. The purpose of leaving these months flexible was to see to it that Yom Kippur would never fall on a Friday or Sunday and that the seventh day of Sukos would never fall on a Saturday.

As for the problem of equalizing the solar and lunar years, an extra month was added seven times in every cycle of 19 years the third, sixth, eighth, eleventh, fourteenth, seventeenth, and nineteenth years are leap year, when an extra month is added, called V'Ador. ( And Ador)

Now every responsible Jewish community could fix its own calendar with the assurance that the same date was followed by all the Jews the world over.

OFFICE OF NASI ABOLISHED 

Again after the death of Hillel II conditions seemed to improve somewhat. The Roman emperor favored the patriarch and bestowed on him titles and honors. But again the good fortune did not last. At the instigation of the Church an attack was made against the Jewish government. In 429 the office of Nasi was abolished and all that remained of the Sanhedrin was a small school in Galilee.

PALESTINE JEWRY'S LAST STAND 

Palestinian Jewry was destined to make one last stand against Rome. In 610 the Persian army was invited into Palestine by one of the contending parties for the throne of Constantinople. The Jews joined the Persians and threw off the yoke of Rome. But the Jewish uprising proved a disappointment. 

The Persians had made promises to the Jews which they failed to keep. When, therefore, a new Roman emperor in 628 sought to regain the country and courted Jewish assistance the Jews were ready to listen to him on the condition that their former opposition would be forgiven them. The Jews accepted the promise in good faith, but when the king was victorious, the

The Arabs in their sweep of empire occupied Palestine, Syria and Babylonia. A new era was thus to start for Palestine. But the Jewish population there was already too far reduced to be of any importance. Thousands had migrated to new homes and now many poor farmers must have accepted Mohammedanism in order to escape taxation. The main stream of Jewish life had left Palestine and was now lodged elsewhere as we shall see presently.

II 

JEWISH LIFE IN BABYLONIA 

Palestine was losing its place as the center of Jewish life. 

Wars, harsh laws, conversions, and large emigration greatly reduced the Jewish population of Palestine. The Jews were becoming a people of wanderers. But before becoming altogether homeless, the Jews were granted a partial rest. There was one country, which while not quite a Jewish land, was yet a second home for scattered Jewry. That country was Babylonia. In Babylonia the 

Jews gained the experience of living outside of their country, independently of Palestine. There a large Jewish population already lived from before the days of the Second Temple. This community flourished and increased in numbers, wealth, and influence. It was now ready to take the place of failing Palestine. 

BABYLONIA ALMOST HOMELAND 

Babylonia was able to replace Palestine because in the districts where the Jews lived they were a majority of the population. 

Cities like Nehardea, Mahoza, Pumbeditha, and Sura were almost entirely Jewish. So, too, was the surrounding countryside. Indeed in some places the non-Jewish population was converted to Judaism. The Jews were thus able to live under their own laws for hundreds of years. In the meantime, they forged those instruments which later helped them to make their home among strangers.

The Jews of Babylonia, mainly living together in one territory, were ruled by their own ruler, called the exilarch (head of the exile). The exilarch was of the royal house of David, a descendant of the kings who had been taken prisoners by Nebuchadnezzar. A Babylonian ruler had already appointed the captive king of Judah head of the exiled Jews. When the Babylonians were conquered by the Persians, these were even more friendly to the Jews and raised their ruler to the position of the fourth noble in the kingdom, directly after the princes of the royal blood. The exilarch would ride in an elegant carriage attired in a costly uniform. Before him and around him ran runners and slaves. He wore a signet ring given him by the king, and when he came to the palace he would occupy a throne in the royal reception room. The Jews were considered one of the important peoples of the kingdom.

FAVORABLE CONDITIONS 

The government of Babylonia was federal, that is, it was a union of separate states, just as our own is. Each state had very wide powers of its own to tax, to regulate commerce, to control its own canals, markets, etc. It was so under the Persians, under Alexander the Great and his generals, and later under a desert people called the Parthians who seized the rule over the Eastern empire. In 226 C. E. the Persians reconquered the country and tried to centralize the government, but without much success. The exilarchs were thus practically independent

governors. They appointed judges and mayors over the Jewish cities who, together with a council of seven or often of twelve persons, looked after the affairs of the town, supervised weights and measures, saw to the cleanliness of streets, kept canals in repair, looked after the courts, and fixed the sale price of goods, They also supervised marriage and divorce, as well as education and matters connected with religion. 

Although the central government was an absolute monarchy, the rule of the exilarch was not quite absolute. The Jews had a law which was their constitution and which neither ruler nor subject was permitted to violate. This became especially true as learning spread in Babylonia and scholars became judges. True, not all exilarchs acted strictly within the law, but officially at least there was the law to restrain them. 

ENJOY GOOD LAND 

In addition to their own government, the Jews enjoyed a good land. The country was fertile and well watered by many canals. 

The staple crop, that which the poor people could depend upon for food, was dates, which grew in very great abundance. The land also yielded wheat, flax, sesame, grapes, many vegetables, and numerous rare plants. Sheep and cattle too were raised in large numbers. While most of the Jews were farmers, many were handworkers, and the fact that their territory lay on the Euphrates River, which is deep and navigable, helped to encourage trade and commerce. Indeed, the Jews in Babylonia enjoyed free choice in their occupations, which, we shall see, was not true of their later centers. Thus, in addition to agriculture, they engaged in milling, pressing wine and oils, and distilling perfumes. They were weavers of cloth and silk, embroiderers, smiths in gold, silver, and iron, masons, canal builders, carpenters, sailors, shipbuilders, bankers, money changers, merchants, and peddlers. Among them, too, were professional people, physicians, surveyors, scribes, and legal officers. Many were professional soldiers.

HARDSHIPS 

Although the Jews felt at home in Babylonia there were some factors which reminded them that they were among strangers. First of all, whether they wanted to or not they were affected by the many wars, both internal and external. Wars against Rome were frequent and destructive, especially of the border districts where the Jews lived. In addition there were internal conflicts, civil wars and battles over the throne. There is even a record of a Jewish uprising against the government which held out for seven years. Even in peace time the Jews had to endure the quartering of soldiers in their homes. They also had to bear the cost of equipping mounted bowmen with coats of mail for themselves as well as for their horses.
