f j 134

Secondly, the Jews underwent occasional persecutions. While the Persians, like the Jews, had a religion which recognized the ethical laws of right and wrong, their religion differed from Judaism in some important respects. The Persian religion, for example, believed that there were two gods, a god of good and a god of evil, whereas the Jews believed in only one God. The 

Persians, therefore, would often try to forbid the Jews to recite the prayer Sh'ma Yisroel, which declares that God is one. 

They also differed in their method of burying the dead. The 

Persians used to put their dead out on a platform for the vultures and the dogs to devour. They thought the corpse made the ground unclean, The Jews, on the other hand, buried their dead in the ground. Accordingly, the Persians tried to prevent the 

Jews from burying their dead. As the Persians were fire-worshippers, they often would not permit people to burn fires in their homes but carried away the lights as well as the burning embers to their temples. The Jews did not dare to illuminate their streets in time of festivals, especially at Chanuko time. 

Later, when Rome turned Christian and began to persecute non-Christians in its territory, the Persians in turn began to persecute the Christians and, together with them, also the Jews. 

Especially oppressive were the taxes, which often robbed people of their fields or liberty. Unless a person paid a head tax to the king, he could be made a slave for the state. And unless he paid his land taxes, his fields might be taken away. 

Any other person might pay the tax for his neighbor, either the head tax or the land tax. If he paid the head tax, he could keep him as a slave,, and if he paid the land tax, he could use his fields for the year. Thus in bad years, when the crops failed, the poor might lose their fields as well as their liberty. In normal years they had to surrender to the government about a third of their crop. 

ADVANTAGES OUTWEIGH DISABILITIES 

Although Babylonian life had its shortcomings, the Jews lived more comfortably there than in Palestine. In view of general conditions of the time, they had not too much reason to complain. Actually we find the Jews in Babylonia a merry people, celebrating their festivals with wine and song. The rich even employed male and female dancers and singers, and wealthy women were well-dressed, with jewelry and make-up. 

Another sign that the Jews lived comfortably in Babylonia was their family pride. The Babylonian Jews were proud of their descent and kept a careful register of their family trees, tracing them back to the very first families which came to Babylonia. 

Most families would not intermarry with freed slaves or converts. 

The condition of the Jews can also be inferred from the fact that they lived on terms of friendship with their non-Jewish neighbors. They were guests in each others homes. Jews made it

13 319859 

MG 03

SA 9.9 BYTE 

! ?

373

a practice to visit their sick non-Jewish neighbors and to console their mourners. They even supported the non-Jewish poor out of their charity funds.

DEMOCRATIC LAWS 

True to the spirit of their religion, the Jews had many democratic elements in their public life. There was among our people a concern for the weak and the oppressed, which is the true mark of democracy. Thus women were protected against irresponsible divorce. A woman might not be divorced by her husband unless he provided for her. There were many laws, too, protecting laborers. These had to be paid promptly as agreed, and if their contract called for food, it had to be of good quality and sufficient. Jews still sold themselves as slaves, but they were surrounded by so many safeguards that it hardly paid to buy a slave any more. Thus a female Jewish slave either had to be married by her master or his son, or else she was to be freed when she became of age, at about twelve and a half. Male slaves could not be asked to perform such tasks as washing the master's feet, bringing him his slippers or doing anything after working hours. All Jewish slaves were to eat at the master's table and were to be cared for in illness. There was therefore a common saying that anyone buying a Hebrew slave was actually buying a master over himself. Non-Jewish slaves were safeguarded from cruel beating and were to be freed if they were maimed in any limb.

A democratic system of charities operated to provide for the needy. Two gaboim, or trustees, would go about making the weekly charity collections, which were imposed as a tax. Only those who received charity were excused from contributing. The main fund, called the kupo, was used to distribute help to the poor before every Sabbath and festival. In addition, an emergency supply was kept for persons in immediate need, called tamchui. Charity was distributed in the strictest secrecy, the receiver not knowing who gave it and the giver not knowing who received it.

In addition to these direct forms of assistance, there were other popular charities, such as a fund to supply dowries for poor brides, a fund to help orphans, a society for extending loans to poor shopkeepers and farmers, and a very important society for ransoming captives who had been kidnapped or taken prisoners during war and then sold as slaves. 

IMPORTANT INSTITUTIONS 

EDUCATION 

The most important institution among the Babylonian Jews was the synagogue. The synagogues were usually crowded and police often had to act as ushers. There being many scholarly men among the Babylonians, they needed no official cantor, but any learned man might read the services,

A special committee appointed by the city council was in charge of the synagogue. It was assisted by the Chazan, who was not the cantor as he is today, but more like our sexton. 

The Babylonian Jews were very careful to teach their children their religion, so that even a so-called ignorant man knew how to read his prayers and something of the Bible. Boys generally started their schooling with the study of the Bible at the age of five. At ten they began the Mishnah, and at fifteen they learned the G'moro, or the Talmud. Unfortunately, the girls were not included in the scheme of education. Classes were limited to twenty-five per teacher, or to forty when the teacher had an assistant. It is estimated that out of the total number of students who entered school about ten per cent continued to what today we would call the high school and one per cent to college. 

The 

At the head of the system of education was the Academy. 

Academy was both a college as well as a law-making body, where the subject of study was the Jewish law and where new laws were made as needed. The academies were headed by scholars who received degrees of honor. The graduates, or rabbis, received no pay for their rabbinic duties, but there were certain privileges which they enjoyed that were quite valuable. that were quite valuable. Thus the rabbis paid no taxes nor custom duties, and were free of other public. burdens. In cases where the rabbis were merchants the choicest places at the market were reserved for them.

We do not know when schools were first established in Babylonia. At first the country lacked institutions of higher education, and anyone wanting to study beyond what he could learn at home had to go to Palestine. Thus we have the example of 

Hillel, who left for the Holy Land to complete his studies. Soon, however, local academies were set up, and advanced subjects, too, could be studied in Babylonia. RAV The two men most responsible for the spread of learning in Babylonia were Rav and Samuel. Both had gone to Palestine to study at the school of Rabbi Judah Hanasi and returned filled with learning and zeal for their people. Armed with the Mishnah, which was prepared in Rabbi Judah's school, they could now begin independent study and teaching in Babylonia. 

Rav's real name was Abba Arika, which means "tall Abba," but so great was he considered by the men of his generation that they simply spoke of him as Rav, the rabbi. Rav came of an important family, and when he returned to Babylonia from Palestine his friends persuaded the exilarch to appoint him market inspector. 

While so working, Rav had occasion to travel up and down the country. He inquired especially into the conditions of schools and studies, and he was very deeply shocked to find schools neglected and children ignorant of the most elementary facts of Judaism. Because of a clash with the exilarch due to a disagreement over a point of law, Rav was imprisoned. As soon as he was

freed, he gave up his post of market inspector and set about correcting the system of education. He himself became assistant head of an academy and later, in 217, organized his own academy at Sura, from which a distinguished line of rabbis was to come forth. The new rabbis were called Amoraim, which means explainers. Their task was to interpret the Mishnah and to apply it to life under Babylonian conditions. Since many new cases arose, a large body of law was soon collected which was studied with great care and which was later to make up the Talmud. 

Rav was a great reformer, as well as a poet. Among his early reforms he set about correcting marriage abuses. Many people pretended to marry for fun, but Rav did not think that marriage was something to be trifled with and punished persons who entered into mock marriages in the market-place. He also ordered that angry parents should restrain themselves and not make life uncomfortable for their children. Laws regarding the fair treatment of laborers were enforced by him. As a poet, Rav wrote the beautiful prayer Olenu L'shabe-ach, in which the hope is expressed that God's kingdom might speedily be established over the entire earth. In a similar spirit he wrote prayers for the High Holydays. But his greatest work was in the field of education, where he provided instruction for both young and old. The new institution which Rav introduced was the meeting of the Kallah. The 

Kallah was an assembly of farmers and handworkers who came one month in six - the sixth and twelfth month - to study Torah. 

Because there were so many of them, they were obliged to camp in the open fields, and so eager were they to miss nothing of their studies that they barely allowed themselves time for meals. During the month they went over the portion of the law which had been assigned to them for study during the previous five months. 

During the last week of the Kallah months, large assemblies were held to teach the people about the coming holidays. 

Rav died in the year 247, deeply mourned by all the Jews. 

SAMUEL 

While Rav was the great rabbi, Samuel might be compared to a college professor. Besides being learned in Jewish law, in which he was outstanding, Samuel also knew Persian law. He was also a famous physician, having invented an eye remedy that cured Rabbi Judah Hanasi, Besides, he was also an astronomer of note and claimed to know the stars as well as he knew the streets of his native Nehardea. The calendar was still being fixed through the testimony of witnesses, but Samuel scientifically worked out a calendar for many years which he presented to the head of the 

Palestinian Academy.* Samuel became head of an Academy at 

Nehardea over which he presided until 257, the year of his death. * The Palestinian patriarchs did not make use of the scientific calendar, because they were eager to retain their authority over the whole Jewish world by their power to fix the calendar. Only when it seemed that Palestine could no longer remain the center of Jewish life did Hillel II give up his authority and fix the calendar scientifically, as we saw on pages 8 and 9.

Samuel, too, was a great reformer. At the time it was the practice for children to be married by their parents without their consent. Samuel strove to stop the practice and forbade the marriage of girls before they were old enough to say whether they liked their husbands or not. He also tried to limit merchants to a reasonable profit on their merchandise. Once, when shopkeepers sought to raise the cost of new dishes for the Passover, he threatened that unless the price were lowered he would permit the use of year-round dishes for the holiday. 

Samuel had wide friendships among the Persian scholars, and he was also friendly with the king. 

Unfortunately, Samuel's academy did not continue long in his native city after his death. Nehardea was invaded by the 

Romans in the year 261 and destroyed. However, the academy was moved to Pumbeditha, where it continued for another eight hundred years.

THE TALMUD CLOSED 

END OF AN ERA 

As the Palestinian academies were becoming weaker, Babylonia assumed supremacy over Jewish life. Thus, even after the calendar was already fixed, so that witnesses were no longer needed to testify that they saw the new moon, the Babylonian academies nevertheless took over the practice. They too heard witnesses and decided on the new moon accordingly. But most important of all, the Babylonian scholars gathered the large body of laws and interpretations of the Mishnah, which came to be known as G'moro, and that, together with the Mishnah, became the Talmud, which means "subject of study." For over one hundred years diligent scholars collected the Talmud. Two great rabbis were mainly responsibile for the collection Rav 

Ashi, who died in the year 427, and Rabina, who died in 499.

A similar Talmud, but on a much smaller scale, had been collected in Palestine about one hundred years earlier. The Talmud contained mainly Halakah, which means law. Talmudic law is distinguished by its desire to enable men in their every-day acts to live in accordance with the will of God. All Jewish law is religious law, and therefore the Jew must always be mindful of what is right in the sight of God. The portion of the Talmud which is not strictly Halakah, or law consists of Haggadah, which means legend. The rabbis told many a story or bit of poetry, gave their opinion about life or interpreted a verse of the Bible in an interesting way, or taught a lesson in ethical behavior. All of these ideas are included in the Haggadah part of the Talmud. Thus,

The Talmud is more in the nature of an encyclopedia of Jewish knowledge than a law book, In the Babylonian Jewish Academy separate subjects were not studied, but all knowledge was used in order to determine what the law should be. besides law, we find in the Talmud moral teachings, religious regulations, instructions regarding good manners, and information concerning medicine, health, agriculture, etc. The

! 

CLIF 

1 1

Talmud too is written not as a law code but in the form of interesting discussions. Thus during the fourteen hundred and fifty years since its completion the Talmud has been the most popular book of Jewish study. Only in modern times has its popularity somewhat waned.

The completion of the Talmud ends one period in the life of the Jews in Babylonia. Political conditions in that country became very unsettled, and it seemed too difficult to carry on organized life. However, soon we shall see the Babylonian Jews once more starting a brilliant period in Jewish history. We are prepared now for the study of the next part of Jewish life in Babylonia, called the period of the g'onim.

III JEWISH LIFE IN BABYLONIA UNDER THE ARABS 

Babylonia was the second home of the Jewish people. During the long period which we have studied we saw how our people lived in Babylonia in closely settled villages where the majority of the population was Jewish. They were thus able to take over the leadership of the Jewish people and to create a Talmud there. 

How was it to be in the following period?

Many Jews still lived in compact villages but more and more they moved to the big towns. Here they still enjoyed the rights as Jews and citizens. But they were citizens of the second class. 

The new religion, Mohammedanism, like Christianity, restricted their liberties. It forbade them to hold office, to wear fine clothes, or to build new synagogues. After a few centuries the Moslem government weakened the rule of the exilarch. Jewish communities were no longer directly responsible to him for the payment of their taxes. On the other hand, during this entire time the Jews enjoyed the rule of a religious head who was officially appointed by the government, The Jews were thus stronger in one respect and weaker in another. 

Babylonia therefore was still the leading center of world Jewry. It created much Jewish law and important books were written by Babylonian Jews, but it could no longer make a new Talmud.

Since the Jews no longer lived in their own land, their fate was in the end decided by the people among whom they lived, When the Babylonian government began to decline, Jewish life declined with it. The Jews were dependent on their surroundings. 

When the great Moslem empire of the East disappeared, Jewish life too was swallowed up as though it had never been. 

But let us see how this period unfolds and what happened to the Jews during the next few centuries. 

HOW THE ARABS AFFECTED JEWISH LIFE 

Jewry recovered its lost position in Babylonia with the coming of the Arabs. Like a whirlwind,

Like a whirlwind, the Arabs swept out the Persian ruling house and established themselves in its place. 

They brought new vigor to the country, a new flowering of civilization in which the Jews naturally shared. A new life began for the Jews, a life of handicrafts and trade and extensive migrations. An

But what forces brought the Arabs out of their desert homes and set them on the path of world conquest? What suddenly spurred them on to seek to control the world? The Arab awakening was the result of a new religion which the Arabs accepted. 

Arab prophet appeared who taught the Arabs a religion of monotheism. The prophet's name was Mohammed, and his religion, strangely, was an offshoot of Judaism, Let us see how Judaism

ཎཱི། རོ, ནད ༈ནྡྲ

helped to give birth to the new religion and how this new religion in turn affected the Jews. 

JEWS IN ARABIA INFLUENCE ARABS 

Jews had fled to Arabia after some of the disastrous wars which Judea fought. A group of Jews settled in the marshes of inner Arabia, drained them, and introduced agriculture and handicrafts, smithing, weaving, and dyeing. They prospered and built strong, beautiful castles. In the course of time they became like the Arabs, accepting the language and customs of the Arabs, but they continued to observe their religion and to teach it to their children. The Arabs were in the main, an ignorant people, and the Jews exercised great influence over them. Many of them accepted Judaism, and others who did not quite accept the religion entirely observed the dietary laws or kept Yom Kippur and other festivals. They even believed that some day the 

Messiah would come to rule over the Jewish people. They respected the Jews especially because they possessed written holy books, which the Arabs did not have. 

MOHAMMED 

However,

The Arab leader who was to exercise so great an influence on the course of the world was as we said, Mohammed. He was born in Mecca in 571. Both his parents died during his childhood, and so he was brought up by an uncle. 

Like other poor

Arab children, he was ignorant of reading or writing. as he grew up he interested himself in Jewish religious practices, keeping such customs as Yom Kippur, the Sabbath, and the dietary laws, and turning toward Jerusalem in prayer. At the age of twenty-five, he married a rich widow and, with her caravans, traveled to distant countries, where he met both Jews and Christians and further learned about their religion. He was deeply stirred by the Bible and by Hebrew and Christian prayers.

Mohammed was moved by all that he learned, and he became more and more absorbed in religion. Knowing that the Jews were awaiting the Messiah, the thought occurred to him that he might be the expected one. He was too much influenced by Judaism to think of himself as a god, but he did regard himself as a prophet, a second Moses sent to the Arabs as well as to the rest of the world. He believed in all the former prophets, among whom he included Jesus, but he considered himself the greatest of them all. The Arabs, Mohammed believed, according to the 

Bible are descendants of Abraham's son Ishmael. As such they too can be heirs to the religion of Abraham, to the belief in one God. He therefore went about teaching the Arabs to abandon their idols and to worship only the God of Abraham. "There is no God but Allah, he preached, "and Mohammed is his prophet.

He warned his followers of God's judgment day and urged them to prayer, charity and good deeds.

Mohammed began to spread his ideas among his acquaintances, and little by little he persuaded others to believe in him, Soon he convinced an entire tribe that had lived near the Jews and knew of the Jewish belief in the Messiah. When his relatives at home were making life uncomfortable for him and for his followers, and were even possibly planning to kill him, Mohammed fled to join this tribe. The year of the flight, 622, is regarded by the Mohammedans as the year 1, the beginning of Mohammedan history.

In his new home Mohammed was closer to the Jews. He studied their religion more carefully, but at the same time he began to demand that the Jews recognize him as their Messiah. When the 

Jews laughed at him, because for all his learning he still seemed to them an ignorant man, he became embittered and turned from a friend into a hating enemy. As his followers increased, he was able to make war on the Jews, whom he massacred or expelled from Arabia. Mohammed wrote his hatred of the Jews into the Koran, which is the Mohammedan Bible. Thus the dislike of the Jews became a part of the Mohammedan religion. 

Having successfully made war on the Jews, Mohammed now turned to the conquest of all of Arabia, Shortly thereafter he died, but the war was carried on by his followers, who occupied Egypt, Palestine, Syria, Babylonia, Persia, and parts of India. Soon the Arab Empire became one of the largest the world has ever known.

JEWS ENCOUNTER ARABS 

The Jews looked with fear to the followers of the new religion which had sworn hatred against them, but the situation proved to be not quite as bad as expected. Jews and Christians though declared by the Arabs to be citizens of the second rank, were nevertheless tolerated. The Arabs needed the Christians and especially the Jews in industry, the professions, and the service of the state. Therefore, though outwardly the Arabs passed many laws against the Jews, in practice the laws were not observed. Thus the Arabs enacted a law punishing intermarriage between Jews and Arabs with death; forbidding the Jews to enter the city of Mecca; prohibiting them from holding any high office, riding on horses or camels, or carrying arms.

Jews were ordered to wear clothes of special color, special kinds of hats, and rope girdles instead of colorful sashes.

Their houses were to be lower than the houses of the Mohammedans. 

They might build no new synagogues and might repair existing ones only by special permission. But these laws were for the most part not enforced, because the Arabs too soon reached a high degree of civilization, and civilized men are not intolerant. They had poets, philosophers, scientists, architects, and wealthy merchants. Only when civil war caused the breakup of the state did prejudices once more flare up, and the 

Arabs turned to persecution.

ARABS APPOINT POLITICAL AND RELIGIOUS HEADS OF JEWS 

The Arabs recognized the right of the Jews to their own national life. They spoke of the Christians as "the nation of Jesus, and they called the Jews "the nation of Ezra. When the Arabs occupied Babylonia they reappointed an exilarch, Bustanai, to be the political governor of the Jews, and then even went further and appointed the master of the y'shivo at Sura as the religious head. Because the y shivo was called 

Gaon Jacob (the Glory of Jacob), the religious leader became known as the gaon or illustrious one. Later the head of the other y'shivo, of Pumbeditha, also became recognized as gaon, and so now the Jews had a government head and two religious heads.

CHANGES IN JEWISH LIFE 

The new Arab governments exerted themselves to promote trade and industry among their subjects. They built roads, dug canals, terraced hills, and constructed bridges, so that commerce increased and the population grew. The Jewish population also increased in number and influence. 

Had the government preserved law and order equally well, the Jews would have been quite happy in Babylonia. Unfortunately, the country was often torn by civil wars. Besides, in peace as well as in war the tax system was very harsh. Taxes were the same for lands of equal areas regardless of the quality of the soil. The government took two-fifths of the produce and collected it with torture and imprisonment if the owner failed to pay. Nor did the government enforce law and order well, so that pirates roamed the seas, and thieves filled the land.

The government tax policy resulted in the discouragement of agriculture. Some of the Jews continued their agricultural pursuits in spite of tax hardships. Besides actual farming, they had to engage in dairying and wine-making in order to produce kosher milk and wine. The exilarchs and the g'onim farmed large estates on which worked many poor Jewish peasants. 

But the majority of the Jews were becoming city dwellers. 

They were to be found in the principal cities, forty thousand living in Bagdad alone, which at the time was the richest city in the world. A quarter of a million people lived in Bagdad, a city of many markets, in which all manner of goods were bought and sold. Many Jews lived in Persia, where there have been found the remains of an apparently all-Jewish city with fifty caravan inns and over two hundred banks.

Living in the cities, the Jews naturally engaged in handicrafts. They wove all sorts of textiles and became experts in dyeing. The Arabs as well as the Jews had a love of beautiful colors which the Jewish dyers satisfied, They wove rugs, did

CT 

And be 1. ? 1.

tailoring of fine garments, set precious stones, and made silver and gold bowls, amulets, rings, and Torah ornaments. The Jews also worked as blacksmiths in iron and copper. They cut coats of mail, weapons, and cutlery. Besides they were well known as glass-blowers, making flasks, goblets, Sabbath lamps, as well as beautiful painted tiles. They also worked in wood and leather, making rare parchments, fine leather, and saddles. Many were architects, building stately synagogues with marble columns and gilded verses inscribed on them. Synagogues were adorned with figures of men, eagles, lions, and bulls. Many practiced the professions, especially that of the physician and the scribe. 

More rewarding, but far more dangerous, was trade. Thieves and thieving governors were equally a danger to be guarded against, but a successful journey might bring rich rewards. 

The Jews at the time had a monopoly on trade with Europe, since the Mohammedans were not permitted to enter European countries. 

Another advantage which the Jews enjoyed was that they had friends all over the world with whom they could advise about market conditions. A great advantage, too, was the fact that all 

Jews were governed by one common Talmudic law, and thus they could be certain that in Jewish affairs the same law would apply wherever they might be.

In the cities the Jews were no longer by themselves, since they formed part of the general population. They still lived under Jewish law and the exilarch and the g'onim wielded authority over them. But theirs were Diaspora surroundings like those which the Jews were soon to encounter in Europe. However, as handworkers and merchants they were among the upper classes of the population, a condition which, as we shall see, was also true of Europe.

INNER LIFE 

THE EXILARCHS AND G'ONIM 

Authority over the Jews was still exercised by the exilarchs. 

Indeed, the exilarch's power now reached farther than ever before, The Jews throughout the entire Arab empire - not merely Babylonia were under his rule. But unlike the earlier period he was now obliged to share his rule with the g'onim. This naturally led to differences and disputes between the three heads. 

At first all claimed the right to appoint judges and heads of cities and to collect the taxes. After a time, they divided the empire between them, each one exercising rights over a given region. Further, the exilarch was given a voice in the choice of the g'onim, and these in turn were consulted in the selection of the exilarch.

The Jews prized the few symbols of self-government still left them. Impressive ceremonies were enacted when an exilarch had to be chosen. The ceremonies lasted a week, and the g'onim came to take part in them. The festivities were especially marked in the synagogue on the Sabbath. A choice choir was selected to chant the service, and a special platform with

costly hangings was erected for the exilarch. When the exilarch's turn arrived to read the Torah, the Torah was brought to him, an honor reserved only for royalty. After the service a procession went to the exilarch's home where the celebration continued for many days.

The exilarch alone had permission to drive a horse-drawn carriage and to have runners running before him. Other Jews might only use donkeys or mules. 

The g'onim, though of lesser authority in matters of state, were more widely honored by the Jewish community than the exilarch. Even the Jews in Christian lands obeyed them, though the exilarch's rule did not reach there. 

The school or y'shivo over which the gaon presided was at once a college, a high court, and a legislative body. It consisted of the gaon, the vice-gaon, three assistants, seven heads of the Kallah, and sixty other members, all arranged in seven rows with the gaon at their head. The number seventy-one corresponded to the membership of the Jerusalem Sanhedrin. To these academies were addressed questions of law as well as inquiries about the meaning of passages in the Talmud from all parts of the world.

The g'onim continued the custom of the Kallah meetings instituted in the third century by Rav. At the Kallah gatherings they discussed questions addressed to them and dictated answers in Hebrew, Arabic, or Aramaic, in accordance with the language in which the question was received. The decision of the gaon was final, his being the supreme court. 

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

The local governments in each village and city were carried on by the dayon, or judge, selected by the exilarch or gaon, together with a council of from three to twelve, depending upon the size of the city. The council usually consisted of men of property. Two men sat with the dayon and formed a court of three which tried all cases. Other officials of the community included market inspectors, scribes, policemen, charity supervisors, synagogue officials, teachers, a chazan, etc. Thus we see how far the Jews were self-governing even at this time. Pen Though the Jewish courts were not always given the author ity by the government to enforce their decrees, they were nevertheless of great value to the Jews in that they kept Jewish business private and confidential. Seeking to settle cases in a non-Jewish court was regarded as treason by the Jews. alties usually imposed by the Jewish courts were fines, imprisonment, beating, and especially excommunication from the Jewish fold. There were various types of excommunication. A mild form lasted seven days; a more severe penalty lasted thirty days; and if the offender did not repent the excommunication might be permanent. During the time when one was excommunicated,

2 b 

PA LDS 

D J 21 37 JA 973 G. 17.6 ra que

no one might talk to him, he might not be admitted to the synagogue, no one might touch his food, his children could not be taught, nor would he even be buried according to the usual Jewish custom.

In the earlier Persian period all the Jews of the empire were governed as a unit. Now the Caliph, the Mohammedan ruler, no longer felt that that was necessary. He could collect the taxes more easily, directly. Accordingly, early in the eight hundreds he greatly weakened the office of the exilarch by decreeing that the latter should no longer collect the taxes. 

Thereafter only a few exilarchs are heard about. In 1040 the 

Caliph executed an exilarch on the suspicion of rebellion and with him the office closed forever. Babylonia became like other 

Diaspora centers.

ARAB INFLUENCE ON JEWISH RELIGION 

As the Jews were no longer among their own people, but in the midst of a large Mohammedan population, it was but natural that the latter should exert considerable influence over inner Jewish life. The Mohammedans seriously affected the religion of the Jews. To begin with many Jews actually believed in Mohammed and accepted his religion. The fact that an Arab could suddenly appear and through the power of religion change the face of the globe led many Jews to believe that he must have been sent by God. Many, while not themselves believing in 

Mohammed, thought him a prophet to the non-Jews. Others believed that the great changes which had taken place were intended as a sign that the coming of the Messiah was near at hand. Pretender Messiahs now began to appear. Some of them accepted the New Testament and the Koran as holy books. Others thought the dietary laws should be abolished. Out of these movements many new sects were born, most of which did not last very long.

One new sect, however, formed upon Arab example, did survive. The Arabs had a written Koran in which their law was set forth. But whenever a question arose that was not found in the 

Koran, Mohammedan scholars tried to determine what the prophet and his friends did or would have done in such a case. In time, the Arab doctors of the law added many new laws on the pretext that the prophet and his friends would have acted in that way. 

That became Arab Oral Law. But the Oral Law grew so large, and so much was added to it, that many disbelieved it and accepted only the Koran. In the same way, a sect arose among the Jews denying the binding power of the Talmud and believing only in the written law, or the Bible.

THE KARAITES 

The sect was founded by a man called Anan ben David. This man, in the year 767, was involved in a quarrel over the office of the exilarch. The g'onim had to choose between two brothers, one of whom, Anan ben David, was the more learned. But his religious beliefs were suspected, and so he was passed over in favor of his brother. When Anan actively opposed the choice,

T A 16 D MOS 42 fo WO" *? 2 PL 

E. 5 

19 J

he was cast into prison.

He might have been dealt with harshly by the authorities as a rebel against the king, but he was advised to claim that he was the founder of a new religion which had nothing in common with the religion of the exilarch. Upon being freed, Anan actually began to preach to his followers to believe only in the Bible and to pay no heed to the Talmud any longer. A large number of Jews were prepared to join him, and these Anan advised to search the Bible diligently and to find whatever laws they needed for Jewish life in it. Thus a new sect was formed called first the Ananites and later the Karaites. 

This sect rebelled against the authority of the g'onim and their academies. The leaders studied the Bible, but naturally new situations existed which had not been present when the Bible was written. They were thus at a loss to know what was and what was not permitted, and in the end the Karaites were obliged to make oral laws in the same way as the Talmud was composed. each leader explained the Bible differently, many divisions were formed within the sect which weakened each other. Since

Although they rebelled against the severity of the Talmud, the Karaites in practice proved to be more severe than their opponents, the Rabbanites (the followers of the g'onim). Their difficulty grew out of the fact that they took the Bible too literally instead of trying to enter into its spirit. On Fri. day evening, for example, they lit no lights and sat in darkness, because they claimed, the Bible forbids lighting fires on the 

Sabbath. The Rabbanites translate the Hebrew to mean "you shall not kindle on the Sabbath, but you may light lights before the

Sabbath, or a non-Jew might light them. The Karaites, on the other hand, translated it "you shall not burn" and forbade fires no matter when they were lit. They warmed no food and did not leave their homes to go anywhere except to the synagogue. Many of them interpreted the dietary laws as forbidding the eating of ducks or chickens or buying fish from non-Jews. Although Jewish law permits cooking food on holidays, many Karaites restricted themselves even from that.

At one time the Karaites were a very large sect. But they were very unprogressive and resisted all change. Time has therefore passed them by. Today they have dwindled and live in only a few places. Altogether only about twelve thousand of them are left.

BIBLE STUDY 

Of course, the formation of a new sect and the quarrels attendant upon its organization were not in the best interests of the Jewish people. However, the 

However, the presence of Karaites was not without some benefits, for in order to answer them, the

Rabbanites had to know the Bible as well as the Karaites. Thus the quarrel led to the careful study of the sacred writings, of their grammar, and of their correct pronunciation. In part we owe to this dispute the vowel points, which were invented at that time in order to aid the inexperienced Hebrew reader. Before that time Hebrew had been printed without vowels. Since

many words have similar consonants but differ only in vowels there could be different readings of the same word. With vowel points this was no longer possible.

SAADIA VANQUISHES KARAITES 

The Karaites led a fight against the Rabbanites and might have been more successful than they were. Fortunately, a great gaon, Saadia, who lived from 882 to 942, was a finer scholar by far than the Karaites even in Bible study, and proved them to be wrong.

Saadia was born and received his education in Egypt. At twenty-three he had already written a book attacking the Karaites. For some reason Saadia was obliged to leave 

Egypt. He came to Palestine and then traveled to Babylonia, where he was appointed one of the heads of the Kallah. At this time a quarrel arose between the Babylonian College and the head of the Palestinian Academy. A new Academy had been organized in Palestine, and its head wanted to restore to himself the right of fixing the calendar, which was now being determined by the Babylonian rabbis. There was thus the danger that the Jewish people would be divided by two different calendars and different dates for the holidays. It seemed that all the Babylonian rabbis were powerless to stop the Palestinian rabbi. Saadia now entered the fray and through a convincing book proved the Palestinian rabbi wrong and retained the practice of fixing the calendar for Babylonia. A short time thereafter, when the gaonate of Sura was left vacant by death, Saadia was appointed as the first outsider to head the Academy. Now once more he took up his fight against the Karaites, aiming to prove them wrong from the Bible itself, He made a translation of the Bible into Arabic and a commentary or explanation of it. In order to make his translation accurate, he was among the first to study the rules of Hebrew grammar,' which no one before him had as yet organized. His work aided greatly in strengthening the Rabbanites.

PROVES TRUTH OF JUDAISM 

Not only was Judaism endangered by the Karaites, but there were many students of philosophy who attacked the Bible and the Jewish religion as untrue. Saadia, who in addition to being a great rabbi was also a philosopher, was very anxious to prove that philosophy and Judaism are not opposed one to the other. 

It happened that he had a quarrel with the exilarch because the latter wanted to act contrary to the Jewish law. Trying to restrain him, Saadia was deposed and was in retirement for seven years. During these years he wrote a great book entitled Emunos 

Videos, (Beliefs and Opinions). 

In his book Saadia proves that God exists and that..He created the world. God made man free to act justly or unjustly. 

Right conduct joins us to God, while evil separates us from Him. 

There is reward and punishment in the world to come, and the Jews will be repaid for all their suffering when the Messiah comes.

OTHER WRITERS AND ARTISTS 

Even during the Persian period we saw the Jews learned much from the Persians. Now that the Jews lived more closely to the non-Jewish population it was natural that they should learn and be concerned with general Eastern civilization. 

Thus in addition to Saadia there were other learned Jews engaged in the study of the sciences, especially medicine. 

They wrote many books on medical subjects, such as fevers, snake poisoning, diseases of the eye, etc. There were also many books written on astronomy, mathematics, astrology, and alchemy. Jews were also interested in the arts. They wrote stories, made metal works of art, and artistic textiles, and their artists illuminated many manuscripts. They had much fine music sung by choirs, and they played instrumental music during the half-holidays in the synagogue. 

ARAB EMPIRE BREAKS UP 

Unfortunately, civil wars and inner revolutions broke up the Arab empire. Bagdad became poor, and the position of the 

Jews declined with it, for when times were bad the Jews and the Christians suffered especially. The academies too became poor, and fewer students interested themselves in studies. 

Large parts of the Arab empire, such as Egypt and Spain, had broken away and formed separate countries. The rulers of these lands looked unfavorably on the Jews' keeping up their contact with Babylonia and sending gifts of money there. It happened, however, that toward the end of the period of the g'onim there were two especially great rabbis, the G'onim Sherira and Hai, who were the most distinguished Talmudists of the century, and to them questions were addressed from all parts of the world. Young men were sent to study under these masters, even from distant Spain. But even these g'onim suffered from the unsettled conditions, and when they died, few Jews turned any more to Babylonia with questions, although the schools stayed open for some centuries. Babylonian 

Jewry gradually declined while the Jews turned to new centers in Europe.

IV THE JEWS BECOME EUROPEANS 

The Jews came to the United States mainly from Europe. 

Originally, as we saw, the Jews lived in Palestine and then in Babylonia. But while Jewish life was developing in Babylonia, Jews were at the same time settling in Europe. 

At first they were few in number, but gradually the stream increased, and when the Eastern Empire began to decay there was already a strong 

Jewish settlement in Europe. 

How did the Jews fare in Europe? Not as well as they might have, since over five million Jews were obliged to leave that contínent and come to the United States and other American countries. On the other hand, the majority of them still live in 

Europe. The Jews enjoyed a long, fairly prosperous period in 

Europe, but it was followed by disaster. Thus for centuries they lived happily in Spain and France, but they suffered occasional massacres, and in the end they were expelled. For many centuries they lived in Poland and, while they were never expelled from there, they did endure terrible slaughter. 

It was a rare country where, as in some places in Italy, the Jews lived continuously without serious difficulties. 

In ancient times the Jews were a people of several million. 

They always had large families until modern times. Therefore, the Jews should have multiplied and become one of the most numerous peoples of the world. Instead, we find that at the close of the Middle Ages, in 1492, Jewish numbers were the lowest in their entire history. There were only about a million and a half Jews in the entire world. Forced conversions, epidemics, and massacres reduced the number of Jews very greatly. Altogether, the world's population did not increase very rapidly during the Middle Ages, but the Jews were among the worst sufferers. Why did the Jews allow themselves to be so badly misused? They could have avoided the massacres if they had been willing to accept conversion and give up their own faith. though some Jews did, the vast majority remained steadfast because they felt their own religion more developed and superior to the surrounding beliefs. They were unwilling to exchange their faith for any other. Thus the Jewish people kept alive in 

Europe one of the greatest of human rights--the right to be different. At a time when to be different meant paying with their lives, the Jews paid the price, and so they preserved their sacred beliefs to this day. They paid the penalty for their stubbornness, but they served the cause of liberty which we enjoy today.

JEWS OF ITALY 

But

Let us now turn to the story of the Jews in Europe. Among the earliest settlements to attract our attention is that of the Jews of Rome. There was to be found one of the oldest Jewish communities in Europe. Jews lived in Italy certainly since the time of Pompey, and possibly even before him. Pompey had brought

TA ་ c།་

Jewish captives from Palestine who were sold as slaves in Rome. 

After a time these became free and formed a community on the bank of the Tiber River. In their pioneer days many were peddlers and artisans, textile workers, glass blowers, dyers, and smiths. In time some of them rose to great wealth and had their shops on the principal street of Rome, Campus Martius. They carried on Jewish life, and at the same time, they shared the life of Rome the same as all other Roman citizens. 

The government gave them grain and oil and even saw to it that the oil was kosher. When distribution of foods took place on Saturday, the Jews got theirs on Sunday. Jewish soldiers received kosher food and the Jewish poor were given money whenever non-kosher food was distributed.

The Jews were given the right to their own synagogue and to their own worship. They were excused from the emperor worship, but were permitted to pay their respects to the emperor in their own way. The government also excused them from being called to trial on Sabbaths and festivals. The Jews had their rabbinical courts, while at the same time they enjoyed all the benefits of the Roman government.

The Jews in Rome had a whole chain of Jewish institutions. 

In places they even had their own markets. Their relation to the Roman population was very friendly, the heathens frequently being the dinner guests at Jewish homes. 

Some Roman writers made fun of the Jews. They called them lazy because they would not work on the Sabbath, or described them as atheists because they did not believe in any god that could be seen by human eyes. But on the other hand, numerous 

Romans were attracted by the purity of the Jewish religion. 

Many kept the Sabbath or the dietary laws, and some were outright converts.

Even after the Temple was destroyed, the condition of the Jews was not much affected for the worse. The only penalty imposed on them was that the tax of a half shekel, which they had formerly paid to the Temple of Jerusalem, was now taken for the 

Temple of Jupiter Capitolinus and was not abolished til1 229

C.E. In spite of their having to pay this tax however, the Jews continued sending gifts to the patriarchs in Palestine.

The Jews in Rome named their synagogues after emperors or distinguished Romans. Thus they showed their close connection with the Roman state. They had many schools for children and a college for grown-ups where the Torah was taught. Their cemeteries have been discovered in underground tunnels called catacombs. We find tombstones written in Greek and later in Latin, thus telling us the languages that the Jews used. In addition there were Hebrew inscriptions showing that the Jews continued to use the Hebrew Language for sacred purposes.

7. Cal. 20 90g Mag Ni

THE BARBARIANS COME 

Rome was declining in importance. The city was becoming poor because of the heavy taxes which the kings drained from it while the population did no work. Then Rome turned Christian, and we already saw what changes took place in the relation of the Church to the Jews. After the conversion of the government of Rome there began a series of destructions of synagogues and attacks upon Jews all over the country. Fortunately, the threatening barbarian invasions forced the Romans to realize that they could not afford to make enemies of the Jews. Most of the harsh laws were therefore relaxed. 

The barbarian kings who conquered Rome soon turned Christian, but they were Arian Christians, a sect not considered true believers by the Catholic Church. The Arian kings were not as intolerant as the Catholic clergy. Thus, even though they forbade the Jews to build new synagogues, they ordered that those already in existence must not be destroyed. If any harm were done to them, they would punish the offenders.

After a twenty years' war (534-555,)Italy passed under the rule of Byzantium, the Eastern Roman Empire. The Jews, who had reason to fear and hate Byzantium, fought against it with all their might, and when they lost the war, they suffered greatly as a consequence. But Northern Italy was soon overrun by the 

Lombards, another tribe of Arian barbarians, and the Jews breathed easier. Even in the South the rule of the Byzantines proved not to be as severe as in Byzantium itself.

Between the 700's and 1000's we know very little about what transpired in Italy. The land was a battle-ground for dukes, barons, and their retainers. Finally the German kings won out and became the heads of the Holy Roman Empire. In the 

South the Arabs conquered Sicily from Byzantium and held it for about three hundred years. The Jews continued living in Italy throughout the period.

JEWS IN SPAIN 

Jews also settled in Spain, but the first period of their settlement did not end as happily as the corresponding period for the Jews of Italy. The Jews came early to Spain and took to cultivating the fields and vineyards with the aid of slaves. 

They also engaged in trade, sending their goods to Africa. They were considered Roman citizens and enjoyed full rights as such. 

For a while conditions in Spain became strained when Rome accepted Christianity, but when the barbarians conquered Spain they too, like those of Italy, belonged to the Arian sect which was not as intolerant as the official Catholic Church. Friendship thus continued between Jews and Christians. Christians ate in Jewish homes, Jews blessed Christian fields, and there were even occasional cases of intermarriage between Jews and Christians. 

Jewish soldiers were set to guard the passes of the Pyrenees against outside foes.

!'". ་ ་ ་ ་

Unfortunately, the happy period did not last. In 589 the

Visigoths turned Catholic, and straightway the Church began to direct attacks against the Jews. First it reenacted the laws forbidding Jewish slave-holding or holding public office. Then it forbade Christians to visit with Jews or to attend synagogues. 

But all these restrictions did not satisfy the clergy. They secured a law to convert the Jews forcibly to Christianity. Those 

Jews who lived on the estates of important noblemen could defy the law because the nobles could disobey the king. Many others had no choice but to accept Christianity or leave the country. 

But even those who accepted the Christian faith continued to observe the Jewish religion secretly, though the penalty was very severe. The Jews who somehow escaped conversion were harshly persecuted. Merchants were forbidden to trade with 

Christians, farmers had their fields and orchards taken away, and handworkers were forced to rest on Sunday, Christian holidays, and saints' days. But the Jews had, nevertheless, to pay the full measure of taxes for themselves and for all their brethren who had been converted, so that the state would lose nothing by the decrease in the number of Jews. All these measures so ruined Jewish business undertakings that the Jews became too poor to pay their taxes. The king then accused them of plotting to invite the Mohammedans to invade Spain. He used this as an excuse to seize all the Jews, then confiscated their property and sold them into slavery. Thus the first period of Jewish settlement in Spain ended in tragedy.

JEWS IN FRANCE AND GERMANY 

The Jews also settled in Gaul, or France. They too, were artisans and traders, and as Roman citizens they enjoyed full rights. Even after the conversion of the Gauls to Christianity, friendly relations continued between them and the Jews. But as the Roman Empire was breaking up, cities began to disappear and trade dwindled. The Jews, as handworkers and merchants, were thus especially affected. 

The Franks, conquerors of Gaul, considered the Jews as Romans and treated them accordingly. But soon the Gauls became mixed with the Franks and formed ore people, while the Jews were now more than ever a people apart. The church then began to attack them. It forbade their owning slaves, which made agricultural work difficult for them. Jews were also forbidden to act as judges or tax farmers. Then laws were passed prohibiting them from appearing in the streets four days before Easter, Finally, in the 600's, persecution against the Jews broke out in full force. It reached a point where the king ordered them either to be baptized or to leave the country, Thus the first Jewish settlement in France also ended in disaster.

Similarly, a Jewish settlement in Germany came to a sad end. We find Jews living in the Rhineland cities early in the 

300's, but after that period there is no mention of Jews for five or six hundred years. The German cities, too, dwindled

راد is you

and the Jews probably left the country.

Thus we see that Jewish attempts to settle in Europe were only slightly successful at first.

SECOND PERIOD OF JEWS IN EUROPE 

The second period of Jewish life in Europe was somewhat more successful, though it too, resulted in disaster. However, for many years the Jews lived happily and well in Europe, and, though later, troubles overtook them, they nevertheless remained there to this day.

First let us turn to Spain, the country which, at the close of the 600's, enslaved its Jews. In 711 the Arabs invaded Spain and occupied the entire country except a small region in the northern mountains. The Jews

The Jews cooperated with the Arabs and helped them, Now, under friendly Arab rule, Jewish life began to flourish and prosper. Indeed, Jewish life in Spain was so much happier and more prosperous than any that the Jews had enjoyed for many centuries, that in later times that period came to be known as the Golden Age.

At first Spain was a part of the larger Arab kingdom, with headquarters at Damascus and later at Bagdad. Soon, however, it declared its independence of the rest of the Arab empire. After many inner wars a strong government was finally established. 

From 912 on, for about one hundred years, kings ruled over a united Spain. Unfortunately, this government did not last more than one century and broke up into several warring principalities. The Christians in the North, who were waiting for an opportunity to rewin their country, fell upon these states one by one, and by the year 1248 had recovered all but one small district, Granada. Naturally, the Jews too suffered from these constant wars. They were often uprooted from their territory and driven from their cities. Nor did the Arabs always, when at peace, encourage Jewish life. A fanatical Arab sect, the 

Almohades, which for a time ruled Spain (during the 12th century), forced the Jews either to leave Spain or become converted to Islam. There were even occasional mob attacks against the 

Jews to remind them that they were living in exile. 

ALLOWED FULL OPPORTUNITY 

But the Jews consider the age golden because they were allowed free opportunity for work and for cultural expression. 

The Jews of Spain enjoyed full freedom to engage in any occupations they chose. Many were farmers who brought to Spain rare fruits and nuts and who cultivated the silkworm and thus helped to develop a large silk industry. They also carried on the same handicrafts and trades as in Babylonia, Spanish Jews were especially distinguished in medicine. Every learned man studied medicine as part of his education, and many were famous doctors. 

But above all, their leaders attained to important positions in the state, such as was no longer possible in most of the Christian countries. Thus Hasdai ibn Shaprut rose to the post of

adviser and finance minister to the king of united Spain, holding one of the most important offices in the kingdom. The wealthy Jews of Spain dressed like knights and rode on horses, while ladies rode in covered carriages, privileges which were reserved only for the nobility. The Jews participated in writing poetry and music and in building beautiful buildings. 

JEWISH STATESMEN 

As already mentioned, in spite of Christian or official Moslem law, Jews were permitted to occupy public office. The first great Jewish statesman was Has dai, the distinguished physician, who was also the inventor of some important remedies. 

In 912 he was first appointed body physician to the caliph. (Moslem ruler), then finance minister, and finally he was given charge of receiving ambassadors of foreign countries. In that connection he helped stop a war which would have broken out because of an insult to one of the ambassadors by the caliph. 

Hasdai helped to collect a large library for his ruler, who was much interested in books, and especially in foreign medical works.

Hasdai also helped the Jews by taking up their difficulties in various Christian countries and using his influence to see to it that they were peacefully settled. Many a Christian king made the position of the Jews easier because of his desire to please the Jewish finance minister of the court of Spain. 

Another Jew, Samuel ibn Nagdela (993-1055), rose to the post of premier of Granada. A refugee from Cordova, he owned a small spice shop in Malaga. Samuel's store was located near the palace of the vizier, and the vizier's servants used to come to ask Samuel to write their reports for them. The vizier was deeply impressed with the reports which his servants sent him, and when he found out who the writer was, he made him his personal adviser and secretary. Later, on his death-bed, the vizier advised the king to appoint Samuel to his post, saying that he found him to be the finest statesman in the kingdom. 

Thus Sameul became vizier of Granada, and so fairly did he treat both Jews and Arabs that even Arab poets testified to his wise rule.

Other Jews also held important offices in Arab countries. 

Even in Christian Spain a Jewish physician, Amram, was put in charge of tax collections in spite of the objections of the pope. 

But the Jews were not entirely happy in their public positions. For example, Hasdai heard of a Jewish kingdom in the land of the Khazars somewhere in Russia. A Russian tribe called the Khazars had embraced Judaism and was living as an independent state in Southern Russia. Accordingly, anxious to find out all about the kingdom, he wrote a long letter to the king of the Khazars, In the letter Hasdai told him how prosperous the Jews were in Spain, how they owned houses, fields and vineyards, and occupied important public offices. Yet, he declared, he

would gladly give up his post as finance minister for the lowest office in the Jewish kingdom. Unfortunately, by the time the answer reached Hasdai inviting him to come to the land of the Khazars, the Jewish state was already destroyed by the Russians.

As for Samuel ibn Nagdela, he left his office to his son, so highly was he respected. But his son, because he put on princely airs, was murdered by the Arabs in his own palace, and with him perished 4,000 Jews. Thus we see again that the age was not quite as golden as we might have desired.

JEWS OF FRANCE AND GERMANY 

Similarly a new golden age began for the Jews of France and Germany. Whether any of the original Jews remained in 

France we do not know. But we do know that a new group of 

Jews came to that country while Southern France was in Mohammedan hands. The famous Jewish Radanite merchants, who traveled throughout Europe and Asia and knew all the languages of the various countries, visited France and Germany as their terminals. These were especially invited by Charlemagne and his successors to come and trade there. At the time the country was largely barbarous, and it was mainly the Jewish merchants who brought some little of the civilization of the East to the rude West. Since no Mohammedans were admitted into France or 

Germany and few Christians were as yet engaged in trade, the Jews had an especially large share of the foreign commerce. 

They brought spices, rare cloths and tapestries, and gold and silver work in exchange for tin, furs, weapons, and slaves. 

They also dealt in wine, grain, and oil. So influential were the Jews that in the city of Lyons they even had the market day changed from Saturday to some other day of the week in order to make it more convenient for them to trade. Tolls collected from them equaled those Christians paid. In spite of 

Church decrees, the Jews were permitted to own Christian slaves and to employ Christian house workers.

There was a wide difference however between the position of the Jews in Spain and those in France or Germany. The Jews of 

Spain were citizens of their country whereas the Jews of France or Germany were not. The Jews of those countries were altogether without rights. They enjoyed only those privileges which the kings or barons gave them. At the time, the feudal system was in effect in Europe. In accordance with the feudal system all land belonged to the king. The king gave it to his barons and in return the barons were the king's men and were obliged to render him military service. The barons divided their land among their knights who in turn were responsible to give military service to the barons and were the barons' men. The knights in turn divided the land among the serfs who were obliged to perform military service for the knights, The majority of the people were serfs. Serfs were partial slaves who could not be sold without their land, but had no right to leave their land.

The Jews were not generally expected to give military service and therefore did not fit into the feudal system. Besides, everyone in the feudal system took an oath to serve his superior on the true faith of a Christian. The Jews not being 

Christians naturally could not take such an oath. However, the 

Jews were considered the men of the king or of their barons. 

Their position was somewhat like that of the knights. 

Thus anyone injuring a Jew was responsible as for injury to a knight. 

Even though they were not offically responsible for military service, the Jews did fight at times in defense of their own homes. And Jews were to be found in the armies of the various 

European nations. They also owned fields and vineyards, thoughí the Church, for various reasons which we shall see presently, tried to deprive them of their landed property. 

The Jews, we said, had no rights except such as they were granted by their rulers. The kings at thisttime gave the Jews a charter of broad privileges. The charter protected Jewish life and honor. It granted them the right to own their own possessions, to trade freely and to use Christian servants and slaves. The Jews were excused from paying special taxes; and they were given the right to govern themselves and to have their own law courts.

WERE FRIENDLY WITH LOCAL POPULATION 

The Jews dealt mainly with the upper classes, with the king and the nobility. They were friendly with them and visited their homes, and the nobility in turn visited the Jews at home and at synagogue, enjoying the services. Proud ladies had the rabbis bless them. Jews were appointed collectors of taxes and stewards both for the central government as well as for the various nobles. As they were permitted to set up their own government and their own law courts, they were free of French courts and could neither be flogged nor tried by the ordeal of fire or water. Otherwise they lived like other Frenchmen. They spoke French, dressed as the French did - indeed, they lived scattered throughout all the towns and villages of France.

Throughout this period the Jews were permitted to build synagogues freely, and their sermons attracted many Christian listeners. They enjoyed the confidence of the royal court because they contributed a large share of the income of the state. 

MONEY LENDERS Of- 

The Jews soon came to perform another useful service. ten, instead of sending men to fight for the king, the nobles preferred to pay the king in cash to hire an army in their place. Thus they needed ready money, but they had little of it, since most trade was carried on by barter. Thus a shoemaker might exchange a pair of shoes for a sack of potatoes,

* a carpenter a wooden pail for a hen or eggs. Only the Jews, who carried on larger transactions, had ready money, and the nobles were very anxious to borrow it. Thus arose the business of Jewish money-lending. At first the monasteries used to lend money to the nobles, but the monasteries were later forbidden by their Church laws from charging interest, or, as the Church called it, usury. The kings and nobles encouraged the 

Jews in their money-lending business, taking a considerable share of the profits for themselves. As a result of making loans, the Jews came into possession of many forfeits. Houses, fields, gold and silver articles, even Church vessels thus came into Jewish possession. The notes were written in Hebrew and dated in accordance with the Jewish way of reckoning time (supposedly from the date of the creation of the world). However, as the Jews were to find out soon, money-lenders are always hated, and they had to bear all the hatred, while the kings and nobles pocketed the gains. 1. 

QUESTIONS 

How did the Jews fare in Europe during the Middle Ages? 

What proof have we of the facts? 

2. To what extent were the Jews of Rome part of the general population and to what extent were they self-governing? 

How were their fortunes affected by the coming of the barbarians? 3. 4. 5.

What happened to the early settlement of the Jews in Spain? 

What to that of France and Germany?

What brought about a renewal of Jewish life in Spain? 

In what respects was it a Golden Age? 

Was there anything to darken the picture? 

How did the Jews of that time live in France and Germany?

The fact that the Jews possessed ready cash does not mean that they were the richest people at that time. Wealth in those days was measured by the landed property which a person owned and the Jews owned little land on the whole.

V 

JEWS UNDER ATTACK 

Two factors operated against the Jews - the Church and the townsmen. The Church had a long list of grievances against the 

Jews. Judaism, as we saw, was a permitted religion, but we also saw why Judaism was permitted. The Jews were to serve as an example of the 111-fortune which overtakes a people that "denies God. The Jews were to wear the brand of Cain among the nations of the world. Yet instead, the Jews prospered and seemed to be more wealthy than the average Christian, or so at least the Church claimed. Many a Jew ruled over Christians, holding important public office or employing Christian servants. These latter were forced to rest on Jewish holidays rather than on Christian rest days. Jews also traded freely in slaves and kings forbade the Church to set a slave free merely by his acceptance of Christiantiy; a slave had to be paid for if the Church would set him free.

Christians paid a tithe, that is, a tenth of their produce or a tenth of the value of their land, to the Church, When a Jew bought the same land he no longer was obligated to pay a tithe and so the Church was deprived of revenue. The Church therefore objected violently and insisted that Jews should also pay the tithe.

Furthermore the Church was afraid of Jewish conversionist influence. Jewish-Christian friendship was very common in the early days. Christians ate in Jewish homes and made merry at 

Jewish parties. We have already mentioned the attraction of 

Jewish preachers for leading Christians. Besides, the latter respected the Jews as authorities on the Old Testament. Jewish arguments in support of their religion sounded more reasonable than Christian arguments against it. Thus, even though it was always much more difficult to be a Jew than a Christian, there was in London a synagogue of twenty-two converts to Judaism who were all unfortunately exterminated in 1189. Converts were also to be found in Germany, France, and Poland. In addition to outright converts anyone who differed from the official Church was accused of Judaizing, especially if he was influenced by the Old Testament. 11 The Church therefore preached against the Jews with violence and abuse. A Church writer declared that Jews should be avoided "like a common pestilence and a disease of the entire world, since they are more savage than wolves, Continued anti-Jewish preaching over several centuries was bound to inflame the common people against the Jews, and prepared the ground for the frightful massacres which were soon to take place. 

THE TOWNSMEN AND THE JEWS 

The other factor which undermined the Jewish position was the townsmen. When the Jews first came to Europe in the 700's there were very few towns and these were merely overgrown.

grew. villages. There were therefore few merchants other than the Jews.

But during the next 200 years trade developed and cities The Italian cities especially entered into competition with the Jews and, since they were Christians, naturally had the advantage. In 945 a law was passed in Venice forbidding 

Jewish merchandise to be carried in Venetian ships. At about the year 1000, the Magyars of Hungary became converted to Christianity. A new all-land route to the East was thus opened to the Christians who could not formerly enter that country. The 

Jews were thus slowly being pushed out of international trade. 

More and more they turned to trading inland, in their own countries. They helped to develop the new towns and they pioneered in many a trading venture. But here again, a native trading class was developing which sought to push the Jews out of their positions. The native merchants organized guilds which controlled all the trade in a given town or in a larger territory. 

Other guilds were formed of handworkers who decided upon all matters affecting their trade, including who might and who might not work. But in most cases the guilds admitted Christians only and thus many a source of livelihood was cut off from the Jews. The Jews still persisted in trade wherever they could, but more and more they had to turn to money-lending. In this field too the Jews soon encountered competition. Many 

Christians disregarded the laws against usury and engaged in prohibited banking and usury. Christian usurers, as a rule, took higher interest than Jews did, but the Jews bore the brunt of the hatred entertained for money-lenders, as we shall see presently.

THE CRUSADES 

The policy of the Church regarding the Jews was to be as oppressive as possible, short of taking their lives. But when abuse was piled upon abuse and the Jew was constantly pictured as the enemy of mankind, it was difficult to expect of either the lower clergy or the peasants that they control themselves. 

Nevertheless, so high was the respect in which the Jews were held, that they were left in peace for several hundred years. 

Then something dramatic happened in Europe which took the Christian population out of its accustomed grooves and turned it against the Jews. This dramatic happening was the Crusades.

The Crusades were wars waged by the Christians of Europe against the Turks in Palestine. The pope himself called upon all the Christians of Europe to wrest Palestine from the control of the infidel Turks. The reasons given for the Crusades were many the immediate cause is said to have been the complaint of Christian pilgrims that they had been mistreated by the Moslems, masters of the Holy City of Jerusalem. Since going on pilgrimages to the Holy Sepulchre was becoming increasingly popular, the opportunities for trouble were numerous. 

---

f 1. CNC C L. ;'י'

Re- In a larger way the Crusades were a struggle to determine whether Christians or Moslems should control the Mediterranean Sea.

The Byzantine Empire had called upon the Christians of the West for aid against the Turks in 1074. At that time the call had remained unanswered but now (1095) the pope decided to respond. Many went to war out of religious motives. ligion being the most important single interest of the time, it was not strange that men should fight over it. The Christian knights had fought the Mohammedans in Spain and Sicily and having recently been defeated in Spain, sought for an opportunity to avenge themselves. Others, mainly the citizens of the Italian cities, were concerned over their trade with which the Turks were interfering. The Italians wanted to free Palestine from the Turks in the interests of their business, but they thought they could accomplish their purpose better if they declared a holy war against them. There were still other reasons. Some knights had estates that were too small and they hoped to carve out larger territories at the expense of the Turks. Large sections of Europe were too thickly populated and the many people who could not earn a living sought new homes. The Church promised that everyone who took up the Cross and went to fight the Turks would have all his sins forgiven. 

JEWS FIRST VICTIMS 

The great enthusiasm created for the undertaking led to the formation of bands which had no leaders, nor equipment, nor provisions and did not know where they were headed. At first these bands began to ravage the countryside for supplies, and many a battle was fought between them and the local population in which hundreds of Crusaders lost their lives. However, the 

Crusaders discovered the defenseless element in the population, the Jews. They were supposedly rich and at the same time enemies of the Church. Why, said they, shall we go to seek nonbelievers in distant Palestine when we have them right here at hand? They attempted to attack the Jews in France but were driven back by the nobles on whose lands the Jews lived. But in Germany the situation was different. There no strong central government existed, and the individual cities where the Jews lived were ruled by bishops who were either unable or unwilling to defend them.

Thus tragedies were enacted which have remained for all time examples of Jewish heroism and martyrdom. In the city of Worms eight-hundred Jews permitted themselves to be slain rather than accept baptism. The Jews of Mayence paid the archbishop for protection, but at the last minute he failed them and turned them over to the Crusaders. In despair mothers slew their own children and old men in taleisim sat quietly, ready to die for the holy name. They even refused to listen to the suggestion that they accept conversion, and all were slain. The same thing happened in Speyer. A mob broke into the synagogue and demanded of the worshipers that they accept conversion. On refusing, the whole assembly was murdered. 

The rest of the Jews hid in the bishop's palace where for-

10: A 1 7. I. 533 £ 1 9194 095 mg 6,65

3

tunately they were able to defend themselves. At Cologne, on the other hand, the Jews hid for three weeks in a cemetery; then they were discovered and murdered.

The tale of martyrdom of those days is too tragic to recount. Many Jews threw themselves into the flaming synagogues. 

Women tied stones around their necks and threw themselves into the rivers to drown rather than to fall into the hands of the Crusaders. Some few, to save themselves, accepted conversion but hastened to return to Judaism when the danger was past. Altogether between four and twelve thousand persons lost their lives. 

The first Crusade was only one of several that were to blight the life of the Jews. Fifty years later the second Crusade was preached, and once more feeling was incited against the Jews. Again some Jews were slain, mainly in France. This time the German Jews paid heavily for protection and were secure behind strong castle walls, but traveling merchants found on the roads were brutally killed. The third Crusade passed peaceably in France and Germany but brought destruction in England. 

CHANGED FEELING TOWARD JEWS 

Much worse, however, than the actual deaths which the Jews suffered, was the change of feeling on the part of the Christians toward the Jews. Until that time, the Christians, though they might have disliked the Jews, nevertheless respected them. Now, to excuse their own murderous acts, the Christian population began to accuse the Jews of being guilty of the fate which overtook them. They claimed the Jews were practicing witchcraft, something of course, which is sheer nonsense, but which people believed in those days. Jews were also accused of killing Christian children to use their blood for the Passover, even though Jews were forbidden to use even the blood of animals. This accusation is known as the "blood libel. Christians themselves had been accused of that in the early days of Rome, but now they forgot they were the sufferers from this accusation and fastened it on the Jews. Thus in the city of Blois, in France, a servant of the lord of the manor, going out to water his horses, was frightened by the white garments of a passing Jew. At once he reported that the Jews had cast a dead Christian body into the water, and, although there was no proof of the fact, twenty-three leading Jews were executed for this supposed crime. 

Another accusation trumped up against the Jews was that they stabbed the host. The host is a little cake which the Christians eat as a part of their service. The legend had it that when the host, which was supposed to represent the body of Jesus, was stabbed, blood spurted from it. The legend of the stabbed host caused many a Jew to lose his life during this dark time. 

The Jews were still important in the community. 

In many places they were still indispensable as merchants and handworkers, and theirs were still the precious silk, the most
