beautiful dyes, and the manufactured objects of gold and silver.

But Christian workers were rapidly learning these trades, and as they learned them they formed guilds or societies, which as we saw excluded the Jews. In certain towns they were barred from trading altogether.

Even money-lending declined in importance. 

The rich moneylending business, which involved large sums, was taken over by the Italian money-lenders, and the Jews were left to make loans to the poor on pledges. These began to hate the Jews the more when they could not repay, and thus Jew hatred grew.

And now, in 1215, the Church aimed its cruelest blow at the Jews: It ordered that the Jews wear a badge on their garments to mark each Jewish man and woman from the rest of the population. 

Every ruffian could now tell the Jew apart from the non-Jew and could fall upon him in the streets. For some time the Jews tried to fight off the badge, but they were forced to wear it in the end.

In addition, the Jewish religious books suffered persecution. The Talmud was publicly burned at the hands of the Church. 

The only protection the Jews enjoyed was from the kings. 

But they, in turn, were greedy and taxed the Jews very heavily. 

The kings especially took a large part of the profits of the loan business. Often they excused Christians from paying their debts if part was paid to the treasury. The king of France at one time had all his Jews arrested and forced them to pay a ransom for their freedom. In 1182 he expelled all the Jews, keeping their homes and lands for the royal treasury. Fortunately, at the time the king ruled only over a small part of the country, the rest being under the control of the nobles, who did not follow his example. But even he found he could not do without the Jews, and sixteen years later he invited them once more to return. Their homes were restored to them, but forever after they lived as on a volcano. 

JEWS IN ENGLAND 

Worst of all was the position of the Jews in England, which was the first country to expel them completely. 

There may have been Jews in England in ancient times, but their real history on that island begins with the Norman conquest in 1066. The Jews came to England as merchants, but the English had early developed trade of their own. They did not want the 

Jews to compete with them, and so the Jews were obliged to enter the money-lending business. This branch of activity was carefully controlled by the king, who took 10 per cent of the profits and so much also in additional taxes that there was not very much left for the Jews.

So long as the Jews could engage freely in business, they lived happily in England, building themselves houses of stone

.I J 

Mi pentr bevourit

33 1.4.5

for comfort and protection.

But the more they engaged in moneylending, the more they were hated by the populace as foreigners suffering attacks and blood libel accusations. As king succeeded king, each one tried to press more and more money from the Jews, while at the same time the Jews were forbidden to trade in order to earn money.

The Jews complained bitterly and asked to be permitted to leave England, but this permission was not granted. A new king came to the throne who tried to reform the lot of the Jews. He ordered that the Jews engage in trade and handicrafts or that they farm land for ten years. But the guilds would let the Jews neither trade nor work. As for farming on rented lands, that did not pay because all the improvements made would be lost when they had to return the land to its owners. Finally, after a struggle of about two hundred years, England decided to expel its Jews. The Jews, on their part, were glad to leave the hated country behind them and to return for the most part to France whence they came.

Why were the Crusades fought and how did they affect the Jews What especially was the effect of the Crusades on the Jews after they were over? 

3. What briefly is the story of the Jews of England?

VI 

JEWISH SELF-GOVERNMENT 

Today in most countries Jews are considered citizens of the lands in which they live. They share all the rights and privileges of citizenship and are governed by the laws of their countries. In Christian Europe of the Middle Ages even under the best of conditions the Jews were not citizens of their respective states. Since the Jews were regarded as strangers it was natural that they should be governed by their own law. Thus the Jews formed a separate community, a city within a city. The Jews had a special city council and separate law enforcement officers. 

These officers were elected - if not as democratically as today, nevertheless with some measure of democracy for their own time. 

There was no Jewish body in any country which had authority over the entire Jewish population except in Poland, of which we will tell presently. Each city community formed an independent unit together with the surrounding villages. This was due largely to the fact that the governments wanted each community to be responsible for its taxes as a unit. Thus every community was organized into a Kahal, as it was called in Hebrew, or aljama in Spanish, membership in which was compulsory for every Jew. Each 

Kahal received its special charter from the king, giving it the right to appoint its own law enforcement officers and to establish its own system of courts, tax assessors, education, and other agencies of government.

While the organization was democratic, it was often under the influence of men of wealth and learning. 

In some cities the poor struggled against the rich and won representation on the community council, although in the majority of cases the possession of property was required for the right of voting.

At the head of the city organizations stood the b'rurim (the chosen ones), who were usually elected for one year, from one Rosh Ha-shono to the next. The number varied from one to twelve, according to the size of the town. In addition, some communities, especially in Spain, had a council of elders consisting of from forty to fifty members. At times, when very important matters had to be decided, a meeting might be called of the entire community. Other officers included tax gatherers, censors, judges, supervisors of weights and measures, police, inspectors of buildings, streets, bridges, walls and gates, and synagogue officials.

The elders passed laws on many matters, including such questions as marriage and divorce, prohibitions against gambling or dancing, and deciding what dress or ornaments Jews should wear in order not to appear to be showing off their wealth before non- Jews. The council also regulated the rents to be paid by Jews in rented houses, laws designed to keep Christians from raising the rent of the Jews. The council's tax assessors divided among individual Jews the amount of taxes the Jews had to pay to the government as well as to the Jewish community.

NOAGLUS

In New laws were passed by the b'rurim and the council. most places a majority was sufficient to pass a law, though there were cities which required agreement by the entire council. On the Sabbath the chazan read the new law in the synagogue, and it then became binding.

The possession of their own courts was a great boon to the Jews, for it enabled them to keep their personal affairs private. 

No Jew was allowed to bring a case against a fellow Jew before a non-Jewish court. Another advantage which Jews enjoyed in their courts was the speed with which their cases were handled. 

Government courts might take several years to settle a case. 

Large amounts had to be spent on lawyers, and judges were not beneath accepting bribes. The Jewish courts permitted no lawyers, and often even the judges were men drawn from the community. 

CENSORS 

Each community had a censor who punished those who overstepped the moral or religious law of the community. The censors were often quite severe. The record tells of a man punished because work was done on his house during the Sabbath, though he claimed he knew nothing about it. 

SUPERVISORS AND JUDGES 

The supervisors of weights and measures also fixed the price of food, especially of meat and wine. The charity supervisors gathered the charity funds and often had control also over schools and teachers. As for the judges of the courts, they could try cases where money damages were to be awarded or could punish crimes by flogging, imprisonment, fines, the ban of excommunication, and at times in Spain even by the death penalty. 

The death penalty was especially employed against reformers who told of private happenings in the Jewish community to the Church or to the government. The informer was like a traitor who exposed the innermost secrets of his people for gain or to satisfy a personal grudge. In an age, for example, when there were forced converts to Christianity, the information that such a convert was secretly observing Judaism might cost him his life. 

Or the informer might tell the king that one of the Jews was very wealthy and the king might employ torture to extract extra money from him. Even kings consented to the death penalty for informers. Otherwise crimes of murder or robbery were very rare among Jews, though they were quite frequent in the rest of the population. The judges mostly served without pay, it being considered a great honor. Even non-Jews at times made use of the Jewish courts, consenting in advance to be bound by the decisions.

JEWISH INSTITUTIONS 

The principal institutions of the community consisted of the synagogue, which was, of course, the center of Jewish life; shelters for poor wayfarers; the community bake-house, where

matsos were baked for the Passover and the Sabbath dinner was kept during Friday night and Sabbath morning; the wedding hall, where wedding dances took place for a week at a time; a public bathhouse which enabled the Jews to preserve their cleanliness everywhere; and, of course, their own cemeteries. 

THE SYNAGOGUE 

The synagogue was primarily a place of prayer and study, but it filled every public purpose. In it announcements were made both for the general government as well as for the Jewish selfgoverning institutions. All new laws especially were announced in the synagogue. In addition there was a special custom whereby a person wronged might interrupt the service and ask that Justice be done him before letting the service continue. All sales of land were first announced in the synagogue. Charity was administered there, and the poor wayfarers were invited to homes through the synagogue. And of course all happy and sad family events, births, Bar Mitzvahs, marriages, or deaths, were announced or celebrated there.

The synagogues were taller and finer than other buildings,. the Spanish synagogues being especially beautiful. These were built with tall pillars, carved capitals and Moorish arches. 

Many gilded inscriptions adorned the walls and columns. They might not, however, be taller than the church and often, in order to give them sufficient height, they were built below the street level. They had floors, which homes generally did not have, and the walls were decorated with pictures of birds and other animals. The synagogues were lighted by hundreds of candles, gifts of pious worshippers. Men of wealth donated gold and silver ornaments to the synagogue, while pious women embroidered curtains for the ark. When it was impossible to obtain permission to build new synagogues, rich Jews established them in private homes.

Every Jewish man and boy of Bar Mitzvah age was expected to attend services daily. However they did not always live up to expectations. In some places men absenting themselves from services were fined. Elsewhere ten free men were engaged to be always at hand for a minyon. While the Torah was read the congregation remained standing. Some synagogues had as yet no Torah scrolls and were obliged to read the weekly portion from a Bible. 

Some men went up to the Torah bareheaded. On certain fast days they were barefoot. Women usually sat in a balcony separate from the men, and there were even some places where the women were housed in an adjoining building. 

The Spanish Jews were not as faithful in religious observance as their brethren of Germany. Their synagogue attendance was not as regular, and excommunication had to be threatened against those absenting themselves regularly from services or against those leaving in the middle of the services.

་ ས་ ག 3735

The seats in the synagogue were privately owned, and might be rented, bought, or sold. When a father died each of his sons owned part of his seat, and a way had to be found of settling the ownership of it. Since each person owned his own seat, it was left to him to provide his own seating, and it was not uncommon to find different types of chairs, benches, or divans, in accordance with the individual taste of each worshiper. 

Small children in Aragon sat on stones at their parents' feet. 

In Barcelona they sat on mats on the: floor. 

The courtyard of the synagogue was laid out as a park, so that people could sit there and chat before and after services. 

Before and after services many men, handworkers and business men attended the daily lessons in Mishnah, Talmud, and other studies given in the beis ha-midrosh, or house of study. 

The Jews were forbidden to build any new synagogues, and, while they might repair old ones which needed repairs, they might not decorate them any more beautifully than they were originally. However, with the aid of the king, the Jews managed to get around these limitations.

CHARITABLE ACTIVITIES 

The charity activities which were carried on in the syna gogue were in the hands of two supervisors. These gathered the regular contributions, called the kupo, from which the poor received a weekly sum, and an emergency fund for immediate needs, called the tamchui. A fund called k'sus provided clothes for the needy. Besides, there were the usual Jewish charities for the poor, the sick, the orphaned, etc.

Great care was taken to watch over the self-respect of the poor, So secretly was Jewish charity carried on that for a time it seemed that there were no Jewish poor at all. Wherever possible, families were helped to become self-supporting, so that they would not need charity any more. 

The members of the community engaged in such charitable undertakings as visiting the sick, burying the dead, making free loans to those in need, caring for old people, and collecting money for Palestine. One of the most popular public charities was the redemption of captives, which was a form of insurance for many members of the community. Charity was also distributed to the poor among the non-Jews. All of these activities were carried on through the synagogue because the Jewish people felt that by doing them they were carrying out the will of God, whether it was praying to Him, or announcing new laws which would make His justice rule, or giving charity, which would make His spirit of love and helpfulness prevail. 

ELEMENTARY EDUCATION 

Since there were no public schools, schools were private, and the teachers were paid directly by the parents. Ignorance,

L IL 誓 ​IN 1 X 1.3 རྞྞ * ༄། * ,,,",

especially among boys, was almost unheard of, poor children 

Girls were being provided an education from charity funds. taught only to read their prayers. A town having as few as fifteen families had to engage an elementary teacher, or it was threatened with excommunication. A city with as many as forty families was obliged to appoint a teacher for higher studies.

At the age of five a ceremony took place of bringing the boy to school. Usually on Shovuos the father wrapped his son in a talis and brought him to the synagogue to listen to the Ten Commandments which are read in the synagogue on that morning. Later, in school, the teacher held up a slate on which were letters of the Hebrew alphabet, as well as honey. 

The child repeated the letters and licked the honey from the tablet as a sign of the sweetness of the Torah. He was also given cake, an egg, and other goodies to make the study of the Torah sweet and pleasant.

Schools and teachers, though mainly private, were under strict control. The program of studies was somewhat different in Spain than in France and Germany. In the latter countries the child was expected to learn to read in three months. Then he began studying the Bible, which he continued until the age of ten. For the next three years he devoted himself to the books of the Mishnah, and at thirteen he commenced the study of the Talmud. 

Here too, the 

After mastering

In Spain the program was somewhat wider. child began school at the age of five or six. the alphabet and the Torah chant, he began translating the Bible. First he studied the Five Books of Moses, and then the 

Prophets and Writings, At ten he began the study of Mishnah and a little later of grammar and poetry. The study of simple portions of the Talmud was begun at fifteen. A professional student studied philosophy, logic, mathematics, geometry, optics, astronomy, music, mechanics, medicine, and nature study. Such students generally possessed a knowledge of Arabic and Latin. 

In France and Germany studies consisted mostly of religious subjects. Rabbis and other learned men sometimes also studied mathematics and, rarely, astronomy and music. 

Study and teaching were wide-spread, and ignorance of reading and writing was very rare among men. Higher Talmudic schools were established by the community, though they were not free except for the poor. As for higher non-Jewish studies, although small numbers of Jews were admitted to Mohammedan and Christian universities, most students studied them privately. 

Books were very rare, and their owners were urged, and at times forced, to lend them to students needing them.

31.

THE HOME 

The most important institution in Jewish life was the home. 

In the earlier days before ghettos became compulsory for Jews, they lived in houses much like the rest of the population. Many 

Jewish houses were built of stone. They were of two stories, the upper story being reached by a ladder outside of the house. 

After ghettos were established most families lived in large apartment houses, because the ghetto was very crowded, but to make up for their cramped quarters, many had fine gardens and vineyards outside of the city. The houses were built around a courtyard, several houses using one yard. Many of the Jews owned their own homes. The streets in which the houses stood were narrow. Dirt was thrown into the streets, which made the cities unclean and unhealthy. In

In the homes there lived father, mother, and children. 

France and Germany men had but one wife, in accordance with the law as laid down by Rabbi Gershom, "light of the Exile. In 

Spain there were occasional cases where men had more than one wife.

Parents honored and respected one another, while children were expected to be obedient and highly respectful of their parents. A child might never occupy the father's seat, and parents were always permitted to enter and leave the room first. 

Fathers were very anxious to set an example to their children in good moral conduct. The father was generally an upright man who honored his word. The parents also carried on serious table conversations which the children learned to imitate. On 

Sabbaths and holidays the children and parents sang beautiful hymns at the dinner table. Parents also set an example of cleanliness to their children. At a time when the majority of the non-Jewish population bathed only in the summer-time, the 

Jew, even the poorest, visited the bath house weekly in honor of the Sabbath.

As a rule, Jews dressed well and neatly, Even the poor, when they asked for garments, were not questioned, although if they asked for food their cases were inquired into. In general, the Jews dressed in the usual mode of the country. 

They trimmed their beards and cut and parted their hair like the rest of the population.

The medieval Jewish household included servants and slaves. 

The Jewish servants were to be treated as members of the household. On Sabbath and festivals they sat at the table with the rest of the family and enjoyed rest like their masters. There were no Jewish slaves, and the Church forbade the Jews to own Christian slaves. Heathen slaves had to be converted to Judaism within twelve months or else sold.

Slaves had to be treated kindly, for the Jewish religion dictated kindness to all human beings and even to animals. 

Thus Jews were forbidden to take part in tournaments except

when carried on in jest at weddings, nor were they permitted to go hunting or to kill any animal except for food. Household animals were supposed to be fed before the humans. 

Thus did the Jews live during the Middle Ages, masters over their own fate, as far as they were permitted. In their own homes they practiced the nobility of living which has always marked the Jewish way of life. They practiced charity, mercy, and brotherly kindness. Husbands and fathers treated their wives and children with love and tenderness and masters did not abuse their servants and slaves. For they lived in the feeling that all their acts were done in the sight of God, who Himself was a just and loving Father. 1.

VII 

FAMOUS JEWS 

Very many famous Jews crowd the pages of the Middle Ages. 

Great rabbis, lawmakers, philosophers, poets, writers on legal subjects, writers of explanations of the Bible and the Talmud, physicians, astronomers, map-makers, mathematicians, pass before us in imposing array. Every age had its great men who carried forward the torch of learning. In Spain and Southern 

France the Jews were also active together with the Arabs in spreading general European culture.

The earliest seat of Jewish learning during this long period was in Italy. In imitation of the Biblical phrase "for out of 

Zion shall come forth the law and the word of the Lord from Jerusalem" the men of the time substituted "from Bari shall come forth the law and the word of the Lord from Otranto. Indeed it was the Italian rabbis who carried Jewish learning to France and Germany and helped to establish there a center of Jewish learning.

Unfortunately in a short history such as this we can mention only very few men by name, and even these must be told about briefly. We have selected the most famous men to tell about, remembering however that they represent but the smallest fraction of the distinguished personalities of the period. 

RABBI GERSHOM, LAWMAKER 

The first of these men is Rabbi Gershom ben Yehudah (born 960), called the light of the Exile. He was born in the city of Metz and was educated in France. Later he moved to the city of Mayence where he established a famous Talmudical college. 

His claim to fame rests on two things. First of all he knew the Talmud better than any man in Europe in his day and therefore his explanations were eagerly sought by hosts of students. 

Secondly, added to his learning, he had great energy as a lawmaker. Nobody officially appointed him to rule the Jewish community, but his extensive learning won for him the respect of all other rabbis. When, therefore, Rabbi Gershom invited them to an assembly, or a synod, as it is called, they all came. 

At this synod important laws were made for the improvement of Jewish life. Among the laws passed was one forbidding the private carrier of a letter to read its contents. This regulation was very important, because in the days before the post office was established mail was carried by private persons. 

These would often read the letters entrusted to them in order to find out about the writer's affairs. Another important law forbade one Jew to rent a house where another Jew lived without the other's permission. This law was needed to keep Christian owners of homes from raising Jewish rents. The most famous law of Rabenu Gershom was his order of excommunication against anyone who would marry more than one wife. Until that time Jews had been permitted to marry several wives, but from then on all but very few Jews practiced monogamy (having one wife).

RASHI, GREAT TEACHER 

A greater judge and teacher even than Rabenu Gershom was 

Rashi (1040-1105). Both his parents stemmed from a distinguished family of scholars, so that Rashi naturally developed a love for the holy books. His native city having no academy, Rashi went to study at Mayence, at the school established by Rabenu Gershom. 

He was often hungry and in rags, and his wife struggled at home to maintain herself, but he persisted in his studies and became so famous that at twenty-five he was invited to become judge and rabbi of his own native city. The position of rabbi at the time carried no salary. Rashi sold wine to earn his livelihood, but his energies were mainly devoted to his position as rabbi. Questions were sent to him from all corners of France, and from the wealth of his knowledge he answered them. He showed his love for his felow men when he ordered that persons who were forcibly converted to Christianity should be taken back into Judaism without 

A famous teacher of the Talmud died and all his students began flocking to Rashi. Rashi's lectures were so clear and his explanations so satisfying that the students copied his lectures in little books which they spread throughout the Jewish world. 

With the help of these notes students could now study by themselves without a teacher. Rashi's explanations replaced all previous commentaries of the Bible or of the Talmud by the Baby lonian g'onim or by Rabenu Gershom. Soon they became included in every copy of the Bible and Talmud, and every student was expected to study Rashi together with the rest of the text. 

Rashi did not finish his great commentaries, but he left the task to one of his famous grandsons to carry on after him. 

Rashi's life was saddened by attacks on the Jews of Germany during the Crusades, of which he lived to witness the first one. 

MOSES BEN ENOCH 

Rabbi Gershom and Rashi were Franco-German Jews. We now turn to Spain whose great court-Jews were also deeply concerned with the inner life of their own people. Hasdai ibn Shaprut, finance minister of the caliph, generously supported the Babylonian schools. When these seemed to be in danger of closing, he, to whom the people often referred as Nasi and who held a position of leadership similar to that of the Babylonian exilarch, took steps to protect the interests of his fellow-Jews. 

From Babylonia he obtained information on how to fix the calendar, and he was active in the promotion of Jewish learning in Spain. In this endeavor good fortune helped him along. Moses ben Enoch, a learned Babylonian rabbi, had sailed from Bari, Italy, bound for Spain. His ship was overtaken by pirates, and 

Moses and his family were made prisoners. Moses' wife committed suicide, while he and his young son were brought to Spain as poor captives. The pirates, not knowing that he was a famous rabbi, permitted the Jews to redeem him for the price of an

f 

hot 05 126

​Cop C.

ordinary slave. Moses naturally found his way to the house of study. The head of the school was explaining something incorrectly, and Moses ben Enoch offered to help him with the correct interpretation of the law. Recognizing at once that before him was a man of superior learning, the head of the school resigned his post in favor of the newcomer. Moses was thus chosen the head of the college, which now became the equal of those of Babylonia,

Hasdai also had Hebrew poets in his court who wrote many lovely poems and who helped to discover the rules of Hebrew grammar, which they needed in order to know how to write their poetry.

Moses ben Enoch was followed as head of the academy by his son, Enoch, but after Enoch's death Cordova was sacked by enemies, and the students were scattered. But Jewish learning moved to 

Granada, where Samuel ibn Nagdela, the great statesman, was also a poet and a Talmud scholar.

SOLOMON IBN GABIROL 

Far greater than Samuel as poet and philosopher, was Solomon ibn Gabirol. His parents had fled from Cordova in 1013, and he was born in Malaga in 1020, Early, he was left an orphan, and, perhaps because of the poor care which he received, he developed a serious disease which remained with him throughout his short life. As a sickly lad he began to write poetry, at the age of sixteen. Various persons tried to befriend him, among them 

Samuel ibn Nagdela, the great statesman, but he quarreled with

Samuel because the latter did not like his poetry. Another of 

Gabirol's benefactors was killed. Thus the poet struggled all his life to gain a livelihood. But he wrote deeply religious poems, many of which today are found in the prayers, especially of the S'fardim. He also wrote a famous philosophical work called Fons Vitae, the Fountain of Life, which the Christians studied for many years in the belief it was written by one of their own monks. Only in the nineteenth century was it discovered that it was written by Gabirol. 

There were many other distinguished writers and poets in Spain. We can only pause to mention the greatest Judah Halevi and Maimonides.

JUDAH HALEVI 

The most gifted poet, who ranks with the writers of the Bible in the greatness of his poetry, was Judah Halevi (10861145). He himself had few personal sorrows. He had loving parents who gave him a good education in the Talmud, Bible, and Arabic, so that he wrote Arabic as fluently as Hebrew. By profession Judah Halevi was a physician, and as such he enjoyed a wide practice. But at heart and in his soul he was a poet. 

Early he began writing songs for various occasions, joyous songs

.} 179

of love and friendship.

But he had one deep sorrow, his concern over the fate of Palestine. He had a strong yearning to live in 

Palestine, to be restored to the holy land of his fathers. Many touching poems bemoan the fact that he is in the West while his heart is in the East. Edom, the Christians, and Ishmael, the 

Arabs, are laying the land waste, while Israel stands helplessly by.

He had reference, of course, to the Crusades which were then being fought in Palestine. The one hope of his life was to be able to go to Palestine in person and spend his last days there. 

In addition to writing poetry, Halevi also wrote a book of philosophy. He tried to explain the fate of his people, which, unlike other peoples, did not seem to prosper. Why should the 

Jews suffer more than all the other people of the world? This book is written in the form of a dialogue. The king of the 

Khazars sees a vision in which God tells him that He is pleased with his thoughts but not with his acts. The king thereupon awakes and determines to seek a new religion which will be more pleasing to God. First he invites the Greek philosophers, but they fail to satisfy him. Then he consults the philosophers of the two leading religions, the Christian and the Mohammedan, but both of them describe their religion as originating in the Jewish. Thus, although he did not intend to invite the Jews, he now finds that he must ask them, since theirs seems to be the original religion. Judah Halevi then proceeds to explain the 

Jewish religion. He shows that the Jews are a chosen people gifted with prophecy. The Jews are the heart of the world beating for all of mankind, so that if anything hurts the world, the Jews feel it first. Though the Jews are poor and lowly, so were the leaders of the other great religions. However, a bright future awaits the Jews, to which the poet confidently looks forward.

After completing the Khuzari, Judah, more troubled than ever by his longings for Palestine, determined to go to the country of his dreams. He left his daughter and much beloved grandson as well as an academy of eager students and a host of patients. His journey through Spain was like a triumphal march. 

In Egypt a distinguished committee met him, showered honors upon him, and detained him for a few months. They were eager that he remain with them, but he refused. Finally he arrived in Palestine, and there the story of his wanderings suddenly is hidden in darkness. Legend tells that a Moslem knight rode over him with his horse as he was bowing down to kiss the soil of Jerusalem, but the story is doubtful, since Jerusalem at the time was in Christian hands.

MOSES MA IMON IDES 

The greatest Jew of the period was Moses Maimonides (11351204). Maimonides was the greatest scholar of his age. He was a distinguished doctor, a great writer of philosophy, and withal a humble, modest man. His father, the dayan (judge) of

Cordova, had taught his son Torah and, in addition, astronomy and mathematics. He also engaged private teachers for him in nature study and philosophy. So bright and gifted was the young man that at twenty-three he already had written a distinguished book on the calendar.

At thirteen, Moses, together with his family, was forced to leave his home because of the fanatical Arab rulers, who demanded of the Jews conversion or death. The family first wandered through Christian Spain and finally settled in Fez, North Africa. Outwardly they pretended to be Mohammedans, but in practice they were pious Jews. Tired of having to hide their religion, the family sailed for Palestine, but not being able to establish themselves there, they left for Egypt. On the way his father died. Maimonides' brother went into business, sellin jewels and precious stones, to support the family, but he was drowned in a storm in the Indian Ocean. Moses became ill with grief, for a long time hovering between life and death. On his recovery, he began the practice of medicine for a living. At the same time, he started on his great explanation of the Jewish law of the Mishnah. 

Soon Maimonides became the physician to the vizier of Egypt. 

But as his reputation for great learning spread, religious questions were addressed to him from all over the world, and 

Maimonides answered them all with great friendliness and liberality. Toward the Karaites, for example, he was particularly well disposed. He permitted visiting with them, taking part in their celebrations, comforting their mourners, all except praying with them. At the same time, Maimonides completed a great book called Mishneh Torah, or Yad Ha-chazoko, in which were gathered and organized all the Jewish laws which had been compiled during the period of the Mishnah and the Talmud down to his own day. This book made him very famous, and the Jews of Egypt declared him the head of their community. As a physician, too, he was so highly regarded that Richard the Lion- Hearted of England asked him to come to London with him, but he refused.

The most distinguished of Maimonides' books is one called Guide to the Perplexed. It was written in Arabic. This important work sets out to prove that the Torah and the Greek philosophy which was generally accepted at that time are in complete agreement. The book made the author famous among the 

Jews, Christians, and Arabs. It was soon translated into Hebrew and hailed as one of the greatest works of all times. 

Maimonides also wrote a number of books on medical subjects. 

His constant labors at the sultan's court and at home with throngs of private patients, and in addition, all his scholarly work, told heavily on him. His body was old and wasted by disease and overwork, and he died at the age of seventy. So highly was he regarded that it was said of him that between Moses (the lawgiver and Moses (Maimonides) there was none like unto Moses.

---

JEWS IN OTHER BRANCHES OF LEARNING 

Not only did the Jews of the period enrich Jewish learning and culture, but they contributed much to the general culture of Europe. Europe at the time was living through its dark ages. 

Ignorance was wide-spread even among the clergy and few men knew anything of the rich civilization of the ancients which had developed in Greece. Only the Arabic world knew and made use of it, especially in Spain. There among Arabic scholars the ancient learning of Greece once more found a home and through the avenue of the Arabs was brought back to civilize Europe. 

But few Arabs knew Greek and fewer knew Latin. How therefore could the works of the Greeks reach the Arabs and how, later, could the Arabs influence the Christian world? Here the 

Jews came into play. The Jews, scattered as they were throughout the world, spoke the various languages and at the same time knew Hebrew. A book translated into Hebrew from a country where the Jews spoke Greek could be retranslated into Arabic, and late into Latin. The Jews thus translated hundreds of books from 

Arabic or Hebrew into Latin. Among them were great philosophical works of Aristotle as well as Arabic explanations of them. 

Kalilah V'dimnah, Indian fairy tales, were first brought by Jewish translators to Arabic countries and later translated from the Arabic into the languages of European countries. 

There were three main centers of translation - one in Toledo, Spain, another in Naples, Italy, and the third in a Provence between France and Spain. There lived the Ibn Tibbon family, famous translators of philosophical works into Hebrew. 

When Alphonso the Wise, though not overfriendly to the Jews, wanted many important Arabic books translated into Spanish and later into Latin he secured his Jewish physician, together with other Jews, to translate them for him. Similarly when the king of Naples wanted some of the most important Arabic works translated, he invited Jews for the purpose. The work of translation was carried on under the patronage of kings and archbishops; so important was it for the future of Europe. 

The Jews not merely translated the works of others but wrote many original books even in general philosophy. We need only recall such writers as ibn Gabirol or Maimonides, to cite only two examples of a long list of great philosophers.

In medicine too, the Jews contributed both as translators and as original writers. Over fifty known writers wrote on medical themes, thus laying the foundation for modern medicine. 

Jews contributed considerably to the science of astronomy and especially to navigation. All important astronomical tables used by navigators were either translated or compiled with Jewish aid. The quadrant, an instrument by which mariners measure the distance from the sun, was improved by a Jew, Jacob ben Makhi;

and was later perfected by another Jew, Rabbi Levi ben Gershom. 

Known as Jacob's staff, it was used by all explorers, including Christopher Columbus, and was not displaced till 1734. Similarly, the astrolabe, an instrument to measure the position of the stars, was improved considerably by Jews, especially by Abraham Zacuto, who prepared an astrolabe for Vasco de Gama on his trip around the Cape of Good Hope.

Jews from the island of Majorca were famous map-makers, Abraham Crescas, head of the famous family, made a beautiful map of the world for King Juan of Aragon which is to this day treasured in the national library of France. His son was later the head of the nautical observatory of Portugal which equipped the expedition that sailed around the Cape of Good Hope in quest of a sea-route to India.

Jews contributed richly to the development of mathematics, the basis of all modern science. Until that time numbers were written clumsily in Roman letters. To write a large number was in itself an achievement and to multiply or divide numbers of any magnitude was almost an impossibility. The Jews had brought our present numbers originally from India where they were used many centuries ago. They also brought the use of the zero without which our entire number system would be valueless. The Jews then introduced these numbers from the Arabic into the Latin. 

John of Seville translated the first book of Persian arithmetic into Latin. Later another author translated Indian geometry. 

Thus the foundation was laid for the vast structure of European mathematics. 

Thus we see how the Jews contributed to the general knowledge of Europe. What we have listed is by no means complete. 

There were Jewish writers, philosophers, scientists and explorers whom we cannot mention. We merely want to give some idea of the extent to which Jews took part in the cultural life of Europe. 1.

VIII END OF SECOND PERIOD OF EUROPEAN JEWISH LIFE 

We are now approaching the end of the second period of Jewish life in Europe. The last half of the 1200's was a very un→ happy period for the Jews of France. Kings were greedy and superstitious, charging Jews with blood libels and crimes of stabbing the host. In 1306 one of the kings ordered all Jewish debts to be recorded, and knowing who owed money to the Jews, he decreed that all the Jews be expelled within one month in order that he himself might collect their debts. Of course, he kept all the 

Jewish property. Only those accepting baptism were permitted to remain.

This king was enriched for the moment because he drove out not only the Jews but also the Templars and the Lombards, who were Christian money-lenders. But soon a great cry arose from the common people because they had no one from whom to borrow. 

The Christian usurers who were ready to lend them money were cruel and grasping. Nine years later, therefore, the king was forced to invite the Jews to return. Now they were readmitted for a period of twenty years with the promise that they would receive a year's notice if they were to be asked to leave again. 

But when the kings were ready to leave the Jews in peace, the common people started attacking them. First a peasant uprising spread destruction through 120 communities. The following year a colony of lepers accused the Jews of poisoning the wells. The Jews realized that they had best flee from France, and many left the country. Nevertheless, some Jews were still hopeful, and in 1356 a new group of Jews was invited into France for a period of twenty years. This group was given the right to trade and to lend money at interest. 

But the period proved too stormy. The poor people, when they had to pay their debts, accused the Jews of witchcraft and black magic. The Jews saw that they could not hope to remain in France, but the end came sooner than they expected. In 

1394 the king decided to give up the struggle with the common people and to expel the Jews. Actually the Jews were no longer needed because there were enough Christian money-lenders to look after the needs of the population, and thus ended a chapter of 

Jewish life in France.

SUFFERING IN GERMANY 

After the Crusades, life in Germany too became unbearable. 

First of all, the Jews suffered from exceedingly heavy taxation. 

In order to win some safety, the Jews sought the protection of the German emperors. These took advantage of the Jews' need and claimed they were entitled to complete control over them. 

Titus, they declared, had secured control over all the Jews during the conquest of Jerusalem, and that right now descended to the German emperors who ruled over the Holy Roman Empire. 

The Jews were called servi camerae, meaning "wards of the royal

chamber," whose existence was mainly for the purpose of enriching the kings. Usually in debt, the kings hit upon the plan of pawning the right to tax Jews to persons with ready cash - to nobles and to cities. This meant in practice that the Jews had to pay taxes to the cities or noblemen as well as to the king, and the only way to meet the taxes was to make ever larger profits from money-lending.

DECLINE IN TRADE 

Up to and through the 1100's the Jews were still active in trade. They used to buy and sell at the fairs, and even the poor teacher or beadle would invest some of his money with the rich merchants in order to share in the profits. Even Christians often invested their money in Jewish enterprises. The Jews dealt in everything, in salt, meat, wine, dyes, medicines, furs, and slaves. The Jews living near the Rhine carried fish in their barges up and down the river. Even agriculture claimed its share of Jews until the middle of the 1100's in the Rhine district and for another hundred years in Eastern Germany. Until the middle of the 1200's too, Jews held public office, such as that of tax collector. And of course the Jewish physician was to be seen everywhere, often a trusted friend of princes and nobles.

But the cities were viciously fighting to force the Jews out of trade, and by the middle of the 1200's they were succeeding. Also during the same time Jews of practically all countries were deprived of public office. As their trade decreased, money-lending operations had to be on the increase.

In many places the Jews were forced to open loan banks at low rates of interest from which the poor could borrow. However, the borrowers hated to repay their loans and found it cheaper to attack the Jews either directly through bloody massacres or through blood accusations. The kings ordered these blood accusations investigated and found that they were utter falsehood, but neither the kings nor even the Church could stop the populace from making these mad claims against the Jews. Thus as massacre followed massacre the Jews began to flee the various cities of Germany and to go eastward. They went especially to

Austria, where the generous king received them, and to Poland, where they were granted many privileges.

JEWS ALMOST EXTERMINATED DURING BLACK DEATH 

The greatest of all calamities befell the Jews during the time of the Black Death (1348-49). Carried by rats from India, this disease killed more than one-third of the whole population of Europe. The reason for the rapid spread of the plague was the unsanitary conditions, the utter absence of soap, and the bad method of disposing of garbage in the narrow city streets. 

But the population did not understand all this. Crazed by fear, they blamed the Jews and accused them of poisoning the wells. 

Even the fact that Jews were also dying did not stop the mobs.

Jon L

Thus massacres took place greater than any which the Jews had as yet endured. Within two years, 350 communities were exterminated, the population having been burned, hanged, or drowned. No Jews were to be found in any large cities of Germany, and only in smaller villages did some Jews continue their residence. 

After the Black Death, workers were few, and cash was even more scarce. Some cities now became eager to readmit the Jews, but protection at this time, instead of being granted to a whole community, was sold to each person separately. A person received the right of residence for himself, but when his son grew up and wanted to marry he had to receive special permission, or else he had to leave town. Certain streets and houses were set apart for the Jews. This district was surrounded by walls and gates which were locked on Sundays and Christian holidays. The 

Jews were forbidden to go out after dark or on prohibited days, and anyone caught doing so was either fined or whipped. The streets were narrow, seldom wider than twelve feet, and because of the great crowding the houses had to be quite tall, shutting out the light from the skies. Thus as the tax burden grew and the Jews were being pushed out from one occupation after another, the Jews had no resort but to leave Germany. Even money-lending was now restricted to small loans, the Christian money-lenders having taken over the larger business. Most Jews, therefore, sought new homes, especially eastward in Poland, where kings who wanted to build a new country were eager to avail themselves of Jewish energy and talent. A few Jews remained in Germany, but the bulk of German Jewry transferred its residence to Poland. 

THE JEWS OF SPAIN 

The Jews had been expelled from England and France, and in Germany life was made unbearable for them. But how were they faring in Spain, the country which contained most Jews? Did the wave of persecution reach there also? We recall that the Jews lived happily in Mohammedan Spain for many years, until the fanatical Almohades overran the country and demanded that the Jews either accept Mohammedanism or else suffer death. Many Jews then escaped to Northern Spain, which was held by the Christians, and aided the Christians in the reconquest of their country. tween 1212 and 1257 the Moors were gradually driven southward, until they were finally bottled up in Granada.

Be- And what happened after the conquest was completed? After the war the kings on the whole continued to befriend the Jews. 

The city dwellers, or burghers, however, did not. So long as they were busy with the war, and after the war so long as their cities remained weak and poor, they needed the help of the Jews, but once they no longer needed their help, they were ready to cast them off. At the beginning of the 1300's the cities were well established, and they no longer needed the Jews. They were angered, too, by the fact that the Jews, because of their fear of the townsmen, kept apart from them. They neither accepted the city laws as binding upon them nor did they join the cities in the payment of taxes to the king through which the cities

pressed for greater privileges. Instead the Jews paid their taxes directly to the king and depended in all matters directly on him. The burghers thus looked upon the Jews as competitors to be curbed or, if possible, to be expelled. The Jews, therefore, found it safest to live in their own quarters behind their own walls and ramparts. 

STILL HOLD HIGH OFFICE 

Nevertheless, for a century the Jews continued to live happily in Spain under friendly kings. Of course, the kindness of the kings was purchased by large payments of money, but in exchange the Jews enjoyed many benefits, among them that of being tax collectors and financial advisers to royalty and the nobility. One Jewish treasurer, Joseph Beneviste, a man of imposing appearance and high learning, was placed at the head of the state by King Alfonso XI, and sons of the nobles were sent to his household to receive training there. Unfortunately, because the Jewish tax collectors were obliged to demand high taxes for their royal masters, opposition generally developed against them. Thus Joseph Beneviste whom we just mentioned, was slandered before the king by jealous enemies, with the result that he was arrested, his estates were confiscated, and he died in prison. 

ENGAGE FREELY IN ALL OCCUPATIONS 

However, the bulk of the Jews of Castile enjoyed a freedom of occupations which they did not know in other countries. They were mainly farmers who worked their land with hired labor. 

Some Jews owned castles and villages, enjoying the rights of feudal overlords, but most of the holdings were small enough to be worked by the owner with hired help. 

Jews were also distinguished in Castile as handworkers. 

To list all the occupations that Jews here engaged in would fill an entire page. Suffice it to say that they made and worked with textiles, metals, wood, leather, paper, and every other product known in their day. In trade too the Jews enjoyed full freedom of movement, and a penalty of one thousand maravedis was imposed for molesting them. They went to the fairs, where they traded in cloth, cattle, jewelry, spices, and other valuables, paying one-eleventh of their income to the king. A number of Jews, though not too large a number, were engaged in money-lending. Jews also leased from the king royal mills, salt marshes, and mines. A large group of Jews were seafarers, sailing between Spain and Africa.

Similar conditions prevailed in Aragon. Here, too, Jews were engaged as bailiffs (that is, overseers or stewards of large estates), finance ministers, and tax farmers. The Jews also enjoyed complete freedom of trade. Especially were traders protected who were engaged in foreign commerce. As for farming, there were many Jewish farmers in Aragon, some large, but mostly modest landowners, tilling their own fields. A considerable number were also engaged in cattle-raising. The Jews of Aragon

vdon J 1. L.

were well represented in the handicrafts and were particularly outstanding in weaving and dyeing. Altogether in Spain the Jews were strongly organized and prosperous, Thus we find strong Jewish guilds, such as a Jewish tailors' guild, which possessed its own hospital; a weavers' guild, with its own synagogues; and a strongly organized guild of shoemakers. 

JEWS AND SPANIARDS FRIENDLY 

The relation between the Jews and the rest of the population was on the whole friendly, and were it not for Church influence, would have continued so. For outwardly the Jew looked as much like a Spaniard as the American Jew resembles the American non- Jew.

In language, clothes, and general outward appearance, there was no distinguishing between the Jew and the Spaniard. The wealthy Jews had their Moorish slaves and owned villas and hunting grounds just like the Spanish grandees. The most distinguished scientists and physicians came from the Jewish quarter, The latter especially mixed with all the elements in the population, the rich as well as the poor, whom they often treated without charge, In commerce, too, there was no distinction between Moors, Christians, and Jews, who interchangeably worked for one another. Nor was it uncommon, in spite of Church prohibitions, for non-Jews and Jews to exchange gifts on their holidays and to visit each other on happy occasions. 

The Jewish quarter was far from being the dark and dingy ghetto which we find in Germany or Italy. It was a bright, cheerful, residential section with gardens surrounding the houses, and much cleaner than the non-Jewish quarter, Indeed, many Christians, including the king's own bailiff, lived there, On the other hand, many Jews lived outside of the Jewish district.

CHURCH STIRS UP HOSTILITY 

Jews would have lived in harmony with their non-Jewish neighbors were it not for the Church. We already saw what the attitude of the Church was toward the Jews. In a world in which both Christians and Jews depended largely on slave labor, the Church forbade the Jews to own Christian slaves, forcing them to buy heathen and Moorish slaves instead. It tried to set even these slaves free by offering them conversion to Christianity, but here the kings interfered. They demanded payment from the Church to the Jews for each slave thus freed, and later decreed that any slave who became baptized must remain a slave of the king. The slaves apparently were not anxious to exchange masters, finding it more comfortable in Jewish homes, where they were treated humanely and permitted to rest on the Sabbath Day. Similarly, the Church tried to prevent 

Christian women from acting as domestic servants in Jewish homes, but there it failed almost to the middle of the 1400's. 

Finally, it tried to discourage the use of Jewish physicians by frightening the population with tales that they administered

harmful medicines to Christians. But not only kings, princes, and the nobility, but even the cities, continued to employ Jews as body physicians and as health officers.

Under the constant pressure of the Church some Jews accepted baptism. This practice was especially common when a Jew was charged with a crime, for by accepting Christianity he escaped punishment. But what is more significant, some noble Christians, of their own accord accepted the Jewish religion, even though when they were found out they were subject to death by fire.

As for the common people, they were steeped in ignorance and superstition and could very easily be aroused to befriend as well as to attack the Jews. The fact that there were not frequent attacks is proof of the good relations which as a rule existed between the Jews and the rest of the population. 

ENGAGED IN DISPUTATIONS 

In the hope of securing wholesale conversion, the Church often resorted to disputations, or arguments, with the Jews. 

The most famous of these was one between the great Rabbi Nachmanides and a Dominican monk, a converted Jew, Pablo Christiani. It was with the greatest unwillingness that Nachmanides undertook the debate. Nachmanides had no difficulty in proving that Jesus could not be the Messiah because the Messiah was supposed to bring an era of peace and brotherly love, which certainly had not yet come to the world. Nevertheless, the 

Dominicans spread the report that Nachmanides lost the debate.

To prove that he was right, Nachmanides then published everything that he had said. This brought upon him the wrath of the Dominicans, and, fearing for his life, he decided to emigrate to Palestine, in 1267.

MOB ATTACKS IN 1391 

In general, feeling between Jews and non-Jews was becoming worse. Yet another hundred years were to pass before a serious tragedy was to occur. The disaster finally came in 1391 in the city of Seville. At that time one of the monks preached a very bitter sermon against the Jews which led to a mob attack against them. The government, anxious to restore order, arrested two of the ringleaders and had them whipped. This, however, only infuriated the mob. The Jewish quarter was surrounded and set on fire, the Jews were slaughtered in great numbers, and seven thousand were forced to accept Christianity. The same thing happened in Cordova, in Toledo, and in seventy other Castilian towns. Similar happenings took place in Aragon and on the 

Spanish islands. The bloody epidemic continued for over three months, during which time fifty thousand Jews were massacred and many thousands were converted.

After the attacks, the country was left bare. Christian laborers had no employment, and the king lost much of his

G SA 

VI.. 6. 

1 67 7.

J.. 1 1 op of ) wat tead

revenue. The king tried to punish the guilty persons, though the monk who started the trouble escaped with only a light sentence. The king also invited Jews from North Africa, extending to them freedom from paying special taxes. 

But the Cortes and the Church were unbending in their hatred of the Jews. In 1410 a monk by the name of Vincent Ferrer, followed by a murderous mob, went through the cities of Spain seeking converts, and many Jews, too frightened to resist him, accepted the new faith.

MANY JEWS BECOME MARRANOS 

The massacre of 1391 and the activities of Ferrer created a new problem in Spanish Jewish life. The number of Jews converted to Christianity out of fear became so large that they represented a complete section of the population. Thousands had accepted Catholicism, though few were sincere in their new belief. The Church, on the other hand, although condemning forcible baptism, nevertheless felt that once the step was taken it could not be recalled. The Jews, on their part, however, did not take the baptism seriously. As soon as they arrived home, they washed off the baptismal water. When a Jewish convert married, he had a public ceremony performed by a priest and then a private ceremony with a rabbi. Smokeless chimneys gave proof of Sabbath observance, and kosher meat was bought by many of these Jewish Christians. 

But while these converts did not fulfill their obligations as Christians, they received the benefits of their conversion. 

All the restrictions which they suffered as Jews suddenly disappeared. They became free to mingle with all classes of society and to occupy the highest posts in government, education, and even the clergy. The Christians therefore hated these newcomers to their faith. Thus, while the Jews regarded their converted brothers with pity and called them anusim, or "forced ones, the Christian population spoke of them with contempt as Marranos, which is an old Spanish word meaning "swine." The 

Marranos were not even free from violence. An unpopular minister, Alvaro de Luna, sought to gather a forced loan of one million maravedis and entrusted the collection to a treasurer, who was a converted Jew, The hatred which should have gone to the minister fell instead upon the heads of the new Christians. 

In the city of Toledo, when an attempt was made to collect this tax, a mob formed and wrecked the house of the tax collector and the homes of other Marranos. The example of Toledo was quickly followed by other cities.

INQUISITION ESTABLISHED 

In the meantime, the Church and the nobility were not satisfied with mere mob action. They wanted to establish courts of inquisition to inquire into the religious behavior of the Marranos. For thirteen years the rulers were unwilling to

listen to these arguments.

At last, however, they were persuaded, and asked the pope for a court of inquisition to be established. In Seville, where the first court was opened, the 

Marranos plotted to kill the inquisitors, but the plot was revealed by the daughter of one of them. The conspirators were thereupon arrested and publicly burned. This horrible spectacle was called the auto-da-fe, which means in Spanish an 'act of faith.

The Marranos of Spain now became terrified. Many of them fled to the homes of nobles, but the nobles, fearful of their own safety, were obliged to send back as many as eight thousand. 

Within a month or two thirty victims were burned alive at Cordova. In 1485 an inquisition was established at Toledo. 

Here 750 persons of both sexes were compelled to march through the city bareheaded and barefoot, with unlighted tapers, as a sign that they were sorry for their acts. They were fined onefifth of their property and were forbidden to hold public office thereafter.

The king appointed Torquemada Inquisitor General of the entire country. Torquemada was a fanatical and blood-thirsty man. 

Upon assuming office, he granted a thirty or forty-day period to those accused of Judaizing in order that they might confess. 

Those who failed to confess immediately were tortured in the cruelest manner. To discover secret Judaizers, Torquemada sent spies to all parts of the land. Actually it did not matter whether the accused was guilty or not, because once in the hands of the Inquisition, he never went free. Torquemada even demanded that every person act as informer. Children were to inform against their parents, husbands against their own wives, sisters against their brothers. The total number burned by the 

Spanish Inquisition during forty-five years ran into thousands. 

JEWS' POSITION GROWS WORSE 

What of the Jews who remained faithful to Judaism after the tragedy of 1391? Their position rapidly became worse. A few still practiced medicine, and a very few were tax farmers, but the majority were handworkers, peddlers, peasants, and growers of vineyards. The merchants carried on small-scale business. 

No longer were glamorous voyages undertaken to distant lands, but whatever trade could be had was carried on inland, Separate Jewish quarters were now enforced in the towns; nevertheless, the townsmen tried to enforce the towns' taxes upon them in addition to their own. On the other hand, the guilds were constantly struggling to restrict their right to work.

EXPULSION ORDERED 

In 1470 a marriage, which we already noted, took place between Ferdinand of Aragon and Isabella of Castile, thus joining the two ruling houses of Christian Spain. United, these two monarchs now determined to overcome the remaining Moors in Granada, the last Spanish stronghold. So long as Granada held

お ​י 

ہم 1. 1 1、 3

out against Spain, the aid of the Jews was needed, and they were not molested. But when Granada fell, in November 1491, there arose the demand at once that the Jews be expelled. It was difficult at first to find a reason for the expulsion, but soon a ritual murder plot was revived. The Jews were accused of killing a Christian child for its blood. The Christian population was so aroused by this legend that the monarchs were compelled to order the expulsion of all the Jews from Spain. The Jews were permitted to take their property with them, except gold, silver, or coin. The order was issued in

March, and the Jews were given until the end of July to prepare for the expulsion.

Upon hearing the news, Isaac Abrabanel, an important financier, and Abraham Senior, royal treasurer, hurried to the king and tried to offer him money to recall the cruel decree. Abrabanel had been the queen's financial adviser and had loaned the government large sums of money to carry on the war. Abraham Senior had been the man who had arranged the match between Ferdinand and Isabella. These men now offered the king thirty thousand gold ducats if he would permit the Jews to remain. At this point, however, it is reported, Torquemada ran in with a crucifix and threw it before the king and queen, crying: "Judas sold our savior for thirty pieces of silver; now you want to sell him for thirty thousand. he is take him!' 

Here

SPANISH JEWS SCATTER 

The doom of the Jews was sealed. The edict was issued 

March 30, 1492, and by July 30 not a single Jew was to remain in Spain, Sicily, and Sardinia, on the penalty of death or baptism. Many weak Jews yielded and became converted to Christianity. Abraham Senior, tax collector and chief rabbi of

Spain, was forced to become converted. The majority, however, was ready to leave. Some went to Navarre, a French kingdom under Spanish protection, from which, however, they were soon expelled. Three ships foundered in a storm. Others cast their passengers ashore in Spain, and five hundred more in despair turned Christian. Some arrive at Fez, where an epidemic broke out among them in which twenty thousand lost their lives. Genoa permitted some Jews to land, but only on condition that they turn Christian. Several ship masters sold the 

Jews as slaves. The only fortunate ones were those who reached the shores of Turkey, where a friendly government was ready to receive them.

MANY GO TO PORTUGAL 

A large number of Jews naturally left for the country nearest to them, namely, Portugal. The king of Portugal had been friendly to the Jews before and had received many exiles in 1391. Marranos, too, had fled to Portugal and there openly or secretly had thrown off their make-believe Christianity.

But when a new migration of Jews threatened to enter Portugal, neither the native Jews nor the Portuguese government was ready to receive them. The Jews especially were afraid that a sudden large increase in their numbers would awaken the ever present Jew hatred against them. The king, however, hoping to profit from the misery of the Jews, invited thirty important families and an additional six hundred householders who could pay a tax of one hundred cruzados each, to settle in the country. 

Weapon makers, too, were welcomed. Others who could not pay this large sum were permitted to enter on the payment of eight cruzados for each adult, on condition, however, that they would remain no longer than eight months. The king promised within the eight months to provide boats to take them to other countries. 

ORDERED TO LEAVE, BUT FORCIBLY DETAINED 

But when the time came, very few ships were provided, and the unfortunate Jews who could not or did not leave on time were sold as slaves. Only the rich and the handworkers were permitted to remain longer. In the meantime, the cruel king died, but the following king, although kindly disposed himself and annulling the slavery decree, planned to marry a daughter of Ferdinand and Isabella. She would have none of him unless he expelled the Jews from his kingdom. This king, therefore, issued an order in 1497 that all Jews and Moors were to leave the country within a period of ten months. But on second thought he was unwilling to lose so many valuable citizens, and so he decided to restrain the Jews by forcibly baptizing their children. 

When he found, however, that he could get few Jews to remain in that way, he prepared the final blow. The Jews who were supposed to have ships provided for them found no ships, and when the appointed time for leaving was over, they were all declared to be slaves of the king. Most of them now gave up and helplessly permitted themselves to be baptized. Thus there really never was an expulsion of the Jews from Portugal. The Portuguese Jews were converted by force. However, they remained faithful to Judaism much longer than the Jews of Spain, for, while the Spanish Marranos practically died out in the sixteenth century, the Portuguese continue to the present day. 

SUFFER ATTACKS AS MARRANOS 

Even after their conversion to Christianity, the Portuguese Marranos knew no peace. There were riots against them in 1506, and there were complaints about their faithlessness to the religion of their choice" throughout the century. In 1531 an earthquake occurred which was interpreted as a sign of divine anger against the Jews. This led to the demand for the establishment of the Inquisition in Portugal. The Marranos, knowing how terribly the Inquisition worked in Spain, paid large sums of money to the pope to have the Inquisition delayed. However, in 1579 it became established in Portugal, and thereafter it continued imprisoning and burning new Christians, the last burning taking place in the year 1765.

7.

Most of the Marranos in time were mixed with the native population, but a considerable group remained loyal to Judaism. 

Since there were no Jews in Portugal, Spain, or France, their Jewishness became very much influenced by Christianity. They dared not teach their children when young, fearing that they might give them away through some thoughtless childish act. Instruction, therefore, was given only to grown-up sons and daughters, and only in a few elements of Judaism. Thus the Marranos of Portugal grew up ignorant of Judaism and were regarded as Christians even by the Jews themselves. Only recently have they been discovered by Jews, who are trying to bring them back to the Jewish fold. 1. ள்ல்

IX 

JEWS IN ITALY 

Jewish settlement in Spain, France and England again ended disastrously, and in Germany almost equally so. The Jews fared a little better in Italy. In Rome the popes, who became the rulers of an independent kingdom, protected the Jews. However, they strictly enforced the law against Jews owning Christian slaves, which made many Jews very poor, since their slaves could leave them and become converted. In general, though, the Jews lived well in Rome until the middle of the 1500's, being recognized as one of the elements of the Roman population. They took part in the coronation ceremonies of the emperors, later of the popes, and carried a Sefer Torah in the procession. The Christian population continued to be friendly with them, ate and celebrated at their homes, and carried on business with them. 

The lower clergy was constantly stirring up strife, and created many uncomfortable situations for the Jews. Churchmen engaged in frequent debates with the Jews, in which, according to their own admission, the Jews were often victorious. The popes, however, would not permit attacks against the Jews or even forced conversion. But they did insist that the Marranos, whether they had accepted Christianity willingly or not, remain faithful to their new faith, otherwise the Church persecuted them.

One of the popes even stemmed from the Jewish family of Pierleoni, Italian Jewish bankers who became converted to Christianity, This pope, under the name of Anacletus II, was very friendly to the Jews. At the time, a rival who claimed to be the rightful pope, accused Anacletus of Judaizing and of robbing the Church in favor of the Jews. The accusation did not effect the condition of the Jews, however, till Anacletus died. Only then did the rival pope enforce anti-Jewish laws outside of Rome, though not in Rome proper. The Jews were somewhat frightened when a large Church council gathered in 1179 to pass laws against them, but the council only reenacted the old laws forbidding the use of Christian servants or building new synagogues.

The Jews of Rome were a great source of help to Jews in all parts of the Christian world. Whenever any calamity threatened the Jews anywhere, they communicated with the Jews of Rome, and these pleaded with the popes. Often the pope's private Jewish physician was asked to intercede in behalf of the Jews. At times large sums of money were spent by the Jews to give the popes costly gifts.

Many Jews who were expelled from other lands found refuge in Rome, Even some of the Spanish exiles in 1492 found a haven there. When the Jewish badge was introduced anywhere else, for a long time it was not enforced at Rome. Later, when it was enforced, Jewish physicians and even merchants at times were excused from wearing it.

Life at Rome was not without unpleasant interruptions. 

There were popes who were unfriendly toward Jews, and some who attempted to forbid the use of Jewish doctors, though they themselves continued to employ them. One pope wanted to destroy Jewish schools by ordering that instruction be limited to the Bible only and forbidding the teaching of the Talmud. It took a large sum of money to upset this evil decree. The preaching monks, too, succeeded in stirring up hatred and even a blood accusation. In the city of Trent, in the Tryol, a child named 

Simon was killed on the first day of Passover in 1475. After three days of search the body was discovered near the stream of a Jew. Although the Jew himself brought the body to the authorities, he, the leading doctor, and other heads of the community were arrested. After a long period of torture, six Jews were burned alive, two ohanged their religion, and one died in prison. 

The monks claimed that the blood of the dead body performed miracles, and accordingly they did a thriving business with superstitious believers. Simon of Trent was later made a saint by the Church.

But in spite of unhappy exceptions, the popes on the whole remained tolerant. One pope, for example, was so friendly that, as we shall tell later, he protected David Reubeni, who came as a representative of the Jews of Arabia to enlist his aid against the Turks. The pope gave him letters to the king of Portugal and to other kings of Europe. Similarly, Solomon Molcho, a 

Marrano who gave up Christinity and claimed to be the Messiah, was several times saved by the pope from the Inquisition. Another pope stopped a play supposedly showing the life of Jesus which tended to stir up hatred against the Jews. 

CHANGE AFTER THE REFORMATION 

But after the Reformation in the middle of the fifteen hundreds, the Church became fearful of losing its power and began to oppress everyone who differed from the leading religion. 

The Talmud was publicly burned and a censorship was imposed, so that no books could be printed without the consent of the pope's authorities. The Jews, in order to make sure that nobody presented books for publication which might be found objectionable by the Church, organized a censorship of their own. The Jewish censors examined all books and would not permit any to be published on which they disagreed. This was one of the harmful effects of the new Church policy. One of the popes imposed a tax of ten dinar on each synagogue to support the houses of converts to Christianity. In 1555 a very cruel pope, Paul IV, forced the Jews into a ghetto which was enclosed with walls and gates. The Jews could not buy their own houses and were forced to sell what real estate they already owned. They might keep but one synagogue and had to abandon all the others. Every man had to wear a yellow hat and every woman a yellow veil. They could use no Christian servants and were not even to associate with Christians. The Jews were strictly forbidden to trade in food or in new garments. These laws, like so many other unfortunate happenings in Jewish history, were published on the ninth of Ov.

​2

The Church also decided to pursue the Marranos with greater zeal. A large number of Marranos was arrested in Ancona, the port city of Rome. Many were citizens of Turkey, and these were freed at the demand of the Jewish vizier of the Turkish king. 

Of the others, twenty-four were publicly burned. In protest the Turkish Jews decided to boycott Ancona and to send no more goods there, but to do all their trading with Pesaro instead. 

However, the Jews of Ancona became frightened that the pope would turn his wrath against them, and so they begged their fellow Jews of Turkey to call off the boycott.

Other unpleasant happenings marred the period. Jews were expelled from all parts of the papal states except from Rome and Ancona. Taxes kept on mounting, and Jewish business was constantly interfered with. The Jews became very poor. Instead of lending money, they had to be the borrowers, and the lenders were severe and demanded constant payment, forcing them into prison if they could not pay. Some Jews thus left Rome, though many remained to witness the dawn of a better era, when the ghetto walls finally fell.

JEWS IN VENICE Of the two warring republics of the time, Genoa and Venice, in Italy, only Venice had any Jews. They first appeared there in about the 1200's. They were permitted settlement because of the large business which they carried on and especially because they opened loan banks where the poor Christian population could borrow money. Several times the government tried to close the loan banks and organize others run by the Church, but the Church banks did not succeed, and the Jews were forced to continue operating them, even though they were carried on at a loss. 

A number of times the Jews were almost expelled from Venice, but they were too useful to be spared, and always the order of expulsion was recalled. But the permission which the Jews received to live in Venice was always limited to ten years, and whenever the permission had to be renewed the government would press extra taxes from the Jews. 

Though the Jews were never expelled from Venice, they were finally confined to a particular quarter, which was the first district to be called the "ghetto. "Ghetto" probably means a cannon foundry, which was located near the Jewish quarter. The district was small and crowded, and there were walls built around it. The Jews had to pay the gatekeepers who opened and closed the gates at different hours, according to the seasons. 

There were three groups of Jews in Venice, the Germans and Italians, the Spanish, and the Oriental Jews. The first two groups were forced into a ghetto in 1516. The last group was excused for a time, but in 1541 they were also confined, though their ghetto was somewhat pleasanter than that of the other Jews. 

Every Jew in the ghetto had to wear a yellow hat, except when traveling abroad. The ghetto was so crowded that there was constant danger of fire.
