However, while the Jews suffered confinement, they were not otherwise seriously interfered with. There were practically no mob attacks and no blood accusations. Forcible conversions were strictly forbidden, and even kidnapping Jewish children to bring them up as Christians, though often practiced in Rome, was not permitted in Venice.

JEWS AT HOME IN VENICE 

The ghetto itself was a very active place. Jews and non- 

Jews from every part of the world were to be found there. Under the pretext of selling second-hand objects, the Jews were able to include in their business transactions the most precious goods, such as jewels, rugs, expensive furniture, and antiques. 

The Jews also carried on a large maritime trade. The more important merchants had their own trading fleets, which went to different parts of the world. The Jewish doctor, too, was quite a familiar sight in Venice. The government tried to limit the doctors to Jews only, but they could not succeed, and soon the regulation broke down.

It took a long time before the Jews of Venice were permitted to build a synagogue. Not until 1527 did the Jews receive permission to build one for each community, the German, the Spanish, and the Oriental. Each synagogue community was governed by a council of its own, but together sent delegates to one council for the entire city. 

The rabbis at the time were unpaid, their office being merely one of honor. But there were paid preachers, and these were very eloquent. Often Christian nobles and important business men came to listen to them and were very much impressed. 

Education in the ghetto was quite modern. In addition to the study of the sacred texts, all the important Italian works were studied, as well as Latin and Greek, the sciences, music, and even dancing. Even one of the rabbis was known to teach dancing. Music flourished considerably in Venice. The Jews had an orchestral society which for many years gave concerts which the leading noblemen attended. There was also a Jewish theater which gave performances, and the Church had to fight hard to keep Christians from attending it. Other arts were also well represented. Jews did work in precious metals, making beautiful objects of gold and silver. They illuminated manuscripts and even practiced portrait painting. They also helped considerably in introducing the sciences into Venice, such as astronomy, geography, and mathematics. Jews of Venice are said to have been the first to use handkerchiefs.

As trade shifted from the Mediterranean to the Atlantic, and as the East lost its importance, Venice dwindled as a trading city, and the Venetian Jews left for other parts. A small community remained to witness the destruction of the ghetto in the 1800's.

JEWS IN SOUTHERN ITALY 

We

In Southern Italy three religions and three cultures lived side by side. Jews, Christians, and Arabs inhabited Sicily. saw how the Byzantines had conquered the country in the sixth century. In 827 the Arabs came, and they ruled the country until 1071. They in turn were driven out by the Normans, who were later dispossessed by the German kings. These gave way to the 

French, who in turn yielded to the Spanish. Through this constant change of rule Jewish life went along fairly peacefully in Southern Italy, and especially in Sicily. The Jews developed a large trade in silk and in dyes. Thus, for example, the German emperor gave the Jews complete control over silk and over the dyes of the country. Nobody else might engage in those occupations except the Jews recognized by the Government. Many Jews, too, carried on extensive farming and date plantations. 

The Church, as elsewhere, tried to hinder the Jews and to stir up feeling against them. Jewish books, especially the 

Talmud, were publicly burned. But in general the kings protected the Jews until Naples and Sicily came under the rule of Aragon. Even the governors sent by the kings of Aragon, like the rulers before them, protected the Jews and punished attacks against them. Although forbidding the use of Jewish doctors, they used Jewish doctors themselves, which of course put their prohibition to naught. The condition of the Sicilian Jews was especially favorable. They lived in thirty communities numbering about one hundred thousand. They even tried organizing all the communities of the island into one body and planned the establishment of a university. But after 1492, when the Jews were expelled from Spain, the same order was now to be applied to Sicily also. The Christian burghers in the cities raised loud objections to the order of expulsion, because the Jewish townsmen were mainly handworkers while Christian handworkers were few. Aside from Jewish taxes, which the state collected, the 

Jews spent much money in ordinary expenses which would be lost to the Christians. Jewish debts to Christians would remain unpaid.

Most of the handworkers were Jewish, and if the Jews left, the few remaining Christian artisans would demand high prices. It would be especially dangerous if war broke out with 

Turkey, for, though the Jews were not fighters, they best knew how to make weapons of war. But it was all to no avail. The 

Jews were merely given a few additional months, until December, 1492, and then they were all expelled. In 1505 Naples fell to the Spaniards also, and the Jews were expelled from there likewise.

IN CONCLUSION 

Thus we have rounded out a period in European Jewish history. We began with the golden age in Spain, France and Germany, an age of freedom and opportunity for the Jews. At the time there were as yet no cities in Europe. The Jews and Mohammedans were the principal merchants and since the latter

be bett . ?

Mt Josteus vit et qu 

J 30.

Viest mud sub er 

20 IN 10.

were not admitted into Europe, the Jews had practically a monopoly of the European trade. The Jewish handworkers too had little competition. The Jews were therefore well received, and had rich opportunities for carrying on business and industry. 

They meant to establish themselves permanently in their new home and set up local councils and courts to enable them to carry on self-government.

But gradually European cities began to develop. Christians became merchants and began to compete with the Jews. The Jews were now at a serious disadvantage because they were persons without any established rights. No longer were they Roman citizens as in days of old, but they were considered strangers. In the feudal system they were the king's men which proved to be a privilege as well as a disadvantage. True they held the position equivalent to knights and anyone who hurt a Jew was as responsible as for hurt to a knight. But they were not rooted in the land. Besides, they always had powerful enemies such as the Church eager to prove that Jews must suffer because they did not accept Jesus, or the burghers who were anxious to be rid of too clever competitors. In consequence, the Jews were slowly being pushed out of the better paying trades and handicrafts. 

They took to money lending and later when kings found that Jewish money business spelled large profits for them, they forced the Jews more and more into lending, by shutting off other avenues of making a living. Even there, Christian competitors, though forbidden by their religion to engage in usury, nevertheless did engage in it and got the lion's share of the trade. 

Although, therefore, the Jews flourished in Europe for many years, spoke the European languages and were even in advance of the Europeans in general culture and education, and even though they wrote many books and had a long line of distinguished men in every country of Europe, it all counted for naught. Beginning with the Crusades there were frequent attacks on the Jews, now in one part of Europe, now in another. Massacres, ritual murder trials, accusations of stabbing the host, witchcraft, and poisoning of wells became the custom in Europe. 

For many centuries the kings continued to protect the Jews as well as they could because the Jews were profitable to them. When the 

Jews were made poor through constant heavy taxation and draining of their profits, they were no longer profitable to keep and the kings expelled them. They were thus expelled from all of Western Europe and most of Central Europe. Only in some cities of 

Germany and in parts of Italy did the Jewish community continue to live in an unbroken line. Most of the Jews migrated eastward. They went to Poland, which at the time was just beginning to develop and needed their energies. Thus it was that the Jews became mainly East European. We shall tell of the life of the 

Jews of Poland in the following chapter.

JEWS IN POLAND The 

American Jews come from three parts of the world. The first comers were the descendants of the Spanish Jews. The next group, which began arriving here during the 1840's, came mainly from Germany, while the last wave brought immigrants from Eastern and Southern Europe and consisted mainly of Russians and Poles. majority of the Jews in the United States, indeed of the world, seem to come from Poland. A short time ago, less than sixty or seventy years back, the bulk of the Jewish people must have lived in Poland. How and when did they come to Poland?

JEWS NEEDED The Jews came to Poland because Poland needed them. Early 

Poland was composed of only two classes on top, the schlachta, who were squires and nobles, and on the bottom, the peasants, or serfs, bound to their soil. The schlachta were too proud to work even their own estates. They considered such occupations as trade or industry as altogether beneath them. The peasants, on the other hand, were too oppressed to rise above their lowly station. Poland thus needed merchants and handworkers who would develop trade and industry. The Polish kings were therefore happy to welcome German townsmen to Poland, among whom there were many Jews. Both Jews and Germans were promised many privileges, among them the right to live under their own laws. 

The first large stream of Jews came to Poland in about 1098, after the anti-Jewish outbreaks of the first Crusade, though there were Jews in Poland much earlier. Thereafter, with each new Crusade the stream of Jews increased. Throughout the thirteen and fourteen hundreds large waves continued to flow into Poland. By the end of the 1500's immigration had come to a close.

RECEIVE CHARTER OF PRIVILEGES 

The kings gave special privileges to the German burghers and to the Jews. The Germans were permitted to live and to be governed under their own laws. The first charter given to the 

Jews, largely to protect them against their German competitors, was issued by King Boleslav in 1264. The charter, modeled after an earlier Austrian document, gave them freedom of residence and travel and the right of carrying on their own business. Their safety was entrusted to two special officers directly responsible to the king. The Jews might not be tried in the city courts, the judges of which were usually their competitors and therefore their enemies. In the trial of cases of Christians against Jews, Christian witnesses were accepted only if there were Jews to prove the same facts. Murder or injury of a Jew was punished like injury to a member of the schlachta. It was strictly forbidden to mistreat a Jew entering a Christian home or a oathhouse. There was even a special fine imposed on Christians if

they refused to come in answer to Jewish cries in the middle of tne night. Ritual murder accusations had to be supported by three 

Jewish and three Christian witnesses. Thus the Christian accusers of innocent Jews might suffer the death penalty. Later 

King Casimir the Great (1333-70) welcomed Jewish business men and granted them many new privileges. 

Of course, the Jews paid for all these rights. Their taxes went largely to the kings, who were on the whole just in their method of collection. But, while the Jews paid at every step, their taxation was mild in comparison with that of Jews in other countries. The Jews therefore prospered and increased from a few to sixty communities spread all over Poland. 

ACTIVE IN ALL OCCUPATIONS 

Since they had freedom of trade and movement, we find Jews in all occupations, Large numbers were engaged in farming, there being even Jewish noblemen, large landowners whose lands were worked by serfs. Many a Jewish banker owned not only fields, but entire villages of serfs which he got when the Polish borrower could not pay his debt. Others were given stretches of territory in newly cleared forest lands on which to organize new villages. 

Naturally, the Jews were much more active in trade than in agriculture. Up to about 1500 there were practically no pro hibitions placed on Jewish trade, and so the Jews dealt in all kinds of goods, such as rugs, tapestries, silks, jewels, spices, wax, furs, salt, English cloth, horses, and slaves. Jews also carried on an extensive trade in farm products and live stock, such as grain, sheep, and horses. There was also a considerable trade in fish and caviar. Various liquors too passed in Jewish commerce. In connection with their money-lending activities, the 

Jews of Poland, as in other European countries, developed a trade in second-hand objects, which ranged from the costliest jewels and furs to worn garments and cheap household articles. The largest undertakings were carried on by Jewish bankers, who not only loaned money on interest, but obtained concessions from the king. Thus they farmed the taxes, leased salt mines and timber forests, and, as we already saw, controlled villages. Rich Jewish bankers were given the control of the minting of money, and there are still some coins found with some Hebrew inscriptions on them.

But lest we get the impression that all Polish Jews were rich and engaged in big business, we shall do well to note that merchants comprised at most one-third of the population. Two thirds were handworkers who engaged in a variety of trades. In 

Poland guilds did not yet exist at this early period, but later they were to carry on a bitter warfare against the Jews. Certain crafts were practiced by Jews for religious reasons. We already know that Jews needed their own dairying, wine-making, meat-slaughtering, tailors, bakers, etc. The record also reveals that the Jews were engaged in tanning, shoemaking, glazing

and as furriers, cap makers, and brewers. Jews are also mentioned as weavers. Indeed, in Posen the Jewish street was known as the Street of the Weavers. Jews also continued to practice their ancient trade as jewelers. At that time too, a considerable number of Polish Jews practiced the profession of medicine. 

GROUPS ARRAYED AGAINST JEWS 

Thus the early years of Jewish life in Poland were on the whole happy and prosperous. But there were already forces at work against the Jews. The elements which fought the Jews were, first, the Church, second, the German burghers, third, the handworkers, and fourth, the lower schlachta. 

Almost as soon as the Jews arrived in Poland the Church began inciting the Christian population against them. It demanded that Jews be separated into special quarters in order to be kept apart from the non-Jews. The demand was further made that the 

Jews remain in their homes during Church processions and whenever they appeared in public that they wear a red badge as a sign of disgrace. Each community of Jews was to be permitted but one synagogue. Christians were to be forbidden to invite Jews to eat or drink with them or to buy food from them for fear the Jews might poison them. The Jews were not to hold any office over 

Christians, nor were they even to be permitted to employ Christian servants.

Joined with the Church in opposition to the Jews were the German merchants, or burghers. Their hostility was connected with business rivalry, the Jews being their competitors. The burghers did not yet dare ask that the Jews be completely expelled from their cities, though later they obtained even that privilege in a number of towns. But they tried, with the aid of the Church, to drive the Jews out of the more favorable business districts to special quarters of their own. 

The burghers enlisted not only the Church in the war on the Jews, but even the laborers, handworkers, and the city rabble. To ward off the demands of the city workers that they be given representation in the government, the burghers turned their wrath on the Jews. If prices rose too high, if money was hard to obtain, or if there was not enough work, the blame was cast on the Jews. Thus, instead of revolting against the burghers, the handworkers would satisfy themselves at the expense of the Jews.

In addition to Church, burgher, and handworker, the lower schlachta joined in the anti-Jewish activities, because it saw the Jews occupying many important offices which it wanted for itself. The Jews, as

The Jews, as we have already mentioned, farmed the taxes or held various concessions from the government which members of the schlachta felt by right belonged to them.

JEWS ATTACKED 

Finding the Jews too strongly entrenched to be attacked directly, the various groups began a series of accusations, of blood libels, host stabbings, etc. These led to pogroms which in time had disastrous results for the Jews. In 1447 a fire broke out in Posen, and that was considered reason enough for a pogrom on the Jews. With the entire population thus ranged against them, the Jews found themselves hard-pressed indeed. 

The kings, who usually protected the Jews, at times were compelled to yield to the demands of the mob. Thus in 1494, after a fearful pogrom, the king consented to the Jews' being expelled from Cracow into a suburb. The same series of events happened in Posen. After fires and pogroms, the burghers finally obtained a decree from the king in 1553 forbidding Jewish residence in the market streets where the fairs took place. 

Nine years later they succeeded in shutting the Jews up in ghettos. The right of residence naturally went hand in hand with the right to trade. In 1485 the Jewish merchants consented, under pressure, that hereafter they would deal only with abandoned pledges, and only on fair and market days. Jews were forbidden to sell cloth and they were limited in the number of cattle that they could sell in any one year. Similarly, in banking only a few large firms managed to continue as before, whereas the majority now became pawnbrokers, dealing with the city's poor.

As long as strong kings ruled Poland, the Jews were protected and even regained many of the rights which they had lost earlier. But as the ruling family died out and the Polish kings were elected by the schlachta, the kings grew more and more dependent on them as the actual rulers. Together with the schlachta the burghers now bore down heavily on the Jews. They destroyed their synagogues, made attacks on the Jewish quarters, and forced them to submit to their law courts. The handworkers too were now better organized and could, in certain cases, force the Jews to restrict themselves to Jewish customers. 

FORCED INTO SMALL VILLAGES 

The Jews did not give up the fight. Some of them struggled to retain the rights which earlier kings had granted to them, but more and more Jews were compelled to transfer to the smaller villages. They found homes especially on the estates of the schlachta, where they acted as stewards, or where they rented the privilege of dairying, operating the flour mill, keeping the inn, and distilling the liquor. They also collected the taxes and managed the financial affairs of the estate while their masters were entertaining themselves with politics. 

The interests of the Jews of Poland became more and more bound up with that of the pans, or nobles, and soon the Jews were to pay dearly for this union. But before we tell this story, let us pause for a while to see how the Jews of Poland lived during this period.

DETAHD po

JEWISH INNER LIFE 

Ev- So long as conditions were not too difficult, the Jews managed their inner affairs in a very satisfactory manner. ery Jewish community in Poland had a leader who was its ruler in worldly matters and a rabbi who was the religious head. Each community, too, depending upon its size, had a local council of from twelve to forty members. It was a high honor to be a member of the council, but at the same time it carried a considerable responsibility, for if the taxes due from the Jews were not properly paid, the members of the council might have to make up the shortage from their own purses.

THE INSTITUTIONS 

Vil One of the first concerns of the community was the obtaining of a cemetery, a privilege which only large cities enjoyed. lages were obliged to convey their dead to the nearest city. 

Next in order of institutions came the synagogue. The Polish 

Church councils tried to limit the number of synagogues to one in a city, but the Jews disregarded the prohibition and built many beautiful synagogues. Another institution that every wellorganized Jewish community had was a bath-house. We are used to baths in our own homes, and so we may not realize how important this institution was for the health and cleanliness of the people. 

The institution which had a most important effect upon the life of the Jewish community was the court. The Jews could try their own cases, where only Jews were involved, or where one of the parties was a non-Jew, if both sides consented to being tried in a Jewish court, Another item which no city could do without as early as the thirteenth century was the teacher of school children. Though many a Polish village has remained to this day without a knowledge of reading and writing, the Jews taught their children from earliest times. Most large cities possessed a hospital - not the spotless, sanitary buildings of today, but a place for the poor sick. Each community, too, had its own butcher and enjoyed the right to slaughter animals in accordance with Jewish law and sell the t'refo part to the non-Jews. 

One of the very important duties of the local government was the supervision of education. In addition to the elementary teacher, a city of any size at all maintained a y'shivo, or academy for higher study. Learning became very wide-spread in Poland, and, in order to remove poverty as an excuse for not studying, the poor students were aided with food and lodging. The poor young men, as a rule, slept on the hard benches of the beis ha-midrosh (house of study). Food was supplied by families of the community, who would share their meager crust with the poor y'shivo bochur. 

It was arranged that the students would "eat days, as it was called - that is, eat every day of the week in a different home. 

A village of fifty might thus support as many as thirty young men. The result was that, at least in Talmudic studies, Poland forged ahead, far beyond any country in which the Jewish people had found a home.

.J 2899 1 3 

STU POA, D STAD 

157 41.3

In addition to the educational agencies, the Jews possessed many organizations, as in other countries, to care for the sick, feed the poor, clothe the needy, bury the dead, and provide poor girls with dowries. Above all, g'milus chasodim, the free loan agency, sought to help persons to become independent through extending to them small loans.

COUNCIL OF FOUR LANDS 

Not only did the Jews have well-organized local governments, but they were even organized on a country-wide basis. The Jews of Poland enjoyed an excellent national organization which represented all of Polish Jewry, The organization was called Va-ad Arba Ho-Arotsos, The Council of the Four Lands, and, as its name implied, it consisted of representatives of the four states which composed Poland. At first a fifth state, Lithuania, was also included, but after a while that country set up its own council. 

The Council of the Four Countries grew out of rabbinic gatherings which used to be held at the time of the great fairs. We have already mentioned that the rabbis acted as judges and were called upon to settle legal disputes. One of the greatest rabbis of the sixteenth century, Rabbi Sholom Schachna of Lublin, before whom many questions came for appeal, established the custom of inviting a number of leading rabbis to help him settle the more important problems. This became a kind of supreme court. Soon members not rabbis joined the meetings and together considered problems of concern to all the Jews of Poland. Thus there developed the council of the Four Lands, which consisted of thirty persons - six rabbis and twenty-four other leaders. Starting as a court, the council soon grew into a legislature as well. Among the large variety of laws considered by this council were such questions as prohibiting too rich dress for fear of arousing Gentile jealousy; forbidding a man to give his wife and children as pledges for a debt; restraining unreasonable competition in business; or prohibiting the payment of increased rent by one Jew for a house occupied by another. 

In order to deal responsibly with the Polish government, the council appointed special representatives, who were called shtadlonim. If any harsh laws were planned against the Jews, it was the duty of the shtadlonim to try to have them set aside. shtadlonim were provided with special funds, for often lavish gifts had to be given in order to recall or stop an evil decree. 

THE CHMELNICKI MASSACRES The 

Jewish life in Poland was soon to experience a disaster before which all earlier calamities, such as the Crusades, the attacks during the Black Death, and the expulsions seemed of minor importance. A massacre was in store for the Jews on a scale never before equalled. 

Large stretches of Southeastern Poland, now called the Ukraine, under the domain of Polish noblemen, were inhabited by Russian

BAIJ 110 1 1:|:|: : 14.9 1 ས ། 

Why 

DA

peasants. The Poles were not interested in their peasants except to oppress them, bleeding them and taxing them as heavily as they could. Every act of these unfortunate peasants cost them a tax. To go fishing, to pasture their flocks, to keep beehives, to grind their flour, to go to Church everything had 

to be paid for. On top of it all, the landlords made fun of their peasants and called them khlops, meaning "stupid, ignorant peasants. 11 The Russians were of a different religion from their Polish landlords. The Polish pans sought not only to extract money from their Russian subjects, but to change their religion as well. 

They wanted them to become Roman Catholics instead of being Greek Catholics.

Since the Polish noblemen did not tend their own estates but left the management largely to their Jewish stewards, it fell to the Jews to collect all the burdensome taxes. The Jews being altogether of a different religion and nationality from the Russians, the hatred against them was redoubled, and all the bitterness which the Russian peasants stored up against their landlords was nourished in even greater measure against the Jews. Some day they hoped to repay both.

Their opportunity came in 1648. The Polish government had armed the Cossacks, as they were called, in the hope that they might defend the country against the Tatars, who lived beyond the Dnieper on the east. Driven to revolt by the treatment that they were receiving, and inspired by a band of independent Cossacks who lived east of the Ukraine, the Polish Cossacks, together with their free brethren, made an alliance with the Tatars, and together the savage host fell upon Poland. Their leader was a bold and utterly cruel chieftan called Chmelnicki. A Polish army sent against them was defeated in a decisive battle in May, 1648, and with the main Polish force disposed of, the country now lay open before the wild barbarians. The Cossacks first and foremost fell upon the Polish nobles and the Jews. The cruelest torture was inflicted upon the Jewish population. Men were flayed and roasted alive. Women were cut open and live cats sewed into their bodies. Everywhere entire communities were wiped out. The Jews tried to defend themselves and fought valiantly, but the Cossacks overcame them either by ruse or treachery. In the meantime, the king of Poland died, and more than six months elapsed before his successor was chosen. For a half a year there was thus nobody to oppose the fury of the Cossacks. A city here and there or an individual nobleman tried to stop the mad peasants, but without success. Through seven hundred communities the storm raged, with the Jewish dead mounting to hundreds of thousands. Some estimate it as high as from five to six hundred thousand. Never before had the Jews suffered such wholesale murder in their entire career. 

For a short time a peace was patched up, but soon the Cossacks united with the Russians, and the united army once again started an invasion, not only of Southern Poland, but of the northern region, which had been previously spared. Wherever the Cossacks came, the fate of the Jews was either conversion or destruction.

Sa J.. 10 (:

AFTER MASSACRES KINGS TRY TO HELP, BUT ENEMIES MAKE LIFE DIFFICULT 

Poland finally signed a peace, making large concessions to the conquerors. The kings now turned to rebuilding their countries, and among their other subjects they sought to reestablish their Jews. But while the kings were planning to ease the lot of the Jews, the burghers, the schlachta, and the Church councils continued to legislate against them with all their former bitterness, and the students of the Jesuit schools made pogroms in their villages.

EDUCATIONAL STANDARDS LOWERED 

The depressed condition had an influence on the level of learning of the Polish Jews. While the kahals, both local and national, continued to provide schools of some sort, these were no longer of the same high standard as before. Especially in the southern districts, where the destruction by the Cossacks had been most severe, schooling was meager and ignorance was widespread, with attendant superstition, Thus it was popularly believed that the air is filled with the ghosts of the dead who cannot find rest in their own world or with demons who thirst for human blood. A public trial was held against evil spirits who were said to have possessed a poor handworker and laid claim to his house.

SELF-GOVERNMENT ABOLISHED 

POLAND DIVIDED 

In 1764 the Polish government dealt a death blow to the inner Jewish organization. It decided that it would collect its taxes from the Jews directly and, no longer needing the Jewish self-governing organization, ordered it abolished. In 1768 the 

Jews were once more the victims of a Cossack uprising, and once more thousands lost their lives, although the slaughter was not on as wide a scale as the earlier one under Chmelnicki. Finally, in 1772, the partition of Poland took place between Russia, Prussia, and Austria. A quarter of a million Jews were now trans ferred to Russia, and a large number to Austria. Hereafter the story of Polish Jewry will fall under the history of both these countries.

JEWS IN TURKEY 

During the month when the Jews were being expelled from Spain an expedition of three ships under Christopher Columbus was setting out to find a new land. Jews had much to do with that expedition. Two Marranos helped to finance the voyage. 

Jewish scientists had devised the instruments which Columbus used. A Marrano sailor is said to have been the first to see land and a Marrano interpreter was the first white man to set foot on American shores. Shortly thereafter, Marranos were to flee to the new world to escape the dreaded Inquisition and to worship their God as Jews.

However, the Jews who were expelled from Spain did not yet know of the lands to be discovered. Instead they went eastward, settling in North Africa, Italy, and the Greek Islands. A settiement of special importance grew up in Turkey, where for a time it seemed as if Jewish life would regain the splendor which it enjoyed in Spain.

Within a short time there were about 100,000 Jews in Turkey. 

Not only did many Spanish exiles settle there, but large numbers of Marranos fled to that country and threw off the mask of Christianity. In addition, Jews expelled from various cities in Germany or Italy found a haven there. Each group spoke its own language and organized its own synagogue, so that in the city of Constantinople alone there were forty-four synagogues. There were important Jewish communities in other Turkish cities, such as Saloniki and Smyrna. The new settlers were either large merchants who traded by land and sea and controlled the trade and banking of Turkey, or skilled craftsmen, among whom the most prized were the weapon makers, who could make small arms, gun powder, and cannon. Important, too, were the printing houses, which were new in Turkey and which printed books in all languages, Many Jews with a wide knowledge of languages became translators, public officials, and farmers of the revenue. The Jews who were divided at first into various communities, little by little began to unite. They joined especially to form a Talmud Torah, a school for their children.

JOSEPH NASI 

Several Jews held high offices in Constantinople. The most important Jew in Turkey was Joseph Nasi, a former Marrano, He encountered many adventures in Antwerp and Venice, until he finally came to Turkey, settled there, married, and threw off his assumed Christian religion. Soon he was established in commerce, and his business grew so large that he was competing with the largest of the Venetian merchants. He became very friendly with a son of the ruling sultan and through him grew influential in public affairs. 

Thus, through his help he was able to force Pope Paul IV to free all the Jewish subjects in Turkey who, as Marranos, were arrested by the officers of the Inquisition.

When the sultan died and his son Salim II, the friend of Joseph, came to the throne, he gave Joseph as a gift for life the Island of Naxos. Joseph became the Duke of Naxos and a very important person in Turkish politics. The Jewish statesman now took revenge on the Venetians by making war on them and taking the island of Cyprus from them. The French king had refused to pay a debt which he owed to the house of Joseph Nasi. Accordingly. 

Joseph seized all the French ships in Turkish harbors until the debt was paid.

Joseph, Duke of Naxos, tried to do something for his poor brother Jews. He bought the ruined city of Tiberias and wanted to settle Jews there as silk weavers. Unfortunately, the Arabs were opposed to a large settlement of Jews, and Joseph became too busy with other matters to give the rebuilding of Tiberias proper attention. Thus the scheme failed to develop, and the whole matter was abandoned.

When King Salim died, Joseph Nasi lost his influence in politics. He spent his last years in retirement in his castle. 

SOLOMON ASHKENZAI 

Another Jew, Solomon Ashkenazi, a famous physician, became assistant to the grand vizier after Joseph Nasi. At the end of the war between Turkey and Venice, Ashkenazi was sent to arrange for the peace of terms. Just at that time Venice was planning to expel the Jews and through Ashkenazi's influence the evil decree was set aside.

TURKEY DECLINES 

Jews continued to live and work in Turkey, but Turkey itself soon declined. Governors were forcing their provinces to pay very heavy taxes, while the government did nothing to help or serve the people. Kings were becoming willful and tyrannical. 

Gradually Turkey lost its position of importance, and with it the Jewish community too, in time, became one of the backward and more ignorant groups of the Jewish world. 

JEWS IN PALESTINE 

At the same time that Turkey became a temporary haven for the Jewish people, Palestine too flared into life for a little while. Some of the expelled Jews from Spain went to Palestine, especially after 1517, when Turkey conquered the country. Most of the Jews of Jerusalem were very poor and often could not pay their taxes without outside help. But the principal settlement at that time was in Galilee, in the city of Safed, which exported provisions to Damascus. The main occupations of the Safed 

Jews were weaving, goldsmithing, shoemaking, tanning, and building. But the chief distinction of Safed was that it was a city of great scholars. Very pious men who studied the mysteries of 

Judaism, called Kabala, lived in Safed and gave the place the name of the "holy city."

47 Abi 163 น. 3 00 medo. 1 1 3 

KULDER. VI OWN, 1 Wigus

JOSEPH KARO 

The most famous rabbi of Safed was Joseph ben Ephraim Karo, who lived from 1488 to 1565. Karo was born in Spain four years before the expulsion. After many wanderings, his parents settled in European Turkey, in the city of Saloniki. Joseph was a great In scholar who knew the entire Talmud and all the rabbinic laws. 

1522 he undertook a great work. He was going to examine all the rabbinic laws and to set them down in order. For twenty years he worked on this task and finally finished it in Palestine. At first his book was planned for scholars only. Then he decided to rewrite the book for the ordinary reader. Soon he had a practical collection of laws telling the average man what he might and what he might not do. Every detail of life was included in the law, how to eat, sleep, dress, pray, observe the Sabbath and festivals, prepare food. The Jew needed only to follow the laws as outlined in the Shulchan Aruch (set table), and he would be sure to fulfill the law of God. The Shulchan Aruch became the most popular book. 

It was followed faithfully until modern times and is still followed by large numbers of Orthodox Jews all over the world. 

Many other rabbis of Turkey and Palestine left books, most of them mystical. However, Palestine, like Turkey, also declined, and it was only after 1880 that the rebuilding began which is making Palestine bloom once again.

MESSIANIC MOVEMENTS 

With the break-up of the Jewish center in Spain, it seemed to many Jews that the end of the world had come and that the Messiah was therefore soon to arrive. Various Jewish leaders made guesses when the Messiah was due. One said he would arrive in 1531, another set it for the year 1505. In 1502 a man appeared in Venice who said he was sent to announce the coming of the Messiah in six months; in the meantime the people must pray very fervently. When the Messiah failed to appear he said that the people had not prayed hard enough.

DAVID REUBENI 

But in 1524 a mysterious wanderer appeared in Venice by the name of David Reubeni. Reubeni means a person from the tribe of 

Reuben. He claimed that he was descended from Reuben, one of the ten lost tribes. These tribes, he said, formed a Jewish kingdom somewhere in the desert headed by his brother Joseph. David, as general, was now sent to Europe to enlist aid in the reconquest of Palestine from the Turks. He came to ask the pope and the European kings to give him weapons. He was a short, thin, bald man who prayed with great earnestness, fasted often, and spoke only Hebrew. The impression he made on the Venetian community was very favorable.

From Venice he traveled to Rome, where he entered the papal court riding on a white charger. David appeared before a meeting

of the cardinals, and later a cardinal who knew Hebrew introduced him to the pope himself. The pope, troubled by many enemies, believed David and thought he could gain help through him. 

Accordingly, he gave him letters to the king of Portugal asking the king to help David with weapons and with ships. 

ANOTHER MESSIAH 

SOLOMON MOLCHO 

David's success made a great impression on the Jews of Portugal, for the king actually stopped persecuting the Marranos while David was there. The Marranos thus looked upon him as their deliverer. One, especilly, Diego Pires, was moved to great dreams. 

He had been a government secretary at Lisbon, but now he threw off the Christian religion, changed his name to Solomon Molcho and proposed to Reubeni that together they undertake a program of freeing the Jews, with himself, Solomon, as the Messiah. For a time Reubeni tried to shake him off. Solomon Molcho went East, studied the Kabala in Turkey, and met Joseph Karo in Safed. Then he returned to Rome and for three days sat among the beggars at the Tiber Bridge opposite the papal palace, as the Messiah was supposed to do. He claimed he heard voices and saw visions. On the Sabbath he preached in the synagogue. He predicted that the 

Tiber would overflow and that an earthquake would shake Portugal.

For a time he was forced to leave Rome and went to Venice. The 

Venetian senate tested him, but thought he was an Eastern fakir. 

However, in 1530, Rome actually was flooded as he predicted, and in January of the following year an earthquake shook Portugal.

Molcho was now convinced that his prophecies were true, and so were many others, including the pope, who received him and gave him letters of protection against the Inquisition. 

The Jews of Rome, however, feared that a Messianic movement might bring them harm. One of them, a physician, reported to the Inquisition that Molcho, a former Marrano, was now prophesying the destruction of Rome. The Inquisition sent men to arrest 

Molcho, but with the aid of the pope, he succeeded in escaping. 

A short time thereafter Molcho rejoined Reubeni, and together they decided on a desperate step. They would go to Regensburg, in 

Germany, where the emperor was meeting his nobles, and they would ask him to help the Jews go to war against the Turks. But the emperor was not as kindly as the king of Portugal. He ordered them both arrested. Molcho was turned over to the Inquisition and burned alive, while Reubeni was put in prison and there died a few years later.

SABBATAI ZEVI 

A far more serious Messianic movement took place more than one hundred years later in the city of Smyrna, or Ismir, as it was then called. That city being connected with all large centers of European trade, Jewish traders spread the word quickly, and a movement began which almost swept all of Europe's Jews off their feet.

D A 

UT MO 

4073) 

DC 

30. 194.

This movement was initiated by a man called Sabbatai Zevi, born in Ismir in 1626. The lad was so brilliant that at fifteen he already had a thorough knowledge of rabbinic literature and studied the Kabala. He lived a very severe life, fasting and purifying his body through frequent bathing. He often prayed by himself and sang aloud various Messianic songs.

In 1648, when the Cossacks attacked the Jews of Poland and news began to reach Ismir about the murder of thousands of Jews in the Ukraine, Sabbatai was deeply upset. Soon refugees began streaming into Turkey. Sabbatai was greatly moved by the calamity that befell his people, and he began to dream of what might be done for them. By reading mystic numbers, he claimed that 1648 was the year when the Jews were to be freed from their exile, and he decided that he would be the one to free them.

In order to show that he was the expected redeemer of the Jewish people, he arose in the synagogue and pronounced the name of God publicly, something which only the high priest used to do in the Holy of Holies on Yom Kippur when the Temple stood. 

At such boldness the rabbis were frightened, and Sabbatai's own teacher excommunicated him. Sabbatai was forced to leave 

Ismir. On the way, it is reported, he met a man who claimed to be a miracle worker and who gave him a secret writing which stated that he would be the Messiah. In Saloniki he invited his followers to a feast and declared that he was going to marry the daughter of God, namely, the Torah. This angered the rabbis of Saloniki, who forced him to leave the city. Finally he came to Jerusalem. 

There he continued his habits of fasting and praying and lying for hours on the graves of the great men of old. 

Because of the wars in Europe, gifts from the pious Jews for the residents of Jerusalem stopped. At the same time the government imposed especially heavy taxes. The Jews were desperate, being unable to pay their taxes, and feared that they would be sent to prison. But Sabbatai undertook to bring help. From a rich admirer in Egypt he succeeded in getting enough money to pay the needed taxes. This raised Sabbatai in the esteem of the Jerusalem community, and they now really believed that he was the Messiah. 

There was a strange girl in Poland whose name was Sarah.

She had been stolen from her home at the age of six during the Cossack uprising and brought up in a monastery. At the age of twelve she fled to Amsterdam and returned to Judaism, but she was very hysterical and believed that miracles would happen to her. She believed especially that she was destined to become the wife of the Messiah. When stories of this strange girl reached 

Sabbatai, he sent for her and married her. This too was to be a sign that Sabbatai was the true Messiah.

PROCLAIMED MESSIAH 

An enthusiastic prophet presented himself and announced that on Shovuos he had heard from heaven that after a year and a few months the Messiah, the son of David, would appear. This Messiah he insisted, was none other than Sabbatai Zevi. Sabbatai would soon remove the sultan of Turkey from his throne and would reign in his stead. As these reports began to spread, the Jews of Palestine became frightened that they might be accused of treason, Accordingly, Sabbatai was forced to leave Jerusalem and to return to Ismir.

There the earlier excommunication had been forgotten. As he appeared in the synagogue on Rosh Ha-shono in 1665, some of the people began crying out, "Long live our king! Long live our king the Messiah! The enthusiasm spread, and soon everybody was throbbing with excitement about the Messiah, who had arrived. People began to repent of their sins publicly. Many women spent nights and days praying and fasting. In their excitement many fell into strange fits and began talking words the meaning of which they themselves did not know. People called it prophesying and considered it as further proof that Sabbatai Zevi was the genuine Messiah.

From Ismir reports spread far and wide, and everywhere people were stirred to the utmost. Even in Amsterdam many a citizen sold his house and goods, dividing his property among the poor, and sat' waiting for the call of the Messiah to go to the Holy Land. Men went about singing and dancing in the streets. Many Christians, even, believed that the Jewish Messiah had come. Young men dressed in holiday clothes and wearing blue bands, the symbol of the Messiah, danced in the synagogues. The same scenes took place in 

Hamburg and in all other cities where many Jews lived. Even in 

Venice the excitement was great. And in Poland, where the Jews had been broken by the terrible persecution, there was renewed hope and great joy. Only one rabbi Rabbi Jacob Sasportas, of

Hamburg, was clear-headed enough to oppose this wild movement, but he was quickly silenced.

ARRESTED IN CONSTANTINOPLE 

Sabbatai had done nothing as yet toward winning the throne which he claimed for himself. He was finally forced to take some action, and in 1666 he decided to sail for Constantinople to take the crown from the sultan. There was tremendous joy in `Ismir, but there was consternation in Constantinople, where the Jews feared they would all be involved in a charge of treason. Accordingly, before the ship even landed, the authorities were informed of its approach, and Sabbatai was arrested as soon as he set foot on Constantinople.

But even in prison so many people came to visit Sabbatai and so much money was spent on bribes that his prison soon became like a triumphal palace. The authorities therefore decided to transfer him to the fortress of Abydos in nearby Gallipoli.

The fact that Sabbatai was not sentenced to death seemed to his followers nothing short of a miracle. Further, the keeper of the fortress was glad to receive bribes and permitted Sabbatai to live in great luxury and to have friends and visitors in a constant stream passing in and out. Sabbatai's rooms were decked with the costliest rugs, and his household articles were made of pure gold. Sabbatai kept writing to every part of the Jewish world, urging the Jews to abolish all fast days, which reminded them of the destruction of the Temple and to celebrate the new birth of the Jewish people which he, the Messiah, was bringing. 

TURNS MOHAMMEDAN 

Among the many men who came to visit him, however, there was one Polish Kabalist by the name of Nehemiah. Nehemiah was considered a very holy man, and Sabbatai was very anxious to prove to Nehemiah that he was indeed the Messiah. For three whole days Sabbatai and Nehemiah debated. But Nehemiah became convinced that Sabbatai was not the Messiah. So greatly was 

Nehemiah moved by his discovery that as he left the fortress he threw down his hat towards the Moslems and put on the turban as a sign that he was accepting the religion of Islam. 

It may have been his keen disappointment that this Messiah whom he had dreamed of turned out to be a fraud. Thereupon Nehemiah went to the authorities and told them that Sabbatai Zevi was trying to persuade the Jewish people to rebel against the sultan. When the sultan heard of it, he ordered that Sabbatai be brought at once to Adrianople. The court doctor, a converted Jew, laid before Sabbatai the choice either of undergoing all sorts of horrible tortures or of accepting Mohammedanism. Sabbatai yielded and accepted the Mohammedan religion. He put on the turban and now became officially a Mohammedan. But even then the Jewish world was at first slow to be shocked by Sabbatai's treason. Many of his followers believed that God had commanded him for a time to take on the guise of a Mohammedan. Many refused to believe his conversion altogether. Others said it was only his shadow that became a Mohammedan. And thus for over a year the people continued to believe in him. But in the meantime his opponents were growing stronger. Sabbatai's prophet promised that during the coming holidays Sabbatai would win a victory over the sultan, but the miracle did not happen, and so he had to leave the city.

MOVEMENT BREAKS UP 

He

Sabbatai, in the meantime, claiming that he wanted to convert Jews to Mohammedanism, was permitted to visit the synagogues freely. Soon he was once more acclaimed as the Messiah. But the grand vizier decided to put a stop to Sabbatai's excursions. ordered him sent to the little city of Dulcigno, where he was forced to live in complete isolation. In 1676 Sabbatai died after a short illness, and soon the whole Messianic bubble burst. 

Jews shamefacedly went back to their old ways. They had learned a lesson which they were not to forget for many years. Nevertheless, even after Sabbatai's death a small sect continued to be -

lieve in him. In time many followed the path of Sabbatai Zevi, and in 1687 became converted to the Mohammedan religion. 

A NEW SECT 

CHASIDIM 

The Jews of Poland were terribly shaken by the Sabbataian movement. They had been left broken and bleeding after the frightful massacres inflicted upon them by the Chmelnicki bands and by the armies which followed, and now the Sabbatai Zevi bubble destroyed their last spiritual hope. Nor was that all. 

Another movement, the Frankist, for a while roused Messianic expectations, then ended in the Church. The Jews of Southern 

Poland were thus utterly without hope.

In the North, where Jews were still learned and devoted to scholarship, they could study and find in the holy books solace from the cruel outside world. The southern Jews were mainly ignorant as well as poor. As such they felt altogether unworthy in the eyes of God and man. Therefore they hated the rich and the scholars; for they felt they had no joy in this life, and not even religion as practiced by the scholarly rabbis of Lithuania could afford them any hope either of pleasure in this world or of a place in Heaven in the world to come. Fortunately for these poor, depressed Jews, a movement was soon to be started which was to bring them hope and joy, to make life once more happy and worth while for them. This movement was called Chasidism, meaning piety. 

Chasidism is not strictly a Messianic movement, but its followers hoped that through it they could hasten the coming of the Messiah and above all that they would all be assured of a place in Heaven after their death.

THE FOUNDER 

ISRAEL BA-AL SHEM TOV 

The founder of the sect of the Chasidim was Israel Ba-al Shem Tov, born in the year 1700 in the Wallachian Mountains. 

Left an orphan early, Israel was brought up by kind people in the community. He studied in a Talmud Torah for the poor, but he did not distinguish himself in his studies, and more often than he studied he used to run away to the forest, where he spent many hours by himself. Despairing of making an educated man of him, the community hired him out, at the age of twelve, to a m'lamed, a Hebrew teacher, as a helper. On the way to school he used to sing beautiful songs to the children and entertained them with delightful tales, so that he became most beloved by them. Later he was made assistant beadle in the synagogue. Dur- ing the day he seemed to sleep, but at night when nobody was watching him he delved deeply into the works of the Kabala, the secret Jewish lore, and became quite a student of it. 

At the age of twenty he settled in the Galician town of 

Brody and married the sister of a distinguished rabbi. His brother-in-law tried in vain to get him to study the Talmud. 

Being ashamed of his ignoramus brother-in-law, he advised him

2 RJ 

S176 POLI MELO 

eng ps 

30 13 心​い ​10 

L t;

to settle away from Brody. Israel settled in a small town, where in turn he became a Hebrew teacher, a shochet, and a chazan, or else supported himself by digging clay which his wife took to market.

At the age of thirty he decided to become active as a ba-al shem. A ba-al shem was a person who performed wonderful cures by means of Kabalistic sayings. However, together with his Kabalism the ba-al shem also used healing herbs, and the herbs may have been more effective than his Kabalistic words. A ba-al shem was a common sight in Polish cities. Thus he traveled about dispensing his remedies among Jews, peasants, and nobles. Soon he became a distinguished ba-al shem and came to be known as the Ba-al Shem Tov, the "good ba-al shem, for in addition to curing bodies, he could also cure souls. He began to teach people a new approach to religion, and people took to it eagerly and happily. It was a message for which they had waited a long time, and at last it had been brought to them.

HIS TEACHINGS 

The 

What was the substance of the Ba-al Shem's teaching?

Ba-al Shem Tov taught that it is not learning or a knowledge of the Torah which makes a person religious, but rather faith, clinging to God with strong devotion and prayer. God, he told his followers is in everything in every human being, in every blade of grass, in every home, and in every heart. A person must desire 

God, and God will be with him. But God cannot be served in sadness and in self-torture. He can only be served in joy and in gladness. Let the person be happy that God has given him life and that he has the powers with which to serve God. 

If the individual person is plain and humble and does not know how to approach God properly, he need have no fear for his future in the world to come. The tsadik (righteous man) who acts as the rabbi in the Chasidic sect will join his soul with the soul of all his followers and together lead them triumphantly before God's throne of glory. Thus all the believer needs to do is to believe in a tsadik, or rabbi, and through his rabbi he will achieve far more than the learned scholars of Northern Poland or Lithuania could through their cold study. 

In 1740 Israel Ba-al Shem Tov, or the Besht, as he is called for short, settled in the town of Miedzyboz. There a large group of students gathered around him, and he instructed them in his Torah by means of proverbs and parables. He taught them that they must concentrate all their thoughts on God and that they could achieve such concentration through prayer, when their soul would be free from all earthly thoughts. He advised them to read books of moral teachings and Midrashic tales rather than to study books of halacha, or law.

CHASIDISM SPREADS 

The founder of the Chasidim died on Shovuos in 1760 in Miedzyboz. After his death various leaders took up the movement and

spread it to all centers of the Ukraine and even to Lithuania and White Russia. In the South Chasidism became corrupted by the many dynasties of rabbis who wanted to use their position as a source of acquiring money and power. The ignorant Chasidim soon came to believe in the tsadik as a worker of magic. They went to him with all sorts of requests for children if they were childless, for good business if their business did not prosper, for health if they were sick. Every request was accompanied by a gift of money to insure its proper attention, and thus the rabbis usually became very wealthy. On the other hand, in the North, the movement took a different turn. Chasidic joy and prayer with great devotion became joined with deep Talmudic learning and knowledge of the Torah. It was the northern Chasidim who left the more important stamp on the movement.

OPPOSITION 

ELIJAH, GAON OF VILNA 

At first there was great opposition to the Chasidic movement by the misnagdim (opponents), as the other Jews were called. 

At the head of the Lithuanian rabbis in the second part of the 1700's stood Elijah, Gaon of Vilna (1720-97). He was the greatest Talmudist in many generations. At the age of six he had already been able to study Talmud without a teacher, and at ten he had surprised all the rabbis with the depth of his knowledge. In addition to religious studies, he also knew geometry and other branches of mathematics, something which was unusual for a rabbi in that part of Lithuania. All day long he engaged in the study of Torah, seldom speaking to anyone of worldly affairs. He ate sparingly and slept little. His only relationship with the outside world was through his students to whom he used to teach the Talmud. This rabbi, who was most highly regarded by all his generation, thought he saw in Chasidism a dangerous repetition of the Sabbataian and Frankist movements. He called together a rabbinic court and decided to ban all the books published by this new sect, and to excommunicate all those who belonged to the sect. No relationship was to be had with them; there was to be no intermarriage with them, and their dead were not even to be buried in Jewish graves. During his entire life Elijah Gaon remained a bitter opponent of the Chasidim.

NEW DANGERS UNITE PARTIES 

Soon, however, new winds began blowing from other parts of the world. New movements were to start, movements of haskalah, or enlightenment, which the pious Jews thought threatened to destroy not only Chasidism, but all of Judaism as well. After much mischief had already been done, the leaders of the various movements realized that they had better get together to meet the common enemy. 

New times were starting for the Jewish people, both good and bad. The Chasidic movement may be considered as the closing event in the Jewish Middle Ages and as the beginning of modern times, to which we shall now turn.

HOW DID THE COMING OF DEMOCRACY FREE THE JEWISH PEOPLE? 

We have reached a turning point in the story of our people. 

The Middle Ages, as we have seen from the previous section, threatened to destroy the Jewish people altogether. people altogether. Driven out of their homes time after time, by one country after another, they were attacked and sometimes massacred by lawless bands. Even in the best of times, unfair laws kept them from owning land and from working in the good trades and professions. They were forced to wear peculiar clothes with a Jewish badge and were hounded by many bad laws and special taxes. The Jewish people in the seventeenth century was thus reduced in number to less than a million in the whole world. This was the lowest number since ancient times. The future looked bad for Israel.

Something must have happened, then, you are probably thinking for you know that Israel was not destroyed. You know, from your own lives, that the Jewish people was not brought down still lower, but instead rose up high and had honored positions among the nations. Jews have become governors and judges, professors, artists, and captains of industry. You probably know also, that when the war began in 1939, there were not one million, but sixteen million Jews in the world, or the largest number that there has ever been.

How did the Jew among the nations rise from his bitter depths to heights, in some ways, as great as those in Spain? The Jewish people trusted, even in those hopeless Middle Ages, that God would inspire a new, fairer spirit into mankind. They saw before them the fulfillment of the promise given to the prophet Isaiah: "The people that walked in darkness have seen a great light; they that dwelt in the land of the shadow of death, upon them hath the light shined." That light, in one word, was democracy. It was the coming of democracy that brought happiness and freedom to our people in the modern age.

What is democracy? It is the idea that men are equal. And since men are equal, every man, whatever his class, his race, or his religion, should be given a fair chance to earn a decent living for himself, to develop his mind, and to worship God according to his own belief. It is easy for an American to understand democracy, for we enjoy its blessings every day. 

It is easy to understand, also, why the Jewish people should have hoped and longed, more than any other, for the coming of democracy. For it is the Jewish people that gave to the world the idea upon which true democracy can be built. That idea is the belief in one God, Who created all men. With one Father, all men must be brothers, each one equal to the other. The prophet 

Malachi put it this way: "Have we not all one father? Hath not one God created us?" The Jewish people has spent its history struggling for freedom. We were born as a people when Moses organized us to free us from slavery in Egypt. And each year we celebrate that freedom on Passover. Each year, likewise, at Purim

2. time, we remember how we were freed from persecution by Haman. 

And every winter we celebrate religious freedom when we light the Chanuko candles telling of the defeat of Antiochus, the Syrian tyrant who sought, by armed force, to stamp out Judaism and make our people worship Greek idols. No other people is more devoted to democracy and freedom than our people. 

BEGINNINGS OF DEMOCRACY: 

CAPITALISM 

In How then did democracy come into the modern world, and how did it help the Jew? It came very slowly and only after long and difficult struggles. It had its beginnings in the field of economics, that is, in the way men make their living. The society of the Middle Ages was organized in a manner known as feudalism. it, as you learned before, the average man was a vassal, or servant, of a lord. The lord owned a large area of land and he let the peasant work it on condition that they pay him a part of their produce, work several days a week on his estate, and fight in his army. The lord was like a king, giving each peasant protection and administering justice. The lords in turn, would make a similar promise to the king. You can see that under such conditions men were not equal. Instead, men were divided into various classes, each serving the class above it. Some men broke away from field labor and became merchants and handicraftsmen, shoemakers, tailors, smiths, etc. But these, too, received only certain rights from the kings or nobles in whose territory their town lay. 

In this feudal society even Christians were not equal, so you can imagine that the Jew certainly was not considered an equal. 

Indeed, he was a complete outsider. For the most part, he could not own land or participate in many of the occupations controlled by the guilds, which excluded Jews. Some few Jews still continued large-scale trade, but most Jews were forced into being money lenders or dealers in second hand articles, or peddlers, selling little household objects from door to door. Forced to live under such difficult conditions, the Jews nevertheless often saved up large sums of money or precious jewels, so that, if suddenly their privileges were withdrawn, or if an angry mob would drive them out of the city, they would have something with which to begin life anew in another city.

As time went on, however, a new way for men to earn their living developed. Bright men began to recognize that people could produce more and better things if they would specialize in their labors. Instead of having one man make every part of a shoe himself, if many men would cooperate, some making soles, others heels, others uppers, others putting these together, they could produce many more shoes together than they could by making them separately. 

To accomplish this, capital, or money invested, was needed. It would take weeks or months, between the time when the leather was first bought, and the shoes were finally sold, often in some distant city. During this time, money had to be paid out for the wages of the workers even before the income from the shoes came in. 

This money, so invested, is called capital. And the method of doing business in this manner, is called capitalism.

3. 

When capitalism began its growth in the Middle Ages, Jews came to play a more and more important part in it. There were several reasons why this happened. During the Middle Ages, the 

Jew had been forbidden to own land, and therefore, whatever wealth he had was in the form of ready cash. But when capitalism came, it was money, as we have just seen, and not land, that was urgently needed. Many Jews, therefore, who had money, or had long experience as treasurers or bankers, provided capital for the new factories. Some thus became men of large wealth and influence in the new era.

Another factor from which the Jew had suffered was the fact that he was scattered throughout various countries. This had worked hardship and pain upon our people but it left them with friends and fellow Jews in other lands. This made it possible for Jewish merchants to trade with other countries much more easily than for the average Gentile merchant. As capitalism developed, the need to trade one's products with foreign lands grew immensely. 

Indeed, trade is often called the life-blood of capitalism. 

With these advantages, the Jews made great contributions to the growth of this new and promising way of producing goods. 

As forward-looking non-Jews saw the important work that Jews were doing in helping to build up capitalism, they began to regard Jews less and less as outsiders. Records show that as capitalism began several centuries ago, certain Italian cities which previously had excluded Jews now admitted them, sometimes even inviting them specially for the purpose of encouraging commerce. 

In the seventeenth century, when merchants of Marseilles objected to the presence of Jews who were in competition with them, their ruler replied that the Jews were useful to the state as a whole. 

And Christian IV, King of Denmark, sent a special invitation to

Portuguese Jews to make the city of Gluckstadt their home because he wanted to encourage commercial development. Holland likewise prospered because it admitted Jews who fled from Spain and Portugal following the time of the Inquisition.

Later, in 1655, a Dutch rabbi, Manasseh ben Israel, pleaded with Oliver Cromwell to allow the Jews to settle in England. One of his strongest arguments was that his people would help develop English trade. Equally interesting is the fact that Holland feared that Manasseh might take the Jews out of Holland to England. 

But he declared that he only wanted to save the Jews from the 

Inquisition. 

The coming of capitalism, besides bringing immediate advantages to the Jew, did an even greater service for him and for humanity later on it helped to bring democracy. You can see at once how, when men began to work on a contract for wages instead of as servants on an estate, the old idea of upper and lower classes began to break down. Men began to feel themselves equals. 

Workmen would save part of their wages, and when they had gathered enough, would start their own factories or stores.

THE RENAISSANCE 

The idea that men should be treated as equals was strengthened also by another change that came in with the modern age. In the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries a movement began in Europe, called the Renaissance, or rebirth. It was the rebirth of man's interest in literature, art, and science. Men turned back to study again the great books of ancient times. Through them they learned that other peoples, the Hebrews, the Greeks, and the Romans, had also lived civilized lives, developed good laws, wrote inspiring poetry and plays, and created beautiful statues. They also learned that these peoples had made important discoveries about astronomy, medicine, and other scientific matters, which were the bases of all knowledge up to their own time. Helped by the knowledge of the past, the people of the Renaissance carried that culture forward, making new scientific discoveries, writing new poetry and plays, creating new works of art, and planning a new and brighter world. 

Now the 

About the same time, printing was invented. Before that time, books had been very scarce and expensive, since it took weeks for a single volume to be copied out in longhand. printing-press could produce books cheaply and in large numbers, so that all men could buy them.

The rebirth now changed the whole course of the world's history. In the first place, men began to think for themselves, instead of thinking only the thoughts that their lords or teachers of the church put into their minds. They now began to compare their own way of living with other possible ways. A desire for freedom was aroused by the reading of the courageous thoughts found in the Greek and Roman philosophers, and especially in the 

Jewish Bible. ...Proclaim liberty throughout the land......Ye shall return every man unto his possession (his own land)." 

Leviticus 25:10.

Common men now began to feel that they were just as good as those who had been born with titles and deserved just as much as they, a chance to live a free and happy life.

THE REFORMATION 

Out of the Renaissance arose also ideas of religious freedom which helped in bringing equal rights to the Jew. Even as men began to criticize their working conditions, so they began to wonder whether or not they were living under the best possible religious organization. In those days, for most of Europe, there was but one Christian church, the Catholic Church. No one could disagree with the meaning of Christianity as explained by the Pope, the head of that church, or by the priests, who were his representatives. Now, however, as more and more people read the Bible for themselves, they began to form their own opinions. Finally, in the sixteenth century, leaders beginning with Martin Luther in Germany arose, who declared that men need not follow the Pope to be true Christians. They need only to follow the Bible as their

ཝཱ དྷཝེ 64 2 ​11.... C 'I

5. conscience directs them. In this way the various Protestant churches in Christianity were born (so called because they protested against the rule of Catholicism). This event is known in history as the Protestant Reformation (started in 1517). 

This Protestant Reformation helped the Jew in two ways. In the first place, by encouraging men to read the Bible for themselves, it brought many people to see the Jew in a better light. 

Before that time, Christians knew the Old Testament only as explained to them by the priests. This often led the listener to despise the Jew. Now, as the Protestants read the Bible for themselves, they began to appreciate the greatness of the Old Testament and later many came to admire the people that follow it - the Jews.

As the Bible became more widely read, many Christian teachers turned to learned Jews to help them understand what the Old Testament meant, in the original Hebrew. Indeed that is what made 

Manasseh ben Israel of Amsterdam so popular in his day. Manasseh was a rabbi who combined great learning in the Bible with an ability to write in Latin, Spanish, Portuguese, Dutch, and Italian. 

He was therefore, called upon by Christians of all lands to help them explain the Bible.

The second way in which the Protestant Reformation helped the Jews was indirect, but even more important. The Reformation brought the idea of freedom of religion into democracy. Protestantism began by insisting that it be given the freedom to worship in its own way. It was but the next logical step to declare that anyone, even non-Christians, should be given the freedom to worship God in their way. Even though at first the Protestants denied to the Jew the freedom which they demanded for themselves, they finally came to the conclusion that government must allow full freedom of religion to all its citizens. 

You now have a picture of how democracy came into the modern world. You saw how capitalism, with its new opportunities for employment and trade, tended to bring economic freedom to men. 

You saw how it, plus the Renaissance, with its rebirth of knowledge, with men thinking for themselves, brought about the idea of political freedom and equal rights among men. And you saw how the Protestant Reformation developed the idea of religious freedom. 

EMANCIPATION AND DEMOCRACY ADVANCE 

As democracy advanced, Jews won increasing freedom and equal rights known as emancipation. The liberals, as they were called, insisted likewise upon justice and freedom for our people; and those who tried to block the coming of democracy the reactionaries sought to keep the Jew in his old wretched condition. 

Holland and England were two great lands which helped the Jews gain equal rights in modern times. Many Jews had fled to these lands after being driven out of Spain and Portugal. Holland, especially, opened its doors to large numbers and gave them a chance to establish an influential and wealthy community.

These Jews, who had grown up as secret Jews or Marranos, at first feared to express their Judaism openly, but before 1600 Holland gave them permission to have their own synagogues. There were good reasons which led the Dutch people to support liberty. 

As Protestants they had suffered bitterly because they were different from the ruling church of Europe, which was Catholic. 

Therefore they saw the need of religious freedom. As a nation they were ruled by the mighty empire of Spain. So they understood the desire for political freedom. By the end of the 1500's when the Dutch nation had won for herself both religious and political liberty, she granted the freedom-seeking Jews a haven of refuge. 

Another reason prompted the Dutch to encourage our people to enter. She was eager for the Jews, who had had experience as seagoing merchants, to help build up Holland's foreign trade. 

In establishing that trade, the Jews of Amsterdam, the capital of Holland, became powerful shareholders in the Dutch East, and West India Companies. These companies soon became so strong that they destroyed the commercial leadership of Spain and Portugal that had persecuted the Jews so bitterly. So important were the leading Jews regarded, that Rembrandt, the most famous painter in all Holland's history, made portraits of them. 

But the Jewish leaders of Amsterdam were not content merely to enjoy their own prosperity. They remembered that old Jewish motto: "All Israelites are responsible for one another," and they used their power to open the doors of new lands for their persecuted brothers even as the Jews of America have done for the Jews of Germany and other lands today. Dutch leaders secured the admission of Jews into Denmark, Belgium, parts of France, America, and most important at that time, into England. 

Since 1290, as you have learned, the Jews had been driven out of England, and no Jew had been officially allowed to enter the land. Now, however, with England's growing power on the sea and in trade, the Dutch Jews thought England might now admit Jewish merchants. Therefore, in 1650, Rabbi Manasseh ben Israel, of whom we have told before, sent a petition to Oliver Cromwell, Lord Protector of England, to allow the Jews to return. Manasseh urged two reasons for England's permitting Jews to enter: First the 

Messiah, whom both Jews and Christians were expecting in those days, would come only when the Jews would be scattered "from one end of the earth to the other. Second the Jews made themselves very useful to every country to which they came, and could help England build up its prosperity. 

He himself

Cromwell was in favor of granting the petition. was a fighter for freedom for his religious party, the Puritans. 

He had been inspired by the Jewish Bible and had used phrases from it to lead his followers into battle, comparing himself with Gideon and Judah Maccabee. Some English leaders felt that the 

Christian peoples had sinned against the Jews in persecuting them, and should make up for it by kindness. Others were interested by 

Manasseh's argument about the Messiah. But, above all, they were
