7. moved by the commercial benefits which he showed they could gain from the Jews. Manasseh, addressing himself to the English government, repeated God's promise to Abraham: "I will bless them that bless thee, and him that curseth thee will I curse.

Victory, such as it was, was not won, however, without a struggle. Merchants opposed Jewish competition. Christian ministers of religion protested against the Jews because they claimed the Jews had killed their savior, Jesus. A war between England and Holland delayed action. Manasseh pushed his case further by writing "The Defense of the Jews. In it he denied the old charges repeated against his people. The Jews, he declared, do not use the blood of Christian children on Passover, nor do they cheat in business. On the contrary, he

On the contrary, he pointed out that the Jewish law demands the highest conduct. Even though Manasseh personally had come to London on Cromwell's invitation and had addressed a great conference of English leaders on the subject, Parliament refused to grant his petition. Shortly thereafter, however, Cromwell unofficially allowed the Jews to return and a large community began to grow up in London. 

Official rights, however, still had to be won, and they came very slowly. Exceptional Jews were allowed to be citizens, but most Jews were not. In the middle of the 1700's when England granted the Jews in its colonies the privilege of being citizens, Englishmen called upon their country to give the Jews the same rights at home. Both Houses of Parliament passed the law, but so great was the objection of the common people to it, that it had to be repealed. English Jews continued to advance, but legal equality was granted only a little here and a little there, over a long period of time.

About this time certain great Christian friends of democracy in various countries began to demand a fair chance for the Jew. 

Charles de Montesquieu, a Frenchman whose book, The Spirit of Laws (1746), became the most important book on government in his time, wrote in it that Christians have no right to call themselves civilized if they continue to treat the Jews in the manner in which they do. Three years later, Gotthold Lessing, a widely-read 

German writer, wrote a play, "The Jews," which attacked Christians for their ignorance in failing to understand the excellent qualities of the Jewish people. As if to fulfill his words, a few years later, Lessing met a brilliant young Jewish philosopher and writer, Moses Mendelssohn. He was attracted to Mendelssohn because of his beautiful style, his keen mind and his love of the human race; and they became intimate, lifelong friends.

The story of Mendelssohn's education was itself an adventure. 

At fourteen, he walked seventy miles from the small city of his . birth to come to Berlin. He knocked at one of the gates of the city, for people were not permitted to enter freely in those days. 

Jews, especially, were kept out unless they were wealthy. There young Mendelssohn stood. His clothes showed that he was poor, for his father, being a writer of Torah scrolls, had little money that he could give him. His appearance was sickly and weak, for

8. he was humpbacked. Ordinarily, the watchman, who happened to be a Jew, would have kept him out. But Mendelssohn pleaded that he came to study Talmud under the new Rabbi of Berlin who had come from his own city of Dessau. The watchman let him in. Mendelssohn studied. He lived in an attic. He had no money with which to go to high school or university. But under the rabbi's instruction he studied by himself, mastering the great Hebrew books, including those of Maimonides, as well as important books in German, French, English, and Latin. He had one goal, to become a learned, cultured man in the broadest sense, as well as an observant and honorable Jew. He succeeded. After seven years, he no longer had to worry about poverty, for he became a tutor of the children of a rich man. He began to have greater opportunities to meet other Jewish and non-Jewish students of the city. 

It was then that he and the great Lessing happened to become acquainted. Their friendship grew. Using Mendelssohn as a model for the ideal character of the Jew, Lessing wrote a book which stirred all the thinking people of Europe. He called it Nathan the Wise. In it, the nobility of the Jew stands out in sharp contrast to the cruelty of some of the Christians who persecute him. In this book, Lessing wrote the famous story of the three rings: A man once owned a magic ring which had the power to make all people love him. When he was about to die, each of his three sons asked to be given the ring. Since he loved them all, he had a jeweler make two other rings which looked exactly like his own. 

He then gave one ring to each son without telling him about the other two. After his death, each saw that the others had rings too. No one knew which was the real one. So they asked a judge to examine them. He could not tell them apart. Therefore, he told the sons: "There is no need to decide now. When one of you becomes liked better than his brothers, we shall know that he has the real ring. Each then acted as if he had the real one and by being good and considerate, each won the love of all men. 

The meaning of the story was that Jews, Christians, and 

Mohammedans are the three brothers. Which has the true ring? As long as they act uprightly and honestly and for the good of others, the ring of each is true and will win the love of mankind. 

Lessing expressed this truth in another way, also, At one place in the story, the Christian Priest, praising Nathan for his charitable deeds said, "Nathan! Nathan! You are a Christian. 

There never was a better Christian!" Nathan replied: "We"agree.

For that which makes me, in your eyes a Christian, makes you, in my eyes, a Jew!"

Think of what it meant for the whole world to be told by a leading playwright that Christianity and Judaism, instead of being enemies, were really partners! People talked about it everywhere. 

Even though most people still felt that no Jew could be as good a man as Lessing described, they began to wonder whether they were being fair to the Jews.

Mendelssohn's own writings were becoming more and more popular

9. and won for him many friendships.

People began to call him the "German Plato. (Plato was a famous Greek philosopher.) Naturally, this brought credit to the Jewish people. Men saw that the Jew, given a chance to advance as Mendelssohn had been given, could do fine things for his country. More than that, Mendelssohn's influence led a Prussian councillor of State, Christian William von Dohm, to publish a whole pamphlet, "Concerning the Improvement of the Civic Condition of the Jews. In it he stated that whatever unworthy qualities the Jew might have were due only to the unfair Christian laws. It isn't fair, he said, first to restrict the Jew to such despised professions as peddling and loaning money, and then to condemn him for doing the only things that were left for him to do. He proposed giving the Jews equal political rights, a chance to get ahead and to enter the schools and colleges of the country. A close friendship with Henrietta 

Herz, a charming and cultured German Jewess, and an acquaintance with the educated Jewish circles in Berlin, also had a great influence on Mirabeau, the most powerful statesman in France, who urged the freeing of the Jews in his country. 

In time, these ideas began to bear fruit. In 1782, Joseph II, Emperor of Austria, issued his "Edict of Toleration, in which he removed some of the worst laws under which the Jews had to suffer, such as those forcing them to wear special Jewish clothes and to pay a special tax for just being Jews. Though they were by no means given equality, this was the first time the Jews received an act of friendship from a great European government. At the same time, in France also there were signs of friendliness. 

But nowhere in Europe were nations as yet ready to carry out the idea of real equality for Jews which their liberal leaders had urged. Something striking had to happen to shake Europe out of its old ways. That something did happen, in the form of the 

French Revolution.

FRENCH REVOLUTION: 

EMANCIPATION COMES TRUE 

The French Revolution marked the sudden appearance of democracy in Europe. The forces making for democracy had been growing stronger: new methods of trade and manufacture had helped merchants and city-dwellers to become more and more independent and seek greater freedom, while the spread of knowledge and reading had led people to think for themselves. Peasants began to demand freedom from their landlords. People now asked openly: Why can't we be ruled by ourselves, instead of by the king and by the 

Church? Democracy had already been born in America: with the

Revolutionary War in 1776, with its Declaration of Independence, asserting that all men are equal and that governments derive their just powers from the consent of the governed. In 1789 in France, the people's representatives demanded that the king grant fairer laws and greater equality. When the king and the upper classes tried, instead, to break up the people's assembly, a long bloody struggle began. In this struggle, called the French Revolution, the people's representatives finally won out. The king and queen were put to death, and a National Assembly was established by which the people began to rule themselves.

{1.

10. 

The French Revolution had as its slogan, "Liberty, Equality, Fraternity. Nevertheless, when the great leaders of the Revolution tried to use this slogan to bring real freedom to the Jews, many objected. After a struggle of about two years, the Assembly was forced to declare one way or the other, and finally voted to grant Jews full rights. These were included in the French Constitution of 1791. Thus France was the first nation in Europe to give the Jews equality.

The rulers of the nations surrounding France now feared that the new liberty which the French people had won would be demanded by their own subjects. Therefore they made was against France in order to crush the revolution. Soon Napoleon was at the head of the French armies fighting in all corners of Europe. His victorious forces now carried the spirit of the French Revolution into the rest of Europe, and with it freedom and equality for the Jews. 

Wherever Napoleon entered, he tore down the walls of the ghetto. 

It was like a dream come true. The darkness of the Middle 

Ages was lifted. Jews could now live with their neighbors, wear clothes like their neighbors, go to regular schools, and enter various occupations. They could vote, and had to pay no special taxes for the mere privilege of living. In short, they were now no longer outsiders, but became part of the country in which they lived. In Spain and Portugal the terrible Inquisition (see III, page, 68) was ended, and later a number of Marranos openly proclaimed their Judaism. The Modern Age 

Emancipation - had. dawned for Israel. 

Democracy 

But all was not paradise. Some who had given the Jews freedom had hoped that after receiving it they would not care to remain Jews any longer. Others were not altogether certain that the Jewish people deserved it. The Jew still looked like a foreigner to them and they were not sure that he knew how to be patriotic, or fair in business, or that he was sufficiently educated and well-mannered.

Napoleon himself seems to have had these thoughts in mind. 

For in 1806 he called together a brilliant Assembly of Jewish notables - over one hundred famous rabbis and laymen, mostly of France and Italy- who declared that the Jew is patriotic for his country, "even unto death, keeps its laws, considers Christians his brothers, and hates unfairness in business. The Assembly was also forced to consider the question of marriages with Christians. 

It declared that Judaism does not forbid them, but that rabbis could not conduct such ceremonies. The members were of course, sincerely garteful for all that Napoleon had done for the Jewish people. In spite of these promises, however, Napoleon still seemed to feel that the Jews did not deserve full freedom and decreed laws which, for ten years, restricted the Jews in their lending of money, in trading without permission of the officials, in their right to live in sections of France where they had not lived before, and in other matters. 

Another thing that stood in the way of full freedom for our

1. C GOL 

ران قادة

7 CHIT. 1 [1... 138 CEA 2 ་ ་ ་ ་

11. people was the fact that most of the nations of Europe had not granted this freedom willingly. They had merely been forced to do so by Napoleon. And when, And when, in 1815, Napoleon was defeated and exiled to the island of Saint Helena, most of the conquered countries took back the freedom that they had given, Metternich, the new master of Europe, was opposed to democracy and to reform. 

Europe swung away from freedom. New writings appeared_against the 

Jews. Riots broke out. Ghettos were reestablished. Even the awful Spanish Inquisition was set up once more in Spain and Italy. 

But the common people of Western Europe had tasted the flavor of liberty, and their hunger for more rights and greater equality was not to be denied for long. New revolts for liberty broke out in various countries in 1830 and especially in 1848. 

The power of kings was limted more and more by the people who forced their kings to accept constitutions. These turned more and more control over the affairs of the country to the Parliaments and Assemblies representing the people. The day of special privileges now finally gave way to the period of rights for the common man. And as part of these rights came equality for the Jew.

In this long struggle to win back the freedom that the French Revolution and the conquering Napoleon had suddenly brought them, Jews did all they could for the cause of liberty. Their power was as yet not very great, because most of the people considered them outsiders who could not even go to school with them. A few Jews, however, could make their voices heard and these few became great leaders in the fight for freedom, especially in Germany. 

One such leader was Ludwig Borne. He never forgot how uncomfortable he had been made to feel as a boy in Frankfort, Germany, where he was not allowed to walk on the sidewalk, but had to walk in the dusty roads, because he was a Jew. Therefore, in his brilliant writings in books, newspapers, and magazines, he used the power of his words to lead men to a love of liberty and equality. He went to France, which had retained its freedom, even after the defeat of Napoleon, and wrote what he called, "Letters from Paris," in which he showed how beautiful the freedom which Frenchmen enjoyed was compared with the injustices and stupidities which Germans suffered because they continued to allow themselves to be ruled by their noblemen.

Another leader was the writer of the best-loved poetry in the 

German language, Heinrich Heine. His beautiful verses and his sharp wit won for him millions of readers. He led them to love simple, beautiful things and to despise all tyranny.

Both Borne and Heine had themselves baptized as Christians as they grew into manhood because they thought this would bring them greater chances to advance. But they included within their writings constant references to the disgraceful manner in which Jews were still insulted and treated as outsiders. And when brutal anti-Jewish riots and murders occurred, they stirred the conscience of the people with their biting protests. But they wisely understood that the freeing of the Jews could come about

? 

NJ. 

NOW, 9 2 TI

12. only with the freedom of Germany as a whole; therefore, they sought to bring democracy to their country. To do this, they built the political movement called "Young Germany.

As this movement went forward, another Jew helped to carry it on to victory. His name was Gabriel Riesser. Although he was an able student of the law, his town refused to allow him to practice, because he was Jewish. He sought to become professor of law at 

Heidelberg, but the University would not have him for the same 

From then on, Riesser dedicated himself to the winning of rights for his people. He wrote pamphlets in which he insisted that Jews could become true and good citizens. If the state now accepts as citizens Jews who are cowardly enough to become Christians, he said, it ought to welcome all the more those who are firm and true enough to remain Jews. If some non-Jews, he said, use the word "Jew" as a term of insult, his people ought not to avoid it; rather they ought to do their best to make it a term of honor. Therefore, when he edited a magazine, he purposely called it "The Jew," and the courage which the magazine displayed really did bring honor to that name. He answered false charges against his people, and explained the meaning of its religion. Entering the "Young Germany" movement, he succeeded in making emanicpation for the Jews a definite part of its program. Finally, in 1848, the liberal party, "Young Germany, won its victory, and with it the Jews of most of Germany were made citizens. Even though these equal rights were not fully carried out at first, due to a new period of reaction, they became the basis of the real equality which the Jews enjoyed from the 1860's until the time of Hitlerism. 11 

Through labors of Borne, Heine, Riesser and others less prominent, the Jews thus played a large part in bringing democracy to Central Europe - all the more remarkable when one considers their small numbers and the little chance they had to express themselves. 

Although kings still ruled in most countries, the fruits of the French Revolution the ideals of liberty, equality, and fraternity - now brought their blessings to most of the peoples of Europe.

II. 

HOW DID THE JEWISH PEOPLE USE ITS NEW FREEDOM? 

As this new freedom came to our people, how did they act? 

Did they make good use of their new liberty? Did the coming of the new life produce any changes in their religion and in their hopes for the future? These are the questions which we will seek to answer in this section.

ATTEMPT TO MAKE THEMSELVES FIT FOR EMANCIPATION 

The first thing that our people did as they saw their hopes for freedom beginning to come true was to try to fit themselves for this new life, by learning the language, literature, and customs of the larger, non-Jewish community in which they expected to live.

To understand why they did this, let us try to recapture the spirit of the 1700's which we described at the beginning of the last section. The general European world was alive with new and hopeful thoughts. The Renaissance (the rebirth of learning) had been followed by the printing of great numbers of books. Men now had increased knowledge of the history of nations, of the sciences of nature, and of the teachings of great men of all ages. This new knowledge led thinking people to demand new freedom and fairness for all men. These demands in time led to the Declaration of Independence and the French Revolution. As yet most of the 

Europeans remained ignorant of this new knowledge. You might imagine, however, that the Jews who were much better educated took active part in this new learning. A few did, such as Baruch 

Spinoza, the great philosopher, but the great majority of Jews were even farther away from these matters than they had been in the Middle Ages. At that time, in addition to religious studies, Jews learned natural sciences, medicine, and mathematics. Now, however, Jewish studies were almost altogether religious. Practically no attention was paid to the new sciences and thoughts that were developing. The fault was not altogether that of the Jews.

Confined to their ghettos, the Jews were kept out of the schools of the country and were without knowledge even of the new languages in which the new books were written. However, a few 

Jews with great ability, like Moses Mendelssohn (see pages 7-9) had a special chance to help their people and they devoted their lives to making the educated men of their day understand that freedom for manking must also include freedom for the Jew. They wrote books defending their people, and helping others to understand them. They showed by their own noble lives and brilliant writings that Jews are a people of quality that have something fine to give to the nations of the world. They

But when Mendelssohn looked at his own people, he saw why it was so hard to make others understand their good qualities. seemed strange and foreign. They did not speak German, but

Yiddish, which, to the non-Jew, seemed to be a corruption of Ger- Their manners were different. They just didn't seem to be part of the Germany of the future. (The fact is that the average 

Jew was more cultured than his neighbor, but his learning was in 

Hebrew, which the others could not understand.)

Mendelssohn therefore set out to make educated Germans of his people. He began by translating the Pentateuch (the Torah) into beautiful German, printed in Hebrew characters. Most of the

Jews eagerly grasped it, and as they read it over and over they learned to speak German correctly. Able now to handle the language, they turned to other German books and were brought close to the new ideas of their times. Mendelssohn and his followers likewise wrote a commentary to the Bible in Hebrew, in which he stressed that the great book is to be read not merely for religious reasons but for enjoyment, as fine literature. He likewise published a Hebrew magazine (the first in modern times), in order to imrpove the people's taste for fine Hebrew writing and in order to bring them knowledge about Judaism and about the world, in a modern spirit.

Mendelssohn and his disciples began another important thing: a change in the education of the Jewish child. Bible and Talmud are very necessary, they said, but in addition, the child should learn science, history, philosophy, French, and German, thus fitting him for life among his neighbors. The first changes in this direction were made in the Jewish Free School, founded in Berlin at Mendelssohn's suggestion. - Mendelssohn's death just three years before the French Revolution - did not stop these efforts. Bibles for Jews were now appearing in Polish, French and Italian. The use of Hebrew to teach modern culture spread quickly in the great Jewish centers of Russia and the East, as well as in Germany, Italy, and France. 

Famous European books were now translated into Hebrew and were eagerly read. New Jewish schools in more modern spirit sprang up in various cities, and Jews sincerely tried to prove that they were fit to be welcomed as true equals in their lands.

Some of the great leaders of German Jewry, however, opposed this movement. These leaders said that the Jews were giving up too much. If they tried so hard to become like the others, they would soon give up their Judaism altogether. The great faith that the Jewish people had preserved and which enabled them to triumph over so many obstacles for thousands of years, would be lost. And without Judaism, the Jewish people would disappear. On the other hand, these leaders said, the Jews are not getting anything of value in return. There is, indeed, the hope of freedom and equality. But will equality ever be given? And even if it is given now, will it not be taken away at some future time when conditions are not so rosy, as happened before in his long story? 

Were they right, or was Mendelssohn right? Even today, we are still uncertain who was right. Let the reader keep this question in mind and make up his own answer from the facts that we shall relate.

1. .7

16. 

Certainly the people of that time followed Mendelssohn more and more, and his program won out. 

THE SCIENCE OF JUDAISM 

Moses Mendelssohn himself was not troubled by the question we have just asked, for he was sure that even while he was preparing his people for life in the new Germany, he was at the same time keeping them loyal to Judaism. It took some time for his effort to strike root, but in time it led to a new interest in Jewish learning everywhere. In Germany, it led scholars to seek to discover how Judaism developed. Before the modern age, learned Jews knew the Jewish religion and its books of law, story, and prayer almost by heart, but they had no clear understanding of how these books came to be created. Now the Christians were beginning to study the origins of Christianity and the Bible. Jewish scholars, therefore, saw that if the Jew was to be accepted as an equal, it was necessary to show that he had a history as interesting as that of other peoples. They therefore began to write that history by doing amazing feats of detective work, piecing together scraps of information from thousands of books. They showed how the Jewish religion grew and developed from one period to another. These studies came to be known as the Science of Judaism, or Jüdische Wissenschaft, as it is called in German. 

It is very interesting to learn how the first of these studies came to be written. It had happened that, in order to attract the more modern Jews to the Synagogue, some congregations began to give a few prayers and the sermon in the German language. The 

Orthodox community was opposed to reading prayers in German or to a rabbi preaching a sermon on the Sabbath morning; these sermons were always preached on the Sabbath afternoon in Yiddish. They, therefore, secured an order from the reactionary Prussian government stopping such services, and ordering that Jewish worship must be conducted "without the slightest change." Leopold Zunz, a brilliant scholar, angered by such medieval actions of the government, thereupon wrote a book on The Sermons of the Jews, in which he proved that Judaism always had permitted the introduction of new prayers and that prayers and sermons in the language of the country were allowed since ancient times. The liberals then used his book to overcome the governmental restriction. 

Zunz hoped that if the larger world understood the Jews better, it would grant them rights more readily. Many prejudices against the Jew and many obstacles to Jewish hopes, he said, were simply the result of lack of knowledge. 

To reveal this knowledge, as well as to awaken the Jew's own love for his faith and people, Zunz continued his work, writing histories of Jewish prayers and synagogue poetry. Others, in 

Germany, Austria and Italy, carried forward the movement of the Science of Judaism and wrote the history of the great personalities of the Talmud, of Hebrew poetry, of the writing of the Bible, and of the Jewish religion. One great scholar searched all the libraries of Europe and made a bibliography of all books of Jewish

17. knowledge, to aid other students in their studies. Finally a scholar, Heinrich Graetz, who had mastered many languages which enabled him to search through thousands of records, gathered together his own findings and those of the scholars before him, and wrote the first truly scientific History of the Jews. Jewish history could now stand on equal terms with the accounts of all other important peoples.

THE FLIGHT FROM JUDAISM 

Unfortunately, however, Mendelssohn's efforts were not altogether helpful to Judaism. And not even the Science of Judaism was able to keep the people firmly attached to their faith. Enlightened Jews, who had come to read more and more German books, agreed that in theory, Judaism had been a great religion, but in practice, the old Orthodox customs and restrictions just did not seem to fit the modern life which they sought. Many could not understand the long services which were all in Hebrew, and many considered them boring. The wearing of hats at prayer seemed foreign to them. And the dietary laws (kosher food), preventing them from eating in non-Jewish places, were simply in the way. 

Judaism, as it was practiced, had little to hold them; but the outside world seemed to possess a magic charm. If we think back over history, we realize that the Jew of Germany, during the first half of the 1800's, was like a Jack-in-the-box. The French 

Revolution and the spread of freedom by Napoleon had let him pop out for a moment and enjoy the fresh air of hope and opportunity. 

But the fall of Napoleon and the coming back of the enemies of freedom in 1813 pushed him almost completely back into the old coop of the ghetto again. Each little lifting of the lid, giving a new sniff at freedom, was then followed by another period of pushing down hard again. At last the strain became too great for some, and since they could not be free as Jews, they found a way of breaking out by becoming Christians. Conversion to Christianity held for them immediate hope of being free and of advancing in their chosen fields without restriction. 

Thousands of Jews, especially in Germany, became Christians in these early years of the 1800's. Many of these, indeed, were the ablest of our people, and some had been its leaders. Edward 

Gans had labored to stop conversion by helping to organize a club which spread the knowledge of the Jew a club which helped to begin the Science of Judaism. But he became discouraged with his labors and took up the Christian faith himself. Heinrich Heine (see page 11), who had been a member of that same club, likewise was converted, as was Ludwig Borne, who worked with Heine to ad vance the political party for freedom (Young Germany). Mendels sohn's own daughters and one of his two sons were converted. His gifted grandson, Felix Mendelssohn-Bartholdy, who has written some of the loveliest of all music, wrote hymns for the church. 

Henrietta Hertz (see page 9) and other brilliant Jewesses, likewise fled from their own religion.

Many of these converts later realized that their actions had

COG (

18. not really gained the freedom that they had desired. Heine wrote in 1826 (only a year after he was baptized): "I am hated alike by 

Jew and Christian. I regret very deeply that I had myself baptized. I do not see that I have been the better for it since. the contrary, I have known nothing but misfortune. On But the progress of conversion did not stop. Instead it went rapidly forward. Soon it became clear that the teachings of Moses 

Mendelssohn and the learned writings of the Science of Judaism were not enough to hold the Jew to his faith. Mendelssohn said that as long as Jews practiced the same customs, they could think about Judaism as they pleased. But this did not settle the problem; something more profound had to be done if Judaism were not to lose all its brightest minds, if not its very existence in the 

German-speaking lands.

MAKING JUDAISM MODERN: REFORM 

Something indeed was done. Reform Judaism was created to make the Jewish faith modern both in thought and in appearance. 

The early beginnings of Reform were Israel Jacobson's efforts to make Jewish worship more appealing and inspiring. He was a rich merchant of Westphalia, Germany, who had established a school there in 1801 in order to carry out Mendelssohn's idea of more modern education. In the religious services conducted at the school he had some of the prayers said in German instead of in Hebrew. He also had a regular sermon, which was a new thing for those times. This, too, was in the German language. He was the first, likewise, to introduce the singing of hymns in German.

Later he built a temple with an organ in it. Finally he developed the lovely ceremony of Confirmation. All of these were changes from Orthodox ways which he made in order to make the Jewish faith more appealing to the modern Jew. Later such changes were taken over from the school to a regular congregation, where great scholars and modern preachers were invited to deliver inspiring messages to Jews. Leopold Zunz was one of them. You will recall how he had to defend this new practice of preaching in German against the Orthodox Jews whose fear of any changes led them even to get the government to stop the services (see page 16). 

Other changes took place. The Orthodox service was shortened by dropping prayers, such as those referring to angels or to the Amalekites and other ancient enemies of Israel, and those which simply repeated other prayers already spoken in the service. Some even gave up the dietary laws. The second day of the Holy Days was dropped since, with the exact knowledge of the calendar, there was no need to keep two days to make sure that the right one was observed as Orthodoxy had insisted. Women were given greater recognition in the synagogue and were allowed to sit together with men, which had not been permitted before. In general it was declared that the Shulchan Aruch, or Jewish code of laws, no longer could be considered binding in Jewish life. In cases of business disputes, even in such questions as divorce, they said the Jew should follow the general laws of his country. In religious matters, conferences of rabbis should decide what may and may not be done in the spirit of the new times.

.h PJ 1 034 ༢ྩོད "น

19. 

The Orthodox Jews claimed that the rabbis had no authority to make such changes, because Judaism is a religion that was written down in the Torah for all time and can never be changed. 

But a great rabbi and builder of the Science of Judaism, Abraham Geiger, showed that Judaism has changed many times and in many ways since its beginning. He pointed out that in times of persecution the Jew clings to his customs very strictly, but as soon as freedom opens up for him, he eagerly takes part again in the life of his land, as in Alexandria and in Spain, and changes his religious customs to the spirit of his day. "The purpose of the Reform movement," declared Geiger, "therefore, was to make Judaism a progressive religion again, which means to adjust Judaism's customs, prayers, and manner of teaching to the conditions and needs of each new age."

In that way, Geiger showed that Reform was not destroying Jewish tradition but carrying it forward in the only way in which it could possibly live. He won for the movement the most modern and progressive Jews of the time. Abraham Geiger and the many other rabbis who met together in several conferences (1844-46) to develop Reform thus checked the flood of conversion - although they by no means stopped it completely. 

Geiger also tried to meet another great problem of his time. 

Strong arguments had been made against treating the Jews as equals in Germany on the ground that they were a separate nation and therefore could never become true Germans. People in those days, especially in Germany, looked upon love of their country in a very narrow manner. They could not imagine how one could be a loyal German without cutting himself off from a love of any other group outside of that land. These objections stood in the way of granting the Jew German citizenship. To answer them, Geiger and other Reform leaders declared that their people were Jews by religion only, and that they gave up any desire to have the Jewish people rebuilt as a nation in Palestine. Therefore prayers expressing such hopes, which had been a part of the Jewish prayer book ever since our people were driven out of their homeland by Rome, were removed from the service. In their place prayers were added that stressed more strongly than before that Israel has an important mission, or task, to bring the true ideas of God and of justice to all the nations.

In our day, many Reform Jews believe that Geiger went too far in cutting off the German Jews from the Jews of the rest of the world, who were praying for the restoration of Palestine. In

America we have learned that various peoples can live together happily; they can all be loyal to America even though they keep on loving their people and helping them in other parts of the world. Many Reform Jews have also shown a renewed interest in Palestine. It

Little by little, the Reform movement. spread in Germany, although not nearly as fast as Geiger hoped that it would. spread to England and to Paris; but it was only in America, where

LC 1.

20. it had been brought by Isaac M. Wise and others in the 1840's and 

1850's, that it became the great moving force that Geiger had hoped it would be. We shall learn about this later on. 

CONSERVATIVE JUDAISM 

Within Germany, some of the rabbis who had agreed with Geiger on the main idea of progress and development in Judaism refused to accept his idea of Reform Judaism. They thought that Reform was going much too fast in making changes. Their great leader, the scholarly Rabbi Zacharias Frankel, had come to one of the conferences called by Geiger, but soon withdrew on the ground that the Reform rabbis were not paying enough respect to past traditions.

Shortly after that, Rabbi Frankel was chosen director of the first modern training school for rabbis in Breslau. Here he developed what came to be called Conservative Judaism, which differed from Reform in that it kept the religious service much closer to the Orthodox form, with the prayers in the original Hebrew without much change. It retained the observance of the dietary laws and the second day of the festivals. It kept prayers hoping for the rebuilding of the Jewish nation in Palestine. In general, it was much closer to Orthodox Judaism, except that it, like Reform, admitted the possibility of making changes and of fitting Jewish customs to new conditions. 

Other great scholars, like Leopold Zunz, were attracted to the Conservative movement and helped to strengthen it in Germany. 

In America, also, the Conservative movement grew among scholars and rabbis who saw the need for a more modern Judaism but felt that Reform was going too far. The Jewish Theological Seminary, founded in New York in 1886 by Rabbi Sabato Morais, became the center of a thriving Conservative movement in America, as we shall describe later.

NEW ORTHODOXY 

Even among circles of the Orthodox, who held fast to all the Jewish practices of their fathers, the need was felt for a new interpretation, while keeping their old customs. They sought to explain them in the light of the new science and learning and in the light of the new hopes for freedom of the Jewish people. 

Rabbi Samson Raphael Hirsch led in this movement in Germany. He said the coming of freedom for the Jew was to be welcomed. This was an important statement because many Orthodox leaders before him had opposed and feared its coming lest it lead away from the observance of all the traditions. And agreeing with Reform, Rabbi 

Hirsch said that Israel has a mission to the world, to bring it to that day when all people will acknowledge that God is One. But, he insisted, the Jew can perform that task only by keeping separate from others through observing all the commandments of the Torah. They should perfect themselves by keeping the customs, rather than changing the customs to fit their wishes.

21. 

Thus there were many methods by which the Jew of this period sought to fit himself for his new life as part of the general West European world. First came Moses Mendelssohn's German Bible and his pleas for European learning and manners which led to general enlightenment among his people, then to the scholarly Science of Judaism, then to Reform Judaism, to Conservatism, and to a new interpretation of Orthodoxy.

22. III 

THE NINETEENTH CENTURY IN EASTERN EUROPE 

In Eastern Europe, however, among the great Jewish masses in Russia, the efforts at modern learning led to quite a different development. This was because, during this period in which freedom was growing in Western Europe, Russia held on to its backward, oppressive rule. The Jews of Eastern Europe for a time hoped that they would be freed as were the Jews of Western Europe. But they were not, and their repeated disappointments led them to take a different course, as we shall see. We can best understand this course only after we tell the story of their experience in Russia. 

RUSSIA FIGHTS OFF THE URGE FOR FREEDOM 

One European country alone successfully held back the growing demand for liberty: Russia. This is particularly important because during this period, half of the Jews of the world were living in Russia. Not until World War I and the Russian Revolution of 1917, did democracy have any real effect on Russia. While the rest of Europe was developing new factories and trade, Russia, away from the busy highways of Europe, was still clinging to her old system of serfs and landlords. While the Renaissance and printing brought new learning to the West, Russia remained in ignorance. The Reformation which brought freedom of religion to the peoples of Germany, of France, and England, simply passed by the Russian masses who knew nothing but their Greek Orthodox Church. 

Under such conditions, democracy could make only the slowest progress.

You can imagine that in such a land the Jews had little chance to advance out of their medieval restrictions. Even the explosion of the French Revolution created but a faint glow in Russia. And, when it burned itself out, all hopes for liberty died. There were no forces within Russia to take up the torch for democracy again, as there were in Germany and other lands. 

Indeed, as the 1800's went on, the rise of democracy elsewhere tended to make conditions even worse for the Jews. The tyrannical 

Russian Czars became frightened that their power might be destroyed. Therefore, they fought against all things that seemed to be in the direction of liberalism, and among these things, of course, was freedom and equal rights for the Jews. Besides, they did not want to see the influence of the Jew increase, for they knew that the Jew is a courageous fighter for freedom.

The Let us follow this story in greater detail. It was really only an accident, in the first place, that Russia came to rule over the largest Jewish community in the world of that time. 

Jews really lived in Poland. But when the land was divided at the end of the 1700's, Russia came to rule most of it and found herself with a million Jewish subjects. She did not want them and regarded them as a problem. To deal with this problem, her first step was to order all Jews to stay within the area within which they had already been living. This area she called the "Pale of Settlement. (Pale means fenced-in area.)

23. 

Then certain rules were applied to the Jews. Some of the rules seemed friendly and appeared to be in keeping with the emancipation which the French Revolution had brought to Jews elsewhere. These granted permission to Jews to attend all public schools and even the universities. Jews could likewise set up their own public schools as long as they were conducted in Russian, Polish, or German. They were also allowed to hold certain political offices within the community. 

However, the Jews soon learned that these measures were not to lead them to true freedom, for from the very beginning the government introduced other measures restricting Jewish life. 

One took away most of the power of the Kahals, the Jewish assembly for self-government, which had given our people strength and unity, as you learned on III, p. 85. With these now limited to religious matters only, Jewish self-government was destroyed. 

Other laws prohibited Jews from keeping inns and selling liquor occupations in which many of them had earned their living after the Poles had excluded them from other occupations. Most severe of all, they were to be removed within a few years from villages, in some of which they had lived for centuries, and made to live only in the cities of the Pale. The excuse for these last two harsh measures was that being an innkeeper in a village did not produce anything, but only made money off of the peasants. The law provided that the Jews were to be encouraged to go into handicrafts, manufacturing, and farming. Had the government really taken steps to bring about this change, it would not have been bad, since in the end, the Jews would have been established in safer and happier livelihoods. But very little land was made available and no money was given to transport Jews to this land. 

Conditions in those colonies that were established became unbearable for the Jews, so this measure proved of no help. In the towns, the handicrafts were already overcrowded. The result was that thousands of Jews were driven out of their village homes and dumped into the cities without money and without anything to do. 

Conditions became so bad that the government stopped these expulsions after a period, only to reintroduce them again time after time during the century.

From the very beginning, the Jews of Russia saw that these acts of the government were leading not to true emancipation as in France, but to destruction. Even should they give up their 

Judaism, as the government was trying to have them do, they would have no equality or opportunity. Therefore they refused to attend the government schools.

Later, Russia herself made it clear, that the true purpose of her program was to destroy the Jews rather than to free them. 

Jews were not only offered advantages to become converted to Christianity, but a horrible method was used to force them into it. Czar Nicholas I, called "The Iron Czar," set up a program supporting absolute power for the king and the Greek Orthodox Church. As a means of accomplishing this, Russian men were forced to serve in the army for twenty-five years, starting at the age of eighteen. For Jews, however, the twenty-five years of

ཤ་ 

21. W 1 1.

24. service were not considered sufficient. They were ordered to serve an additional preparatory period of six years, beginning with the age of twelve. The Jewish community itself was forced to provide the necessary number of such boys. Since no parents willingly gave up their children for such cruel service, the community employed the lowest and meanest men to act as catchers or snatchers. They would break into a home in the middle of the night and carry off sleeping children. Nor were they always particular about the age- if the child was only eight or nine, they pretended he was twelve. Those who were thus snatched out of their mother's arms were then taken to the farthest provinces, to separate them completely from the influence of their parents. 

Only the hardiest survived these long trips in springless open wagons. Once there, their "training" included all sorts of torture to persuade them to become Christians; they were kept awake at night and made to stay on their knees, or they were beaten, starved, or made to eat salt fish and then given no water unless they agreed to be baptized. The boys were thus forced to give up their Judaism.

By this system, Czar Nicholas hoped that when the young Jewish recruits (called Cantonists) would return they would influence their families and lead them all to become Christians. 

What a stupid Czar! The scheme, of course, resulted only in a hatred of the whole system of government that could be so cruel and for the church that could allow such methods to be used supposedly to further religion. 

To escape child snatchers, Jewish parents would hide their children in cellars or in the forest; some even would cut off the child's finger so that the army would reject him. Such was the suffering that Russian tyranny brought to our people at the very time when the rest of Europe was blossoming into new freedom and fairness. us; 11 When the same Czar began to tempt the Jews to attend Russian schools and learn the Russian language and the new sciences of the day, they naturally suspected his motives. "He is not really trying to advance us they reasoned, "but is only using another means to destroy us. And they were right, for it was finally revealed that the plan was, in the end, to close the Jewish schools altogether and to cut off all Jewish knowledge from the Jews of Russia and thus force them to become Christians. Rabbi 

Max Lilienthal, a German University graduate, had been brought to Russia to superintend the founding of schools for Jews. But when he learned their true purpose to convert them he resigned, and the whole scheme was dropped. 

Conditions improved somewhat under the next Czar, Alexander 

II. 

He freed the peasant serfs, set up some self-government in the cities, and later abolished the terrible "Cantonist" system of child service in the army, after the Jews had suffered under it for thirty years. Industry was beginning to make some slow progress by this time (after the middle of the 1800's), therefore money for investment and skilled laborers were needed throughout

;

25. Russia. To meet this need, Alexander II allowed certain Jewish university graduates, hand workers, and wealthy merchants to live outside of the "Pale of Settlement. And when he encouraged the 

Jews to attend the general schools and universities, it seemed like an offer really for their own good. They now began to be a part of the general Russian world. Many became lawyers and physicians, some even judges. The few wealthy Jews, who had always enjoyed special privileges, now really forged ahead, helping to build Russia's railroads, mines, and industries. 

It seemed, in the middle of the 1800's, that freedom was at last beginning to dawn even on backward Russia, The dawn proved to be a false one, bringing on an even darker night, as we shall see.

But up to this point, the Jews of Russia still had hopes for the same kind of freedom that their brothers in Germany, France, and England had received. 

ENLIGHTENMENT IN EASTERN EUROPE: HASKALAH 

Up to this point, the movement for enlightenment, likewise, followed a course similar to that in Germany. Under German inspiration it started off by leading the Jewish people to become modern in taste, manners, and action, so that they would be fit to share the life with their non-Jewish neighbors in a free new world. Hand in hand with this, it sought to give the Jew, long oppressed and discouraged, a deeper pride in himself and in his religion. Thus by the middle of the 1800's, we begin to find modern Jewish schools teaching sciences, European languages, and Bible in the East just as they were in the West. Eastern writers contributed to Hebrew magazines and wrote books teaching new culture and tastes. 

As the Haskalah (enlightenment) advanced, however, certain differences appeared. First, whereas Mendelssohn and his followers gave up the use of Hebrew very quickly just as soon as their people had been led to a knowledge of German the Haskalah of the East continued to use Hebrew. Thus Krochmal, leader of the Haskalah in Galicia, wrote his Guide for the Perplexed of Our Time in Hebrew. One of the reasons for this was that the 

Jews of the East, who continued to live in large crowded Jewish settlements, still knew their sacred tongue. The Jews of Germany on the other hand, were much fewer in number and were scattered throughout the country. Because of this, they lost their Hebraic knowledge.

There was a second difference: while the German Science of 

Judaism was interested mostly in proving to the world that Judaism was a modern and worthy faith, the Haskalah sought above all to prove the worth of Jewish things to the Jew. 

A third difference was that the enlightenment of the West thought that it could make the Jew a modern man most quickly by transforming him into a German, Frenchman, or Englishman. But the Haskalah of the East was in no hurry to make the Eastern Jew a Russian, Austrian, or Pole. Therefore, it sought to make the

* A MEN • J 1 7

26. 

Jew a modern man by bringing his own Jewish community up to date. 

It tried to cut away from Jewish life all things that were backward or superstitious, or hindered the Jew's advancement in profession or business or in sustenance of health and comfort. 

Here is how the movement grew: Besides the great Nachman 

Krochmal, a leader in the Haskalah in Galicia was Joseph Perl, who introduced a more modern Jewish school in his city. Perl wrote a book attacking the ignorance and superstition of Chasidism as then practiced. Another was Solomon Rapoport, who wrote the life stories of the great Talmudic personalities. Z'vi Chajes wrote an introduction to the Talmud, as well as other works which helped Jews to understand this great book in modern, orderly fashion. 

Others, such as Isaac Erter, wrote comic stories making fun of the backward conditions of the people. 

After living among these men in Galicia, a great writer by the name of Isaac Baer Levinsohn returned to his land of Russia. 

There he started the work of leading the masses to modernism through Hebrew. He was joined by many others who wrote, not such difficult books as did Zunz and Geiger, Krochmal and Chajes, but books which were easy to understand essays, novels, and poems that had an effect on the people immediately. Levinsohn's first book, published in 1828, sought to improve and widen the education of his people. He showed in this volume, called Instruction in 

Israel, that the famous early Jewish teachers were educated not only in Jewish studies, but in the history of foreign nations the sciences, in the affairs of the world. "Art and science, said, "are steadily progressing; to study and know them we must use non-Jewish books and sources. These were bold words to speak in the midst of Jewish fathers who whipped their children if they found them reading non-Jewish books because they feared that, amid the persecutions and schemes of the Czars, the Jews would be altogether destroyed if they did not give all their attention to their own books. Levinsohn's work was well done, and although some burned it in rage, in the end, it carried the day. in he As the movement for enlightenment went forward in Russia, Abraham Mapu was the first Jew to write a Hebrew novel. Called 

Love of Zion, it was a moving tale of the life of the people in ancient Judea, describing their joys, high ideals, and love of their land. This novel stirred new pride in Israel in the hearts of Jews. In another novel, Mapu poked fun at the backward types in the ghetto, such as the man who claimed he could work miracles, the shadchon (marriage broker), the pious hypocrite, as well as those leaders who remained enemies to progress. A great Hebrew poet, Judah Leon Gordon, called to his people to "Awake to a better life, to the life of those about you. He, like most early maskilim (supporters of the Haskalah), attacked the complete control of life by medieval law and custom as backward and superstitious.

The long labors of the Haskalah were now bearing fruit; Jews were becoming modern, many indeed, were taking up the Russian language. The leaders were encouraged by the early liberal

BEGINNINGS OF ZIONISM 

But the attitude of Alexander II began to change, and it soon became clear that there would be no freedom either for Russia or for its Jews under the Czars. In the 1870's, Alexander II decided that he had been wrong in giving more privileges to the Jews, since they were not disappearing as a group, as he had hoped. 

Besides, many Jews were now active in the revolutionary parties within Russia, which constantly called for real freedom for the whole land. New laws were therefore passed against the Jews, and there began a new era of false charges and ritual murder trials. 

It was then that the leaders of the Haskalah began to realize that instead of fitting the Jew for the future, their labors had really unfitted him, For they had taken away the strength of his old Jewish life, without gaining him rights as a Russian in return. Where could he now turn? Would he not be lost now, being neither really a Jew nor a Russian?

A new feeling had been spreading over Europe that man must take his future into his own hands instead of depending so much upon merely praying and waiting. In the Jewish world, this new feeling gave rise to the call: "If we cannot win freedom and equality for ourselves as individuals, let us win freedom for curselves as a whole people. Let us return to Palestine, rebuild our ancient land, rebuild our ancient spirit, and be independent of Czars and Jew-haters. If Russia will not give us freedom, we must take it for ourselves. This was the new foundation which the Haskalah began to build for the future of Israel. It became to be called Zionism, "Zion" being another name for Jerusalem. As the writers of Haskalah now turned to build up this new movement, they recognized that the progress which the previous writers had already made in teaching the Jews the use of the Hebrew language again could be of great help to them.

The more the Jewish people would use the Hebrew language in reading, writing, and speaking, the more it would become proud of its past. For it was in this language that Israel had made its greatest gift to the world - the Bible. Great Jewish literature since that time was likewise in this language. Therefore, a knowledge of it would also give the Jews a sense of unity. 

The great leader who succeeded in leading the people from the Haskalah into Zionism was Perez Smolenskin. In 1872 he published his Am Olam (The Eternal People), in which he said: "You wish to be like other people. So do I. Only, I pray you, be like them. Search and find knowledge, avoid superstition. Above all, be not ashamed of the rock whence you were hewn. Yes, be like the other peoples, proud of your literature, jealous of your selfrespect, hopeful, even as all persecuted peoples are hopeful, of

༨.༔ 055 A

CL £33

A4 J ༈- 1.. 1.

28. the speedy arrival of the day when we too shall reinhabit the land which once was and still is our own. His Hebrew magazine, which held great influence for many years, he called Hasha char (The Dawn), because he was bringing in a new day of unity and strength for his people.

The events of 1881-82 ended once and for all the dream of the old Haskalah. They proved clearly and terribly that Smolenskin was right and that the Jews needed a land of their own. In 1881, Alexander II was blown to bits by a revolutionist's bomb. Alexander III, whose original hatred of freedom was increased and made bitter by the murder of his father, now came to power. He made up his mind to grind into the dust every move for liberty. 

His right-hand man was Pobyedonostzev who said he had a solution for the Jewish situation in Russia: One-third of the Jews should leave the country, one-third should be converted to Christianity, and one-third should perish! Then with no more Jews, there would be no more "problem."

The 

When the anti-Semites of the land saw how the government felt about the Jews, they organized a series of pogroms (Russian word for murderous riots) against them. First, lies would be told about the Jews, such as that they had murdered the old Czar. word was spread that there would be an attack. Then when the people would be all stirred up, someone would pick a fight with a Jew, and an organized gang, supported by peasants from the countryside, would appear and invade the Jewish neighborhoods. They would loot the houses, smash them and burn them, mistreat, torture, and sometimes murder the men and women that lived in them. The government took no real steps to stop the terror; in fact, the light way in which it treated those rioters who were caught encouraged more communities to try the cruel sport. Indeed, the newspapers which the government itself controlled continued to stir up the people against the Jews. The pogroms spread until over a hundred Jewish communities suffered from their terror in 1881, and others in the spring of 1882. In Warsaw (then ruled by 

Russia) alone, fifteen hundred Jewish houses were destroyed; in 

Balta, forty Jews were killed or crippled, 170 wounded, and fifteen thousand Jews robbed of all they possessed. 

How can we explain that a strong government like that of the Czar let such things occur? Part of the answer is that every military-minded tyrant wants every one in his country to be alike and to dislike those who seem different from the great majority of the people. But there was another part of the answer which proved even more important as time went on. The pogroms could be used to help keep out of the country the democratic forces that were trying to overthrow the absolute rule of kings and nobles. 

For if the democratic party could be called a "Jewish" party, then the ignorant people would come to despise the new democratic ideals, rather than welcome them. Jew-hatred could thus be used as a smoke-screen to hide and keep the Czar in power. When conditions became particularly bad, as when the peasants were faced with famine, the Czar had his agents put the blame on the Jews. 

The people, instead of attacking the Czar for robbing them, would

-- 
-- མ་ Di ! i.

29. get rid of their anger by beating up the Jews. called using a "scapegoat. 

This is what is

So shocked were the other nations by what Russia was doing that they protested vigorously, and finally the Czar acted to stop the pogroms. But the government refused to admit that it had been at fault. Instead, it had officials study the situation and declare that the Jews themselves were responsible for the pogroms! As a punishment, the "May Laws" of 1882 were passed, restricting Jewish rights in many ways. The Pale of Settlement was made smaller, the Jews were driven out of many communities. 

The special privileges that had been granted to certain Jews to live outside of the Pale were now put in doubt, so that they often had to pay enormous bribes or be expelled on a day's notice. 

Only a very small percentage of Jews were now allowed to go to the University or to practice law. From then until 1917, Russian Jewry lived in constant fear of more pogroms and new restrictions.

All hope for freedom under the Czars was turned to bitterness. The spirit of democracy, which had brought glorious emancipation to the Jews of Western and Central Europe, only stirred up in Russia the most brutal and crushing terror that the Jews had known since the days of Torquemada (see III, p. 69).

The Jews had to look beyond their own land for hope. They began to stream to America. From 1881 up to World War I (1914), two million Jews, mostly from Russia, came to this land. Others went to South America. Those who did not leave were divided into two groups. One group turned to the revolutionary movement, seeking to overthrow the cruel Czar and the system of wealth for a privileged few for which he stood. The other group labored to bring a movement which would provide Israel with a home of its own in Palestine, free from the rule of Czars and the pogroms of ignorant excitable peasants. People now realized that Smolenskin and his fellow writers were right. Merely to become modern, as the old Haskalah had sought, would not be enough; if the Jew wanted freedom, he would have to take his fortunes into his own hands and build a homeland for himself.

From now on Hebrew literature gave its attention to this one ideal. The period of the Haskalah was now over and that of 

Zionist writing came to take its place. The movement toward becoming modern, which Mendelssohn had started, had led to different results in the East than in the West. Under the sunny hopes of freedom in Germany, it had led first to the Science of Judaism and then to Reform, Conservative and Neo-Orthodox Judaism. 

But under the dark oppression of Russia, it had led to the Hebraic Haskalah, and through it to Jewish nationalism. 11 In 1881, Leon Pinsker, a Russian physician, wrote a little book called Auto-Emancipation, which means "freeing oneself. 

He urged Jews, as its title tells, to free themselves, by building their own home. He outlined a definite plan to do this and

30. became head of the Chovevei Zion (Lovers of Zion) Society which was organized for this purpose. The members of the Society wrote numerous other books and articles, stirring the stirring the people to the need of a Jewish home in Palestine, and labored in a practical manner to support the tiny colonies that had been started there. 

In December, 1881, a group of young Jewish college students, seeing that there was no future for them in the midst of the terror of pogroms, journeyed to Palestine to set up colonies there. 

They called their group of forty brave pioneers "Bilu" from the initials of the Hebrew phrase "Beis Ya-akov L'chu v'ne-1'cho" (House of Jacob, come ye and let us go).

The history of this movement from then on has been a series of obstacles, one after the other. Each seemed more hopeless than the one before, but each was overcome by Jewish determination, and each resulted in even greater advancement for the home land.

IV. 

THE PALESTINE WHICH THE PIONEERS SAW 

It took great courage on the part of the members of the Bilu and those pioneers who followed them to carry on their task, instead of a "land flowing with milk and honey, as the Bible For described it, the Palestine they came to was one of the most neglected lands on earth. Most of the trees had been allowed to die, or had been destroyed by goats and sheep that ate the leaves away. The top soil, therefore, had nothing to hold it and it was washed away from the sides of the rolling hills that make up most of Palestine, leaving them rocky and hard to cultivate. Gathering in the valleys, the same good soil chocked up streams and wells, making marshes where no food could be raised but where the mosquitoes that carry malaria could multiply. In other parts, the winds had swept sand in from the desert or from the seashore, covering the soil and making it barren. Even the best sections were farmed with the most ancient wooden ploughs. 

The Jews who lived in Palestine could help the newcomers very little. They numbered but 35,000, and most of them were supported by money sent to them by their home towns in Europe. 

They had come for the religious purpose of studying or of ending their days in the Holy Land. A Jewish agricultural school and farming settlement, however, had been set up in 1870 and this provided some training to the pioneers who knew nothing of hard physical labor.

The rocky soil and the diseases, however, were still too much for the newcomers. They were just about forced to give up their four new colonies, when Baron Edmond Rothschild of Paris was encouraged to help this venture. He bought new land, cattle and equipment, sent over instructors to help the pioneers, and even bought up crops to make them succeed. With his funds and those of the Chovevei Zion, twenty-six colonies were set up by 1895.

Yet they were only a handful of struggling Jewish farmers in Palestine, unable to support themselves. They had no rights there; and the Turkish government threatened time and again to put a stop to the whole thing. This was not enough to build a 

Jewish home land. Something greater had to be done. The

Two different ways of going about this were suggested. first suggestion was that of Ahad Ha-am. He felt that while it was important to build colonies, the most important thing to do first was to build schools, to write books, and to make of Palestine a center of Jewish learning. In this way the Jewish people would be awakened to new life and a new pride in itself. Then it would have the strength needed to rebuild its home land. And then too, he declared, the Jews could go on living wherever they might be, strengthened by the spirit which would come forth from Palestine.

VACT. L' 0. Ex

33. 

THEODOR HERZL 

But another leader arose with a different program, Dr. 

Theodor Herzl, Viennese playwright and newspaper columnist. He was very much disturbed by the Dreyfus trial in Paris which took place in 1896. A Jewish captain in the French army was accused of selling military secrets to the Germans. From the very beginning there was great doubt of his guilt. He, himself, constantly insisted that he was innocent. There were witnesses who declared that the letters, which he was accused of having written to the German government, were not in his handwriting. Many who studied the case were convinced that another officer was the real traitor. 

Nevertheless, not only was Dreyfus declared guilty, but the trial seemed to be directed against the entire Jewish people. As 

Dreyfus was called forward and his sword broken, the mob began yelling, "Death to the Jews.

Theodor Herzl was assigned to write up this trial for his newspaper in Vienna. He was struck not so much by the possible injustice done to Dreyfus, as by the attempt to use this trial as an attack against the entire Jewish people. He had imagined that anti-Semitism was dead in free France. He was shocked to discover it in this awful form in the land which was first to free the Jews. His people, he

His people, he felt, must do something about it. 

What were they to do? He sat down and wrote his answer, The 

Jewish State, calling upon Jews to set up a home of their own, stating again the very arguments of Smolenskin and Pinsker, even though he had never heard of these men. He wrote as a man on fire, and no sooner had anyone read the book than they saw that here was the man of will to lead them.

Accepting the leadership, he was determined to organize the Jewish efforts for Palestine in a more powerful way. He immediately called for a great Zionist Congress in 1897. Now, for the first time, Jewish leaders from the corners of the earth came together at Basle, Switzerland, to plan for their people. The very thought of such a gathering stirred Jewish pride. The Congress declared that the purpose of Zionism is to create a "publicly recognized, legally secured home for the Jewish people in 

Palestine. This is known as the Basle Program. 

Thus the first task which Herzl set before him was not to build colonies, but to secure a "Charter." The purpose of the "Charter" was to give Palestine to the Jews so that those who are oppressed by anti-Semitism might go and settle there. The colonies would thus be built on a firm political foundation. The 

Charter was to be given by the Turkish government, which then owned Palestine, and to be approved by all other nations. Herzl spent months and years, therefore, speaking to important statesmen, even to the kings of Europe and the pope, telling them in a moving way why the Jews needed the country. 

Herzl also built up a World Zionist Organization, by securing members for it from all over the world. He set up a bank to gather money for the Jewish National Fund for the special purpose

Jer1 0. 心 ​PRA f. Feat {3/ NO ני 

CON 

26 1 ... JO 

---

34. of buying land in Palestine to be held forever as the property of the Jewish people.

Although doctors had warned him that his heart was bad, Herzl, spirit flaming, labored night and day until suddenly, in 1904, his heart simply gave out and the great leader was no more. He

Shocked, Zionism now stopped for a while to see how matters stood. Its great representative to the world was gone. He had failed to gain anything like a "Charter" despite years of dealings with the Sultan (ruler of Turkey) who could have given it. had failed to gather any real support from the Jewish men of wealth, who did not understand that a home land could help the Jews when they were in trouble anywhere. Some, especially in 

Germany, imagined that nothing could possibly happen to them. 

The government of Russia, where the masses of Zionists lived, was forbidding Zionist meetings and the gathering of funds. And 

Palestine itself, the colonies were struggling, and in 1902 had suffered a terrible season without rainfall, which ruined crops and drove many off the land for food. in Among the Zionists there was disagreement as to what should be done. Some gave up all hope of success in Palestine and left 

Zionism to find other places where Jews might go to live. These formed the Jewish Territorial Association which sought to find lands in East Africa and in Irak as possible places for Jews to go. These efforts did not succeed.

THE SECOND ALIYAH (WAVE OF IMMIGRATION) 

The needs of the Jewish people, however, simply would not let the Zionist movement die. Pogroms broke out once more in 

Russia. In 1903, the government itself plotted with the police and a newspaperman to attack the Jews of Kishinev. For two full days they told mobs to go ahead. Forty-five Jews were killed after terrible tortures; nine hundred were wounded, and all the

Jewish shops and homes were robbed and wrecked. Two years later, when many Russians wanted to remove the Czar, because the government had shown itself to be stupid and weak in losing the war with Japan, the Czar determined to use pogroms to turn the attention of the common people from the government. Hoodlums were organized under the name of "Black Hundreds" who, for over a year, set upon one Jewish community after another. It was 1881-82 (see page 29) all over again, and worse. In Odessa alone, over three hundred Jews were killed and forty thousand had their businesses ruined. This time the Czar deceived the people again. 

He gave them what was supposed to be a parliament of the people, called the Duma; and then shortly thereafter, he simply closed its doors and locked the representatives out. Many

Russia's millions of Jews were bitter and desperate. 

Joined the movement to overthrow the Czar. Many fled to America. 

And great numbers became more convinced than ever that the Jew needed a homeland of his own.

35. 

As a result of these conditions, there came to Palestine a new immigration of pioneers of steel will, known as the Second Aliyah. They were determined to avoid the failures of the First 

Aliyah by being self-supporting and by doing all the work themselves, whether it was draining of swamps or guarding crops or building roads. Even though this was hard and dangerous, they refused to hire Arabs to do it for them, as was done before. 

They also determined to get fair wages and working conditions for Jewish laborers working for others. 

Recognizing that the hard task before them was to save not just themselves but a whole people, they said, "Let us work in groups like large families, putting together our money and our efforts. In this way, we shall not have some starving and others rich, but all of us shall have to share whatever we have. Therefore, they formed colonies called Cooperatives, which means that all the people work together and own everything together. The members ate together. They decided what labor each was to do and they enjoyed their successes and met their difficulties together. 

These colonies in turn cooperated with each other, sending their vegetables and their milk to market together, buying their supplies together, and loaning each other funds and workers in time of need.

For some city workers, too, cooperative methods were used. 

Today, the Jewish buses in Palestine are owned by a company composed of the drivers themselves. Many restaurants, laundries, theaters, and companies for the building of roads and houses and for the care of orange groves, are conducted the same way. 

The Second Aliyah likewise established a firm system of Hebrew education and culture. A Hebrew High School was founded in the new all-Jewish city of Tel-Aviv, as well as a daily newspaper in the Hebrew language, a great trade school called the Haifa Technicum, and the Bezalel School for Arts and Crafts. 

By 1914 Jews in Palestine numbered almost one hundred thousand.

WORLD WAR I AND PALESTINE 

But the coming of the World War in 1914 brought terrible hardships and almost destroyed the movement. Trade stopped and many businesses were ruined. In 1915 a plague of locusts ate up many of the crops, and epidemics of cholera (a deadly disease) and other sicknesses broke out. Thousands of the new settlers had to flee or serve in the Turkish army. Many colonies were given up. Those that limped along were persecuted by the Turks, who accused them of helping the enemy, the British. By the end of the war, only fifty-five thousand Jews remained. 

The future was dark, but Zionist leaders were hopeful. Led by Dr. Chaim Weizmann, a chemist whose scientific knowledge helped the English make explosives when they needed them desperately,

1: T 3 2 T 3: OLC Y 72 ༔『* 10 ATMA

36. they won a victory even out of the war. In 1917 they succeeded in having Britain issue the Balfour Declaration, saying that it viewed "with favor, the establishment in Palestine of a national home for the Jewish people and that it would help them to gain it.

This was the "Charter" toward which Herzl had taken the first steps. After the victory of the Allies, it was recognized by the world and made part of the treaty of peace. The League of 

Nations, which was organized, gave England the mandate over Palestine. A mandate means that a highly civilized government like England is given charge of a country which is not yet ready to govern itself, until the people become ready to set up their Own rule. The Zionist Organization was to act as the representative of the Jews to help England govern the Jews of Palestine. 

The Jews rejoiced. They had suffered terribly during the war, whose Eastern front had swept right over the areas of Poland and Russia where most of the European Jews lived. Then had come the 

Russian war. The Red armies, the Bolsheviks, fought the White armies of the Czar, The White armies stirred up new pogroms against our people. War, murder, and hunger destroyed a quarter of a million of them. Thousands of those who were left now began to study Hebrew and farming so that they could be Chalutsim, as the pioneers of the Third, or post-war, Aliyah were called. 

THE THIRD ALIYAH 

Palestine itself was put back on its feet. New land was bought, swamps drained, forests planted. Old colonies were rebuilt and new ones set up with better equipment through money provided by Zionist funds and private individuals. Hundreds of businesses and factories were set up in the growing cities by men of business experience who brought in their own money. This group was known as the Third Aliyah. A powerful labor organization, called the Histadruth, was formed, to obtain for the workers fair wages, to provide care for the sick, as well as study classes, concerts, and a theater. and a theater. The Histadruth published its own newspaper and elected many representatives to the Jewish self-government which controls all strictly Jewish affairs. 

A large station was built on the Jordan River to generate electric power for the growing land. A plant was set up to extract valuable minerals from the Dead Sea. New roads, New roads, docks, and homes were built, making parts of the ancient land quite modern and appealing. By 1929, the Jews of Palestine numbered 170,000. 

The Arabs numbered 770,000.

THE ARAB PROBLEM 

In 1929, however, another difficulty threatened to stop the movement. Certain Arab leaders organized an attack on the Jews, killing over a hundred, and destroying several colonies. Brave

Jewish self-defense prevented the losses from being greater. 

The Arabs' opposition to Zionism has been based upon their belief that after World War I they were to rule themselves. For hundreds of years the Arabic world had been ruled over by Turkey.
