When the war came, Britain encouraged the Arabs to revolt. After 

Britain, with Arabic and Jewish help, had defeated Turkey and the Allies had won the war, the Arabs in the southern portion, known as Arabia, were given their independence. Those in Syria and 

Irak became partly independent. Those of Palestine hoped they would become a self-ruled Arabic country too. But Britain declared that here she had promised a homeland for the Jews. This claim was supported by the League of Nations. 

The Jewish claim was based not only upon their great need, but upon a program of fairness. For if Palestine were to become a Jewish home land, two peoples could gain self-rule from the war, instead of only one. Since all of Palestine on both sides of the 

Jordan is but a tiny fraction only three per cent of the large Arabic lands, many sections of which are undeveloped there is plenty of room for all the Arabic peoples to live under their own rule if they so desire. And those Arabs who would continue to live in Palestine would not be interfered with by the Jews, but would surely be benefited by the better opportunities to make a living and the care for public health provided by the Jews. A11 the land which the Jews sought would be bought from the Arabs willing to sell, and paid for on terms agreeable to them. 

The truth of the Jew's claim of fairness has been proved by the fact that the Arab population of Palestine has grown by hundreds of thousands, even more than the Jewish. Their earnings have greatly increased and their health has been much improved, especially in those places that are near Jewish settlements. 

Even Arabs from surrounding countries have migrated to Palestine to take advantage of the better life.

But the old Arab leaders who were used to ruling over their people in their own way disliked the newcomers and feared that they might upset their rule and their comfortable easy incomes. 

For the majority of the Arabs live in ignorance and poverty on the farms. Like the share-croppers in America, they pay such large rents and at such great interest on their debts to the landowners, that they never have enough to live on. The Arab land- 

Owners realized that the Jews would set an example of freedom to the Arabs. Arab peasants would then seek to free themselves from their slavery. The landowners, therefore, sought to stop the 

Zionist movement and organized riots against it. They stirred up the natural feeling of hostility which people have to strangers, especially if they wear different clothes and have a different religion. They had their newspapers and preachers say that the 

Jews wanted to destroy the Arabs and their religion. They told lies about Jews murdering Arabs. They even printed fake pictures showing the Jews burning down the "Mosque of Omar, the Arabs' beautiful temple of worship in Jerusalem. In this way they stirred up the bloody attacks of 1929.

Similar, although much smaller, riots had broken out in 1920 and 1921. These had so frightened the British Government that it forbade Jews to settle east of the Jordan, a section three times as large as western Palestine. Britain also turned over to the

Arabs government lands which were supposed to have been given to the Jews for Jewish colonies. Now, in 1929, the Arab leaders thought that their riots could produce even greater results and stop the whole movement. And they almost succeeded. The British government, which had never had the courage to enforce the Jewish demands for their homeland or to insist that the Arabs be friendly, did not want to be troubled any longer. She practically called off the Balfour Declaration and almost stopped Jewish immigration and purchase of land. 

Zionist leaders resigned and huge Jewish meetings were held in Europe and America in protest. Statesmen were appealed to. 

Finally the British government declared that the movement could continue again. That was most fortunate for the Jews, for Hitlerism was rising in Europe, and Palestine was soon to save our people by the hundreds of thousands. But let us first see what had been happening in the rest of the world, how the Jews had been living in the democracies, and how Hitler rose to power, seeking to destroy democracy, and with it the very life of our people.

HOW THE JEWS LIVED UNDER FREEDOM 

Let us now return to the story of the Jew in the free lands. 

You will remember that we had to leave that story to tell how Russia held back that spirit of freedom, and how many of our people left that land to find democracy elsewhere. 

IN WESTERN EUROPE 

In France, England, Germany, Central Europe, and America, the Jews were now free. Like corks held down under water, and then let go, they popped forth into every branch of human activity. 

They were eager to enter the new life of commerce and manufacturing, of music and art, of science and invention from which they had been left out. As if to prove that they were truly the equals of others, they worked with great diligence. Thus they made some of the most important contributions to human progress. 

When we tell now of these famous Jews who added to the knowledge and enjoyment of the world, we do not mean that they were the only ones who did so. The great progress of man, in the last 

150 years, was made by all the various Western races and nations working together. Indeed, it came about only because democracy encouraged them to work together. You have read of the great men of other peoples elsewhere. Here we shall tell of the Jews, who worked hand in hand with them to build the modern Western World which we enjoy.

As we might expect, the branches of work in which the Jews of Europe made the greatest progress were those in which they had already had some experience. Retail business was one of these. 

Jews whom old restrictions had forced to be peddlers, going from village to village with packs on their backs, could now, in freedom, set up little shops. Then, by skillful purchase of attractive goods and fair dealings with their customers, they could grow, as many did, into large stores. The growing sales in such stores encouraged manufacturing.

To set up such factories, capital, that is, saved-up money, is needed before one can begin. Money was also needed to secure faster methods of transporting goods, by building steam railways and great merchant ships. Some of the Jewish families that had ready cash now lent their money for these new purposes. As they succeeded and their funds grew, they were able to encourage many valuable improvements. Thus among those who helped build the railroads of Europe was the famous House of Rothschild in Austria, the Pereire family in France, and Baron de Hirsch in Turkey. 

The old method of paying for the goods shipped between countries had been to send gold back and forth on ships. With the growth of such trade, this became very inconvenient and dangerous. Therefore a new system was worked out. Banks were set up with branch offices in foreign lands. An Englishman who bought

American goods paid his English bank which then wrote to its American branch office to pay the American seller. An American who bought English goods paid the American bank which then wrote its English representative to pay the English seller. 

In this way, both sellers were paid without gold having to be shipped in two directions. The House of Rothschild was particularly fitted for this new system of banking, inasmuch as the many sons of the founder, Mayer Amschel Rothschild, settled in London, Paris, Frankfort, Vienna, and Naples. They became very successful in their own countries as well as in their business between nations. Later on, however, many non-Jews put money together to form very large banks and crowded the Jews out. Jew- By the middle of the 1800's, the Jews were beginning to show great skill in the sciences. The Jews helped to increase the knowledge of mathematics upon which Albert Einstein, a German- Jewish scientist, later an American citizen, built his theory of relativity, which gave men a new understanding of the world. ish inventors helped in the creation of the automobile and the airplane, the telephone, microphone, and sewing machine. German

Jewish chemists discovered new ways to make dyes which made Germany the center of a great new industry. 

But the greatest of all Jewish progress in science has come in medicine. Jews have long been famous as doctors. Moses Maimonides of the Middle Ages was not only the greatest Jewish philosopher, but so good a physician that the Sultan of Egypt called him to be his personal doctor. Indeed, during the very period when the Christian 

Church kept Jews out of all honorable occupations, popes and kings still very often had Jews for their private physicians. 

Under the friendly rule of Democracy, the Jewish talent for medicine flourished as never before. Indeed, so important were the studies of five Jewish doctors, that they won for themselves the much desired Nobel Prize, given in various fields to the men whose service has been outstanding in the entire world for that year. 

Paul Ehrlich taught the science of curing diseases with specific chemical medicines that attack the germs that cause them, and discovered cures for some of the world's worst diseases. August von 

Wassermann discovered a test to show whether or not a person has syphilis. Other Jewish doctors, Otto Warburg and George Klemperer, increased mankind's understanding of cancer. Another, George

Widal, perfected a sure test for the presence of typhoid fever. 

Oskar Minkowski laid the basis for the understanding of diabetes, so that later scientists could develop a cure for it. Jewish surgeons, such as Heinrich Neumann, throat specialist of Vienna, have been among the best in the world. 

In the understanding of the human mind and in curing its diseases, Sigmund Freud, and other Jewish physicians, created the entirely new science of psychoanalysis.

The quality of Jewish work in science may be seen from the fact that although the Jewish people have been only one per cent of the general population of the Western World, they have been about ten per cent of all the Nobel Prize winners.

42. 

The bright new world of culture had a special attraction for Jews, who joined hands with their countrymen in creating the great literature, art, and music of modern times. Jews founded some of the world's leading newspapers: the Berliner Tageblatt, the Frankfurter Zeitung, the London Daily Telegraph. They developed the idea of the newspaper column and formed the first great European agencies for gathering news: Reuters in England, Havas in France, Wolff in Germany. They

They wrote fine novels, such as The World's Illusion, by Jacob 

Wasserman and The Case of Sergeant Grischa, by Arnold Zweig. 

became great writers of drama, such as Franz Molnar and Arthur 

Schnitzler. Andre Maurois of France and Emil Ludwig of Germany created a new and more interesting form of biography. George 

Brandes of Denmark became the outstanding student of literature of his day.

Some were numbered among the world's best producers of plays: 

Max Reinhardt. Some were great sculptors: Mark Antokolski and 

Jacob Epstein. Others were famous painters.

Music especially appealed to our people. Jacques Offenbach created the Comic Opera. So great was the urge to play music that even under the oppression of Russia, talented Jews came forward and won audiences throughout the world: Anton Rubinstein the pianist, Bronislav Huberman, Mischa Elman, Jascha Heifetz, Toscha Seidel,

Leopold Auer, who with Jews born in other countries Joseph Joachim, Fritz Kreisler and Yehudi Menuhin - include most of the greatest concert violinists of modern times. 

A few of our people even rose to the highest office in public life. Benjamin Disraeli (Lord Beaconfield) of England led the Con servative Party for over a generation, and was twice Premier of his country (1868-69; 1874-80). He brought to Britain the control of the Suez Canal and had the Queen proclaimed Empress of India. 

Although baptized as a Christian at twelve, his loyalty to the Jewish people was as clear as his name was Jewish (Disraeli meaning Israel). He used his office to protect Jews in other nations, and his many famous novels urged people to give the Jew complete freedom.

In France a brilliant lawyer, Adolphe Cremieux, became Minister of Justice in 1848, and a member of the Government of National Defense in 1869. Throughout his life he was a powerful fighter for full equality for his people, and was active in the Alliance Israelite Universelle, a society to join together all French Jews in defense of Jewish rights everywhere in the world. In Germany, Ferdinand Lassalle founded the first workers' party in the middle of the last century, and wrote books and pamphlets that led governments to help the working man in many lands. Eduard Lasker later helped found the National Liberal Party. Walter Rathenau, as 

Foreign Minister, tried to rebuild German industry and prosperity after her defeat in World War I. In Italy, Luigi Luzzatti became 

Prime Minister (1909-11). In our own times, Leon Blum led France as its Premier.

IN AMERICA EARLY SETTLEMENT 

In America, too, the democratic spirit brought to our people a period of joy, full of opportunities. Our people which has had an intimate part in the development of the Western Hemisphere since its first settlement, came to love the United States as the democratic hope of the world. For here the oppressed of all peoples 

Puritans and Quakers, Catholics and Jews, Germans and Irish and Poles - kept coming to fashion together a land which would be free for all of them. "Give me your tired, your poor, Your huddled masses yearning to breathe free, Send these, the homeless, tempest-tost to me. 

I lift my lamp beside the golden door.

Thus wrote the Jewish poetess, Emma Lazarus, in words inscribed upon the Statue of Liberty. 

Jews, indeed, had a great part in Columbus' discovery of America. Marranos of the Spanish Court, especially Luis de Santangel, secured the permission and provided much of the money for the journey. Famous Jewish map makers and inventors of instruments to guide ships at sea had made the charts and instruments which he used. Five Marranos were in his crew; one, its interpreter, Luis de Torres, was the first to set foot on American soil. 

Jews converted by force, were among the earliest Spanish and Portuguese colonists. They introduced the sugar cane industry into Brazil in 1548. When the Dutch and the English began to establish colonies, Jews went to these in particular, to escape the Courts of the Inquisition (see III, pp. 68-69) which Spain and 

Portugal had brought even into the New World. Indeed, the first book written by a Jew in America - Great Remembrance by Rabbi 

Isaac Aboab da Fonseca • describes how courageously the many Jews of the prosperous city of Recife (Brazil) fought together with the Dutch to ward off the Portuguese siege. But they lost (1654) and the Jews were forced to flee northward to the West Indies. In 

Curacao and Jamaica, especially, they developed the vanilla and sugar industries and a flourishing foreign trade. 

The first Jewish group to come to the United States arrived in New Amsterdam (now New York) in 1654. They came from Brazil.

Others then came from other colonies in the South and some directly from Holland. They fought for equal rights from the beginning, and often won their case. Asser Levy was their first courageous spokesman. The Dutch governor of New Amsterdam declared that the 

Jews could pay a tax instead of taking their turn guarding against unfriendly Indians. But Asser Levy did not want to pay the tax and won for the Jews the right to stand guard together with the other citizens. When the English took over the city, the Jews continued to make progress. In 1728, organized as Congregation 

Shearith Israel, they built the first synagogue in North America. 

The second Jewish settlement in the United States was in

44. 

Newport, R. I. (1677) where the oldest Jewish cemetery in this country may still be seen. Here, the whale oil industry for lamps was brought to America by Jacob Rodriguez Rivera, who fled from Spain so that he could live openly as a Jew. Here Aaron Lopez, a Marrano from Portugal, built up a fleet of over thirty ships which then made Newport the trading center of the land. Lopez helped support Newport's fine synagogue, and was well known to the whole community. He often brought Christian officials and scholars, such as Ezra Stiles, President of Yale, with him to the synagogue. He helped provide the town with the finest library in the colonies. But when the Revolutionary War came, the British seized the harbor, wrecked the ships and hundreds of homes, and plundered the library. Newport never recovered.

When Washington was inaugurated the first President of the United States in 1789, the six Jewish congregations then existent (New York, Newport, Philadelphia, Savannah, Charleston, and Richmond, Va.) sent him greetings. The Father of Our Country replied in a friendly manner and to the point, speaking of America's pride in having given to mankind a "liberal policy worthy of imitation ......The government of the United States, which gives to bigotry no sanction, to persecution no assistance, requires only that they who live under its protection shall demean themselves as good citizens in giving it on all occasions their effectual support." 

Washington knew how great had been the support which Jews had already given towards the winning of the War of Independence. 

Among these was Haym Salomon. From early youth a lover of freedom, he left Poland when the movement for Polish liberty failed. Upon coming to New York, he joined the Sons of Liberty, who led the way to the American Revolution. Imprisoned twice by the British, once even sentenced to death, he escaped to Philadelphia. Here he used his experience as a broker in foreign securities to give extreme ly valuable aid to George Washington and the Americans fighting the Revolution. He was the official broker to the Government Office of Finance and the right hand man of Robert Morris, head of the

Department of Finance of the Continental Congress. Haym Salomon served as agent of the French Embassy and helped to promote the alliance between France and America. He assisted also in the handling of the loans which the Dutch Government made to America. 

In those difficult days, when the leaders of American Government gave up their private businesses and received little pay from the government, Haym Salomon helped many of them to meet their personal expenses, These men included some of the most prominent leaders of that day - generals and other men who were later to become presidents of the United States. In the course of his duties Haym Salomon likewise expended his own money and when he died suddenly in 1785, his wife and four children had nothing left but the furniture in their home.

On the battle-field, Benjamin Nones came from France to fight for American freedom. So well did he serve that he was made Major and staff officer under Washington. Isaac Franks was captured by the British and escaped to hold many responsible commands, rising in rank until, after the war, he was honored with the Commission

Ci s is (e) ཛྫཱ,、

45. of Lieutenant Colonel. His cousin, Major David S. Franks, was honored after the war with important missions to the American 

Ambassadors, John Jay in Madrid and Benjamin Franklin in Paris. 

Mordecai Sheftall led a bold defense of Savannah, Georgia, and was tortured when captured by the British. Twenty-six Jews of Charleston served in one company, which was often called the "Jew's Company.

Stirring the courage of his countrymen, Rabbi Gershom Mendes Seixas, rabbi of the historic Congregation Shearith Israel of New York, fled when the British conquered the city, taking the greater part of his congregation with him to carry on the struggle from Philadelphia.

JUDAISM AND THE AMERICAN SPIRIT 

For the Jews knew that there were strong ties that bound the spirit of Judaism with the spirit of a free America. The Puritans had fought against kings in England because the Old Testament our Bible - had taught them that only God is the real King. They came to America for the declared purpose of establishing a Godgoverned country - "like that of the children of Israel, in the good old days before their stubborn hearts conceived the desire for a king. Therefore, they and other early colonists based their laws in America not on the English constitution, but upon our Bible. The Pilgrim Code of 1636, which was the first, began by stating that "It was the great privilege of Israel of old and soe was acknowledged by them...that God gave them right judgments and true Lawes...All Christians especially ought always to have an eye thereunto in the framing of their politique constitutions. 

The Codes of Law of the Massachusetts and Connecticut colonies quoted verse by verse the Torah laws upon which their legislative rules were based, and the New Haven Code explained that it was grounded on "the Judicial Laws of God, as they were delivered by Moses.

The first and truest American holiday, Thanksgiving, was inspired by Jewish festivals of thanks outlined in the Bible. 

Governor Bradford, who proclaimed it, compared the experiences of the Pilgrims to those of the Jews. Both of them had fled from oppression, wandered through danger and wilderness and were finally brought by God to a good land. The prayer which he uttered in that early Thanksgiving was patterned directly after the prayer which Israel had been ordered to speak upon first coming into the promised land and offering its first-fruits in thankfulness to 

God (Deuteronomy 26:5-10). Governor Bradford's exact prayer and spelling were as follows: "Our fathers were Englishmen which came over this great ocean and were ready to perish in the wilderness but they cried unto the Lord and He heard their voyce, and looked on their adversitie, etc. 

Then he concluded his prayer with portions of Psalm 107:1-9 which tell of the same Jewish experience:

!

46. "Let them therefore praise the Lord because he is good, and his mercies endure for ever. Yea, let them which have been redeemed of the Lord, shew how he hath delivered them from the hand of the oppressour, etc.

These early leaders loved the Hebrew language. They required all the students at Harvard College to study it four days a week during the first year and to be able to translate any part of the 

Five Books of Moses. 

11

Generations later, Hebraic ideals of freedom continued to inspire the leaders of this country. As they moved toward independence from England, they chose the inscription for their 

"Liberty Bell" from the book of Leviticus (25:10): ...Proclaim liberty throughout the land unto all the inhabitants thereof. 

Their Declaration of Independence itself was clearly based upon the religious ideas which the Hebrew prophets had long before proclaimed, "that all men are created equal, that they are endowed by their creator with certain inalienable rights, that among these are life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness. Just after the 

Declaration was adopted, a committee consisting of Benjamin Franklin, Thomas Jefferson, and John Adams met and designed a Seal for the new United States Government. It showed Pharaoh with upraised sword, pursuing the Israelites through the Red Sea. On the other shore, Moses under a pillar of fire is raising his hand and causing the waters to drown Pharaoh's Army. Although this seal was not adopted, it shows the influence which the story and faith of our people had upon America's "Founding Fathers. 11

This was the spirit of the land for whose independence the Jewish people, though then few in number, labored and sacrificed to the limit of their powers. When it was won, the Constitution then provided safeguards for "freedom of religion. It stated 

(Article VI, sect. 3): "No religious test shall ever be required as a qualification to any office or public trust under the United 

States. And the very first of the Bill of Rights, or first ten amendments, without which the States refused to ratify the Constitution, states that "Congress shall make no law respecting an establishment of religion, or prohibiting the exercise thereof. 

Under this Constitution, people of our faith have enjoyed a continuous period of security, success, and happy fellowship with their neighbors. Complete equality did not come to them at once; some of the States were slow in allowing Jews to hold public offices. Opportunity for Jews to gain employment in all the various kinds of work has never been equal to that of the non-Jews. 

Even during World War II, with the country desperately in need of workers to produce war material for victory, old prejudices against hiring Jews kept them out of countless factories despite the President's order against it. Trouble-makers and movements of prejudice have arisen from time to time to threaten our safety here. But during this long period, the total atmosphere of 

America has been for the Jew, as for others, one of opportunity, security, and friendliness, so that there has been attracted to these shores the largest and most successful Jewish community in 

---

1 こ ​3 7 100% C! ནིཙྩཱ 1ཨཉྫཱ 2 1; 2

47. the world today. Its four and three-quarter million Jews represent almost one-third of all world Jewry. 

How did American Israel grow to such strength? Their early numbers were small but distinguished. After the Revolution, also, they continued to produce men of skill and service to their country. Judah Touro, a son of the Rabbi of Newport, Rhode Island, born on the day after the battle of Bunker Hill, became one of the most charitable men of his day. His wealth, honestly attained in trade and commerce in New Orleans, made possible synagogues and churches, built hospitals and aided charitable institutions for all peoples. He provided for the care of the old Jewish cemetery at Newport; he contributed half of the funds for the Bunker Hill 

Monument. 

Prominent in public life was Mordecai Manuel Noah, editor of various New York newspapers in turn, and writer of the most popular plays of nis time. He was sheriff, judge, surveyor of the 

Port of New York, and United States Consul to Tunis (1813). He was one of the first American Zionists. But he knew that Palestine was not yet ready for the Jewish people. Therefore he bought part of an island on the Niagara River for the Jews to settle. called it Ararat, which was the name of the mountain upon which 

Noah's ark came to settle. The undertaking failed, but twenty years later Noah pleaded for Christians to help Jews resettle in 

Palestine. 

He

Uriah P. Levy spent a lifetime of adventure and seamanship, finally being raised to the rank of Commodore, the highest office in the United States Navy. At twenty he enlisted, becoming master of a sailing ship fighting in the War of 1812. He captured several English ships, but was finally captured himself and kept prisoner until the war was over. He was a fighter and was quick to avenge any insult to this country or his faith. 

A Jewess, Rebecca Gratz, became the founder and active leader of many institutions of charity for all people in Philadelphia, and for many years conducted the first Jewish Sunday School in America. It is said that her beautiful life was the model for the character of Rebecca in Sir Walter Scott's Ivanhoe, THE GERMAN MIGRATION 

Such were the varied services that Jews performed for America even though their numbers in 1840 had grown to but 15,000. Most of these folk were descended from Spanish and Portuguese Jews. 

But in 1840 a new and much larger wave of Jewish immigration came to America, this time from Germany and Central Europe. For it was in the 1840's that the German's hopes for freedom were being crushed by backward forces. Many Jews and Christians fled to this land of liberty and opportunity. America now was growing rapidly and people were moving toward the Central and Western states. By 

1880, the Jews of America numbered 250,000.

The German Jews came mostly without wealth, but they had

48. great energy and a good business sense. 

They soon made progress and started to build synagogues for themselves, as well as charitable and educational institutions that now guide American Jewish life. Many began as petty merchants with packs on their backs and traveled throughout the country, settling down here and there to begin the scattered American Jewish communities. 

RELIGIOUS INSTITUTIONS 

Some of the immigrants brought over with them the ideas of Reform Judaism, which had been developed in Germany (as you read on pages 18-19). Reform now grew

Reform now grew much more than in Germany. 

American ideas of Democracy made it possible for each man to worship God as he pleased. Young and bold leaders came from Germany to direct the growing American synagogues. The greatest among them was the Reform leader, Rabbi Isaac M. Wise, who came to 

Albany, New York, and from there went to Cincinnati. Through the remarkable organizing ability of Rabbi Wise, the Union of American Hebrew Congregations was founded in 1873, which joined the Reform congregations to work for their common good. Through this Union, Rabbi Wise gained the support for the establishment of the Hebrew Union College, America's first permanent school for the training of rabbis, which opened in Cincinnati in 1875. In its 

In its very first class of graduates were men who proved that rabbis born and trained in America could become great builders not merely of their Own synagogues, but of Jewish life as a whole. Joseph Krauskopf founded the Jewish Publication Society, to promote the writing and reading of fine books on Judaism in English, and the National Farm School, to give our people agricultural training. Henry Berkowitz founded the Jewish Chautauqua Society, to bring the knowledge of our people and our faith to the whole country through traveling speakers. David Philipson wrote the standard history, The Reform 

Movement in Judaism, and other important works. 

In the midst of his long labors as President and teacher of the College and editor of magazines for Reform Judaism, Rabbi Wise organized the Central Conference of American Rabbis in 1889. 1889. This is a society of all the Reform Rabbis of the country which meets every year to work out programs for Reform Judaism. 

But the rapid growth of Reform was not without opposition. 

Just as in Germany (see page 20), a movement grew up which came to be known as Conservative Judaism. This branch of Judaism declared that Jewish traditions should not be changed so rapidly, but should be taught and interpreted in a more modern manner. It favored using the English language for teaching and preaching, but demanded that the services be in Hebrew. It also insisted upon keeping the kosher food laws. It preserved the two days of the holidays and in general made very few changes in Jewish customs and laws. Rabbi Isaac Leeser, who was in America long before 

Rabbi Isaac M. Wise, was one of the founders of the movement, even though he refused to allow himself to be called by any special designation, whether Orthodox or Conservative. He translated the whole Bible into English, wrote Jewish textbooks, and edited a good Jewish magazine in English, the Occident, the first one in America.

*.

49. 

Later, in 1886, Dr. Sabato Morais, who succeeded Rabbi Leeser in 

Philadelphia, established the Jewish Theological Seminary in New York City for the training of American rabbis in the Conservative spirit. In this he was aided by several American rabbis well known for their scholarship, such as Rabbi Alexander Kohut, who wrote an eight-volume dictionary of the entire Talmud, and Rabbi 

Marcus Jastrow.

With the coming of a forceful leader who knew how to put his scholarly thoughts into powerful forms, Conservative Judaism began a new period of strength and growth. This leader was Dr. 

Solomon Schechter, who became President of the Jewish Theological Seminary in 1902. It was especially attractive to the great masses of Jews from Orthodox backgrounds in Eastern Europe who were entering America at that period. They sought some more modern ways of keeping their Judaism without going too far from traditional ways. Conservative Judaism now built up its own strong institutions similar to those of Reform: a Seminary for rabbis with a great library, a Rabbinical Assembly which is an association of Conservative rabbis (founded in 1901)

United Synagogue to link together its congregations (1913). 

and a Soon to follow, Orthodox Judaism established its own institutions. It organized several rabbinical colleges, including the great Rabbi Isaac Elchanan Seminary of Yeshiva in New York 

City (1896). In 1928, it added the Yeshiva College which gives complete university training under Jewish auspices. Today, therefore, Judaism has three strong branches, which recently formed the Synagogue Council of America in which all three cooperate in matters of common interest to Judaism. 

CHARITABLE AND OTHER INSTITUTIONS 

We must go back again to the period of the German Jewish migration (1840-80) to find the beginnings of most of our great charitable institutions, such as Mt. Sinai Hospital of New York and the Jewish Foster Home of Philadelphia. The German Jews also founded the "United Hebrew Charities of many cities, an organization to raise funds for many Jewish charitable institutions of one city, in a joint drive.

It was then, too, that the Independent Order of B'nai B'rith the largest Jewish lodge, was founded (1843). It met for social purposes and to provide sick and death benefits for its members. 

Then it built great orphan homes and supported hospitals. Growing in strength, it took action to demand protection and justice for Jews all over the world. In recent years it created Hillel 

Foundations to provide religious education and social activities for Jewish university students. an In 1859, the Board of Delegates of American Israelites was formed to defend Jewish rights here and abroad. For sixty years eminent lawyer, Simon Wolf, was a representative of this Board and of the Union of American Hebrew Congregations (which later took over the work of the Board) at the Capitol in Washington.

He labored with great skill and charm with Presidents and Congressmen to secure justice and equal rights for our people. He was international president of the B'nai B'rith, and at one time Consul-General to Egypt. He knew intimately every President from 

Lincoln to Wilson and wrote fascinating accounts of their friendliness to the Jews. 

GREAT JEWISH PHILANTHROPISTS 

The years following 1840 represented a period of foundation building, not merely for Jewish institutions, but for many Jewish families who were to play great parts in American Society. They found a variety of opportunities for success in the new land. 

Many established retail businesses. Lazarus Straus, who peddled through Georgia with a horse and wagon, in the end came to build, with his sons and others, the great department stores of Macy's and Abraham and Straus. His son, Oscar Straus, became the first 

Jew to be a member of the Cabinet of the United States government, and first to be its Ambassador to a foreign land, being three times appointed to Turkey. He also served for many years as an 

American representative at the Permanent Court of Arbitration at The Hague which settles disputes among nations. Another son, Isidor Straus, served as a member of Congress. 

A third son,

Nathan Straus, held many offices in the City of New York and became probably the most popular of all American philanthropists. 

He distributed coal and food during the hard times of 1892-93 and 1914-15. For thirty years he fought single-handed to have American cities pasteurize their milk. Finally he won his case by proving with his own laboratories and feeding centers that this can save thousands of children's lives. He gave over $2,000,000 to help Jews and Arabs in Palestine, especially through his health centers. During the administration of President Roosevelt, his son, Nathan Straus, Jr., was Housing Commissioner of the United States.

The Guggenheim family built up an interest in copper mining and refining. The family had many sons famous for their support of art and music and the development of aviation. One son, Simon 

Guggenheim, served as United States Senator from Colorado. He established the well-known Guggenheim Fellowships which enable unusually bright American students of any race to continue their studies abroad.

Other Jewish families whose names are well known today came to America at this time. One was that of Julius Rosenwald, born in Springfield, Illinois, who was such a successful manager of business that he became President of Sears, Roebuck and Co. As a very wealthy man he then became one of the greatest givers to charitable causes in America. In 1917, he gave a million dollars to help war-torn European Jewry. His greatest gifts were to those who, he believed, needed them the most. Therefore, he gave enormous sums to the Negro people in America. He helped to build 

Y.M.C.A. and Y.W.C.A. buildings for them in fifteen cities. In addition, he set up the Rosenwald Fund of $18,000,000 which has helped to build over five thousand schools, teachers' homes, and industrial training shops for Negroes.

1978

51. Another family was that of Adolph S. Ochs, born in Cincinnati. As publisher of the New York Times for almost forty years, beginning in 1896, he gave it the motto: "All the News That's

Fit to Print. He made it one of the world's greatest newspapers, bringing the truth, uncolored by personal or party views, before the people, so that they could act wisely for the benefit of Democracy. To help learn the truth quickly, Mr. Ochs encouraged the development of radio, aviation, and geographical exploration. 

He was one of the creators of the Associated Press to gather news. Married to Rabbi Isaac M. Wise's daughter, Adolph S. Ochs was devoted to the Jewish religion. He built a te.nple in Chatta nooga and a Memorial Hall for Isaac M. Wise in Temple Emanu-El, 

New York City. He organized the campaign for an endowment fund of $5,000,000 for the Hebrew Union College. 

Another famous Jewish family rose to prominence in banking. 

It was that of Jacob Schiff, whose ancestors in Germany had long combined a love of learning with great business ability. In New 

York, Mr. Schiff became head of Kuhn, Loeb and Co., a banking concern which, under his guidance, became an important backer of the growing railways of the country. Jacob Schiff used his success to help his people, becoming one of the founders of the American Jewish Committee to defend Jewish rights. He gave funds to build many new buildings for the Y.M.H.A. and the Jewish 

Theological Seminary. He provided a museum of valuable Jewish books and art objects for Harvard College and gave money for Jewish departments in the New York Public Library and in the Library of Congress, and for the English translations of important 

Hebrew books, now known as the "Schiff Classics.

Best known of all were the families of Morgenthau and Lehman. 

Henry Morgenthau, who had been active in the affairs of the Democratic Party, was appointed U.S. Ambassador to Turkey in 1913. 

After the war, he investigated the bad Jewish conditions in Poland for President Wilson. He also helped bring food and better conditions to the people of the Near East, especially Greece. 

His son, Henry Morgenthau, Jr., has served as Secretary of the 

Treasury since 1934. This is the most important Cabinet office ever held by a Jew. It has been handled with great skill through two of the hardest periods of American history, the business depression and World War II. 

The most famous branch of the Lehman family stemmed from 

Mayer Lehman, founder of Lehman Bros., bankers, and one of the founders of the New York Cotton Exchange. His sons, all born in

New York in the 1870's, include Judge Irving Lehman of the Court of Appeals of New York State, and two sons who took over the banking business: Arthur Lehman, a leader in Jewish charitable affairs and Herbert H. Lehman, for ten years (1933-43) Governor of the State of New York. We shall read more of him later. 

Besides bringing to our shores many families that are still very important in American life, the German-Jewish immigration of 1840-80 may be said to have done these outstanding things: helped to spread retail business to the far corners of America, It

ju 20 SAT 1 .í. * "

52. laying the basis for many of the great stores of today. It set up the Hebrew Union College and helped the Jewish Theological Seminary, which are the centers for Reform and Conservative Judaism in our land. It created great hospitals, orphan homes and charitable institutions of all kinds. It then began to join these in United Hebrew Charities to cooperate with one another in raising money an idea which, a generation later, was taken over by non-Jewish charities in what we now know as the Community Chest. 

THE RUSSIAN IMMIGRATION 

In 1881, there began a new wave of Jewish immigration. Two million Jews entered the United States between 1880 and 1920. 

These immigrants came mostly from Russia, which we must remember included most of Poland then, also. Some, however, came from other lands of Eastern Europe such as Rumania. 

The background of these people was quite different from that of the German-Jewish immigrants. The German Jews had been living more or less together with German non-Jews in Europe. They came over in small numbers as part of a large immigration of German Christians as well as Jews. The American people was then spreading rapidly to all its undeveloped sections of the Middle West, the South, and the West, and large numbers of the Jewish immigrants went along with them, although a great many stayed in New York City and in the East. The East European Jews, however, had been terribly oppressed and had been kept away from any free life with educated non-Jews. They came to this country in large groups, separate from the many non-Jewish immigrants who were likewise coming from Eastern Europe at that time. In some cases, whole 

Jewish communities moved over together from Europe to America. 

They felt strange in the new land, and therefore settled together in the large cities to have each other for company and help. 

They also wanted to be able to keep up the Orthodox Jewish life and the strong Zionist groups that had been the very center of their lives in Europe.

It was this migration with its deep knowledge and love of Jewish tradition that at this time gave support to Orthodox and Conservative Judaism, which now became powerful forces in American Israel, as we have just seen. Hebrew Schools likewise grew up in the large cities, and new and more appealing ways of teaching Hebrew literature to Jewish children were developed. Zionism won great support from these people who knew from bitter experience under the Czars how desperately their brothers needed a land which would be a homeland for the Jews. Yiddish newspapers were founded in New York and other large cities, and were eagerly welcomed by the masses of Jews, as were the excellent dramas of the Yiddish Art Theater. 

Three-fourths of the East European Jews stayed right in New York City where they had landed. A great number of them became workmen in the clothing industry. Many of them had been trained to do this work in the old country. From this time on, the

Y OL 61 

MD C1 2 DX ༄!,

53. garment center which the German Jews also helped to enlarge, became one of the most important industries in America. New and faster methods of production were found, so that, for the first time, good clothing could be made at moderate prices. The average man now could afford clothing that used to be worn only by the wealthy. Jewish immigrants to America thus helped to spread the democratic spirit that all men were equal by making it possible for working men and women to be well dressed just like other members of society.

THE LABOR MOVEMENT 

Working conditions in this clothing industry, however, were at first very bad. Men and women worked from early morning until late at night for pay that barely kept them alive. Many brought the garments to their own homes to sew. Working without proper light and fresh air in what were called "sweatshops, they were almost like slaves. This surely could not be considered real 

Democracy, they said to themselves. 

Forward-looking Jewish leaders therefore determined to help the workers. They organized them into unions to gain higher wages and better conditions. One of the ablest of these leaders was Sidney Hillman. At the age of twenty-three, he led a successful strike of Jews and other immigrant clothing workers in Chicago who were making but $2.50 to $4.00 for a work week of sixty to seventy hours. After coming to New York, he helped form the Amalgamated Clothing Workers' Union in 1914. Since then he has been its president, and Joseph Schlossberg, its secretary. Mr. Hillman's policy was to prove that a union is a good thing for the employers too, by using his union to cut down waste and increase production in their factories. He encouraged owners to organize together, too. In 1937, he made an agreement between all the owners and all the workers in the clothing industry to plan ahead so as too keep the factories and men working as steadily as possible.

The Amalgamated Clothing Workers' Union also helped its members by setting up two large banks, by building a $3,500,000 group of cooperative apartment houses for them, and by establishing cooperative markets, laundries, light, and bus service. 

Other Jewish labor leaders include David Dubinsky now head of the International Ladies Garment Workers' Union. Although

Jews later left the field of clothing work and represent only a small fraction of the membership of the two great unions (which together include over a half million workers), Jewish leaders continue to be active in these unions, protecting and advancing the position of the workers. 

We shall mention here that one of the earliest and greatest of American labor leaders was also a Jew, although he took little active interest in his people. He was Samuel Gompers. He had seen poverty and suffering among workers in London and New York, and worked for unions all his life. He was president of the

54. great American Federation of Labor from its second year (1882) until his death in 1924.

Another way in which some Jewish leaders sought to guide their people out of the unjust wages and bad conditions which they met in the factories, was through Socialism. The Yiddish newspaper, the Forward, edited by Abraham Cahan, taught the people their rights and raised their hopes for a system of life favorable to workers. Morris Hillquit, an able New York lawyer, became a leader in the Socialist Party and helped to win public acceptance of some of its proposals for labor. Although the 

Socialist Party has little influence in American life, today, it played a great part in the past and led the country to understand the need for such things. as Workmen's Compensation. This sets up a fund to pay the hospital and doctor bills of a workman injured at work, and also to give him money if his injury cannot be cured. 

The Socialist Party also led the way to unemployment insurance, old-age pensions, aid to widows, safety devices for workers, prevention of child labor, and many other laws to help the common people. 

THE ENTERTAINMENT WORLD 

The Russian Jewish immigrants advanced greatly in the field of entertainment. They helped especially to make motion pictures, a means of bringing high quality enjoyment to the masses of people, first in America, then throughout the world.

Many Jews were among the energetic and far-visioned men who built up the movies from its tiny beginnings to one of America's greatest industries, in which a third of a million people earn their livelihood. They were also among those who raised the standard of pictures from its early wild-west shooting days to a true art.

A typical example is that of Louis B. Mayer who was his own janitor when he opened his first show house in 1907. Later he established a chain of theaters in New England. Then he became a producer. As a partner in Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer, he introduced new types of pictures and photographic methods, as in the spectacular "Ben Hur. He and Samuel Goldwyn would spend enormous sums of money to make a picture just right and to bring many new stars to the screen. During the threat of Fascism to Democracy, they produced many pictures which woke people to the dangers of Hitlerism, and stirred in them a new and powerful love of American freedom.

Irving Thalberg devoted an all-too-brief lifetime to elevating the quality and dignity of motion pictues.

Greatest of all movie producers at present is David 0. 

Selznick, whose father had been one of the first producers in 1911. Selznick has given to the public the most careful reproductions of great books starting with his "David Copperfield.

His "Gone with the Wind cost $4,250,000 to produce and was seen by tens of millions of Americans.

;

NOVI

8: 

LISTA 

---

55. 

In the field of popular stage and radio entertainment Jewish people have shown talent and have won advancement. Comedy has seemed to be their favorite role, perhaps as a way of expressing their relief from the tragedy of Jewish life in Eastern Europe. 

Al Jolson, Eddie Cantor, Jack Benny, Jack Pearl have long held the American spot-light. Outstanding among the more serious actors of America is Paul Muni, whose early experience was gained in the Yiddish theater.

David Sarnoff, as general manager of the Radio Corporation of America since 1921, helped widen the influence and popularity of the radio.

Concert musicians, and especially violinists of such excellence as Jascha Heifetz and others whose names we have mentioned before, widen further the picture of the great personalities whom the East European immigration provided for the progress of entertainment in America.

BLENDING OF AMERICAN JEWRY 

By the 1920's and 30's American Jewry could no longer be thought of as made up of various immigrations: Spanish-Portuguese, then German, then Russian. For the groups were more and more mingling and blending into one. Descendants of one group married descendants of the other. Congregations which had long been of only German stock came to include many Jews of East European descent. All were becoming equally well Americanized. The old manner in which each group had looked down upon those who had come over after it had, was now beginning to be considered not only foolish but harmful. For these prejudices among the Jews themselves prevented them from working together for their common good. It became ever more clear that there were enormous tasks. to be accomplished together: to defend the name of the Jewish people against those who sought to attack it; to aid those Jews who were suffering because of war or because of persecution; to keep Judaism a living, growing religion in the new land. 

It was this Jewry that in the past score of years has enjoyed to the full, the freedom of the United States. It has advanced not only in the fields mentioned above, but in businesses and professions of all types. There is no large American city that does not have Jewish physicians, distinguished for their skill. Some of these men, such as Dr. Simon Flexner, Director of the Rockefeller Institute for Medical Research, have made important discoveries that have helped to cure dreaded diseases.

Jews have advanced in the practice of law, of dentistry, and, to a lesser extent, in public life and in engineering. 

The world of art called forth such creative talents as those of George Gershwin, composer of a distinctive blend of popular and classical music. Jewish playwrights won fame, among them, Elmer Rice, S. N. Behrman, and George Kaufman; Jewish poets, Louis Untermeyer and Robert Nathan; Jewish stylists, storywriters, and literary critics, Ludwig Lewisohn, Fannie Hurst,

(2

56. 

Edna Ferber, Waldo Frank. Freedom called forth the best in a talented people. Freedom let that best bring its gifts to benefit all.

58. VI. 

JEWS OF RUSSIA 

LIMITED FREEDOM 

To the millions of Jews of Russia, however, freedom did not come until much later than in the rest of Europe. Even then, it was only a limited freedom. It began in March, 1917, when the democratic Kerensky government overthrew the Czar. This government immediately abolished all the old laws that forced the Jews to live within the Pale of Settlement, kept them out of Universities, and held them down and insulted them in a thousand different ways.

The Jews were overjoyed. Jewish community organizations, long forbidden, were now formed again. Yiddish and Hebrew newspapers and book publishing houses sprang up. A modern Yiddish school system was organized, so was a great Hebrew system, called Tarbus. Zionism soon became an active leading force. Synagogues and religious schools became more modern and appealing. The 

Jewish labor movement grew. But this full freedom did not last long. The democratic Kerensky government could not keep its power. Russia, weakened by the tyranny of Czarist rule, could not stand up in the fight against the German war machine, and the Russians grew sick of the struggle. When the Kerensky government insisted on continuing the fight, whole regiments of soldiers refused to obey orders. Strikes and riots stopped industry. In industry. 

November, 1917, the Communists, under Nicolai Lenin, seized control. Their first step was to withdraw from the war against 

Germany. Even though it meant accepting a most disgraceful peace, it allowed the Communist government to turn to the task of rebuilding backward and neglected Russian industry and society. 

The Allies tried to stop Russia from withdrawing from the war and from organizing a Communist state. They sent armies to 

Russia to overthrow the new government and gave help to Russian groups who opposed Communism.

For five years civil war continued in Russia. But the new 

Red Army, organized by Leon Trotsky, finally crushed all opponents, and the Soviet government was firmly established. 

The most bitter fighting occurred in the western part of Russia and in the southern part of the Ukraine. Since most of 

Russian Jewry lived there, they suffered terribly. The anti- 

Communist forces of Denikin and Petlura used the old lie that Communism was a Jewish trick to destroy Christian Russia, and they whipped up pogroms against the Jews wherever they went. the midst of such civil war there was no organized government to give the people protection. In the horrible years of 1919-1921, over 100,000 Jews were slaughtered in hundreds of such pogroms throughout the Ukraine.

The victory of the Communists brought an end to this bloodshed. It also brought protection from anti-Semitism in general. 

For the Soviet government declared anti-Semitism to be a crime against the state and punished all those found guilty of it. In

59. 

The trials of such offenders were given great publicity. 

Besides this, over radio and press, by posters, movies, and lectures, the people were taught that anti-Semitism and all race prejudices stood in the way of Russian progress, and of the brotherhood of all workers which they sought to create. 

In this way, Russia, which up to that time had sinned the worst against its Jewish citizens, became the first government to make anti-Semitism a crime, and succeeded very largely in abolishing it.

But this did not mean that the' troubles of the two and a half million Jews of post-war Russia were over. They had just begun. This was because Communism brought with it not merely new freedom, but also many problems and restrictions. 

The first of these was the problem of finding new work under the Communist Dictatorship of the Workers. The whole middle class of merchants and owners of factories and businesses was to be eliminated. Russia wanted to be a society of workers and farmers, with the government owning all industries and land, and taking over the sale of goods to the people. 11 To do away with this merchant and owning group, the government took away most of the property and money of such people and then declared them to be "declassed. This meant that they did not have the same rights as other people. They could not become members of the Communist Party which controlled the state, they had to pay special taxes and higher rent, and their children could not go to good schools.

This program brought great suffering to all the people who were merchants and manufacturers. It was especially hard for the 

Jewish people since three-quarters of them were in this class. 

In the first years of Communism, it was impossible for many of these declassed Jews to get other kinds of work. Many became beggars, others depended upon more fortunate relatives, and some starved to death.

So poor was the condition of these people and their families that the Joint Distribution Committee (an American organization formed to help European war sufferers) spent $500,000 in one year (1928) just to give them medical care.

To fit these Jews to the working life of Communism, the government gave them several large tracts of land to settle on. 

With the help of the Agro-Joint, assisted by the Joint Distribution Committee, which loaned money for seeds, tools, and buildings, the Russian government succeeded in settling about a quarter of a million Jews on the land.

Another way for Russia's declassed Jews to become firstclass citizens was by becoming workers in industry. ORT, an organization of which we shall learn later, trained many Jews for

60. such work. Here, too, the Agro-Joint assisted, and in cooperation with the Jewish Colonization Association (ICA), it loaned money to build up cooperative factories for workers. The opportunity to labor in industry was greatly increased by Russia's Five-Year Plan, which began in 1928. It built great factories, hydro-electric work, and cities, to turn the weak and backward country into a progressive and well-armed power. 

A third way was by entering government service and the army. 

These services were now open to the Jewish people on a free and equal basis for the first time in Russian history. They attracted large numbers of Jews. Through their long tradition of study and by hard work at Russian training schools, many Jews became important officers in the government and in the army. 

Through these methods a normal place in the Soviet working system was finally found for most of the Jews in Russia. Provided with jobs and protected from anti-Semitism, Jews as individuals could now live as free a life in Russia as any other Soviet citizens. But the life of the Jewish people as a group was being destroyed because of certain ideas of Communism which we shall now consider.

COMMUNIST ATTACK UPON RELIGION 

One idea of Communism which made it difficult to continue Jewish life in Russia was its attack upon religion. "Religion is the opiate of the masses, the Communists said, They declared that religious worship and religious obedience are like drugs that put men to sleep, whereupon they close their eyes to the fact that the few who control society are using them as slaves. 

Without that drug, they said, the common man would rise up and throw off that oppression and slavery. 

Such teachings were indeed true with regard to the Church as it had carried on in Russia. It had encouraged the people to be contented with whatever they had, and had done nothing to better the condition of the terribly poor peasants and workers. But such statements are not true about all religion. Indeed, quite the contrary: true religion was the first to wake men out of accepting things as they are, and to call for the destruction of those who enslave and oppress the poor. 

Amos declared that fire would destroy the palaces of Jerusalem, "Because they sell the righteous (into slavery) for silver, And the needy for a pair of shoes (2:6). Isaiah expressed God's rebuke for the haughty, bejewelled "daughters of Zion" who think only of themselves (3:16-26), and declared that God would bring an end to them that join house to house, that lay field to field and thus rob the poor of their property. Indeed, thoughts such as these, right out of the Bible, drove people on to fight for Democracy and freedom, as we saw in our study of the French Revolution (pages 9ff.) and of the coming of freedom in America. 

But Communist leaders overlooked these facts and turned to

61. destroy all religion, good or bad, in their country. They prohibited the holding of religious classes for any children under eighteen by that time it was expected that the children's ideas already would be formed by the anti-religious instruction in the public schools. The State took over the ownership of all churches and synagogues and turned most of them into general clubs. 

Rabbis, religious teachers, and learned students were frequently sent into exile in Siberia, and sometimes executed. 

Although churches and synagogues were not all closed, people were discouraged from going to them by various means. Only members of the Communist Party, all of whom had to promise to oppose religion, had the best chance for advancement in their studies and in their work. On Yom Kippur, young Communists would hold public banquets, or conduct noisy gatherings against religion in front of synagogues. These often led to bloody fights, and it was usually the religious people that were sentenced to hard labor. Other laws made it hard to keep the Sabbath, the Holidays, and the dietary laws. By these methods, the Communists weaned most of the younger Russian Jews away from Judaism.

This was a heavy blow, for the Jewish religion had been the one great thing that had kept the Jews, scattered as they have been among other peoples, from disappearing. Without it, the great purpose for which they continued to live was taken away. 

THE ATTACK ON ZIONISM 

Even more vigorous was the attack of the Communists upon the Zionist movement. They said that it was up to the Jews to solve their problems wherever they lived by creating Communist states there. This would get rid of anti-Semitism and all forms of persecution. Besides, they declared, Zionism is used by England as a tool to keep control over the Arabs. 

Here, too, the Communists were wrong. It was very poor advice to tell Jews, starving to death in Polish ghettoes or being murdered by Hitler's storm troopers, to stay there until a revolution would come. The sensible thing was to save their lives at once by sending them to a land where Jews would be free men. Nor does Zionism help England oppress the Arabs. It has opposed taking advantage of the Arabs, and has insisted that the Jews, not Arabs, do the hard and poorly-paid labor for the building of the Jewish settlement. In fact, the chief reason why some 

Arab leaders have opposed Zionism is that it has shown examples of working together in farms and factories. This encourages the 

Arab workers to throw off the oppression of their rich Arab lords. 

All these facts seem to have meant nothing to the Communists. 

The Jewish Communists especially, known as the Yevsektsia, wanted Jews to work for the world revolution and do nothing to save their own people. Therefore all Zionist activities were ordered stopped. The money and property of the Zionist organizations were taken over by the government. Leaders were arrested. Many devoted Zionists continued their work in secret. But the Soviet

62. government searched them out and exiled them to Siberia, where many of them died.

For a time the Chaluts cooperative farms, training young Jews for farm life in Palestine, were allowed to continue because the people lived like those on other collective farms in Russia. 

Chaluts farms flourished throughout the country. Suddenly the government ordered the farms given up. When many of the Chalutsim who had pledged their lives to the rebuilding of Palestine continued to carry on, they were terribly persecuted. Thousands died of starvation or illness, in prison or in Siberia.

The crushing of Zionism in Russia, where the movement first began and grew, and from which the first pioneers, the Biluim, migrated, was another blow to Jewish group life in that land. 

THE ATTACK ON HEBREW 

The third blow came when the Soviet government forbade the use of the Hebrew language, except for study in the Universities, or for use in those synagogues that remained open. Hebrew was attacked for two reasons: first, it was the language of a religion, Judaism; and second, it was the language of the growing 

Palestine. Being opposed to both religion and Zionism, the Communists opposed Hebrew also. Russian Jews, trained in Hebrew by their religious study, and later by the Haskalah and the Zionist movement, had shown a great new interest in Hebrew books and education during the few months of the Russian Democracy. With the coming of Communism, all this was stopped. The greatest 

Hebrew writers left for Palestine. So did the Habimah, later, the Jewish theater of Moscow.

YIDDISH TO PRESERVE JEWISH GROUP LIFE 

In spite of these attacks upon Judaism, Zionism, and Hebrew, the Soviet government did not want to destroy Jewish group life. 

The Soviet program from the first has been to preserve the culture, language and pride of the different nationality groups that make up the Union of Socialist Soviet Republics. One of the reasons for the revolution was that the Czars oppressed these groups and tried to force them to accept the Russian language and customs. The Soviet government freed them from this. It divided the country into many Republics with smaller divisions. In each division, the majority group has its own language and schools, and so do the large minority groups. 

The Soviet government considered the Jews such a nationality, To strengthen them, it gave them autonomous (self-ruling) regions, with their own language and schools. It also gave them a large and fertile area in Siberia, just north of China, popularly called Biro-Bidjan, as the autonomous region, with official representatives on the Soviet Council of Nationalities. But the government insisted that the language used be Yiddish. It encouraged the growth of that language through a wide-spread Yiddish school system, with higher technical schools and large

63. institutes in which Jewish scholars studied and taught Yiddish literature, Jewish history and sciences in Yiddish. Yiddish books and newspapers were encouraged, and a Yiddish Art Theater opened in Moscow.

Despite all these efforts, however, the use of Yiddish among the Jewish people began to fall off rapidly. By 1939, only a small fraction of the Jewish people read Yiddish newspapers; only a fifth of the children attended Yiddish schools. Yiddish books, cultural activities, and Institutes declined and some were given up altogether.

Nor did the Jews show any great desire to live in separate self-ruling districts. More than half of the Jews who went to 

Biro-Bidjan returned to Western Russia. In 1938, only 20,000 

Jews lived there.

Cut off from the Jewish religion, from the Hebrew languagewhich contains the greatest books written by the Jewish people, over thousands of years and cut off from the Zionist movement, the Russian Jew no longer had any good reason for remaining a Jew. More and more he drifted into the large Russian cities where he was perfectly free to advance as an individual and to speak the Russian language of the majority. Up until the present war, Russia appeared to be a land in which Jewish men and women would live in safety and with equal rights, but in which Judaism and Jewish group life would disappear. 

CHANGES SINCE 1939 

Since the beginning of World War II, however, conditions in Russia have changed somewhat. In the first place, the German- 

Soviet agreement in August, 1939, gave Russia control over many lands on her western border, including half of Poland and part of Rumania. These areas included two million Jews. These lands were later conquered by the Nazis, in 1941, but many hundreds of thousands of their Jewish citizens fled eastward for safety into Russia. These Jews still have a very strong love of the Jewish religion, of Zionism, and of Hebrew. Their presence in Russia will help to check the disappearance of Jewish group life. 

In the second place, since Hitler's smash into Russia and Russia's alliance with Britain, America, and the United Nations against Hitlerism, there have been signs and hints that the Communist program may be changing. The Russian Jewish community for the first time since 1917 had dealings with the Jews of the rest of the world, calling upon them to unite against Hitler, who threatens them all with destruction. The Soviet government for the first time met with the leaders of Jewish Palestine and permitted hundreds of Polish Jewish children to leave for that land. 

It likewise encouraged the sending of food and clothing from Palestine to Jews of Russia. It sent over Jewish representatives to visit and address the Jews of America in the summer of 1943. 

Russian news stories stressed that the new constitution of 1936 allows freedom of worship, and described a large number of

64. In synagogues and rabbis that were still carrying on Judaism. 

1943 the government granted the Russian Orthodox Church permission to organize and elect a Synod (ruling committee). It was also reported that the government created an official body to cooperate with all religious organizations, including the Jewish. 

These moves toward greater freedom may become stronger as Russia continues to cooperate with the other United Nations. If they do, there is still a chance that Judaism and Jewish group life may continue among the millions of our people in that land.

66. 

VII. 

HOW DID THE DECLINE OF DEMOCRACY THREATEN JEWISH LIFE? 

The Russian Jews, as we have seen, were, at least up to the present war, cut off from their fellow Jews the world over. Let us now return to the story of the Jews of America and Western Europe who, when we left off telling of them, were living in peace and prosperity. Democracy was growing among the nations and seemed likely to spread further. Yet later, as everyone knows, the Jew in Europe suffered as he never did before. By 

1944, Hitler had already put three million of our people to death and millions more lived in slavery and starvation. What happened to turn such a glorious period into this terrible nightmare? The answer, in short, is that even long before Hitler came to power, Democracy with its promise of brotherhood and equality had been becoming weaker instead of stronger; and, as we have seen, the freedom of our people rises or falls hand in hand with Democracy. 

WHAT CAUSED DEMOCRACY TO DECLINE? 

Democracy has gone downward in the past twenty-five years, first of all because of poverty. When people do not have enough nourishing food to eat, or when they cannot get jobs for a long time, or their jobs are very uncertain, they become bitter and frightened. They give up their ideas of brotherhood and turn against anyone who seems to be "different. They are ready to blame him for their trouble, or to call him a "foreigner" and take his job or money away or even drive him out of the country. 11 

The growing factory system, you will recall (page 2) brought with it chances for more and more people to come in and make a good living, and to build up good business for everyone. 

This led men to welcome their neighbors (even of different races or religions) as brothers. But when conditions forced the factories to close down and everyone scrambled for the few jobs open, men began to look upon these same neighbors of different races or religions as "foreigners" who were taking away their jobs. 

POLAND 

Poland is a good example of how this happened, and how it brought harm to Democracy and misery to the Jewish people. After 

World War I, Poland had the largest number of Jews of any country in Europe three million. Poland had been part of Russia under the Czars. And although the Jews there had had a bad time under 

Russian tyrants, they had high hopes that things would be different after the war. For the peace treaty then gave Poland freedom to manage her own affairs. It also provided for "Minority 

Rights" to protect all smaller groups. These gave the Jews the right to have their own schools and law courts, and to use their own language. They also promised them equal treatment before the law. But above all, since the war had been won by the democratic allies, it brought the hope that true Democracy would rule in

67. more and more countries in the world. 

Poland did start out with a democratic form. She elected a Parliament and a Premier. But 

Democracy in Poland soon died. The reason for this was, first of all, poverty.

The war itself had killed so many soldiers and civilians and had destroyed so much property, that it had left Europe, especially Eastern Europe, poor. The poor farmers of Poland found it very hard to rise out of their poverty because one-third of the land and most of the money of Poland was owned by just a few people who pressed out large sums of money from the farmers and left them barely enough to keep body and soul together. 

Then too, after the war, the nations did not help each other build up good and prosperous times again. Each country thought only of itself, and that brought great suffering, especially in Poland. For Polish factories had produced quantities of shoes, leather saddles and gloves, and clothing of all kinds. Before the war, as part of Russia, she could sell these things to all parts of Russia. Germany and Austria, and other countries also bought these goods, as well as many products from Polish farms. 

After the war, however, this could not be done. Russia, now separated from Poland, sought to strengthen its new Soviet government, and wanted to enlarge its own factories and farms. 

Therefore it cut down its buying from other nations. Germany and 

Austria did the same thing. Polish shoe and clothing factories were cut off from their old customers and had to shut down. 

Their workers, now unemployed, did not have enough money to buy good food from the farmers. Therefore farms closed down. Farmers now had no money to buy shoes, and more shoe factories had to close, and more men were unemployed. The government had to give these jobless people a little money in charity just to keep them alive. But conditions continued to be bad, and the people were bitter.

The embittered people did two things: They threw out Democracy, calling for a dictator to lead them; and they began to oppress more and more the Jews in their country. When a Polish non-Jew would see a Jew doing some work, he would say, "why should this 'foreigner' have a job, when I, who am a 'real' Pole, cannot find work or food?" They then began to organize a boycott against Jewish workers and merchants, forgetting that the Jews were offered inducements by the government to settle in Poland. 

The government acted in the same spirit and crowded even more Jewish people out of their jobs. The government had been taking over many industries to run them under the control of the state. These included the making and selling of matches, tobacco, liquor, and salt, as well as the running of the mines, forests, trains and buses, and many factories. Jews, having been forced to live in the cities (see page 23) had been working very largely in these businesses. But when the government took them over, it hired practically no Jews back into the very factories and businesses which the Jews themselves had created! A Jew had been the tobacco king of Poland and most of his employees had been

68.

Jews. But when the government took over the tobacco industry, almost every Jew was thrown out of his job. 

By 1931, one-third of Poland's three million Jews had no work and no income at all. They kept alive only through small sums of unemployment relief from the government and through the soup kitchens which American Jews provided through the Joint Distribution Committee. Most of the children were sick or without enough food. Some of the parents committed suicide. 

SELFISH NATIONALISM 

In Poland we see a second thing which also caused Democracy to decline. This was "selfish nationalism. "Nationalism means patriotism, the love of a person for his country and his countrymen. Many of the greatest things in the world have been created through the power of this love, cities have been built, interesting books and beautiful music written. But "selfish nationalism" means that the love of one's own people has gone wild, so that one thinks only of his people, and other nations and groups no longer count.

Their rights need not be considered as long as one's own nation goes ahead.

This spirit has been growing in our world in the last century It is a warlike spirit; it helped to bring World War I, and became greater after it. In Poland it grew especially because this was a country filled with fear. For a century and a half its people had suffered as a divided people, ruled over by Prussia, Russia, and Austria-Hungary. Now, even though it was free, it feared that it might be divided and conquered again (as indeed it was in 1939). People of different nationalities were looked on with suspicion as possible enemies; their school systems were interfered with and some of their rights were taken away. The Jews were considered in the same class. 

Now some of you may recall that during this period the League of Nations was in existence, and it was supposed to give to the nations of the world "security" against attack, that is, a guarantee that no other nation would be allowed to invade and conquer it. That is true, and had the League really given that security, Poland could have relaxed and quieted down. Its nationalism could have lost its selfishness and perhaps, after a generation or two, the minorities within its midst would have been treated as friends and equals. But the League of Nations, unfortunately, was showing more and more that it was powerless to defend nations. Its various members were indicating that they would be unwilling to defend other nations against attack. 

Among the nations, therefore, the fear kept growing that they might some day have to fight for their very lives, And the greater the fear, the more selfish and unreasonbable became the nationalism of the people, and the worse they treated the minorities within their midst. Finally, within the Polish Parliament itself, statesmen spoke of the Jews as "surplus population, that is, people who were not needed and should be sent somewhere
