99. (Indeed, Palestinians sometimes gave the very shirts off their backs to clothe these "illegal" immigrants who arrived half naked and almost drowned on their shores at night.) Many Jews perished in their desperate attempts to reach "home." Of five hundred, who with no government certificates and little money, tried to walk all the way from Russia, only five arrived there. Even these were caught and thrown into prison for coming in "illegally."

ARABS RIOT AND BRITAIN GIVES IN 

How, you must be wondering, could it be "illegal" for a Jew to come to his own home land! And this, at a time when his people wanted him there, and when it meant death at the hands of Hitler to be refused admission!

The answer is that the British government, holder of the Mandate over Palestine, failed to make clear to itself and to the Arab world that a Jewish Homeland was necessary and just, and that she was determined to see it established. The nations had clearly agreed to this following World War I, including the official representative of the Arabs, Emir Feisal. But the 

British, in carrying out this Mandate of the League of Nations, followed a policy of giving in, little by little, to those Arab groups who objected to the Homeland. After every Arab disturbance, Britain would yield to the Arabs, cutting down on Jewish rights. Indeed, she began by completely cutting off from 

Jewish settlement, Trans jordania, or two-thirds of historic Palestine.

This was really the method of "appeasement, "appeasement," the same policy of giving in which had allowed Hitler to become so terribly strong. This was the method which Secretary Anthony Eden condemned when he said that you cannot quiet an angry tiger by feeding him small pieces of raw meat. The tiger, of course, becomes all the stronger and more demanding. That's what happened in 

Europe. It happened in Palestine, too. 

Those Arabs who believed in violence and terror became bolder and bolder. Their party was under the direction of Haj 

Amin al Husseini, the organizer of the first riots, in 1920. At that time, the British rewarded him by making him Grand Mufti, with complete control over the religious institutions of Moslem Palestine, as well as over their rich religious funds. In this position, the Mufti constantly used the mosques, schools, and Arab newspapers to stir up the people against the Jews. He said that they planned to enslave, drive out, and even murder the Arabs. The British government did not stop this propaganda. She

In 1936 Italy supported the Arab terrorists by broadcasting programs in Arabic against the British and against Zionism. gave the terrorists guns and money, according to some reports. 

Italy's purpose was to keep Britain in trouble in Palestine, so that she would not be free to stop Italy's conquest of Ethiopia.

C J J 21 J708 [... 19.18

100. 

In April, 1936, the Arab terrorists struck, robbing motorists along the highways and murdering two Jews. They set about burning down Jewish crops and forests. They announced that all Arabs would stop work until the government would stop Jewish immigration. The more moderate Arabs who opposed the murders, the destruction of property, and the strike, were threatened and many were killed. From the very first, the terrorists were as murderous against Arab moderates as they were against the Jews. homes of the moderate Arab mayor of Haifa was bombed, and the mayor of Hebron, who objected to the strike, was murdered. The more reasonable Nashishibi, the Mufti's rival as leader of all Moslem Palestine, was finally murdered by the Mufti's men in Irak. The For months the British government tried its old policy of doing nothing to offend the Arabs. The Mufti continued to draw his salary from the government, and to use it traveling about the land stirring up violence. Over a friendly cup of tea, the High 

Commissioner would beg the terrorists to stop. Such weakness made them all the bolder.

Finally, after hundreds of lives had been lost and 200,000 precious trees had been uprooted, even the British lost patience. 

They proclaimed martial law and brought in troops who quickly brought the six-months strike to an end. The Arabs, whose businesses had been ruined, were glad to go back to real work again, and to be free from the gangs that had threatened them and blackmailed them for money.

A Royal Commission, sent to investigate the troubles, made its report in 1937. It admitted that every important claim that the Jews had made was ture; that the Jews truly needed the land, and that it brought new freedom and confidence to the Jewish people; that they made the land produce enough to support many more people, and advanced its education and health. The Commission agreed also that the Homeland brought great benefits to the Arabs as shown by the fact that the Arab population had grown from 600,000 to 950,000 since 1920. This increase came only in the areas where the Jews had entered, and was due largely to the health services brought to the Arabs by the Jew. In areas where no Jews had entered, the Arab population showed no increases. 

Arab wealth and prosperity was also increased. Wages were much higher than before. Arab exports of citrus fruits multiplied sixfold. The peasants were better off and their taxes were less. 

The Arab claim that they were being made "landless" and poor by the Homeland was shown to be utterly false by the findings of the Commission.

But after admitting all these things, the Commission simply pushed aside reasons of fairness and need, and yielded to the Arab argument of terror. The mandate was unworkable," it declared, because of the Arab objection to it. Therefore it proposed that Palestine be divided up, with one-fifth becoming a Jewish state, seven-tenths, an Arab state to be joined to Transjordania and one-tenth to remain under British rule. 

---

'T

101. 

The Zionist organization was deeply shocked. Yet it wanted to keep some place open to rescue Jews from Hitlerism. Therefore, it pleaded with the British government for a partition plan more favorable to the Jews.

The Arabs, however, turned down the plan altogether. They had gotten this much by force, they would get it all by more force. In September of 1937, Lewis Andrews, beloved high British official, and his bodyguard were murdered. Britain replied by imprisoning some of the Arab leaders and sending others out of the country. The Grand Mufti escaped to Syria. But the Arab terrorism was begun on a larger scale than before. Jews and 

British officials were shot at in the roads, houses were bombed, and fields and forests burned. 11 The Jewish response to the two years of terrorism had been magnificent. Havlagah, meaning "self-restraint, was their slogan. Defend yourselves like lions when attacked, but do not strike back at innocent Arabs. We will win by building, and more building, and letting nothing interfere with our building. On dangerous roads through hills full of ambushes, Jewish bus drivers continued to carry men and food back and forth. New

Jewish colonies in land surrounded by Arabs and miles from any Jewish help were built in a single day. They had stockades of sand about them, defying the terrorists, as if to say, "Here is the proof that we will go straight ahead with our peaceful labor."

But at last some Jews lost patience with the unbearable situation. In 1938, a member of the Revisionist group fired on an Arab bus. Although no one was hurt, he was sentenced to death and hanged. The Revisionists, who had long attacked the policy of the Zionist Organisation as being too weak and moderate, now refused to keep the official policy of self-restraint. 

That is all the Arab terrorist leaders needed to stir up their people to new frenzy. Arabs began attacking British troops with machine guns and organized bands. England was at last forced to take real action. She brought in large numbers of troops, armored equipment, and fighting planes and conducted a real war against the terrorists. For having delayed so long she now had to go so far as to blow up the houses of Arabs in villages which hid the terrorists. The Arab revolt was put down, But this did not mean that Prime Minister Chamberlain and those in control of British policy were taking a firm stand to maintain the Balfour Declaration. On the contrary, these were the same leaders who, at this time, were employing the policy of appeasement with Hitlerism. Now they tried to use the same method with the Arab leaders. Fearing the coming of war in Europe, and knowing that they were not well prepared, the British leaders sought to avoid further trouble with the Arabs in Palestine and with the Mohammedan world in general, by giving in to them. 

Therefore, early in 1939, they proclaimed an official document called a 'White Paper. In it they said that the promise of the 

Balfour Declaration had already been fulfilled; that for the next

five years only 75,000 more Jews would be allowed into Palestine; and that only certain lands could be sold to the Jews. After that, Jews could come in only if they had the permission of the Arabs. After ten years, the Arabs, as the majority people, would be given greater and greater rule over the land and the British would gradually withdraw their control. 

The Jews protested vigorously against the White Paper, which shut off their hopes for a Jewish Homeland and declared that it broke the promise given to the Jews in the Balfour Declaration. Many 

English statesmen, including Winston Churchill, publicly agreed with the Jews and condemned the White Paper in Parliament, but 

Lord Halifax, then Foreign Secretary of Great Britain, said that "Ethical principles must give way to administrative necessity."

In other words, fairness had to be forgotten in the hope of keeping the Arabs on the British side in the coming war. 

The Jews prepared to resist the White Paper and to stop paying taxes and importing articles into Palestine. But when Hitler plunged the world into war in September, 1939, the Jewish people realized that this fight would decide the very existence of Israel. Therefore, it promised Britain that in spite of the disagreement, it would do all in its power to help her win. 

Jewish Palestine did just that, as we have seen. And

The coming of the war brought an end to the conflict in Palestine. Jews and Arabs began to work together again. Some even fought together in British regiments, Arabs started to attend the Hebrew University again. When Tel-Aviv was bombed by the Italians, Arab mayors expressed their sympathy. Jews and Arabs were joined together in common suffering and in common hatred of the Axis in the numerous bombings of Haifa. 

British policy in Palestine, however, did not change. At the very time when Jews were dying to save Britain and Democracy everywhere, British officials caused the death of thousands of Jews by refusing to admit them into Palestine. Ship-loads of 

Jews, fleeing from Nazi massacres throughout Europe, and especially in Rumania, set out for the Jewish Homeland only to meet tragic ends.

The most tragic of all was the old ship "Struma" which set out early in 1942 with 750 Jewish refugees fleeing the pogroms in Rumania. They wanted to reach Palestine. Lucky to escape with their lives, they of course had no visas of any kind. Therefore, when they arrived at Istanbul, the Turkish government refused to allow them to land and made them stay for weeks in the harbor. 

During this time, the Jewish Agency tried to secure permission for them to come to Palestine. This the British government refused, although it was warned that such refusal would mean certain death to the refugees, who would either have to go back to Nazi lands or sink at sea in their tiny, unsafe ship. Finally the ship was ordered to be towed out of the harbor, although the captain said it was not seaworthy. When it reached the open sea, the ship went to pieces and all but two of the 750 refugees lost their lives.

ape زاد 3 ぱい ​-901- சிக் 92

Other unseaworthy ships sank on their way to Palestine with large loss of life. Some of their survivors finally did reach 

Palestine only to be kept in prison camps there for having entered in the country illegally. Some of these were then taken out of prison in the middle of night and shipped off to the British Island of Mauritius in the Indian Ocean, where many died. 

In November, 1940, 1,770 Jews who had escaped from the Nazis were not permitted to enter Palestine, but were put on board the ship "Patria" to be deported to Africa, The ship was then destroyed by a mysterious explosion, in the Haifa harbor and 250 lost their lives, Perhaps some of the refugees who were frantic with fear, knowing what had happened to other ships, themselves blew up the "Patria" to awaken the world to their terrible situation. In any case, American and British citizens protested vigorously and those who survived the explosion were permitted to remain in Palestine.

As the time grew near for the White Paper to go into effect (April, 1944) the Jews of the world stirred the democratic nations to realize how unfair this action would be. They pointed out that the need for a Jewish Homeland was greater than it had ever been before, for after the war hundreds of thousands of Jews would need a place to start life again under favorable conditions, free from the fears of new persecutions and the memories of the old. They pointed also to the great war service Jews had contributed to the democratic nations from the beginning of the fight. 

Knowing that the plans for the post-war world were progressing rapidly as the armies of the United Nations advanced toward victory, the Zionists became more and more insistent that a proper basis of political rights, recognized by the world, be given to the Jewish Homeland. They therefore urged that Palestine should be "established as a Jewish Commonwealth."

DIFFERENCES OF OPINION 

Certain non-Zionist leaders in America, however, opposed this insistence. Some said that it was not the proper time to try to define the rights that Jews should have in Palestine in the future, and that all that should be done at that time was to try to secure the repeal of the White Paper so that immigration to Palestine could be kept open. Some others said that it would be wrong for Jews to establish a Jewish commonwealth in Palestine at any time because it might lead non-Jews in America to look upon American Jews as a separate nation instead of full American citizens.

THE AMERICAN JEWISH CONFERENCE 

Because this difference of opinion was tending to confuse the leaders of the nations of the world as to what the Jews themselves wanted, Henry Monsky, President of B'nai B'rith, called for an American Jewish Conference to express the will of American Jewry in unmistakable form by democratic means. The Conference

-

was composed of 502 delegates, 125 of whom had been chosen by sixty-five national Jewish organizations to represent them, and 377 of whom had been elected by the hundreds of Jewish communities in America on the basis of one delegate to approximately every ten thousand Jews.

Meeting in New York City in September, 1943, the Conference adopted a resolution calling "for the fulfillment of the Balfour Declaration and of the Mandate for Palestine, whose intent and underlying purpose, based on the historical connection of the Jewish people with Palestine, was to reconstitute Palestine as the Jewish Commonwealth." The resolution was adopted by an overwhelming vote, with but four votes cast against it, and a few other delegates not voting. The Conference thus demonstrated clearly the majority opinion of American Jewry on this issue. 

APPLIED FOR THE IMMEDIATE RESCUE OF JEWS 

There were other purposes of this historic American Jewish Conference. One of these was to secure action from the United 

Nations in rescuing at once as many Jews as possible from the lands where Hitler was exterminating them. Jews had long before pointed out that it was not necessary to wait until Hitler would be defeated to rescue many from his death chambers. Neutral nations bordering occupied Europe could be encouraged to let Jews and other persecuted peoples cross the border, especially if the United Nations would provide funds to care for such people, and transportation for them to other lands. This immediate need had already been brought before the American people in many ways: by full-page advertisements in the great New York newspapers; by a stirring drama "We shall Never Die," written by Ben Hecht and presented in several large cities; by emergency meetings called to tell the public of Hitler's annihilation program; by resolutions addressed to the government by various Jewish organizations. 

Finally these efforts brought some results. In January, 1944, President Roosevelt created the War Refugee Board for this purpose. Directed by the Secretaries of State, Treasury, and 

War, the Board was to rescue at once as many as possible of the various peoples, including Jews, that were being murdered by Hitler. Up to the time of the writing of this book, however, not nearly as much as had been hoped for was accomplished by this Board.

At the same time, a group of prominent Americans, led by 

Justice Frank Murphy of the United States Supreme Court, formed the National Committee Against Nazi Persecution and Extermination of the Jews. Its purpose was to win public support for the government's rescue program, and to fight anti-Semitic propaganda in America.

PETITIONED FOR JEWISH RIGHTS AFTER THE WAR 

The Jewish people sought to help itself also by planning for more secure rights in the post-war world. Surely in the better

སཛྫཱ r. SPO 1 GJ G. udd. La 70 ', "{ 012 ... 104 03

105. world to be established after the war, the Jews deserved governmental protection which would keep away the unfair boycotts, the terrible propaganda and persecution which had made their recent life a nightmare. Various Jewish organizations, even at the start of the war, began to study and draw up programs for such rights. 

It would be a mistake, everyone recognized, however, to have many Jewish groups come before the Peace Conference, each with its own proposals. Therefore, one of the chief purposes of the 

American Jewish Conference was to bring all American Jewish organizations together to plan a single complete and consistent program for Jewish rights. This program, then, worked out with the Jews of other lands, would have a strong influence upon the peacemakers, for they would realize that it comes with the support of the great majority of the Jews of the world. 

Although the details of this program have not been worked out, these general principles were adopted by the 1943 meeting of the Conference: 1. 1. 2. 3. 4. 

An "International Bill of Rights," giving full equality of the law to all citizens, regardless of nationality, language, race, or religion.

Anti-Semitism to be declared illegal, within and among nations. 

Permission for all refugees to return to their old homes. 

When this is not possible, repayment for property and money stolen from them, and for the loss of income that they suffered because of the persecuting laws. 

Punishment of all persons who committed crimes against international law and against humanity. (Such punishment of Axis murderers, terrorists and thieves had been promised some time before by the United Nations and had already been begun in the case of a few men tried and convicted by the Soviet Government.) 

food, clothing, 5. Special relief for persecuted peoples shelter, medical care, and help in uniting families. 

6. Help for Jews to regain old occupations, or to train them and provide opportunities for new ones. 7. 

Permission for every Jew who desires to go to Palestine to leave and take his possessions with him.

X. 

FIGHTING FOR THE VICTORY OF DEMOCRATIC IDEALS 

You might imagine now that all these efforts would certainly be enough to make sure that the Jews of the world will live in peace and happiness after the war. But you would be wrong. The

Jewish people worked for just the same things after the last war. 

And they seemed to have gained most of them: 1) They fought and helped win for the democratic Allies. 2) They cared for their suffering brothers in Europe. 3) They won the Balfour Declaration which gave them rights in Palestine. And 4) The League of Nations promised them minority rights and protection for Jewish individuals. Yet, as you know, in spite of all these things, Jews suffered terribly after World War I. Why? Because the spirit of Democracy throughout Europe grew dim and weak. And as it died, there died with it the idea of fairness and equal justice for all peoples. It is clear, then, that all these rights and efforts to help our people will fail unless along with these, we can help keep up the democratic spirit of the world. 

How can that spirit be strengthened? In our study of Jewish history, we learned that certain factors encouraged the spread of Democracy among men in modern times. The first of these was a new and freer system of producing the world's goods. This broke down the old system of lords and serfs, with its slavery and poverty for the average man. The new system brought opportunities for more and more men to advance and have a better life. couraged people to join in producing greater amounts of goods. 

Thus the old attitude of looking with suspicion on "foreigners" gave way to the welcoming of all people to join hands in building prosperity for all. 

It en A secona condition that strengthened the democratic spirit was the Renaissance and the development of printing, which brought a much wider education to the common people than they had ever had before. The spirit of the Renaissance was to study the great books and works of art of various peoples of the past. The pur- pose was to build a new and better day upon the foundation of all that men had learned before. These studies brought respect and a sympathetic attitude toward peoples of other lands and other religions.

Some of the great writings of the past, especially those of the Bible and the Greek philosophers, made this attitude clear to all, insisting that all men must be considered equal and be given a chance to develop their best talents. 

Through these thoughts, nations came to proclaim freedom for all religions and free and equal treatment for all men, which is Democracy.

We learned that since the last war, however, these conditions were reversed. Instead of prosperity and a chance for all men to work and advance, a period of poverty and unemployment and bitter-

J 23

109. ness swept over the world. This led men to hate one another, and to hate especially anyone who seemed "different. "Why should that foreigner have a job, when I go hungry?" became the attitude. 

Under such conditions, the true and noble ideas of Democracy declined and died.

Then, too, instead of respect for other lands, nations began to think only for themselves. Defeated Germany kept telling itself that it was still the greatest nation and would conquer the world. Other smaller nations feared that another war would come, and thought, "We had better look out for ourselves and drive all the foreigners out of here. 

Poverty plus selfish nationalism worked hand in hand to produce Fascism, which put an end to Democracy in many lands. 

JOBS AND SECURITY 

If we are to rebuild the democratic ideals of the world, we must first help every nation conquer poverty, unemployment, and bitterness. Men must have work, a chance to live better, and the knowledge that they will not have to go hungry in the future. 

The Jews of America have long joined their hands with other forward-looking leaders in efforts to rid America of lack of work, We have already read of the successes of Jewish labor leaders. Louis D. Brandeis, who was the first Jew to become 

Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States, first gained fame as the "People's Lawyer. For he labored in public speeches and in court for those things which would raise the living conditions and security of all the people. He defended the first law which protected women from being overworked in factories. kept the people from being overcharged by great corporations, especially the railroads. He helped garment workers secure better conditions. He worked out plans for reasonable insurance through savings banks, and for old-age pensions for workers. He

The two other Jews who became Justices of the United States Supreme Court likewise worked to protect the rights and income of the common person. These were Benjamin N. Cardozo and Felix 

Frankfurter. The latter was Chairman of the War Labor Policies Board during World War I. As Professor of Law at the Harvard Law 

School he trained many students in his policies for the advancement of the average man. Some of these students became good administrators for the government during the New Deal. 

Felix Frankfurter, as well as many other Jewish leaders, gave great service to President Roosevelt and the Federal Government when it set up the New Deal. This was its plan to give work to people without jobs during the terrible depression, following 1929. At this time, about twelve million American workers had no employment. Democracy might have been destroyed here too, even as it was in Europe, had not the government taken strong action. It gave these people work through building roads, schools, and airports, and improving buildings and parks. Businesses were

by and

helped by loans of money. Deposits in banks were protected by the government, so that people would not lose their money again as they did in 1933 when the banks had to close. Finally a great system of Social Security was put into effect throughout the country. Through it, workers and employers and the government save up money which is used to carry workers over periods of unemployment and to give them pensions to live on when they get too old to work. Such insurance gives the worker a feeling of confidence about the future, and takes away one of the causes of bitterness and ill will toward others. Leading social workers, including two Jews - Abraham Epstein and I. M. Rubinow had long urged the government to adopt it. 

The Rabbinical Associations, through their Commissions on Social Justice, have also labored for many years to encourage the country to make provisions for workers out of jobs because of business depression, sickness, or old age. 

When President Roosevelt and Prime Minister Churchill issued the Atlantic Charter, thinking people were glad to see that it sought to give all nations a chance to share in the prosperity of the world and to provide workers everywhere with food, jobs, and social security. 

As the beginning of this attempt, the Democracies organized the United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation Administration 

(UNRRA). Its great task, is to bring food and medicine to the suffering people in all lands as soon as the United Nations free them from Fascist slavery. It also helps the people to rebuild their homes, hospitals, and factories, and to begin to raise their own food again. This first step toward conquering poverty and bitterness all over the world is directed by a Jew, Herbert 

H. Lehman, former Governor of New York State. 

INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION 

Besides food and work, we have seen that people need to have friendly attitudes toward other nations, if the democratic spirit is to live. Selfish nationalism must be overcome. 

This can happen only if nations truly seek to work together for the good and peace of all. The United Nations has declared that it proposes to form a World Organization for this purpose. 

All nations who believe in the cooperative spirit will be allowed to join. 

Those who do not will be kept from attacking their neighbors by the armed control of the World Organization. A great educational program must likewise be carried out to teach men everywhere how selfish nationalism leads to war and the destruction of mankind and its property.

It should not be too difficult to teach this, with the terrible example of what has just happened before us. 

Many types of organizations are now planning to carry out this educational work. The churches and synagogues have been especially active in it. In 1943 the leaders of Judaism, Protestantism, and Catholicism in America issued a joint statement on World Peace, urging all peoples to recognize the moral law of God,

111. to cooperate in setting up a World Organization to settle disputes and keep peace, and to help each other provide jobs and security for men everywhere.

EQUALITY AND RESPECT FOR ALL MEN 

A third method of strengthening the ideals of Democracy is to appeal directly to men to respect and understand men of races and religions that differ from their own, and to treat them fairly.

Jews have attempted to do this from the first days of their settlement in America. You will recall that Asser Levy (page 43) insisted that he be allowed to stand guard against the Indians like all other men of New Amsterdam. Later, important Jewish organizations, such as the Board of Delegates of American Israelites (page 49) took up the task of insisting upon equal rights for Jews.

In 1906, the American Jewish Committee was formed to speak for Jewish equality throughout the world. It was led for many years by Louis Marshall, a prominent New York attorney, who exercised great influence in all Jewish affairs until his death in 1929. The B'nai B'rith, through its Anti-Defamation League, with offices in large American cities, has also carried on the same type of work, often in cooperation with the Committee. 

These organizations have sought not merely to protect the Jew, but to speak up for the basic ideals of Democracy for all men. 

They have published books and leaflets, sent out speakers, and arranged numerous radio broadcasts for this purpose. Another organization supporting this effort has been the American Jewish Congress, formed at first to give American Jews democratically elected representatives to the Peace Conference in 1920. The Union of American Hebrew Congregations likewise carried on this endeavor through publishing popular pamphlets entitled "The Truth about the Jew and distributing them to non- 

Jewish groups.

One very important place where the movement for true Democracy has recently made progress has been in the field of jobs. 

Many large factories had always refused to give employment to Jews or to Negroes. The President was encouraged, therefore, to establish a Fair Employment Practice Committee. Its purpose was to efforce the government's war-time ruling that prohibited manufacturers making goods for the government from refusing to hire people on account of their race or religion. Although the progress in correcting this long-standing violation of true Democracy was very slow, the Committee did secure equal privileges of work in many new places. Certain states, such as New York, passed laws for the same purpose, and the City of New York took steps to eliminate statement that only Gentiles need apply in the help-wanted advertisements of its newspapers. 

In their calling for true democratic principles, Jewish religious groups have asked the country time and again to give

112. fairer opportunities to its Negro citizens. In a special message on Lincoln's birthday, 1944, the Commission on Justice and Peace of the Central Conference of American Rabbis pointed to the unequal conditions under which Negroes must live, and to the fact that many who "might contribute much to the war effort are denied the privilege of giving their skills to their country. We appeal to all Americans to remedy this grave injustice to millions of decent fellow citizens whose history is bound up with that of our country. We hope that the coming peace will guarantee to men of all races that equality which is man's God-given right." 

COOPERATION OF RELIGIOUS GROUPS 

Since the ideal of the worth and right of every man was first expressed by religion and grows directly out of the belief that one God is the Father of us all, the religious leaders of America realized that it was chiefly their task to keep this great thought before the people. They therefore sought to join their efforts that they might be stronger in unity. Thus the 

Synagogue Council was formed through which all Orthodox, Conservative, and Reform Synagogues work together in actions that can strengthen the religious spriit of all the Jews of America. actions included joint campaigns to enlist all the Jews of a community as members of one synagogue or another, appeals to draft boards and arms factories to allow Jews to observe religious holidays, the setting of days of prayer and fasting for suffering Jews in Europe, and many other projects. Such

To bring an understanding of the Jewish religion and its universal ideals of brotherhood before college audiences, made up mostly of non-Jewish students, the Jewish Chautauqua Society carried out a wide program, arranging for rabbis and Jewish scholars to address over two hundred colleges and universities a year.

Interfaith Institutes were likewise established by many Jewish congregations throughout the country. These brought Jewish scholars before Christian ministerial groups to present the history and meaning of the Jewish faith. The Union of American 

Hebrew Congregations, the Hebrew Union College, and the Jewish Theological Seminary with their renowned scholars, encouraged these institutes, as well as other means to widen mutual understanding among religions.

Similarly, in 1928, the National Conference of Christians and Jews was founded. Its purpose has been to advance understanding and cooperation between Catholics, Protestants, and Jews. It declares clearly that, although no group gives up in any way its own distinctive interpretation of religion, all of them are agreed that religion must play a great role in preserving the ideals of America, and that all groups should give each other encouragement and help in this great common purpose. Through the 

Conference, Brotherhood Week was established throughout the country. During the week of Washington's Birthday, Churches and 

Synagogues unite in programs for Democracy. Radio plays are

Teams of speakers representing the three great faiths have toured the country, speaking before schools and army camps, leading the groups to understand and respect each other, and preaching the doctrine that men are brothers because God is our Father.

More and more people are realizing that there must be a basis of jobs for all and peaceful, friendly relations among nations, for the great religious ideals of freedom and equality to grow strong. Therefore American Jewry is confident that Democracy will win out and flourish after this war as never before. 

And with it will come happiness and safety to all peoples. 

CONCLUDING NOTE 

Dear Student:

This book, like most others, has been written with an eye to the future. You are in that future, many months, or even years after this note was written. You alone know whether the struggle between oppression and freedom, between the spirit of the Swastika and the hope represented by the Mogen Dovid has finally been ended on the battle-field. But even after the armed conflict is over, and the Swastika and its supporters are overpowered, the struggle will continue to go on in the hearts of men. 11

The impulses to keep down those who never had a chance, to enslave those who seem weak, to despise and persecute those who seem "different, rise up in every age of history. They are part of a pattern of thought that considers men essentially selfish, low creatures with no hope for improvement; therefore, it declares be realistic, forget the other fellow and get whatever you can for yourself. 

This pattern must be fought in every age by those who believe that man is essentially great, and blessed by God with the possibility of purifying himself of selfishness and old-time hatreds, and that society can advance to more peaceful and just ways. We the Jewish people were the first to give to the world this unyielding belief in a better future. We have lived for it, fought for it, suffered for it, and often died for it. But we know that this is our glorious destiny, and that by living as the spokesman of freedom in every aspect of our lives, we are fulfilling the historic task which God assigned to us from the day when He spoke to the very first Jew, Abraham, saying: "And through thee, all the families of the earth shall be blessed. (Signed)

JACOB S. GOLUB 

(Signed)

ALAN S. GREEN
